# Modern Indian History Flashcards

> One flashcard per examinable item in the repository Modern Indian History owners (basic + advanced), plus a concept card per owner file. IDs: MOD-1001 onward by agent block.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia and other pages that loaded while building; confirmed items carry a ✅ source tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the cards. Time-sensitive facts carry as-of dates from the owner.

---

## MODERN INDIAN HISTORY — Part 1: 18th Century to British Rule, Expansion and Administration

Prepared 2026-11-01. Sources: repository owners `upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Modern-Indian-History/basic/` and `advanced/` (files 01–08), with fetched checks where noted. Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` owner-grounded; `(✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01)` fetched; `⚠️` unverified or owner-vs-source conflict (flagged inside the card).

### GROUP — The Decline of the Mughal Empire (1707–1740s)

### Flashcard MOD-1001 — Concept: Mughal decline as structural, gradual fragmentation
**Front:** Why did the Mughal Empire decline, and was the decline sudden?
**Definition (learn):** A gradual loss of enforceable central command from 1707, driven by succession wars, noble factionalism, a jagir shortfall and external shocks. The dynasty survived as a symbolic order.
**Breakdown:**
- Structural core: jagirdari/be-jagiri crisis (MOD-1010)
- Political: weak successors and court factions (MOD-1004)
- Fiscal-military: Deccan over-stretch (MOD-1017)
- Regional: successor states took autonomy (MOD-1012 to MOD-1014)
- External: Nadir Shah 1739, Abdali 1748–61 (MOD-1007, MOD-1009)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Aurangzeb died 1707 (✅ owner 01). Authority, not existence, collapsed; the dynasty formally continued to 1857 (✅ owner 01, factual-risk note).
**Links:** MOD-1002, MOD-1010, MOD-1022, MOD-1025.
**Trap:** "Collapsed in 1707" is wrong. The empire fragmented over decades (✅ owner 01).
**Mains hook:** "The decline was multi-causal and gradual; invasions exposed it." Qualified verdict: structural primacy with external shock as accelerant (✅ owner 01 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Decline from strength: the centre weakened by degrees, the throne stayed."*

### Flashcard MOD-1002 — Aurangzeb's death and the succession war (1707)
**Front:** What happened after Aurangzeb died in 1707?
**Definition (learn):** Aurangzeb died at Ahmadnagar in 1707; a war of succession among his sons opened the decline (✅ owner 01 Adv).
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: no settled succession among sons
- Course: war of succession; Bahadur Shah I took the throne 1707
- Outcome: weakening of central authority
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1707 death; Bahadur Shah I (Shah Alam I) 1707–12 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1001, MOD-1003.
**Trap:** The 1707 date marks the start of decline, not the end of the dynasty (1857 is the formal end; ✅ owner 01).
**Mains hook:** Use the succession war as the first-stage cause, then show that structural factors persisted beyond the personal failure of rulers.
**Back:** *"Seventeen-oh-seven: the father dies, the sons fight, the throne weakens."*

### Flashcard MOD-1003 — Bahadur Shah I and the policy of compromise (1707–12)
**Front:** What was Bahadur Shah I's policy and why did it not reverse decline?
**Definition (learn):** A conciliatory ruler who sought compromise with Marathas, Rajputs and Sikhs (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Policy: accommodation, not structural reform
- After him: weak rulers dependent on nobles
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1707–12 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1002, MOD-1004.
**Trap:** Conciliation is not a sign of strength; it did not fix fiscal or noble problems (✅ owner 01 §1).
**Mains hook:** Compromise with rising regional powers acknowledged their force rather than restoring imperial command.
**Back:** *"Made peace, fixed nothing."*

### Flashcard MOD-1004 — The Sayyid Brothers as "king-makers"
**Front:** Who were the Sayyid Brothers and what did they do?
**Definition (learn):** Abdullah Khan and Husain Ali, Hindustani-faction nobles who raised and deposed emperors, notably Farrukhsiyar (1713–19) (✅ owner 01 and 01 Adv).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: king-makers, not emperors
- Effect: throne became a prize for court factions
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Farrukhsiyar 1713–19; four emperors enthroned within 1719 (✅ owner 01 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1005, MOD-1011, MOD-1023.
**Trap:** The Sayyids were powerful nobles, not rulers (✅ owner 01 §5).
**Mains hook:** Use them to show sovereignty passing from emperor to whoever controlled guard and treasury.
**Back:** *"Sayyids made kings, not kingdoms."*

### Flashcard MOD-1005 — Farrukhsiyar (1713–19) and the 1717 farman
**Front:** Who was Farrukhsiyar and why is he linked with the English Company?
**Definition (learn):** The Mughal emperor 1713–19, raised and deposed by the Sayyid Brothers; in 1717 he issued the farman granting the Company duty-free trade in Bengal (✅ owner 01 Adv).
**Breakdown:**
- Personal: raised by Sayyids, deposed by them
- Company link: 1717 farman, see MOD-1015
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1713–19 reign; 1717 farman (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1004, MOD-1015.
**Trap:** The farman granted trade privileges, not territory or sovereignty (✅ owner 01 §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** An imperial concession made from weakness became the fiscal grievance behind the Anglo-Bengal conflict.
**Back:** *"Seventeen-seventeen farman: trade favours, not a throne."*

### Flashcard MOD-1006 — Muhammad Shah "Rangila" (1719–48)
**Front:** What characterised Muhammad Shah's long reign?
**Definition (learn):** A long reign (1719–48) during which court factionalism continued and provinces became effectively autonomous (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Provinces that became autonomous in practice: Hyderabad, Bengal, Awadh
- Sobriquet "Rangila" must not replace structural explanation (✅ owner 01 §1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1719–48 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1007, MOD-1012, MOD-1013, MOD-1014.
**Trap:** Do not explain decline by the emperor's personality alone.
**Mains hook:** Long reign, shrinking authority: duration of rule did not equal control.
**Back:** *"Long reign, lost provinces."*

### Flashcard MOD-1007 — Nadir Shah and the Battle of Karnal (1739)
**Front:** What did Nadir Shah do in 1739 and what was its significance?
**Definition (learn):** Nadir Shah of Persia defeated the Mughals at Karnal (1739), sacked Delhi and took plunder including the Peacock Throne and the Kohinoor (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: a state that could be defeated in one field engagement had lost fiscal-military capacity (✅ owner 01 Adv)
- Course: Karnal, Delhi sacked
- Outcome: shattering blow to Mughal prestige
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1739 (✅ owner 01). Wikipedia dates the battle 24 Feb 1739 in the infobox and 13 Feb in the text (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01; internal inconsistency on the day, year agreed). The plunder estimate on that page (about 700 million rupees) is not in the owner and is not used here (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1008, MOD-1009, MOD-1001.
**Trap:** Nadir Shah plundered and left; he did not annex the empire (✅ owner 01 §5).
**Mains hook:** Karnal was the timing of collapse, not its cause; invasion revealed a prior hollowing.
**Back:** *"Karnal, Delhi looted, the throne carried off."*

### Flashcard MOD-1008 — Peacock Throne and Kohinoor (plunder fact)
**Front:** What treasures did Nadir Shah take from Delhi?
**Definition (learn):** The Peacock Throne and the Kohinoor diamond, carried off in 1739 (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Object 1: Peacock Throne
- Object 2: Kohinoor (later acquired by the British)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1739 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1007, MOD-1024.
**Trap:** Avoid stating a figure for the plunder; describe it qualitatively (✅ owner 01 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Plunder of 1739 links to present repatriation debates in cultural diplomacy.
**Back:** *"Throne and Kohinoor went east to Persia."*

### Flashcard MOD-1009 — Ahmad Shah Abdali and the Third Battle of Panipat (1761)
**Front:** What were Abdali's invasions and how did they end?
**Definition (learn):** Ahmad Shah Abdali, Nadir Shah's successor, invaded India repeatedly 1748–61, ending with the Third Battle of Panipat (1761) (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: opportunity from a weakened centre
- Course: repeated invasions 1748–61
- Outcome: Mughal power reduced to Delhi environs (✅ owner 01 Adv)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1748–61; Panipat III 1761 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1007, MOD-1001, MOD-1028 (Marathas at Panipat).
**Trap:** Panipat III is a consequence of the fragmented order, not a cause of Mughal decline (✅ owner 01 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Invasions drained resources and prestige; Panipat checked Maratha northern expansion but did not end Maratha political weight (✅ owner 02).
**Back:** *"Forty-eight to sixty-one: repeated raids, Panipat ends them."*

### Flashcard MOD-1010 — Jagirdari crisis and be-jagiri
**Front:** What is the jagirdari crisis and what is be-jagiri?
**Definition (learn):** A structural mismatch in which the number of mansabdars and their claims outran the supply of revenue-yielding jagirs; the resulting scramble for assignments is called be-jagiri (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: mansabdar numbers grew faster than assignable revenue
- Effect: nobles maximised extraction from peasants and zamindars; centre lost discipline over nobility
- Scale: debated; present as structure, not as a quantified statistic (✅ owner 01 Adv §8.4 A)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Satish Chandra and Bipan Chandra's core argument (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1001, MOD-1011, MOD-1017.
**Trap:** Be-jagiri is not ordinary corruption; it is a structural shortfall of assignable revenue (✅ owner 01 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** The reward system that bound the elite became the mechanism of its fragmentation.
**Back:** *"More claimants than jagirs: the reward machine seizes up."*

### Flashcard MOD-1011 — Nobility factions: Turani, Irani, Afghan, Hindustani
**Front:** Name the main noble factions at the Mughal court in decline.
**Definition (learn):** Ethnic-factional blocs of the nobility whose rivalries paralysed the centre (✅ owner 01 Adv).
**Breakdown:**
- Turani (Central Asian)
- Irani (Persian)
- Afghan
- Hindustani (Indian-born; the Sayyid Brothers belonged to this bloc, ✅ owner 01 Adv)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Faction was a symptom of the jagir scramble as well as a cause (✅ owner 01 §8.4 B).
**Links:** MOD-1004, MOD-1010.
**Trap:** Do not present court faction as the root cause; it is a symptom.
**Mains hook:** Factional capture of the throne is the operational meaning of "decline" at court level.
**Back:** *"Tu, I, A, H: four blocs, one empty throne."*

### Flashcard MOD-1012 — Murshid Quli Khan and Bengal's hereditary autonomy
**Front:** How did Bengal become autonomous under Murshid Quli Khan?
**Definition (learn):** A Mughal provincial governor who founded an effectively hereditary regime in Bengal while still acknowledging the emperor (✅ owner 01, 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Office converted into dynasty
- Still struck coin and read the khutba in the emperor's name (✅ owner 01 Adv)
- Revenue administration remained strong (✅ owner 01 §8.4 F)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Succeeded by Alivardi Khan (1740–56) (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1016 (legal fiction), MOD-1034 (Bengal successor state), MOD-1048.
**Trap:** Autonomy was not formal independence (✅ owner 01 §5).
**Mains hook:** Bengal's success under a strong revenue administration is evidence against uniform decadence.
**Back:** *"Murshid made Bengal his own, still in the emperor's name."*

### Flashcard MOD-1013 — Saadat Khan Burhan-ul-Mulk and Awadh
**Front:** Who founded Awadh's autonomous line and who succeeded him?
**Definition (learn):** Saadat Khan Burhan-ul-Mulk founded Awadh's autonomous dynasty; Safdar Jang was his major successor (✅ owner 01 Adv, 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Awadh: fertile middle-Ganga state, buffer between Bengal and northwest
- Safdar Jang (1748–54) served as wazir and strengthened autonomy (✅ owner 02)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Safdar Jang 1748–54 (✅ owner 02 Mini-timeline).
**Links:** MOD-1006, MOD-1034, MOD-1110 (Awadh annexation 1856).
**Trap:** Awadh was not annexed in 1765; it was kept as a buffer (see MOD-1044).
**Mains hook:** Awadh's dual role as wazir state and buffer explains why the Company preserved it.
**Back:** *"Saadat founded, Safdar strengthened, Awadh stayed a buffer."*

### Flashcard MOD-1014 — Nizam-ul-Mulk Asaf Jah and Hyderabad (1724)
**Front:** When and how did Hyderabad become autonomous?
**Definition (learn):** Nizam-ul-Mulk Asaf Jah quit the wazirat in 1724 and founded the autonomous state of Hyderabad in the Deccan (✅ owner 01 Adv, 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Formally loyal to the Mughal emperor, autonomous in practice (✅ owner 02)
- Encircled by Marathas and Mysore (✅ owner 02 §8.5)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1724 (✅ owner 01 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1006, MOD-1034, MOD-1107.
**Trap:** Hyderabad was not an independent sovereign state in law; it kept imperial form.
**Mains hook:** Hyderabad was the earliest Indian state to accept British protection, a fact that connects autonomy to the subsidiary system.
**Back:** *"Twenty-four: the Nizam leaves the wazirat and keeps the Deccan."*

### Flashcard MOD-1015 — The 1717 farman: Company privileges
**Front:** What did the 1717 farman grant and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** A farman of Farrukhsiyar (1717) that granted the English East India Company duty-free trade privileges in Bengal (✅ owner 01 Adv, 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Grant: trade privilege, not territory
- Later misuse: dastaks extended to private trade (see MOD-1046)
- Result: fiscal grievance behind the 1750s conflict
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1717 (✅ owner 01, 03, 04).
**Links:** MOD-1005, MOD-1046, MOD-1063.
**Trap:** The farman did not create the Company, which was chartered in 1600 (MOD-1056; ✅ owner 03).
**Mains hook:** A concession made from weakness became the seed of sovereignty conflict.
**Back:** *"Seventeen-seventeen: duty-free trade, with a bill to pay later."*

### Flashcard MOD-1016 — Legal fiction of Mughal sovereignty
**Front:** What was the "legal fiction" under which provinces remained autonomous?
**Definition (learn):** Provincial rulers coined money and had the khutba read in the emperor's name while holding real power (✅ owner 01 Adv).
**Breakdown:**
- Forms kept: coinage, khutba, office titles
- Substance lost: enforcement and fiscal control
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Applies to Bengal, Awadh and Hyderabad (✅ owner 01 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1012, MOD-1013, MOD-1014, MOD-1021.
**Trap:** Do not say provincial governors declared independence (✅ owner 01 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** The legitimacy of decline was orderly fragmentation, not anarchy.
**Back:** *"Coins and khutba stayed Mughal; the power did not."*

### Flashcard MOD-1017 — Deccan over-stretch
**Front:** How did Aurangzeb's Deccan campaigns contribute to decline?
**Definition (learn):** Prolonged Deccan campaigns absorbed treasury and army for decades without extinguishing Maratha resistance (✅ owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Effect 1: fiscal exhaustion
- Effect 2: more jagir claimants than assignable revenue (links to MOD-1010)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Late seventeenth century (✅ owner 01 §8.4 D; date range not given in owner, do not extend).
**Links:** MOD-1010, MOD-1028 (Maratha confederacy).
**Trap:** Attributing decline mainly to Aurangzeb's religious policy is a contested older view (✅ owner 01 §8.4 D).
**Mains hook:** Conquest added claimants faster than revenue, tightening the very crisis it created.
**Back:** *"Deccan wars: the treasury bled, Marathas stayed."*

### Flashcard MOD-1018 — Jadunath Sarkar: moral and religious decline thesis
**Front:** What is Jadunath Sarkar's explanation of Mughal decline?
**Definition (learn):** An older interpretation locating decline in moral or character decline and Aurangzeb's religious policy (✅ owner 01 Adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: shows what a single-cause answer looks like
- Weakness: insufficient without fiscal, agrarian and regional evidence
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Older historiographical reading (✅ owner 01 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1019, MOD-1020, MOD-1001.
**Trap:** Do not present religious policy as the settled cause (✅ owner 01 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Use Sarkar as the foil for multi-causal answers.
**Back:** *"Sarkar: character and creed. Insufficient alone."*

### Flashcard MOD-1019 — Satish Chandra and Irfan Habib: jagirdari versus agrarian crisis
**Front:** How do Satish Chandra and Irfan Habib explain decline differently?
**Definition (learn):** Satish Chandra located the crisis in the jagirdari system; Irfan Habib stressed agrarian crisis from over-exploitation of peasants driving revolts (✅ owner 01 Adv §2, ⚠️ attribution as interpretive position).
**Breakdown:**
- Satish Chandra: structural mismatch of assignments
- Irfan Habib: agrarian over-taxation, zamindar and peasant revolt
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Interpretive positions (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1010, MOD-1020.
**Trap:** Do not quote historians verbatim; the argument earns marks (✅ owner 01 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Add the rural mechanism below the elite crisis.
**Back:** *"Satish: the jagirs. Habib: the peasant."*

### Flashcard MOD-1020 — Revisionist reading: Muzaffar Alam and C.A. Bayly
**Front:** What is the regional "revisionist" view of 18th-century decline?
**Definition (learn):** The eighteenth century as redistribution of power and prosperity to regions, not universal collapse (✅ owner 01 Adv §2; interpretive, ⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: regional reordering
- Evidence: Bengal, Awadh, Maratha and Sikh formations
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Interpretive position (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1012, MOD-1021, MOD-1027.
**Trap:** "Regional prosperity" is uneven; do not say nothing was lost (✅ owner 01 §8.4 F).
**Mains hook:** Supply the two-level verdict: centre declined, regions transformed.
**Back:** *"Alam and Bayly: the centre fell, the regions rose."*

### Flashcard MOD-1021 — Trap: decline of centre versus decline of regions
**Front:** Why must "decline" be specified as a level?
**Definition (learn):** Central authority declined while many regions prospered; the decline label fits Delhi, not necessarily Bengal, Awadh or the Maratha domains (✅ owner 01 §8.2, §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Level 1: imperial centre
- Level 2: regional economies and states
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Two-level thesis (✅ owner 01 §8.2).
**Links:** MOD-1020, MOD-1016.
**Trap:** "18th-century India was uniformly decadent" is wrong (✅ owner 01 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the two-level verdict for any "dark age" wording.
**Back:** *"Centre down, regions up: say which you mean."*

### Flashcard MOD-1022 — Trap: external shock as revealer, not originator
**Front:** Did Nadir Shah and Abdali cause the Mughal decline?
**Definition (learn):** They accelerated and exposed a decline already structural; they did not originate it (✅ owner 01 Adv §5, 01 §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Shock was real: loss of prestige and bullion accelerated provincial disengagement (✅ owner 01 §8.4 E)
- Originator: jagirdari-agrarian and factional structure
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1739; 1748–61 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1007, MOD-1009, MOD-1001.
**Trap:** Do not dismiss invasion as merely "symptomatic"; the shock mattered for timing.
**Mains hook:** "Foreign invasion was decisive in timing but not in causation" (✅ owner 01 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Invasion sets the date; structure sets the cause."*

### Flashcard MOD-1023 — Mughal dynasty: authority lost, existence retained
**Front:** When did the Mughal dynasty formally end?
**Definition (learn):** The dynasty formally continued until 1857; the answer should say authority, not existence, collapsed (✅ owner 01 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Authority lost: 1707 onward
- Form retained: titles, coinage, khutba
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1857 formal end (✅ owner 01 §8.8).
**Links:** MOD-1001, MOD-1016, MOD-1004.
**Trap:** Do not date the "fall" to 1707.
**Mains hook:** The Mughal form outlived its command, which is why fragmentation looked orderly.
**Back:** *"Dynasty to 1857, command gone by 1707."*

### Flashcard MOD-1024 — Kohinoor and heritage repatriation (current link)
**Front:** Why does the Kohinoor's 1739 removal still matter?
**Definition (learn):** A recurring theme in India's cultural-repatriation discourse; the stone passed from Nadir Shah to the British (✅ owner 01 §6, ⚠️ current-affairs pegging not verified).
**Breakdown:**
- Link: 18th-century plunder to present repatriation debate
- Link: GS-I art and culture, GS-II diplomacy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1739 seizure (✅ owner 01). No specific news item is pegged here (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1008.
**Trap:** Do not present the debate as settled law.
**Mains hook:** Plunder history gives a concrete basis for debates on restitution, qualified by legal limits.
**Back:** *"1739 loot, 21st-century claims."*

### Flashcard MOD-1025 — Verdict scaffolds for decline questions
**Front:** What qualified thesis suits "why did the Mughal Empire decline?"
**Definition (learn):** Structural-primacy thesis: decline was a crisis of fiscal-military institutions; succession and invasion were accelerants (✅ owner 01 §8.2).
**Breakdown:**
- Thesis: structural core
- Counter-evidence: regional vitality
- Verdict: two-level, with timing vs causation distinguished
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner's option set (✅ owner 01 §8.2, §8.7).
**Links:** MOD-1001, MOD-1021, MOD-1022.
**Trap:** Single-cause answers lose marks.
**Mains hook:** "Decline was structural; invasion decided the timing." Use for 10/15/20-mark questions.
**Back:** *"Structure first, shock second, regions third."*

### GROUP — Indian States and Society in the Eighteenth Century

### Flashcard MOD-1026 — Concept: three-fold typology of 18th-century states
**Front:** What are the three types of 18th-century states in Bipin Chandra's classification?
**Definition (learn):** Successor states (inherited Mughal provinces), new or rebel states (from resistance), independent kingdoms (outside direct Mughal provincial pattern) (✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Successor: Bengal, Awadh, Hyderabad
- New/rebel: Marathas, Sikhs, Jats, Rohillas/Afghans
- Independent: Mysore, Rajput states, Kerala/Travancore
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Classification by origin (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1027, MOD-1028, MOD-1029, MOD-1036.
**Trap:** Mysore is not a Mughal successor state (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Origin determined legitimacy: successor states borrowed Mughal sanction, rebels built their own (✅ owner 02 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Inherit, rebel, or stand alone."*

### Flashcard MOD-1027 — Successor states: office converted into dynasty
**Front:** What was the commonest route to 18th-century sovereignty?
**Definition (learn):** Hereditary appropriation of Mughal provincial office (✅ owner 02 §8.4 A).
**Breakdown:**
- Bengal: Murshid Quli Khan, Alivardi Khan, Siraj-ud-Daula
- Awadh: Saadat Khan, Safdar Jang
- Hyderabad: Nizam-ul-Mulk Asaf Jah (1724)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1012, MOD-1013, MOD-1014, MOD-1026.
**Trap:** Successor legitimacy was borrowed, which let the Company later claim authority through the same Mughal channel (✅ owner 02 §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** Successor states kept Mughal revenue vocabulary and Persianate forms.
**Back:** *"Office became dynasty."*

### Flashcard MOD-1028 — Maratha confederacy structure
**Front:** How was the Maratha confederacy organised?
**Definition (learn):** A confederacy under the Peshwa at Poona, with powerful chiefs: Gaekwad (Baroda), Holkar (Indore), Scindia (Gwalior), Bhonsle (Nagpur) (✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Peshwa: loose leadership at Poona
- Chiefs: Gaekwad, Holkar, Scindia, Bhonsle
- Reach: widest territorial reach
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Confederate structure (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1029, MOD-1030, MOD-1037.
**Trap:** Marathas were not a unitary state under the Peshwa (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Confederate structure gave reach but weak central command; defeat could be piecemeal.
**Back:** *"Poona leads, four chiefs ride."*

### Flashcard MOD-1029 — Chauth and sardeshmukhi
**Front:** What were chauth and sardeshmukhi?
**Definition (learn):** Maratha fiscal claims on territory, not ordinary land taxes (✅ owner 02 §8.8). The fractions usually given for chauth (one-fourth) and sardeshmukhi (an extra tenth) are standard knowledge not in the owner (⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Chauth: a claim on territory
- Sardeshmukhi: an additional claim
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Claims on territory, not ordinary land revenue (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1028, MOD-1026.
**Trap:** Do not read chauth as ordinary tax; it is a claim on territory (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Cavalry mobility and revenue claims gave the Marathas power beyond their core.
**Back:** *"Chauth claims the land; it is not the land tax."*

### Flashcard MOD-1030 — Sikhs: Banda Bahadur, misls, Ranjit Singh
**Front:** What is the correct sequence of early Sikh political history?
**Definition (learn):** Banda Bahadur's revolt (1708–16), then the twelve misls, then Ranjit Singh's unified Punjab (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Phase 1: Banda Bahadur 1708–16
- Phase 2: 12 misls
- Phase 3: Ranjit Singh (⚠️ Ranjit Singh's death year not in owner; 1839 is standard knowledge, unverified here)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1708–16 (✅ owner 01, 02).
**Links:** MOD-1031, MOD-1113, MOD-1118.
**Trap:** Sikh power did not begin with Ranjit Singh (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Community mobilisation and misl organisation rather than a single centre.
**Back:** *"Banda, then twelve misls, then one Lion."*

### Flashcard MOD-1031 — Jats: Churaman and Suraj Mal of Bharatpur
**Front:** Name the important Jat leaders.
**Definition (learn):** Churaman and Suraj Mal of Bharatpur led Jat political power (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Churaman: early Jat leader
- Suraj Mal: Bharatpur
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Bharatpur (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1030, MOD-1026.
**Trap:** Do not merge Jat leaders with Maratha chiefs.
**Mains hook:** Jats built new military-fiscal orders from a rural base.
**Back:** *"Churaman started, Suraj Mal stood in Bharatpur."*

### Flashcard MOD-1032 — Rohillas and Afghans: Rohilkhand and Farrukhabad
**Front:** Where were the Rohilla and Afghan centres?
**Definition (learn):** Rohilkhand and Farrukhabad were important centres of Rohilla/Afghan power (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: new or rebel state (✅ owner 02 §2)
- Origin: military mobilisation and local chieftaincy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rohilkhand, Farrukhabad (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1026, MOD-1009.
**Trap:** Do not place Rohillas among independent southern kingdoms.
**Mains hook:** Rohilla and Afghan power shows the Ganga-plain military market of the eighteenth century.
**Back:** *"Rohilkhand and Farrukhabad: Afghan swords on the Ganga."*

### Flashcard MOD-1033 — Alivardi Khan and Siraj-ud-Daula in Bengal
**Front:** Who ruled Bengal between Murshid Quli Khan and Plassey?
**Definition (learn):** Alivardi Khan (1740–56) resisted external pressures before Siraj-ud-Daula's accession (✅ owner 02 Mini-timeline).
**Breakdown:**
- Alivardi Khan: 1740–56
- Siraj-ud-Daula: defeated at Plassey 1757 (see MOD-1076)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1740–56 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1012, MOD-1049.
**Trap:** Siraj's accession disputes weakened his enforcement ability (✅ owner 04 §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** Bengal's wealth and semi-autonomy made it the immediate background to Plassey.
**Back:** *"Alivardi held, Siraj lost."*

### Flashcard MOD-1034 — Bengal, Awadh and Hyderabad compared
**Front:** How do Bengal, Awadh and Hyderabad differ as successor states?
**Definition (learn):** Three successor states with distinct strengths and weaknesses (✅ owner 02 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Bengal: rich revenue base; exposed coast; merchant-banker dependence
- Awadh: fertile buffer; locked between Company Bengal and Afghan pressure
- Hyderabad: Deccan territory and Mughal prestige; encircled; first to accept British protection
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 02 §8.5).
**Links:** MOD-1012, MOD-1013, MOD-1014.
**Trap:** Bengal, Awadh and Hyderabad were not identical (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Explain why Bengal, Awadh and Hyderabad became targets or intermediaries.
**Back:** *"Bengal rich, Awadh buffered, Hyderabad hemmed."*

### Flashcard MOD-1035 — Mysore: Haidar Ali and Tipu Sultan
**Front:** Why is Mysore the strongest independent southern example?
**Definition (learn):** Haidar Ali and Tipu Sultan centralised revenue, reorganised the army along European lines and sought European military technique (✅ owner 02 §2, 02 Adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: most deliberate military modernisation
- Weakness: isolated diplomatically; faced repeated coalitions (✅ owner 02 §8.5)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Haidar Ali died 1782 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1026, MOD-1099, MOD-1116.
**Trap:** Mysore is an independent southern kingdom, not a Mughal successor (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Mysore modernised and still lost, showing the defeat was of coalition and cohesion, not of will.
**Back:** *"Haidar built, Tipu fought, Mysore stood alone."*

### Flashcard MOD-1036 — Travancore: Martanda Varma
**Front:** What did Martanda Varma achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Consolidated princely authority in Kerala (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: independent kingdom
- Strength: centralising reform
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mid-18th century (⚠️ date not in owner).
**Links:** MOD-1026, MOD-1035.
**Trap:** Travancore is not a Mughal successor state.
**Mains hook:** Centralising kingdoms in the south were capable of purposive state-building.
**Back:** *"Martanda Varma: the Kerala king who centralised."*

### Flashcard MOD-1037 — Rajput states as independent kingdoms
**Front:** Where do Rajput states fit in the typology?
**Definition (learn):** Independent kingdoms that strengthened local sovereignty outside direct Mughal provincial succession (✅ owner 02 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: independent kingdoms
- Contrast: Marathas and Sikhs were mobilised rebels
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no date range (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1026, MOD-1028.
**Trap:** Rajput states were not one centralised polity.
**Mains hook:** Local sovereignty without imperial inheritance.
**Back:** *"Rajputs ruled their own ground."*

### Flashcard MOD-1038 — Merchant-financiers: Jagat Seths and banking houses
**Front:** What role did merchant-bankers play in 18th-century states?
**Definition (learn):** Revenue and credit connected rulers to commercial groups; the Jagat Seths of Bengal were the leading example (✅ owner 02 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: credit, revenue farming, brokerage
- Example: Jagat Seths in Bengal and banking houses across north India
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jagat Seths present at Plassey (✅ owner 04; see MOD-1049).
**Links:** MOD-1076, MOD-1077.
**Trap:** Company conquest used Indian financial networks; it was not purely European (✅ owner 02 §2).
**Mains hook:** Indian merchant-bankers linked regional states to the later Company state.
**Back:** *"Seths financed the state, then the Company."*

### Flashcard MOD-1039 — Agrarian economy: village-centred but connected
**Front:** How connected was the 18th-century village economy?
**Definition (learn):** Villages were not sealed or self-sufficient; revenue, credit, artisan production and commodity trade linked them to towns and merchants (✅ owner 02 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Base: village agriculture
- Links: revenue payments, credit, artisans, commodity trade
- Surplus zones: Bengal, Gujarat, Awadh-Banaras belt, parts of south (✅ owner 02 §1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No figures (owner forbids precise revenue figures).
**Links:** MOD-1040, MOD-1041.
**Trap:** Do not assert precise revenue or population figures (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Rural society was commercially connected and fiscally squeezed.
**Back:** *"Village-based, market-linked, tax-squeezed."*

### Flashcard MOD-1040 — Composite culture and Persianate court culture
**Front:** How did composite culture survive political fragmentation?
**Definition (learn):** Persianate court culture plus regional languages and devotional traditions (Urdu, Braj, Marathi, Punjabi, Malayalam spheres) (✅ owner 02 Adv §2, ⚠️ grouping of spheres).
**Breakdown:**
- Court: Persianate forms
- Everyday: religious tolerance and shared practice
- Limit: caste hierarchy and women's restricted position persisted (✅ owner 02 §8.4 E)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** MOD-1039, MOD-1041.
**Trap:** Do not write composite culture as social equality.
**Mains hook:** Culture survived political conflict; political conflict did not automatically mean communal polarisation.
**Back:** *"Persian courts, local tongues, shared festivals, unequal society."*

### Flashcard MOD-1041 — Technological-lag thesis
**Front:** What was the decisive Indian weakness according to the technological-lag thesis?
**Definition (learn):** Absence of sustained scientific and technical advance and of an autonomous military-industrial base, rather than lack of political vitality (✅ owner 02 §8.2, ⚠️ thesis is an owner analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: capability gap, not will
- Caution: Indian textiles stayed internationally competitive into the later eighteenth century (✅ owner 02 §8.4 F)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Later eighteenth century (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1040, MOD-1076.
**Trap:** Do not claim Indian production was primitive.
**Mains hook:** Converts "why did they lose?" into a capability question.
**Back:** *"Skill was there; the science and the industry were not."*

### Flashcard MOD-1042 — Trap: uniform decline versus regional state formation
**Front:** Was 18th-century India a dark age?
**Definition (learn):** No. It saw regional state formation, trade and cultural adaptation, alongside heavy taxation and insecurity (✅ owner 02 §5, §8.2).
**Breakdown:**
- Against "uniform decline": Bengal commerce, Mysore reforms, Maratha expansion (✅ owner 02 §8.6)
- Against "golden age": war raised risk for peasants, artisans, small traders
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner's uneven-transition thesis (✅ owner 02 §8.2).
**Links:** MOD-1020, MOD-1021, MOD-1026.
**Trap:** Neither "dark age" nor "golden age" fits.
**Mains hook:** Use the uneven-transition thesis for "was it stagnant?" questions.
**Back:** *"Not dark, not golden: uneven."*

### Flashcard MOD-1043 — Concept: Persianate and regional states as early-modern polities
**Front:** Were successor states modern nation-states?
**Definition (learn):** No; they were autonomous early-modern regional polities that kept Mughal legitimacy while exercising real power (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Legitimacy: borrowed from Mughal sanction
- Sovereignty: practical, not formal
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner's classification (✅ owner 02 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1026, MOD-1016.
**Trap:** Successor states did not formally renounce Mughal sovereignty (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Regional state formation" is more accurate than "anarchy."
**Back:** *"Early-modern and autonomous, not national."*

### Flashcard MOD-1044 — Awadh buffer: not annexed in 1765
**Front:** What was Awadh's status after 1765?
**Definition (learn):** Restored to Shuja-ud-Daula as a buffer rather than annexed, under the Treaty of Allahabad (✅ owner 04 §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: strategic shield between Company Bengal and the northwest
- Later: annexed in 1856 on misgovernance (MOD-1058)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1765 buffer; 1856 annexation (✅ owner 04, 05).
**Links:** MOD-1013, MOD-1110.
**Trap:** Awadh was not annexed in 1765 (✅ owner 04 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Buffers were the Company's cheapest form of control in the 1760s.
**Back:** *"Sixty-five: buffer. Fifty-six: annexed."*

### Flashcard MOD-1045 — Trap: successor states were not nationalist breaks
**Front:** Did successor states represent a nationalist break from the Mughals?
**Definition (learn):** No; they were early-modern regional polities that often retained Mughal symbols of legitimacy (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Continuity: forms, titles, revenue vocabulary
- Innovation: local power and military order
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap list (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MOD-1043, MOD-1016.
**Trap:** Nationalist readings projected backward.
**Mains hook:** Avoid projecting later nationalist categories backward.
**Back:** *"Mughal coat, local cloth."*

### Flashcard MOD-1046 — Dastaks: the instrument of private-trade abuse
**Front:** What is a dastak and how did its misuse matter?
**Definition (learn):** A customs pass; Company servants extended dastaks to their private trade, which cut the Nawab's customs revenue and undercut Indian merchants (✅ owner 04 §1, 04 Adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Misuse: extension to private trade
- Effect: customs revenue shifted to individuals
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-1717 abuse (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
**Links:** MOD-1015, MOD-1047, MOD-1063.
**Trap:** Do not treat dastaks as an ordinary Company privilege.
**Mains hook:** Private-trade abuse is the first step of sovereignty conflict.
**Back:** *"Dastak: a pass that became a loophole."*

### Flashcard MOD-1047 — Concept: the Company's dependence on Indian financial and political alignments
**Front:** How did Indian alignments shape the Company's rise?
**Definition (learn):** Indian rulers, bankers and merchants used the Europeans as instruments as much as the reverse (✅ owner 03 §8.4 F).
**Breakdown:**
- Indian agency: succession disputes, banker networks
- Limit: naval power stayed European
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Carnatic and Hyderabad succession disputes (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1038, MOD-1048.
**Trap:** Do not assume European victory from 1498.
**Mains hook:** Indian agency explains why Europeans could intervene.
**Back:** *"Indians used them too, until the navy decided."*

### Flashcard MOD-1048 — Verdict: regional states and the British opportunity
**Front:** How did decline create an opening for the Company?
**Definition (learn):** Decline did not invite conquest automatically; it created a multi-state field in which a naval-backed company could convert alliance into sovereignty (✅ owner 01 §8.2 opportunity thesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Opening: competing successor states
- Instrument: Company naval reach, credit, Bengal revenue
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner thesis (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MOD-1001, MOD-1034, MOD-1089.
**Trap:** Decline supplied the opportunity, not the outcome.
**Mains hook:** Explain why Bengal, Awadh and Hyderabad became targets.
**Back:** *"Decline opened the field; the Company won it."*

### GROUP — The Beginnings of European Settlements

### Flashcard MOD-1049 — Vasco da Gama reaches Calicut (1498)
**Front:** When and where did the Portuguese first arrive by sea in India?
**Definition (learn):** Vasco da Gama reached Calicut in 1498, opening the Cape route and direct Portuguese sea contact (✅ owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Event: arrival at Calicut
- Consequence: a new maritime-commercial order in the Indian Ocean (✅ owner 03 Adv)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1498 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1050, MOD-1051, MOD-1063.
**Trap:** Do not jump from 1498 to British rule; the Company first built coastal bases (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Mains hook:** The Portuguese introduced the principle that seaborne trade could be taxed and policed by force.
**Back:** *"Ninety-eight: the Cape opens, Calicut receives."*

### Flashcard MOD-1050 — Albuquerque captures Goa (1510) and Almeida's Blue Water policy
**Front:** What did Albuquerque capture and what was Almeida's policy?
**Definition (learn):** Albuquerque captured Goa in 1510 as the Portuguese base; Almeida is linked with the Blue Water policy (naval control of the sea) (✅ owner 03 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- 1510: Goa captured, centre of the Estado da India
- Almeida: Blue Water policy (sea power over land)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1510 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1049, MOD-1051.
**Trap:** Goa was not captured by the English or the Dutch.
**Mains hook:** Goa became the fortified centre of Portuguese maritime control.
**Back:** *"Ten: Goa taken. Almeida rules the water."*

### Flashcard MOD-1051 — Cartaz and the Estado da India
**Front:** What was the cartaz and the Estado da India?
**Definition (learn):** The cartaz was a Portuguese naval pass system for controlling sea trade; the Estado da India was the Portuguese administrative-maritime state centred on Goa (✅ owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Cartaz: licensing of shipping
- Estado da India: fortified ports, fleets, Goa centre
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Estado da India centred on Goa (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1049, MOD-1050, MOD-1069.
**Trap:** Portuguese control was never complete; Indian and Arab shipping continued (✅ owner 03 Adv §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** Converted the Indian Ocean from an open arena into a licensed one.
**Back:** *"Cartaz: a pass for the sea, a fort at the port."*

### Flashcard MOD-1052 — Portuguese introduced fruits: papaya, pineapple, guava
**Front:** Which fruits did the Portuguese introduce into India, per the 2025 PYQ?
**Definition (learn):** American-origin food plants reached India through Portuguese maritime networks; the owner names papaya, pineapple and guava, with chilli, potato, tobacco, cashew, custard apple and maize (✅ owner 03 Adv §8.4 B; diffusion dates approximate ⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- PYQ anchor: 2025 Prelims GS-I Q75 (✅ owner 03 Recent PYQ block)
- Answer status: the official Set-A answer is in the repository but not recorded in the owner, and is not inferred here
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Write "introduced through Portuguese maritime contact", not a year (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1049, MOD-1053.
**Trap:** Do not assign a precise year to a crop's introduction (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Portuguese impact was cultural and material as well as naval.
**Back:** *"Papaya, pineapple, guava: Portugal's fruit basket."*

### Flashcard MOD-1053 — St Francis Xavier, Jesuits and Goa
**Front:** What is St Francis Xavier's link to Goa and the Jesuits?
**Definition (learn):** A founding companion of the Society of Jesus, he reached Goa in 1542 and became the central figure of Catholic mission in the region; his remains are enshrined in the Basilica of Bom Jesus in Old Goa (✅ owner 03 Adv §8.4 B).
**Breakdown:**
- Order: Society of Jesus under Ignatius Loyola (founding companion, not sole founder)
- Place: Old Goa, Basilica of Bom Jesus, part of Churches and Convents of Goa (UNESCO) (✅ owner 03)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1542 arrival (✅ owner 03). 2021 Prelims Q39 routed (✅ owner 03 PYQ block; key unavailable locally).
**Links:** MOD-1052, MOD-1049.
**Trap:** Xavier did not found the Jesuit order alone (✅ owner 03 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** Missionary institutions followed the flag; religion and architecture were permanently altered.
**Back:** *"Xavier, companion of Ignatius, rests in Bom Jesus, Goa."*

### Flashcard MOD-1054 — Dutch VOC: Pulicat, Nagapattinam and Amboyna (1623)
**Front:** What was the Dutch VOC's Indian and Asian focus?
**Definition (learn):** The Dutch East India Company (VOC), founded 1602, was commercially formidable and concentrated on the Indonesian archipelago. Its Indian centres were Pulicat and Nagapattinam (✅ owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Founded: 1602
- Indian centres: Pulicat, Nagapattinam
- Amboyna Massacre (1623): worsened English-Dutch rivalry (✅ owner 03 Adv)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1602; 1623 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1055, MOD-1072.
**Trap:** The Dutch were not the main political rivals of the English in the Carnatic (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Mains hook:** Commercial strength without territorial commitment did not translate into political power.
**Back:** *"Six-oh-two: Pulicat, Nagapattinam, and the spice islands first."*

### Flashcard MOD-1055 — Dutch withdrawal: Bedara/Hooghly (1759)
**Front:** Why did the Dutch fail in Bengal?
**Definition (learn):** Dutch power in Bengal was broken at Bedara/Hooghly in 1759; the English defeated the Dutch challenge (✅ owner 03 Adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Turning point: 1759
- Strategic: Dutch focus remained Indonesia
- Textile and indigo trade stayed significant after political decline (✅ owner 03 §8.4 C)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1759 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1054, MOD-1074.
**Trap:** The decisive European contest in India was Anglo-French, not Anglo-Dutch (✅ owner 03 §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** Explains why the Dutch did not become a territorial power in India.
**Back:** *"Fifty-nine at Hooghly: the Dutch leave the field."*

### Flashcard MOD-1056 — English East India Company charter (1600)
**Front:** When and by whom was the English East India Company chartered?
**Definition (learn):** A chartered trading company given a royal charter by Queen Elizabeth I in 1600 (✅ owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: chartered trading company (monopoly privileges)
- Later: fortified settlements, then territorial power
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1600 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1057, MOD-1058, MOD-1059, MOD-1015.
**Trap:** The 1600 charter created a trading company; territorial rule came later (✅ owner 03 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The company-state grew from a monopoly charter.
**Back:** *"Sixteen hundred: Elizabeth's charter, a trade monopoly."*

### Flashcard MOD-1057 — Surat factory (1613) and the Mughal permission route
**Front:** How did the English secure their factory at Surat?
**Definition (learn):** The English secured a factory at Surat in 1613 after Mughal permission, with Hawkins and Sir Thomas Roe linked to early Mughal contacts (✅ owner 03 §4, §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Route: permission, not conquest
- Figures: Hawkins; Sir Thomas Roe (diplomatic route)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1613 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1056, MOD-1058, MOD-1063.
**Trap:** Early factories were held by permission, not sovereignty.
**Mains hook:** Factory rights by permission governed English presence until 1717.
**Back:** *"Thirteen: factory by permission, not by force."*

### Flashcard MOD-1058 — Madras and Fort St George (1639)
**Front:** What did the English acquire at Madras in 1639?
**Definition (learn):** Madras and Fort St George, an English base on the Coromandel coast, dated to 1639 (✅ owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: fortified settlement for defence of stock and specie (✅ owner 03 Adv §8.5)
- Later: Carnatic centre
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1639 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1056, MOD-1064, MOD-1063.
**Trap:** Madras was returned to the English by the Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (see MOD-1064).
**Mains hook:** Fortification marks the shift from factory to territory.
**Back:** *"Thirty-nine: Fort St George, the first fortress."* (⚠️ "first fortress" is interpretive; owner says Fort St George dates to 1639.)

### Flashcard MOD-1059 — Bombay and the Crown transfer (1668)
**Front:** How did Bombay come to the English Company?
**Definition (learn):** Bombay reached the Company by Crown transfer in 1668, after it came to England through a royal marriage; it was not conquered or granted by Charnock (✅ owner 03 Adv §4, §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Route: Crown transfer/lease to the Company (owner uses "transferred"/"leased")
- Date: 1668
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1668 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1056, MOD-1060.
**Trap:** Bombay was not founded by Job Charnock (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Mains hook:** Bombay's transfer shows that English possessions often came by treaty and transfer.
**Back:** *"Sixty-eight: Bombay comes by the Crown, not the sword."*

### Flashcard MOD-1060 — Calcutta (1690) and the Charnock attribution
**Front:** What is the conventional date and disputed claim for the English settlement at Calcutta?
**Definition (learn):** The conventional date is 1690, linked to Job Charnock; the "founder" label is disputed and urban origins were cumulative (✅ owner 03; ⚠️ dispute noted by owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1690 (conventional)
- Claim: the founder label is contested
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1690 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1059, MOD-1046.
**Trap:** Do not convert the Charnock association into an uncontested founding (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** State the settlement date, not a founding claim.
**Back:** *"Ninety, with a question mark: Charnock, Calcutta, disputed."*

### Flashcard MOD-1061 — French Compagnie des Indes (1664) and Pondicherry (1674)
**Front:** When was the French Company founded and what were its Indian centres?
**Definition (learn):** The Compagnie des Indes was founded in 1664 under Colbert; French centres included Surat, Masulipatnam, Pondicherry (1674) and Chandernagore (✅ owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Sponsor: Colbert, a state-backed company
- Centres: Pondicherry, Chandernagore
- Weakness: state dependence and weaker naval-financial support (✅ owner 03 Adv §1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1664; 1674 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1062, MOD-1064, MOD-1067.
**Trap:** The French were the main European rival in the Carnatic, not the Dutch (✅ owner 03).
**Mains hook:** The French matched the English militarily but not financially or navally.
**Back:** *"Colbert's company, Pondicherry in seventy-four."*

### Flashcard MOD-1062 — Danish Tranquebar: a minor power
**Front:** What was the Danish presence in India?
**Definition (learn):** A small chartered venture with trading posts; Tranquebar was its chief centre (✅ owner 03 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: trading posts only
- Significance: commercially marginal
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no date (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1061.
**Trap:** Danish power was not a political rival.
**Mains hook:** Minor European ventures show how the field narrowed to English and French.
**Back:** *"Tranquebar: a Danish post, politically quiet."*

### Flashcard MOD-1063 — Trade-to-territory mechanism
**Front:** How did traders become rulers?
**Definition (learn):** A sequence in which charter finance led to a factory, fortification, garrison, intervention in succession disputes, client subsidy and revenue control (✅ owner 03 Adv §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Charter finance → factory by permission (Surat 1613) → fortification (Fort St George 1639) → garrison and private trade (dastak abuse after 1717) → succession intervention → client pays subsidy → Company funds standing sepoy army → revenue control in Bengal
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner's mechanism diagram (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1056, MOD-1057, MOD-1046, MOD-1066.
**Trap:** The chain is not a timetable; each step is contingent.
**Mains hook:** "How did traders become rulers?" use this chain with dated anchors.
**Back:** *"Charter, factory, fort, garrison, client, revenue, rule."*

### Flashcard MOD-1064 — First Carnatic War (1746–48) and Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle
**Front:** How did the First Carnatic War end?
**Definition (learn):** The First Carnatic War (1746–48) ended with the Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (1748); Madras was returned to the English (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Treaty: Aix-la-Chapelle (1748)
- Exchange: France received Louisbourg (North America) in return for Madras (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1746–48; 1748 (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1065, MOD-1058.
**Trap:** Aix-la-Chapelle closed the FIRST war, not the third (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** The Carnatic wars followed European settlements, showing contingency.
**Back:** *"Forty-eight: Aix-la-Chapelle, Madras home."*

### Flashcard MOD-1065 — Second Carnatic War (1749–54) and Dupleix
**Front:** What was Dupleix's role in the Second Carnatic War?
**Definition (learn):** The war (1749–54) involved Dupleix's political interventions in Indian succession disputes, converting military support into political influence (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia years 1749–54).
**Breakdown:**
- Method: client rulers in succession fights
- Legacy: subsidiary-system precedent (see MOD-1073 concept)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1749–54 (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1064, MOD-1066, MOD-1073.
**Trap:** The technique of client rulers was French in origin, English in exploitation (✅ owner 03 Adv §8.4 E).
**Mains hook:** The Carnatic wars were European wars fought through Indian succession politics.
**Back:** *"Dupleix backed princes; the English later bought them."*

### Flashcard MOD-1066 — Third Carnatic War: dates and treaty
**Front:** When did the Third Carnatic War take place and how did it end?
**Definition (learn):** The Third Carnatic War ended French hopes; Wandiwash decided it and the Treaty of Paris (1763) followed (✅ owner 03).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner date: 1756–63 (✅ owner 03 §4).
- Wikipedia section headings give 1757–63 (⚠️ conflict; fetched 2026-11-01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** End 1763 (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia). Start year conflicts (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1067, MOD-1068.
**Trap:** Do not assert the Third War's start year without noting the owner-vs-Wikipedia difference.
**Mains hook:** Carnatic wars shaped the shift from trading corporation to company-state (see MOD-1070).
**Back:** *"Fifty-six or fifty-seven: the third and last French struggle."*

### Flashcard MOD-1067 — Battle of Wandiwash (1760): Coote vs Lally
**Front:** Which battle broke French military prospects in south India, and who fought?
**Definition (learn):** Wandiwash (1760) was an Anglo-French battle in which Eyre Coote defeated the French under Lally, after which Pondicherry fell (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Sides: English (Coote) vs French (Lally)
- Result: French field power in south India destroyed
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1760 (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1066, MOD-1068, MOD-1071.
**Trap:** Wandiwash was Anglo-French, not Anglo-Portuguese (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Wandiwash was decisive because it ended French field capacity, not because it ended the war in Europe.
**Back:** *"Sixty, Coote beats Lally at Wandiwash."*

### Flashcard MOD-1068 — Treaty of Paris (1763): French restored, ambitions curbed
**Front:** What did the Treaty of Paris (1763) do for the French?
**Definition (learn):** It returned factories (Chandernagore and Pondicherry) to France but curtailed French political ambition and power in India (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia returns, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Restored: Pondicherry and Chandernagore
- Curbed: political role
- Commercial presence allowed (✅ owner 03 Adv §5)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1763 (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1066, MOD-1067.
**Trap:** The Treaty of Paris did not expel the French from India (✅ owner 03 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Treaties in Europe decided Indian outcomes.
**Back:** *"Sixty-three: factories back, power down."*

### Flashcard MOD-1069 — Comparative company matrix
**Front:** How did the four European powers differ in ownership, strategy and outcome?
**Definition (learn):** A matrix comparing Portuguese (Crown enterprise), Dutch (joint-stock, bulk commerce), English (joint-stock, London credit), French (state-backed, client rulers) and Danish (small venture) (✅ owner 03 §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Portuguese: Goa and the west coast; reduced to enclaves
- Dutch: Indonesia; withdrew as political force
- English: Bengal, Coromandel, western coast; territorial supremacy
- French: Carnatic and Deccan; politically eliminated after 1763
- Danish: Tranquebar; commercially marginal
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1051, MOD-1054, MOD-1061, MOD-1070.
**Trap:** Do not assume the Portuguese and English models were identical (✅ owner 03 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the matrix for "compare the European companies".
**Back:** *"Crown, Dutch trade, London credit, state client, small post."*

### Flashcard MOD-1070 — Concept: the company-state
**Front:** What does "company-state" mean?
**Definition (learn):** A chartered company that combined commerce, diplomacy, fortification and army-building into sovereign power (✅ owner 03 Adv §1, ⚠️ term is historiographical).
**Breakdown:**
- Monopoly privileges
- Armies and forts
- Diplomatic authority
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner marks the Carnatic phase as the embryo (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1069, MOD-1063, MOD-1074.
**Trap:** A company-state was neither an ordinary firm nor a full state (✅ owner 03 Adv historiography).
**Mains hook:** "Commerce and sovereignty" in one institution explains the 1757–1858 structure.
**Back:** *"Trade with a fort and an army."*

### Flashcard MOD-1071 — Trap: Carnatic Wars were not only European wars
**Front:** Were the Carnatic Wars only European wars in India?
**Definition (learn):** No; they were European wars interwoven with Indian succession politics (✅ owner 03 Adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- European layer: Anglo-French rivalry
- Indian layer: succession disputes in the Carnatic
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1746–63 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1065, MOD-1067.
**Trap:** Dupleix did not fail for lack of political imagination (✅ owner 03 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Assess how Europeans exploited Indian rivalries.
**Back:** *"European war, Indian quarrel, one battlefield."*

### Flashcard MOD-1072 — Trap: Dutch versus French as rivals
**Front:** Who were the main European rivals of the English in the Carnatic Wars?
**Definition (learn):** The French (Compagnie des Indes), not the Dutch (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Dutch: commercial rivals, shifted to Indonesia
- French: political-military rivals in south India
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Carnatic 1746–63 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1054, MOD-1061.
**Trap:** Do not place the Dutch as main political rivals (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Mains hook:** Distinguish commercial from political rivalry.
**Back:** *"Carnatic rival is French, not Dutch."*

### Flashcard MOD-1073 — Dupleix's client-ruler method and the subsidiary precursor
**Front:** Why does the Dupleix method matter for the subsidiary system?
**Definition (learn):** Installing client rulers in succession disputes and securing subsidy-paid troops became the direct ancestor of the later subsidiary system (✅ owner 03 Adv §8.4 E, ⚠️ precursor reading).
**Breakdown:**
- French origin: client-ruler technique
- English exploitation: Wellesley's systematic version (see MOD-1106)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1749–54 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1065, MOD-1088.
**Trap:** French failure was owed to state dependence and war settlements, not generalship alone (✅ owner 03 §8.4 E).
**Mains hook:** Trace the precedent of the subsidiary alliance to earlier Carnatic practice.
**Back:** *"Dupleix first, Wellesley perfected."*

### Flashcard MOD-1074 — Verdict: why the English, not the Portuguese, Dutch or French
**Front:** Why did the English become the dominant European power?
**Definition (learn):** Their institution could convert Indian profit into Indian power while rivals depended on European treasuries (✅ owner 03 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Institution: joint-stock, shareholder-driven
- Finance: London credit
- Navy: superior naval links
- Bengal: revenue after 1757
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Carnatic 1746–63; Bengal after 1757 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MOD-1069, MOD-1070, MOD-1055.
**Trap:** The English did not win merely because their traders were abler.
**Mains hook:** Composite-advantage thesis (✅ owner 03 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Institution plus navy plus Bengal."*

### Flashcard MOD-1075 — Colonial port heritage (current link)
**Front:** Why do Goa, Fort St George, Fort William and Pondicherry matter today?
**Definition (learn):** Living heritage sites where conservation, tourism and urban planning keep early European settlements relevant (✅ owner 03 §6, ⚠️ not pegged to a news item).
**Breakdown:**
- Goa; Chennai's Fort St George; Kolkata's Fort William; Puducherry's French quarter
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No current figures used (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1058, MOD-1060.
**Trap:** Do not present heritage debates as settled policy.
**Mains hook:** Maritime history and redevelopment intersect in colonial port cities.
**Back:** *"Old forts, new planning debates."*

### GROUP — The British Conquest of Bengal (Plassey & Buxar)

### Flashcard MOD-1076 — Battle of Plassey (23 June 1757): Clive vs Siraj-ud-Daula
**Front:** When and between whom was the Battle of Plassey fought?
**Definition (learn):** Fought on 23 June 1757 between Robert Clive's Company forces and Siraj-ud-Daula, Nawab of Bengal; won through conspiracy and defection (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: fortification, private trade and jurisdiction disputes (see MOD-1079)
- Course: defection of commanders during battle
- Outcome: regime change; Mir Jafar installed (MOD-1080)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 23 June 1757 (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1077, MOD-1078, MOD-1079, MOD-1089, MOD-1094.
**Trap:** Plassey was a political revolution and coup, not a major battlefield victory (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Plassey was a coup; Buxar was a conquest." Analyse both as stages.
**Back:** *"Twenty-three June, fifty-seven: Clive buys the army."*

### Flashcard MOD-1077 — Plassey conspirators: Mir Jafar, Rai Durlabh, Jagat Seth, Amichand
**Front:** Who were the main conspirators at Plassey?
**Definition (learn):** Mir Jafar (commander, promised the Nawabship), Rai Durlabh (commanded a wing), the Jagat Seth banking house and Amichand (Omichund), a merchant who threatened to expose the plot (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Military: Mir Jafar, Rai Durlabh, Yar Lutuf Khan (✅ Wikipedia)
- Financial: Jagat Seths (Mahtab Chand, Swarup Chand)
- Merchant: Amichand/Omichund (red treaty deception, ✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1757 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1076, MOD-1038, MOD-1080.
**Trap:** The Jagat Seths and Amichand were not Company servants; the conquest rested on Indian elite alignment (✅ owner 04 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** Plassey was decided in Murshidabad before it was decided on the field.
**Back:** *"Mir Jafar, Durlabh, the Seths, Amichand: four hands on the purse."*

### Flashcard MOD-1078 — Black Hole of Calcutta: contested, not the causal centrepiece
**Front:** Should the "Black Hole" episode be treated as the cause of Plassey?
**Definition (learn):** The Black Hole episode is historically contested and must not be used as the main cause of conquest (✅ owner 04 §1, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner stance: treat as contested
- Real causes: trade privileges, fortification, private trade
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None assigned (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1076, MOD-1079.
**Trap:** The Black Hole alone did not cause conquest (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Use it as an example of imperial justification, not as a causal fact.
**Back:** *"Black Hole: a story to test, not a cause to write."*

### Flashcard MOD-1079 — Causes of conflict: 1717 farman, dastaks and Calcutta's fortification
**Front:** What caused the Nawab–Company conflict before 1757?
**Definition (learn):** The 1717 farman granted duty-free trade for Company goods; Company servants extended dastaks to private trade, and Calcutta was fortified without permission (✅ owner 04 §1, Adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Fiscal: customs revenue shifted to individuals
- Sovereignty: fortification and jurisdiction challenged the Nawab
- Market: Indian merchants undercut
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1717 farman; Siraj's objection 1756 (✅ owner 04 Adv mini-timeline).
**Links:** MOD-1015, MOD-1046, MOD-1076.
**Trap:** Do not present the conflict as a pure Nawabi grievance; Siraj's accession disputes weakened him (✅ owner 04 §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** Extending a corporate exemption to private trade made the position unsustainable for any ruler.
**Back:** *"Farman, dastak, fort: the Nawab's revenue leaks away."*

### Flashcard MOD-1080 — Mir Jafar, Mir Qasim and the 1760 succession
**Front:** Who succeeded whom in Bengal after Plassey?
**Definition (learn):** Mir Jafar was installed after Plassey; he failed to meet Company demands and Mir Qasim replaced him in 1760 (✅ owner 04).
**Breakdown:**
- Mir Jafar: puppet Nawab, symbol of Company-backed change
- Mir Qasim: from 1760; capital moved to Munger
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1760 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1076, MOD-1081.
**Trap:** Mir Qasim was not simply anti-English; he sought fiscal equality (✅ owner 04 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** A client Nawab that cannot meet Company demands produces the next crisis.
**Back:** *"Mir Jafar sold, Mir Qasim tried."*

### Flashcard MOD-1081 — Mir Qasim's reforms and abolition of inland duties
**Front:** What did Mir Qasim do and why did it cause war?
**Definition (learn):** He shifted his capital to Munger, reorganised army and finances, and abolished inland duties so Indian traders faced no disadvantage against duty-free Company traders (✅ owner 04 §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Reform: army and administration
- Equalisation: inland duties abolished
- Result: open conflict with the Company
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Munger; inland-duty disputes (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1080, MOD-1082, MOD-1097.
**Trap:** Do not romanticise the reforms; fiscal pressure alienated elites (✅ owner 04 §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** A logically complete answer to the privilege problem produced war rather than settlement.
**Back:** *"Munger: duties gone, war begins."*

### Flashcard MOD-1082 — Battle of Buxar (22–23 October 1764): Munro's victory
**Front:** Who defeated whom at Buxar and when?
**Definition (learn):** Major Hector Munro defeated Mir Qasim, Shuja-ud-Daula of Awadh and the Mughal emperor Shah Alam II on 22 October 1764 (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia gives 22–23 October 1764, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Coalition: Bengal + Awadh + Mughal emperor (Balwant Singh of Benares and Mirza Najaf Khan also on the opposing side per Wikipedia)
- Result: no superior authority left in northern India for Bengal to appeal to
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 22 Oct 1764 (✅ owner 04). Troop numbers in Wikipedia are not used (⚠️ owner forbids casualty and troop figures).
**Links:** MOD-1080, MOD-1083, MOD-1084, MOD-1089.
**Trap:** Buxar was not only a Bengal battle; it defeated a wider confederacy (✅ owner 04 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** "Buxar was the military verdict that settled northern supremacy."
**Back:** *"Sixty-four, Buxar: three rulers beaten at once."*

### Flashcard MOD-1083 — Shah Alam II and Shuja-ud-Daula: the Buxar coalition
**Front:** Who were the allied rulers defeated at Buxar?
**Definition (learn):** Shuja-ud-Daula, Nawab of Awadh and the emperor's grand vizier, and Shah Alam II, the Mughal emperor, who surrendered (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Shuja-ud-Daula: Awadh, later preserved as a buffer (MOD-1044)
- Shah Alam II: Mughal emperor; later granted the Diwani settlement
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1764 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1082, MOD-1084, MOD-1044.
**Trap:** Shah Alam II did not rule Bengal after Buxar; he legitimised Company revenue (✅ owner 04 Adv §2).
**Mains hook:** Defeating a Nawab, a neighbour and the emperor together left no higher authority.
**Back:** *"Shuja and Shah Alam lost the field together."*

### Flashcard MOD-1084 — Treaty of Allahabad (1765): Diwani of Bengal, Bihar and Orissa
**Front:** What did the Treaty of Allahabad (1765) grant the Company?
**Definition (learn):** The Diwani (revenue collection rights) of Bengal, Bihar and Orissa, collected on the Mughal emperor's behalf (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Diwani: revenue right
- Nizamat: administrative responsibility (stayed with Nawab until 1772)
- Buffer: Awadh restored, not annexed (MOD-1044)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1765 (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1085, MOD-1086, MOD-1087, MOD-1044.
**Trap:** Diwani is not Nawabship; the Company did not become Nawab in 1765 (✅ owner 04 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** The Diwani converted a trading company into a revenue state.
**Back:** *"Sixty-five: revenue rights, not the throne."*

### Flashcard MOD-1085 — Diwani versus Nizamat
**Front:** How do Diwani and Nizamat differ?
**Definition (learn):** Diwani is the revenue-collection right; Nizamat is the administration and criminal jurisdiction, which remained formally with the Nawab until 1772 (✅ owner 04 §8.4 E, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Diwani: Company
- Nizamat: Nawab's responsibility, in practice shared under Dual Government
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nizamat formally with Nawab until 1772 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1084, MOD-1086.
**Trap:** Never write Diwani as if it meant Nawabship (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Distinguish the two offices in any Dual Government answer.
**Back:** *"Diwani takes the revenue; Nizamat keeps the Nawab's name."*

### Flashcard MOD-1086 — Dual Government (1765–72): power without responsibility
**Front:** What was Dual Government in Bengal?
**Definition (learn):** From 1765 to 1772 the Company held the Diwani (revenue power) while the Nawab's establishment retained administrative responsibility, so revenue was collected without accountability (✅ owner 04, Adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Company: revenue power, purse
- Nawab: administrative responsibility
- Collection: Indian deputies (⚠️ detail flagged in owner)
- Outcome: 1770 famine during this period (MOD-1088)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1765–72; ended by Warren Hastings in 1772 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1084, MOD-1087, MOD-1088.
**Trap:** Dual Government was a design failure, not one of individuals (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
**Mains hook:** "Power without responsibility" is the classic formulation (✅ owner 04 Adv §4).
**Back:** *"Company held the purse, Nawab held the blame."*

### Flashcard MOD-1087 — Warren Hastings ends Dual Government (1772)
**Front:** When and by whom was Dual Government ended?
**Definition (learn):** Warren Hastings ended Dual Government in 1772 and began direct Company administration in Bengal (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia: local rule abolished 1772, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- 1772: direct administration
- Next step: the Regulating Act 1773 (MOD-1128)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1772 (✅ owner 04; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1086, MOD-1103.
**Trap:** Hastings's 1772 move is not the Regulating Act; the Act followed in 1773 (✅ owner 04 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Hastings's reform was the transition from revenue dominance to administrative control.
**Back:** *"Seventy-two: Hastings takes the whole machine."*

### Flashcard MOD-1088 — Bengal Famine of 1770: aggravation, not a single cause
**Front:** What role did the Company play in the 1770 Bengal famine?
**Definition (learn):** The famine combined crop failure, disease and an extractive, unaccountable revenue order; the defensible claim is aggravation, not sole causation (✅ owner 04 Adv §4, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Natural: harvest failure, epidemic (✅ owner 04 §8.4 F note)
- Institutional: revenue extraction under Dual Government
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1770 (✅ owner 04). Do not cite mortality figures (✅ owner 04 §8.8).
**Links:** MOD-1086, MOD-1087, MOD-1174 (famine policy).
**Trap:** Do not reduce the famine to one cause or to climate alone.
**Mains hook:** Vulnerability and response, not monocausal blame.
**Back:** *"Seventy: drought, disease, and a revenue that would not wait."*

### Flashcard MOD-1089 — Plassey versus Buxar: political entry, military supremacy, legal title
**Front:** How do Plassey and Buxar differ?
**Definition (learn):** Plassey gave political entry through conspiracy; Buxar gave military supremacy; Allahabad (1765) gave legal title (✅ owner 04 §8.2, §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Plassey (1757): coup, pliant Nawab
- Buxar (1764): battlefield supremacy over Bengal, Awadh and the emperor
- Allahabad (1765): Diwani, legal revenue sovereignty
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1757; 1764; 1765 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1076, MOD-1082, MOD-1084, MOD-1095.
**Trap:** Plassey alone did not make the Company sovereign (✅ owner 04 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the two-stage thesis for "which was decisive?" questions.
**Back:** *"Plassey enters, Buxar rules, Allahabad titles."*

### Flashcard MOD-1090 — Causal mechanism: from farman to 1772
**Front:** What was the chain from the 1717 farman to Hastings's administration?
**Definition (learn):** A sequence in which privilege became private trade abuse, sovereignty conflict, a bought elite, Plassey, Mir Qasim's reform, Buxar, Diwani, Dual Government and direct administration (✅ owner 04 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- 1717 farman → dastak abuse → customs erosion
- Nawab asserts sovereignty → Company buys elite → Plassey 1757
- Client cannot pay → Mir Qasim reform → war → Buxar 1764
- Allahabad 1765 → Dual Government 1765–72 → 1770 famine → 1772 Hastings
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1015, MOD-1076, MOD-1082, MOD-1084, MOD-1087.
**Trap:** The chain is contingent, not inevitable (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Use as the spine for a 15-mark causation answer.
**Back:** *"Farman, dastak, Plassey, Buxar, Diwani, Hastings."*

### Flashcard MOD-1091 — Bengal as the engine of empire
**Front:** How did Bengal finance later British conquest?
**Definition (learn):** Bengal land revenue funded export purchases and sepoy armies instead of imported bullion; those armies fought Mysore, Maratha and later campaigns (✅ owner 04 §8.4 G, ⚠️ quantified drain belongs to economic owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Revenue → purchases and army → further conquest
- Link: subsidiary and expansion (MOD-1098)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-1765 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1084, MOD-1098, MOD-1111.
**Trap:** Do not quantify drain in this card (✅ owner 04 §8.4 G).
**Mains hook:** Bengal turned a regional episode into the pivot of the conquest narrative.
**Back:** *"Bengal's revenue paid for the sepoy."*

### Flashcard MOD-1092 — Trap: Plassey was not a battlefield victory
**Front:** Was Plassey mainly a battlefield victory?
**Definition (learn):** No; it was largely a conspiracy-backed coup (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Political: defection and bribery
- Military: brief engagement
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 23 June 1757 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1076, MOD-1077.
**Trap:** Do not moralise Plassey as betrayal alone; analyse the political economy (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Trade privilege, merchant-bankers, revenue rights and military finance together explain it.
**Back:** *"Plassey: a purchase, not a pitched battle."*

### Flashcard MOD-1093 — Trap: Buxar was not less important than Plassey
**Front:** Which battle mattered more for Company supremacy?
**Definition (learn):** Buxar established military supremacy over Bengal, Awadh and the emperor (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Plassey: political entry
- Buxar: military supremacy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1757; 1764 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1082, MOD-1089.
**Trap:** Do not say Buxar was only a Bengal battle (✅ owner 04 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Buxar's significance is what it settled politically.
**Back:** *"Buxar outranks Plassey for the north."*

### Flashcard MOD-1094 — Owner versus Wikipedia: "near-bloodless" Plassey
**Front:** Was Plassey near-bloodless?
**Definition (learn):** The owner calls it near-bloodless (✅ owner 04 §1). Wikipedia's infobox lists 27 British killed and 50 wounded, plus about 500 killed and wounded on the Nawab's side (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: near-bloodless
- Wikipedia: casualty figures are given
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Casualty figures per Wikipedia (⚠️ conflict; do not assert either without flagging).
**Links:** MOD-1076, MOD-1092.
**Trap:** Avoid stating casualty numbers in an answer; use "small-scale engagement with decisive defection" (✅ owner 04 §8.8 style).
**Mains hook:** The decisive factor was defection, not losses.
**Back:** *"Few killed, many bought."*

### Flashcard MOD-1095 — Verdict: 1757 or 1764–65 as the turning point?
**Front:** Which date marks when the Company gained Bengal?
**Definition (learn):** If the question is when the Company gained Bengal, the answer is 1757; if it is when the Company could not be removed, the answer is 1764–65 (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- 1757: entry
- 1764–65: entrenchment and legal title
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1757; 1764; 1765 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1089, MOD-1090.
**Trap:** Do not pick one date for two different questions.
**Mains hook:** Periodisation requires a stated criterion (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Fifty-seven to enter, sixty-four-five to stay."*

### Flashcard MOD-1096 — Current link: colonial drain and Plassey anniversaries
**Front:** Why does the Plassey/Buxar story still matter publicly?
**Definition (learn):** Anniversaries and drain debates keep Bengal's conquest relevant to discussions of economic nationalism and heritage education (✅ owner 04 §6, ⚠️ not pegged to a news item).
**Breakdown:**
- Public memory, heritage, reparations debate
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No current figures used (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1091, MOD-1193.
**Trap:** Do not present reparations as settled.
**Mains hook:** Use for the drain theory's origin in the Diwani settlement.
**Back:** *"Plassey's anniversary, the drain's origin."*

### Flashcard MOD-1097 — Concept: Mir Qasim and the limits of reform
**Front:** Why did Mir Qasim's reforms fail?
**Definition (learn):** The asymmetry was structural: equalising duties produced war, not settlement (✅ owner 04 §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Reform logic: equal duties
- Structural limit: Company's naval and credit advantage
- Elite alienation: fiscal pressure
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1760–64 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1081, MOD-1080.
**Trap:** Do not present Mir Qasim as simply anti-English (✅ owner 04 Adv).
**Mains hook:** Resistance was attempted at the highest level, not foreordained to fail (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
**Back:** *"Reform equal, war follows."*

### Flashcard MOD-1098 — Concept: Bengal's buffer and the Company's expansion logic
**Front:** How did the Diwani settlement set the template for expansion?
**Definition (learn):** Acquire revenue and control while retaining an Indian ruler as the visible authority and an allied state as a shield (✅ owner 04 §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Revenue: Diwani
- Visible authority: Nawab
- Shield: Awadh buffer
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1765 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MOD-1084, MOD-1044, MOD-1111.
**Trap:** Diwani was a revenue right, not annexation (✅ owner 04).
**Mains hook:** The template of British expansion began here.
**Back:** *"Revenue taken, ruler kept, buffer held."*

### GROUP — British Territorial Expansion (Mysore, Marathas, Sikhs)

### Flashcard MOD-1099 — First Anglo-Mysore War (1767–69) and Treaty of Madras
**Front:** How did the First Anglo-Mysore War end?
**Definition (learn):** The First Anglo-Mysore War (1767–69) ended with the Treaty of Madras (✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- Opponent: Haidar Ali's Mysore
- Treaty: Madras (1769)
- Context: Mysore as the southern anti-Company modernising state (✅ owner 05 Adv §1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1767–69; Treaty of Madras 1769 (✅ owner 05 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1100, MOD-1101, MOD-1102, MOD-1121.
**Trap:** Madras closed the First Mysore War, not the second (✅ owner 05 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Mysore's wars trace a learning curve on the Company's side.
**Back:** *"Sixty-seven to sixty-nine: Madras makes the first peace."*

### Flashcard MOD-1100 — Second Anglo-Mysore War (1780–84) and Treaty of Mangalore
**Front:** How did the Second Anglo-Mysore War end and what happened to Haidar Ali?
**Definition (learn):** The war (1780–84) ended with the Treaty of Mangalore; Haidar Ali died in 1782 (✅ owner 05).
