**Breakdown:**
- Treaty: Mangalore (1784)
- Haidar Ali: died 1782 (✅ owner 05)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1780–84; 1782; 1784 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1099, MOD-1101, MOD-1035.
**Trap:** Mangalore closed the Second war, not the first (✅ owner 05 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Mysore faced repeated coalitions and reverses.
**Back:** *"Eighty to eighty-four: Haidar dies, Mangalore signs."*

### Flashcard MOD-1101 — Third Anglo-Mysore War (1790–92) and Treaty of Seringapatam
**Front:** What did the Treaty of Seringapatam require of Tipu?
**Definition (learn):** The Third War (1790–92) ended with the Treaty of Seringapatam; Tipu ceded about half his territory (✅ owner 05; ✅ owner 05 Adv mini-timeline).
**Breakdown:**
- Treaty: Seringapatam (1792)
- Cost: half his territory
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1790–92 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1100, MOD-1102.
**Trap:** Do not attach the wrong treaty to the wrong war (✅ owner 05 §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** Territorial cession shows the Company could demand indemnity from a strong state.
**Back:** *"Ninety-two: Seringapatam, half the land gone."*

### Flashcard MOD-1102 — Fourth Anglo-Mysore War (1799): Tipu dies, Wodeyars restored
**Front:** How did Tipu Sultan die and who was restored?
**Definition (learn):** Tipu died defending Seringapatam in 1799; the Wodeyars were restored under subsidiary terms (✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- Death: Tipu, 1799
- Restored: Wodeyar dynasty under the subsidiary system (MOD-1106)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1799 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1101, MOD-1106, MOD-1035.
**Trap:** Tipu did not die in the Third war; he died in the Fourth (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Mysore's defeat came through coalition, not a lack of modernising effort.
**Back:** *"Ninety-nine: Tipu falls, Wodeyars return."*

### Flashcard MOD-1103 — First Anglo-Maratha War (1775–82) and Treaty of Salbai
**Front:** How did the First Anglo-Maratha War end?
**Definition (learn):** The war (1775–82) ended with the Treaty of Salbai, a negotiated peace (✅ owner 05 Adv mini-timeline; ✅ owner 05 §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Treaty: Salbai (1782)
- Character: negotiated peace, not dictated
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1775–82; Salbai 1782 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1104, MOD-1105, MOD-1117.
**Trap:** Salbai closed the FIRST Maratha war, not the second (✅ owner 05 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** The Company negotiated rather than dictated in the first Maratha war.
**Back:** *"Eighty-two, Salbai: the first Maratha peace."*

### Flashcard MOD-1104 — Second Anglo-Maratha War (1803–05): Assaye and submissions
**Front:** What happened in the Second Anglo-Maratha War?
**Definition (learn):** Wellesley's war (1803–05) included the battle of Assaye; Scindia and Bhonsle submitted (✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- Leader: Wellesley
- Battle: Assaye
- Result: Scindia and Bhonsle submitted; chiefs isolated individually (✅ owner 05 Adv §8.4 D)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1803–05 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1103, MOD-1105, MOD-1106.
**Trap:** Wellesley's Subsidiary Alliance is not Dalhousie's Lapse.
**Mains hook:** By the second war the Company could isolate individual chiefs.
**Back:** *"Oh-three to oh-five: Assaye, and two chiefs bow."*

### Flashcard MOD-1105 — Third Anglo-Maratha War (1817–18): Baji Rao II pensioned
**Front:** What was the outcome of the Third Anglo-Maratha War?
**Definition (learn):** Peshwa Baji Rao II was deposed and pensioned at Bithur; the confederacy ended (✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- Peshwa: deposed, pensioned at Bithur
- Confederacy: broken
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1817–18 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1104, MOD-1107, MOD-1115.
**Trap:** The British defeated a confederacy weakened by rivalries, not a united nationalism (✅ owner 05 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Fragmentation is the argument for the Maratha defeat.
**Back:** *"Seventeen-eighteen: Baji Rao to Bithur, the Peshwa is gone."*

### Flashcard MOD-1106 — Subsidiary Alliance: the clauses
**Front:** What were the core clauses of the Subsidiary Alliance?
**Definition (learn):** A system in which an Indian state hosted British troops, paid for them with cash or territory, accepted a British Resident and gave up independent foreign relations (✅ owner 05 §8.4 A; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- British force stationed in the state (at or near the capital)
- Payment in cash or ceded revenue territory
- British Resident supervises diplomacy and correspondence
- No independent foreign relations or war without British consent
- No Europeans employed; no independent standing army (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Systematised by Wellesley from 1798 (✅ owner 05; ✅ Wikipedia). Wellesley's 1801 dispatch aim: dependence (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1107, MOD-1073, MOD-1111, MOD-1119.
**Trap:** Subsidiary Alliance is not Doctrine of Lapse (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Each clause removed one attribute of sovereignty.
**Back:** *"Troops, subsidy, Resident, no foreign voice."*

### Flashcard MOD-1107 — Subsidiary states chronology
**Front:** In what order did major Indian states accept the subsidiary alliance?
**Definition (learn):** Hyderabad first (1798), then Mysore after 1799, Tanjore (1799), Awadh (1801), Peshwa (1802), Bhonsle (1803), Scindia (1804), Jaipur and Jodhpur (1818), Holkar (1818) (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- 1798: Hyderabad (first well-defined acceptor)
- 1801: Awadh
- 1802: Peshwa (Bassein is owner-named; Wikipedia gives the year only)
- 1803–04: Bhonsle, Scindia
- 1818: Holkar, Jaipur, Jodhpur
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1106, MOD-1014, MOD-1105.
**Trap:** Hyderabad was first, not Mysore (✅ owner 05 §4).
**Mains hook:** The chronology shows a pattern of aggressive acceptance.
**Back:** *"Ninety-eight, Hyderabad leads; eighteen, Holkar comes last."*

### Flashcard MOD-1108 — Doctrine of Lapse: annexation by adoption refusal
**Front:** What was the Doctrine of Lapse and who is associated with it?
**Definition (learn):** States without a natural heir could be annexed by refusing adoption rights; associated with Dalhousie (1848–56) (✅ owner 05; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: refusing adoption
- Result: annexation of states
- Origin: articulated by the Court of Directors as early as 1834 (✅ Wikipedia), before Dalhousie
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dalhousie governed 1848–56 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1109, MOD-1110, MOD-1119.
**Trap:** Lapse was not introduced by Wellesley; do not conflate with the Subsidiary Alliance (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Lapse struck at the adoption practice that legitimised ruling houses.
**Back:** *"No heir of your own, the state goes home."*

### Flashcard MOD-1109 — Lapse cases: Satara, Jhansi, Nagpur and others
**Front:** Name the chief Lapse annexations and their years.
**Definition (learn):** Satara (1848), Jaitpur and Sambalpur (1849), Baghal (1850), Udaipur (1852), Jhansi (after the 1853 succession dispute), Nagpur (1854), Tanjore and Arcot (1855) (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01; owner: Satara 1848, Jhansi 1854 and Nagpur 1854 ✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- Satara 1848
- Jhansi 1853/1854 (⚠️ Wikipedia gives both years in different places)
- Nagpur 1853/1854 (⚠️ Wikipedia's first table gives 1853; text and template give 1854)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ Wikipedia; owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1108, MOD-1110, MOD-1115.
**Trap:** Jhansi was annexed by Dalhousie, not by Wellesley (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Legalistic annexation deepened resentment before 1857.
**Back:** *"Satara forty-eight; Jhansi and Nagpur fifty-three to fifty-four."*

### Flashcard MOD-1110 — Awadh annexed in 1856 on misgovernance, not Lapse
**Front:** How was Awadh annexed and why is it not a Lapse case?
**Definition (learn):** Awadh was annexed in 1856 on the ground of alleged misgovernance; it is separately examinable from Lapse (✅ owner 05 §8.4 B; ✅ Wikipedia: "widely believed" to be Lapse but was misgovernance).
**Breakdown:**
- Ground: alleged misgovernance
- Year: 1856
- Note: Wikipedia's Lapse table still lists Awadh, contradicting its own text (⚠️)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1856 (✅ owner 05; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1109, MOD-1013, MOD-1044.
**Trap:** Awadh was not annexed by Doctrine of Lapse (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Multiple legal pretexts show expansion used different justifications.
**Back:** *"Fifty-six: not lapse, misgovernance."*

### Flashcard MOD-1111 — Expansion mechanism: Bengal revenue to residual states
**Front:** How did British expansion work as a mechanism?
**Definition (learn):** Bengal revenue funded a permanent salaried sepoy army; one state at a time was attacked, with allies recruited; victory was financed by indemnity or ceded land; ceded land raised revenue; subsidiary treaties disarmed states; Lapse and misgovernance absorbed the rest (✅ owner 05 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Revenue → army → war
- Victory → territory → revenue → larger army
- Subsidiary treaty → disarm
- Lapse/misgovernance → absorb
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner mechanism (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1091, MOD-1106, MOD-1108, MOD-1124.
**Trap:** Expansion was not a single deliberate plan (✅ owner 05 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** "How did war, alliance and annexation together build the Empire?"
**Back:** *"Revenue feeds the army, the army feeds the state."*

### Flashcard MOD-1112 — Sindh annexed by Charles Napier (1843)
**Front:** Who annexed Sindh and when?
**Definition (learn):** Sindh was annexed in 1843 by Charles Napier (✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- Agent: Napier
- Year: 1843
- Note: different war and agent from Punjab (✅ owner 05 §8.8)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1843 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1113, MOD-1115.
**Trap:** Sindh (1843) and Punjab (1849) are different annexations (✅ owner 05 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Northwest expansion followed frontier logic.
**Back:** *"Forty-three: Napier takes Sindh."*

### Flashcard MOD-1113 — Anglo-Sikh Wars and the annexation of Punjab (1849)
**Front:** When and why was Punjab annexed?
**Definition (learn):** The First Anglo-Sikh War (1845–46; Treaty of Lahore) and the Second (1848–49) ended in the annexation of Punjab in 1849 (✅ owner 05).
**Breakdown:**
- First: 1845–46, Treaty of Lahore
- Second: 1848–49
- Result: Punjab annexed under Dalhousie
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1845–46; 1848–49; 1849 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1114, MOD-1118, MOD-1112.
**Trap:** Ranjit Singh fought no Anglo-Sikh War; the wars came after his death (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Punjab completed northwest expansion.
**Back:** *"Forty-five, forty-nine: Lahore, then Punjab is gone."*

### Flashcard MOD-1114 — Trap: Ranjit Singh and the posthumous Anglo-Sikh Wars
**Front:** Did Ranjit Singh fight the Anglo-Sikh Wars?
**Definition (learn):** No; the wars came after his death, amid court factionalism and military tension (✅ owner 05 Adv §1, §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Ranjit Singh: unified Sikh power, held cohesion
- After him: instability and defeat
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ranjit Singh's death year not stated in owner (⚠️ standard knowledge 1839, not verified here).
**Links:** MOD-1113, MOD-1030, MOD-1118.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the wars to Ranjit Singh.
**Mains hook:** Internal cohesion, not battlefield quality, decided outcomes.
**Back:** *"Lion dead, Lahore fell."*

### Flashcard MOD-1115 — Dispossession as the pre-1857 grievance bank
**Front:** Who were the losers of expansion whose grievances converged in 1857?
**Definition (learn):** Displaced ruling houses (Jhansi, Nagpur, Satara), Awadh taluqdars and disbanded soldiery after 1856, and pensioned or demoted princely families (✅ owner 05 §8.4 F).
**Breakdown:**
- Ruling houses: Jhansi, Nagpur, Satara
- Awadh: taluqdars and soldiery
- Pensions: Baji Rao II's pension (Bithur)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1848–56 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1109, MOD-1110, MOD-1105.
**Trap:** Annexation grievance does not alone explain the geography of 1857 (✅ owner 05 §8.4 F).
**Mains hook:** Gives causes of 1857 a concrete political-economy base.
**Back:** *"Heirs, taluqdars, soldiers: all lost something."*

### Flashcard MOD-1116 — Mysore: the most systematic resistance
**Front:** Why did Mysore come closest to matching the Company?
**Definition (learn):** Haidar Ali and Tipu Sultan reorganised the army along European lines and sought foreign alliances; the defeat was of coalition, not will (✅ owner 05 §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Method: military reform, diplomacy
- Failure: coalition of the Company with the Nizam and the Marathas
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four Anglo-Mysore wars (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1099, MOD-1102, MOD-1035.
**Trap:** Mysore modernised and still lost; do not claim it failed to modernise (✅ owner 05 §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** Mysore shows the defeat of Indian states was not caused by unwillingness to modernise.
**Back:** *"Mysore built and still lost to a coalition."*

### Flashcard MOD-1117 — Marathas: reach without cohesion
**Front:** How did the Maratha confederacy's structure shape its defeat?
**Definition (learn):** The confederate structure gave Maratha reach but made it defeatable in parts (✅ owner 05 §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- First war: negotiated peace (1782)
- Second war: individual chiefs isolated
- Third war: no united command (1817–18)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1775–82; 1803–05; 1817–18 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1103, MOD-1104, MOD-1105, MOD-1028.
**Trap:** Salbai closed the first war only (✅ owner 05 §8.4 D).
**Mains hook:** The trajectory of fragmentation is the argument.
**Back:** *"Reach wide, command narrow, defeat piecemeal."*

### Flashcard MOD-1118 — Sikhs: late consolidation, late defeat
**Front:** Why did Punjab survive longest?
**Definition (learn):** Punjab had a comparable standing army and a settled succession while Ranjit Singh lived; after his death, internal weakness enabled the British (✅ owner 05 §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: army, settled succession
- Weakness: post-Ranjit succession and court instability
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1845–49 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1114, MOD-1113, MOD-1030.
**Trap:** The wars were posthumous (✅ owner 05 §8.4 E).
**Mains hook:** Correlation of death, court instability and defeat is the strongest evidence for internal cohesion as the decider.
**Back:** *"Strong while the Lion lived."*

### Flashcard MOD-1119 — Trap: Subsidiary Alliance, Doctrine of Lapse and misgovernance are distinct
**Front:** Are Subsidiary Alliance and Doctrine of Lapse the same policy?
**Definition (learn):** No. The alliance created dependency; Lapse annexed by succession doctrine; misgovernance annexed by administrative pretext (✅ owner 05 Adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Alliance: subordination without annexation
- Lapse: annexation through heirs
- Misgovernance: Awadh (1856)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap list (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1106, MOD-1108, MOD-1110.
**Trap:** Never interchange the three instruments.
**Mains hook:** "Subsidiary Alliance annexed sovereignty before Lapse annexed territory."
**Back:** *"Alliance binds, Lapse takes heirs, misgovernance takes the rest."*

### Flashcard MOD-1120 — Trap: author matching for the two key instruments
**Front:** Who introduced the Subsidiary Alliance and who the Doctrine of Lapse?
**Definition (learn):** Wellesley systematised the Subsidiary Alliance from 1798; Dalhousie is associated with the Doctrine of Lapse (✅ owner 05 §5, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Wellesley: alliance; Hyderabad 1798
- Dalhousie: Lapse; Satara 1848
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Wellesley 1798–1805; Dalhousie 1848–56 (✅ owner 05; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1106, MOD-1108, MOD-1119.
**Trap:** Wellesley did not annex Jhansi under Lapse (✅ owner 05 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Attribution matters for any "who introduced" question.
**Back:** *"Wellesley binds, Dalhousie lapses."*

### Flashcard MOD-1121 — Trap: treaty-to-war matching for Mysore and Maratha wars
**Front:** Which treaty closed which war?
**Definition (learn):** Madras (First Mysore 1769), Mangalore (Second Mysore 1784), Seringapatam (Third Mysore 1792), Salbai (First Maratha 1782) (✅ owner 05 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Mysore: Madras, Mangalore, Seringapatam
- Maratha: Salbai
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1099, MOD-1100, MOD-1101, MOD-1103.
**Trap:** Do not attach the wrong treaty to the wrong war (✅ owner 05 §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** Precise treaty mapping is required for Prelims statements.
**Back:** *"Madras, Mangalore, Seringapatam, and Salbai for the Marathas."*

### Flashcard MOD-1122 — Serial defeat thesis
**Front:** Why were Indian powers defeated in sequence?
**Definition (learn):** Indian powers were not weaker in aggregate; they were defeated in sequence because no durable anti-British coalition survived British diplomacy (✅ owner 05 §8.2).
**Breakdown:**
- Individually strong states
- Collective failure: no durable coalition
- British diplomacy: isolation and subsidy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner thesis (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1116, MOD-1117, MOD-1118.
**Trap:** "Indians were weak" is not the owner's argument.
**Mains hook:** Use for "why did Indian powers fail?" questions.
**Back:** *"Beaten one at a time, never all together."*

### Flashcard MOD-1123 — Paramountcy: from ring-fence to dominance
**Front:** What is paramountcy as a concept of expansion?
**Definition (learn):** Graded sovereignty: protected allies, subsidiary states, annexed provinces and frontier buffers, with the Company as paramount power (✅ owner 05 Adv historiography, ⚠️ term used in historiography).
**Breakdown:**
- Ring-fence: buffer policy
- Subsidiary: dependence
- Lapse and annexation: direct control
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Trajectory from ring-fence to Wellesley to Dalhousie (✅ owner 05 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1106, MOD-1108, MOD-1111.
**Trap:** Expansion was a spectrum, not battlefield conquest only (✅ owner 05 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Explain subsidies and Residents as instruments of paramountcy.
**Back:** *"Ring-fence, subsidy, lapse, annexation: a ladder."*

### Flashcard MOD-1124 — Instrument verdict: alliance before lapse
**Front:** Why was the Subsidiary Alliance more destructive than annexation?
**Definition (learn):** It disarmed the Indian state, drained its treasury for a foreign garrison and removed diplomatic independence while leaving the ruler responsible for misgovernment (✅ owner 05 §8.2 indirect-rule thesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Disarm: army
- Drain: subsidy and garrison cost
- Silence: diplomacy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner thesis (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1106, MOD-1111, MOD-1119.
**Trap:** Subsidiary did not annex in name; it annexed sovereignty (✅ owner 05 §8.7).
**Mains hook:** Indirect rule as a more durable form of control.
**Back:** *"Disarmed first, annexed later."*

### Flashcard MOD-1125 — PYQ: 2018 Prelims Q75 on Subsidiary Alliance
**Front:** What does the 2018 Prelims routed demand test?
**Definition (learn):** The Subsidiary Alliance provisions introduced by Lord Wellesley (✅ owner 05 PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Demand: provisions, not dates
- Answer status: official key unavailable locally; key inferred only in the owner's own statement (✅ owner 05)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 Prelims GS-I Q75 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1106, MOD-1107, MOD-1119.
**Trap:** Answer not recorded here; the clause list is the study target.
**Mains hook:** Statement-level distinctions: troops, Resident, no foreign diplomacy.
**Back:** *"Eighteen Q75: troops, Resident, no independent voice."*

### Flashcard MOD-1126 — PYQ: 2022 GS-I Q2 East India Company armies
**Front:** What does the 2022 Mains question test?
**Definition (learn):** Why the East India Company armies won against Indian rulers (10 marks, 150 words) (✅ owner 05 PYQ block; ✅ owner 05 Adv header).
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: "Why" with reasons
- Owner route: Topic 05; key: none (Mains)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-I Q2 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MOD-1111, MOD-1122, MOD-1091.
**Trap:** Do not treat it as an objective key question.
**Mains hook:** Answer with fiscal-military mechanism, serial defeat and indirect rule.
**Back:** *"Money, method, and serial defeat."*

### Flashcard MOD-1127 — Current link: 1857 memory and princely-state heritage
**Front:** Why do Jhansi, Awadh and Srirangapatam still matter in public memory?
**Definition (learn):** Heritage circuits, museums and debates on colonial legalism keep annexation relevant (✅ owner 05 §6, ⚠️ not pegged to a news item).
**Breakdown:**
- Sites: Srirangapatam, Bithur, Jhansi, Nagpur, Lahore, Lucknow
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1115, MOD-1110.
**Trap:** Do not present as settled policy.
**Mains hook:** Annexation memory and colonial legalism.
**Back:** *"Annexed places, living memory."*

### GROUP — Government Structure and Constitutional Development, 1757–1858

### Flashcard MOD-1128 — Regulating Act, 1773: first parliamentary intervention
**Front:** What was the Regulating Act, 1773 and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** The first serious parliamentary intervention in the Company's Indian administration; it created the Governor-General of Bengal, a council, and the Supreme Court at Calcutta (✅ owner 06; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Governor-General of Bengal and Council of four (MOD-1129, MOD-1130)
- Supreme Court at Calcutta (MOD-1131)
- Control over Bombay and Madras in war and peace (MOD-1132)
- Parliamentary oversight of the Company (⚠️ commencement and royal assent dates per Wikipedia: assent 21 June 1773)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773 (✅ owner 06; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1129, MOD-1131, MOD-1133, MOD-1147.
**Trap:** The Act did not create a Governor-General of India (MOD-1143).
**Mains hook:** Parliament's first answer to the contradiction of a commerce-trained corporation governing territory.
**Back:** *"Seventy-three: Bengal's governor becomes Governor-General."*

### Flashcard MOD-1129 — Governor-General of Bengal: Warren Hastings (first holder)
**Front:** Who was the first Governor-General of Bengal?
**Definition (learn):** Warren Hastings, raised from Governor of Bengal to the new post of Governor-General under the 1773 Act (✅ owner 06; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Office: Governor-General of Bengal
- Holder: Warren Hastings (first)
- Successor office: Governor-General of India from 1833 (MOD-1138)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1128, MOD-1138, MOD-1143.
**Trap:** Hastings was not Governor-General of India (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** The office's evolution from Bengal to India is the constitutional story in one title.
**Back:** *"Hastings: first of Bengal, not of India."*

### Flashcard MOD-1130 — Council of four under the Regulating Act
**Front:** Who sat on the Supreme Council of Bengal under the 1773 Act?
**Definition (learn):** Four councillors served with the Governor-General: John Clavering, George Manson, Richard Barwell and Philip Francis, each for five years (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Decision: majority; Governor-General voted only to break a tie (✅ Wikipedia)
- Problem: Council could outvote the Governor-General (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.4 A)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Five-year terms (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1128, MOD-1133.
**Trap:** The Governor-General was not a free executive; the Council limited him (✅ owner 06 §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** Council design created conflict that the 1781 Act tried to settle.
**Back:** *"Four councillors, one tie-breaker."*

### Flashcard MOD-1131 — Supreme Court at Calcutta (1774)
**Front:** When did the Supreme Court at Calcutta begin functioning and who was its first chief justice?
**Definition (learn):** The Supreme Court of Judicature at Fort William, set up under the 1773 framework; it began in 1774, with Sir Elijah Impey as first chief justice (✅ owner 06; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Jurisdiction: civil and criminal, original and appellate (✅ Wikipedia)
- Conflict: with Company courts and the Governor-General's Council (✅ owner 06 Adv)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1774; Impey (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1128, MOD-1133.
**Trap:** Supreme Court at Calcutta was not created by Pitt's India Act (✅ owner 06 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Judicial conflict forced the 1781 settlement.
**Back:** *"Seventy-four, Impey: the Supreme Court sits in Calcutta."*

### Flashcard MOD-1132 — Bombay and Madras subordination
**Front:** How did the 1773 Act place Bombay and Madras?
**Definition (learn):** Bombay and Madras were placed under Bengal in matters of war and peace; Wikipedia adds revenue (✅ owner 06 §1; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: war and peace
- Wikipedia: "war, peace, and revenue" (⚠️ wording conflict)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1128, MOD-1134.
**Trap:** Do not assume full control; the Act's limits were partial (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Presidency subordination was incomplete, and this explains later reforms.
**Back:** *"Bombay and Madras under Bengal in war and peace."*

### Flashcard MOD-1133 — Amending Act (Act of Settlement), 1781
**Front:** Why was the Amending Act of 1781 passed?
**Definition (learn):** It clarified jurisdictional conflict between the Supreme Court and Company administration and protected official actions from excessive judicial interference (✅ owner 06 Adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Problem: 1773 defects
- Fix: jurisdiction and official protection
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1781 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1128, MOD-1131.
**Trap:** The 1773 Act did not solve all defects (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** Constitutional development as "emergency repairs" (✅ owner 06 Adv §1).
**Back:** *"Eighty-one: the courts and the Company, redrawn."*

### Flashcard MOD-1134 — Pitt's India Act (1784): dual control
**Front:** What did Pitt's India Act create?
**Definition (learn):** A Board of Control representing the Crown, with political supervision, alongside the Court of Directors, which kept commercial administration and patronage (✅ owner 06; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Board of Control: up to six Privy Counsellors (⚠️ Wikipedia), President as minister for India
- Court of Directors: Company commerce and patronage
- Result: dual control (classic divided sovereignty)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1784; royal assent 13 August 1784 (✅ Wikipedia). Repealed in 1916 (✅ Wikipedia; ⚠️ not in owner).
**Links:** MOD-1146, MOD-1144, MOD-1128.
**Trap:** Pitt's India Act did not end Company rule (✅ owner 06 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** The Crown supervised without owning the liabilities.
**Back:** *"Eighty-four: the Board watches, the Court trades."*

### Flashcard MOD-1135 — Act of 1786: Cornwallis's executive power
**Front:** What did the Act of 1786 give Cornwallis?
**Definition (learn):** Power to override the council and combine the Governor-General with the Commander-in-Chief (✅ owner 06).
**Breakdown:**
- Override: council decisions
- Combination: Governor-General and CinC
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1786 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1134, MOD-1136.
**Trap:** Strong personal executive was an exception to the council design.
**Mains hook:** Personal executive authority grew as the council was overridden.
**Back:** *"Eighty-six: Cornwallis commands the council and the army."*

### Flashcard MOD-1136 — Charter Act, 1793: renewal of the Company system
**Front:** What did the 1793 Charter Act do?
**Definition (learn):** Renewed the Company's privileges and continued the existing system (✅ owner 06 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: charter renewal
- Link: Permanent Settlement is in the same decade (MOD-1162)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1793 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1135, MOD-1137.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1793 Charter Act with the 1793 Permanent Settlement regulation.
**Mains hook:** Renewal shows continuity of the Company state.
**Back:** *"Ninety-three: the charter renewed, the system kept."*

### Flashcard MOD-1137 — Charter Act, 1813: monopoly ends except tea and China
**Front:** What did the 1813 Charter Act change?
**Definition (learn):** It ended the Company's monopoly of Indian trade except tea and the China trade; sanctioned ₹1 lakh annually for education; permitted Christian missionaries; asserted Crown sovereignty (✅ owner 06, owner 06 Adv §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Trade: monopoly ended except tea and China
- Education: ₹1 lakh annually
- Missions: admitted to Company territory
- Crown: sovereignty asserted
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1813; ₹1 lakh (✅ owner 06). The Wikipedia Charter Act page was not reachable (404); the owner is the source here.
**Links:** MOD-1138, MOD-1151, MOD-1152, MOD-1145.
**Trap:** The ₹1 lakh was modest and its purpose was contested until 1835 (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** A free-trade and cultural turning point at once; free trade ran one way (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.6).
**Back:** *"Thirteen: monopoly ends, tea and China stay, a lakh for learning."*

### Flashcard MOD-1138 — Charter Act, 1833: Governor-General of India and codification
**Front:** What did the 1833 Charter Act create?
**Definition (learn):** The Governor-General of Bengal became Governor-General of India, William Bentinck being the first; a Law Member (Macaulay the first) was added; a Law Commission was provided; the Company became purely administrative (✅ owner 06 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Office: Governor-General of India (Bentinck)
- Council: Law Member (Macaulay)
- Codification: Law Commission (First Law Commission set up 1834 per ✅ Wikipedia IPC page)
- Company: purely administrative agency
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1833 (✅ owner 06); Macaulay chairman of the first Law Commission (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1129, MOD-1139, MOD-1152, MOD-1155.
**Trap:** The first Law Commission followed the 1833 Act; the 1853 Act did not create it (✅ owner 06 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Centralised legislation and codification; non-discrimination language did not open power to Indians (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.4 D).
**Back:** *"Thirty-three: one India, one law, Bentinck first."*

### Flashcard MOD-1139 — The Company ends commercial functions (1833)
**Front:** When did the Company stop being a trading body in India?
**Definition (learn):** The 1833 Act ended the Company's commercial functions in India, making it a purely administrative and political instrument (✅ owner 06 Adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Earlier: monopoly weakened in 1813
- 1833: commercial function ended
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1813; 1833 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1137, MOD-1138.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1813 monopoly end with the 1833 end of commerce (✅ owner 06 Adv §2).
**Mains hook:** Company became an administrative state rather than a merchant body.
**Back:** *"Thirteen loosens, thirty-three ends the trade."*

### Flashcard MOD-1140 — Charter Act, 1853: separation and the end of patronage
**Front:** What did the 1853 Charter Act change?
**Definition (learn):** It separated legislative from executive work in the Governor-General's Council, expanded the legislative council, and ended the Directors' patronage by opening competitive examination (✅ owner 06 §1, §8.4 E; ✅ Wikipedia: nomination system abolished in 1853, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Legislative/executive separation
- Patronage: nomination ended
- Route: open competition, with Macaulay Committee (1854) and first exam (1855) as implementing steps (MOD-1141)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1853 (✅ owner 06; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1141, MOD-1142, MOD-1151, MOD-1152.
**Trap:** The 1853 Act did not itself hold the first exam (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** Merit announced, access limited (✅ owner 06 Adv §2).
**Back:** *"Fifty-three: patronage dies, exams begin."*

### Flashcard MOD-1141 — Macaulay Committee (1854) and first open exam (1855)
**Front:** What followed the 1853 Act in civil-service recruitment?
**Definition (learn):** The Macaulay Committee reported in 1854; the first open competitive examination followed in 1855 (✅ owner 06 §4, §5).
**Breakdown:**
- 1854: Macaulay Committee report
- 1855: first open examination
- Note: separate implementation steps, not the Act itself
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1854; 1855 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1140, MOD-1142.
**Trap:** Do not date competitive exams to 1853 alone (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** Formal eligibility versus effective access.
**Back:** *"Fifty-four report, fifty-five exam."*

### Flashcard MOD-1142 — Competitive exam barriers: London, age 18–23
**Front:** What barriers limited Indian entry to the competitive civil service?
**Definition (learn):** The exam was held in London; the Wikipedia civil-service article gives an age range of 18–23 and a horse-riding test (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01; owner: London access restrictive ✅ owner 08 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Location: London
- Age: 18–23 (✅ Wikipedia)
- Test: horse-riding (✅ Wikipedia)
- 1893 and 1922 changes noted by Wikipedia (⚠️ not owner-verified; do not expand)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** MOD-1140, MOD-1141, MOD-1146.
**Trap:** "Open competition" did not mean Indian access (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Mains hook:** Formal versus effective access is the examinable point.
**Back:** *"London, eighteen to twenty-three, and a horse."*

### Flashcard MOD-1143 — Trap: Governor-General of Bengal (1773) is not Governor-General of India (1833)
**Front:** When did the title "Governor-General of India" begin?
**Definition (learn):** The title of Governor-General of India came with the 1833 Act; the 1773 office was Governor-General of Bengal (✅ owner 06 §5, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- 1773: GG of Bengal (Hastings)
- 1833: GG of India (Bentinck)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773; 1833 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1129, MOD-1138, MOD-1150.
**Trap:** The commonest error in this topic (✅ owner 06 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** The title's change marks the centralisation of executive authority.
**Back:** *"Seventy-three Bengal, Thirty-three India: two offices, one name."*

### Flashcard MOD-1144 — Trap: Pitt's India Act did not end Company rule
**Front:** Did the Pitt's India Act end Company rule?
**Definition (learn):** No; it created dual control, and Company rule continued until the Crown took over in 1858 (✅ owner 06 §8.8, 06 Adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Board of Control: Crown supervision
- Company: continued administration
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1784; 1858 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1134, MOD-1146, MOD-1150.
**Trap:** Crown did not rule directly after 1784 (✅ owner 06 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Divided sovereignty was the design.
**Back:** *"Eighty-four: supervised, not seized."*

### Flashcard MOD-1145 — Trap: 1813 kept tea and China trade
**Front:** Did the 1813 Charter Act end all Company trade monopoly?
**Definition (learn):** No; it ended the Indian trade monopoly but kept tea and the China trade (✅ owner 06 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Ended: Indian trade monopoly
- Kept: tea and China trade
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1813 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1137, MOD-1151.
**Trap:** Do not answer "ended all monopolies" (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** Partial liberalisation with preserved exceptions.
**Back:** *"Thirteen: all India, except tea and China."*

### Flashcard MOD-1146 — Concept: dual control and divided sovereignty
**Front:** What is dual control?
**Definition (learn):** A system in which the Crown governed policy through the Board of Control while the Company kept commercial administration and patronage (✅ owner 06 Adv §1; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Crown: policy oversight through the Board
- Company: commerce and patronage
- Liability: the Company retained burdens
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1784 onward (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1134, MOD-1144.
**Trap:** Dual control did not end Company rule (✅ owner 06 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** The Crown supervised without owning the liabilities.
**Back:** *"Board directs, Court keeps the shop."*

### Flashcard MOD-1147 — Concept: the constitutional ladder 1773–1853
**Front:** How should the 1773–1853 acts be read?
**Definition (learn):** A ladder: each act corrected the previous one while increasing Crown and Parliamentary control and centralising Indian administration (✅ owner 06 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- 1773: single supreme executive and an external check
- 1784: Crown supervision
- 1813: open trade and missions
- 1833: all-India legislature and codification
- 1853: competitive route and legislative separation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773–1853 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1128, MOD-1134, MOD-1138, MOD-1140, MOD-1148.
**Trap:** Read as a trajectory, not as democratisation (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.6).
**Mains hook:** The acts nationalised Indian government, leaving Indians outside (✅ owner 06 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Seventy-three to fifty-three: a ladder of control."*

### Flashcard MOD-1148 — Concept: centralisation and codification as structural direction
**Front:** What is the structural direction across the Charter Acts?
**Definition (learn):** From fragmented presidencies to one Government of India; from Company autonomy to parliamentary sovereignty; from personal discretion to codified law (✅ owner 06 §8.2 centralisation thesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Presidency → central executive
- Company → Parliament/Crown
- Discretion → codified law
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Structural direction (✅ owner 06 §8.5).
**Links:** MOD-1147, MOD-1138, MOD-1155.
**Trap:** Centralisation was legal before it was administrative (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Trace the centralisation thesis with three acts.
**Back:** *"Presidencies to one, Company to Crown, whim to code."*

### Flashcard MOD-1149 — Concept: no Indian representation before 1858
**Front:** Was pre-1858 constitutional development representative?
**Definition (learn):** No; no Indian sat in the councils created by these acts, and law was made by officials for revenue, order and commerce (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.4 F).
**Breakdown:**
- Councils: officials only
- Indian petitions: largely elite and petitionary
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773–1858 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1147, MOD-1150.
**Trap:** Do not project later nationalist categories backwards (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.4 F).
**Mains hook:** Indian constitutional demand begins after 1858.
**Back:** *"Officials legislated, Indians petitioned."*

### Flashcard MOD-1150 — 1858: the Crown transfer as a distinct instrument
**Front:** What is the difference between the 1858 Act and Queen's Proclamation?
**Definition (learn):** The 1858 Act (the Government of India Act title is standard knowledge, ⚠️) and the Queen's Proclamation of 1 November 1858 are distinct instruments; the 1858 transfer ended Company rule (✅ owner 06 Adv mechanism control).
**Breakdown:**
- Act: transfer of government to the Crown
- Proclamation: 1 November 1858
- Continuity: the pre-1858 machinery was inherited (✅ owner 06 §8.7)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 November 1858 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1144, MOD-1149, MOD-1154.
**Trap:** Do not merge the Act and the Proclamation (✅ owner 06 Adv mechanism control).
**Mains hook:** 1858 changed the sovereign, not the structure.
**Back:** *"Fifty-eight: the Act moves the power, the Proclamation speaks."*

### Flashcard MOD-1151 — PYQ: 2019 Prelims Q4 on the Charter Act of 1813
**Front:** What does the 2019 routed demand test?
**Definition (learn):** Provisions and effects of the Charter Act of 1813 (✅ owner 06 PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Provisions: monopoly end, tea and China, education grant, missionaries
- Key status: official key unavailable locally
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 Prelims GS-I Q4 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1137, MOD-1145.
**Trap:** Statement-level distinction: monopoly "ended" vs "all trade" (✅ owner 06).
**Mains hook:** Be ready for "which statement is correct?" traps.
**Back:** *"Nineteen Q4: thirteen's Act, read carefully."*

### Flashcard MOD-1152 — PYQ: 2023 Prelims Q50 on the Governor-General designation
**Front:** What does the 2023 routed demand test?
**Definition (learn):** The designation of Governor-General of India under the Charter Acts (✅ owner 06 PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Focus: 1773 Bengal vs 1833 India
- Key status: official key unavailable locally
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2023 Prelims GS-I Q50 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1143, MOD-1138, MOD-1129.
**Trap:** Do not confuse GG of Bengal with GG of India.
**Mains hook:** Designation changes track centralisation.
**Back:** *"Twenty-three Q50: Bengal then India, watch the title."*

### Flashcard MOD-1153 — Verdict: nationalised, not democratised
**Front:** What is the trajectory verdict for 1773–1853?
**Definition (learn):** The acts did not democratise Indian government; they nationalised it, transferring control from a corporation to the British state while leaving Indians outside (✅ owner 06 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Corporation → state
- Indians: outside both
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773–1853 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1147, MOD-1149.
**Trap:** Do not call the acts a progress narrative (✅ owner 06 Adv §8.6).
**Mains hook:** "Constitutional development before 1858 was the creation of a centralised colonial state."
**Back:** *"Nationalised, not democratised."*

### Flashcard MOD-1154 — Current link: executive accountability and civil-service reform (⚠️)
**Front:** Why link 1773–1853 acts to present debates?
**Definition (learn):** Debates on executive accountability, legislative scrutiny and exam design can be historically anchored in colonial centralisation and competitive recruitment (✅ owner 06 §6, ⚠️ not pegged to a news item).
**Breakdown:**
- Accountability: legislative scrutiny
- Recruitment: competitive exam
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1150, MOD-1140.
**Trap:** Do not present the analogy as a fact about current policy.
**Mains hook:** Use historically for a constitutional-continuity answer.
**Back:** *"Old machinery, new debates."*

### Flashcard MOD-1155 — Supreme Court versus Company courts and the 1781 settlement
**Front:** What jurisdictional conflict did the 1781 Act address?
**Definition (learn):** The clash between the Supreme Court and Company administration, and protection of official acts (✅ owner 06 Adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Court: jurisdiction over Company officials
- Settlement: clarified jurisdiction, protected officials
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1774 court; 1781 Act (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** MOD-1131, MOD-1133.
**Trap:** Do not present 1781 as a judicial reform for Indians.
**Mains hook:** Law as an instrument of order and administrative stability.
**Back:** *"Court and Company argue; eighty-one settles."*

### GROUP — Economic Impact of British Rule

### Flashcard MOD-1156 — Concept: drain of wealth and its channels
**Front:** What is the drain of wealth?
**Definition (learn):** A unilateral transfer of Indian resources to Britain without equivalent economic return (✅ owner 07 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Channels: home charges; salaries and pensions of officials; profits and interest on British capital; military expenditure charged to Indian revenue (✅ owner 07 Adv §1, §8.4 A)
- Nature: systematic public transfer, not only private corruption (✅ owner 07 §5)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Drain theory as a nationalist critique (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1157, MOD-1158, MOD-1159, MOD-1191.
**Trap:** Do not quantify the drain; owner forbids drain totals (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** The drain became the economic foundation of early nationalism.
**Back:** *"Goods out, returns none: the drain."*

### Flashcard MOD-1157 — Dadabhai Naoroji and Poverty and Un-British Rule in India
**Front:** What did Dadabhai Naoroji argue about Indian poverty?
**Definition (learn):** Poverty arose because colonial rule transferred wealth abroad without return, not from climate or culture; the argument is in *Poverty and Un-British Rule in India* (✅ owner 07 §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Thesis: drain as cause of poverty
- Work: *Poverty and Un-British Rule in India*
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Work named (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1156, MOD-1158, MOD-1190.
**Trap:** Naoroji's exact drain estimates are contested; do not quote amounts (✅ owner 07 §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** Naoroji made poverty a political consequence.
**Back:** *"Naoroji: poverty is a policy, not a climate."*

### Flashcard MOD-1158 — R.C. Dutt and the Economic History of India
**Front:** What is R.C. Dutt's major work and its thesis?
**Definition (learn):** Economic History of India (1901), a classic nationalist critique linking revenue pressure, free trade, famines and deindustrialisation (✅ owner 07 §1, Adv §1; the Adv date range 1901–03 is in the owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Themes: revenue, free trade, famine, deindustrialisation
- Use: a systematic critique of British economic policy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1901 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1156, MOD-1157, MOD-1160.
**Trap:** Dutt's work is an economic history, not a parliamentary document.
**Mains hook:** Economic critique prepared the ground for organised nationalism.
**Back:** *"Dutt, nineteen-oh-one: the economy, indicted."*

### Flashcard MOD-1159 — Home charges and the British-paid burden
**Front:** What are home charges?
**Definition (learn):** Payments made in Britain from Indian revenues, one channel of the drain (✅ owner 07 §1, §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: public payments
- Contrast: private corruption
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None assigned (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1156, MOD-1157.
**Trap:** The drain included systematic public payments, not just corruption (✅ owner 07 §5).
**Mains hook:** Net transfer, not the absence of investment, is the drain argument (✅ owner 07 §8.4 A).
**Back:** *"Home charges: Indian money paid in London."*

### Flashcard MOD-1160 — Deindustrialisation: decline of handicrafts and textiles
**Front:** What is deindustrialisation in this context?
**Definition (learn):** The decline of traditional handicrafts and textiles under machine imports, one-way free trade and tariff policy (✅ owner 07 §1, Adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: machine-made British goods; one-way free trade; absence of protection
- Effect: artisans returned to villages
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** MOD-1161, MOD-1162, MOD-1163.
**Trap:** Deindustrialisation is not "all industry disappeared" (✅ owner 07 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Industrial in origin, colonial in transmission (✅ owner 07 Adv 250-word verdict).
**Back:** *"Machines abroad, tariffs at home, crafts ruined."*

### Flashcard MOD-1161 — Pre-colonial export baseline: textiles, saltpetre, indigo
**Front:** What did India export before the reversal of trade?
**Definition (learn):** Cotton textiles were the most important export, with raw silk and silk fabrics, indigo, saltpetre, opium, sugar, rice, spices and precious stones; trade was balanced by precious-metal inflows (✅ owner 07 Adv §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Export list: cotton textiles first; silk; indigo; saltpetre; opium; sugar; rice; spices; precious stones
- Balance: precious metals in
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pre-colonial baseline (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1160, MOD-1191.
**Trap:** India was an exporting manufacturer before it was a raw-material supplier (✅ owner 07 Adv §8.5).
**Mains hook:** Justifies "deindustrialisation" as the correct term.
**Back:** *"Cotton first, then silk, indigo, saltpetre and more."*

### Flashcard MOD-1162 — De-urbanisation and ruralisation
**Front:** How is de-urbanisation different from deindustrialisation?
**Definition (learn):** De-urbanisation is artisans returning to villages; deindustrialisation is the loss of craft production (✅ owner 07 §1, §8.13).
**Breakdown:**
- De-urbanisation: population movement from towns
- Deindustrialisation: production loss
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1160, MOD-1156.
**Trap:** Do not merge the two (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Artisans on the land intensified agricultural pressure.
**Back:** *"Craftsmen walk back to the fields."*

### Flashcard MOD-1163 — Deindustrialisation debate: Morris D. Morris and Daniel Thorner
**Front:** Who questioned the scale of deindustrialisation?
**Definition (learn):** Morris D. Morris and, on census evidence, Daniel Thorner questioned the scale of decline; the nationalist and Bipan Chandra line treats it as central (✅ owner 07 Adv §8.5, §8.11; ⚠️ interpretive positions).
**Breakdown:**
- Nationalist line: central decline
- Morris and Thorner: scale questioned
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1160, MOD-1190.
**Trap:** Present the debate; do not assert employment percentages (✅ owner 07 Adv §8.5).
**Mains hook:** Balance paragraph for "critically examine" answers.
**Back:** *"Morris and Thorner: the scale, not the fact, is debated."*

### Flashcard MOD-1164 — Permanent Settlement (1793): zamindari and fixed demand
**Front:** What was the Permanent Settlement and who made it?
**Definition (learn):** Cornwallis's 1793 settlement in Bengal, Bihar and Orissa, under which zamindars became proprietors and the state's demand was fixed permanently (✅ owner 07 §4, §8.6; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Architect: Cornwallis
- Region: Bengal, Bihar, Orissa (Wikipedia adds Varanasi and northern Madras districts)
- Unit: zamindar
- Sunset Law: revenue due by sunset; default meant sale (✅ owner 07; ✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1793 (✅ owner 07; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1165, MOD-1166, MOD-1167, MOD-1168.
**Trap:** It fixed the state's demand on the zamindar, not the peasant's rent (✅ owner 07 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Cash and calendar-bound demand linked land policy to debt and sale.
**Back:** *"Ninety-three: zamindars fixed, sunset kills late payers."*

### Flashcard MOD-1165 — Permanent Settlement: owner versus Wikipedia on figures and status
**Front:** What do the sources say about zamindars' status and the revenue split?
**Definition (learn):** The owner calls zamindars proprietors. Wikipedia says zamindars were "not the landowners but rather revenue collector agents," but also that they were granted proprietorial rights, and gives a 89:11 split of rent (state 89%, zamindar 11%) (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01; ⚠️ conflict).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: proprietor status and fixed demand (✅ owner 07)
- Wikipedia: internal tension on status; 89:11 figure
- Decision: do not use the 89:11 split in answers without verification (⚠️)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Figures unverified in the owner.
**Links:** MOD-1164, MOD-1168.
**Trap:** Do not state an exact rent split in an answer (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Zamindars as intermediaries versus owners is an interpretive debate.
**Back:** *"Proprietor, or tax agent? Check before you write."*

### Flashcard MOD-1166 — Ryotwari: Thomas Munro and Alexander Read
**Front:** Who architected Ryotwari and where was it applied?
**Definition (learn):** Direct settlement with the individual ryot, periodically revised; associated with Thomas Munro and Alexander Read in Madras and Bombay (✅ owner 07 §2, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Unit: individual ryot
- Region: Madras and Bombay
- Feature: periodic revision (over-assessment risk, ✅ owner 07 §8.6)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Architects named (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1164, MOD-1167, MOD-1168.
**Trap:** Ryotwari was not Bengal-Bihar-Orissa (✅ owner 07 §5).
**Mains hook:** Ryotwari removed the zamindar but not the cash-revenue burden.
**Back:** *"Munro and Read: the ryot pays, Madras and Bombay."*

### Flashcard MOD-1167 — Mahalwari: Holt Mackenzie and the village or mahal
**Front:** What was the Mahalwari system?
**Definition (learn):** A village or mahal-based settlement, jointly responsible for revenue; introduced by Holt Mackenzie in 1822 based on a plan of 1819, with R.M. Bird; applied to North-Western Provinces, Punjab and parts of Central India (✅ owner 07 §2, §8.6; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Unit: village or mahal community (lambardars represented; ✅ Wikipedia)
- Liability: joint
- Revision: 1833 regulation under Bentinck; 1855 Saharanpur Rules (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1822; 1833; 1855 (✅ Wikipedia). Demand rates per Wikipedia (95%, 66%, 50%) are not owner-verified and not for use in answers (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1164, MOD-1166, MOD-1168.
**Trap:** Mahalwari did not settle with individual peasants (✅ owner 07 §5).
**Mains hook:** Joint liability passed defaulters' burden to neighbours (✅ owner 07 §8.6).
**Back:** *"Twenty-two: the village pays together, Mackenzie's way."*

### Flashcard MOD-1168 — Trap: architect, region and unit triad for land systems
**Front:** Match each land system to its architect, region and unit.
**Definition (learn):** Permanent Settlement (Cornwallis; Bengal, Bihar, Orissa; zamindar), Ryotwari (Munro and Read; Madras, Bombay; individual ryot), Mahalwari (Holt Mackenzie and R.M. Bird; NWP, Punjab, Central India; village/mahal) (✅ owner 07 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Permanent Settlement: zamindar
- Ryotwari: individual ryot
- Mahalwari: village/mahal
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** MOD-1164, MOD-1166, MOD-1167.
**Trap:** This is the standard Prelims trap (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Compare the three by incentive and cost.
**Back:** *"Zamindar, ryot, village: three settlements, three units."*

### Flashcard MOD-1169 — Commercialisation and cash-calendar revenue
**Front:** How did fixed cash revenue link to commercialisation and agrarian distress?
**Definition (learn):** A fixed, cash, calendar-bound demand enforceable by sale of land made all three systems produce debt and land transfer, by different routes (✅ owner 07 §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Cash demand: forced market exposure
- Calendar: credit dependence on moneylenders
- Sale: land transfer
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner's integrating argument (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1164, MOD-1166, MOD-1167, MOD-1170.
**Trap:** Commercialisation was not always beneficial to peasants (✅ owner 07 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Strongest integrating argument for a 20-mark answer (✅ owner 07 §8.6).
**Back:** *"Cash due on the day, or the land goes."*

### Flashcard MOD-1170 — Indigo Revolt (1859–60): planter coercion
**Front:** What did the Indigo Revolt oppose?
**Definition (learn):** Collective refusal in Bengal against coercive planter contracts (1859–60) (✅ owner 07 §1, §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: European planters
- Repertoire: collective refusal to grow indigo
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1859–60 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1171, MOD-1172, MOD-1173.
**Trap:** Do not merge Indigo, Pabna and Deccan into one event (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Different forms of exploitation produced different targets.
**Back:** *"Fifty-nine: planters coerce, peasants refuse."*

### Flashcard MOD-1171 — Indigo collapse, tinkathia and Champaran (1917)
**Front:** Why did indigo planters press cultivators in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries?
**Definition (learn):** Synthetic (German) dyes forced natural indigo out of the market; in Champaran, cultivators were bound under the tinkathia system and planters sought enhanced rents and illegal dues as the price of release (✅ owner 07 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Market cause: synthetic dye competition (⚠️ no single year in owner)
- Political consequence: rent extraction in Champaran, taken up by Gandhi in 1917 (✅ owner 07; ✅ Wikipedia: Champaran Satyagraha 1917)
- Wikipedia nuance: wartime disruption of German dye revived indigo for a time (⚠️ owner-vs-source tension)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1917 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1170, MOD-1172.
**Trap:** Do not date the transition to a single year; do not fuse the collapse with the Champaran grievance (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Market cause, planter rent-extraction as political consequence.
**Back:** *"Dyes killed the market; planters squeezed the rent."*

### Flashcard MOD-1172 — Pabna agrarian leagues (1873–76)
**Front:** What was the Pabna movement?
**Definition (learn):** Agrarian leagues in Bengal (from 1873) against zamindari rent enhancement and insecure occupancy, using meetings, funds and litigation (✅ owner 07 §1, Adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: rent enhancement
- Repertoire: organisation, legal action, rent strikes
- Limit: largely legal-organisational, not anti-colonial nationalism (✅ owner 07 §8.7)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1873–76 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1170, MOD-1173, MOD-1164.
**Trap:** Pabna targeted zamindari, not moneylenders (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Agrarian anger was structured by the form of exploitation.
**Back:** *"Pabna: rent is the enemy, the courts the weapon."*

### Flashcard MOD-1173 — Deccan Riots (1875) and the Deccan Agriculturists' Relief Act (1879)
**Front:** What were the Deccan Riots and what followed?
**Definition (learn):** In May–June 1875 peasants in parts of Poona and Ahmednagar attacked moneylenders, destroying bonds and decrees after the cotton-boom collapse (✅ owner 07; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01). The Deccan Agriculturists' Relief Act followed in 1879 (✅ owner 07; Wikipedia did not confirm).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: moneylenders and bonds
- Cause: cotton boom and bust after American Civil War; credit to debt; high revenue
- Wikipedia: 1859 Limitation Law and renewal of three-year bonds also contributed
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1875 (✅ owner 07; ✅ Wikipedia); 1879 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1170, MOD-1172, MOD-1174.
**Trap:** Deccan targets moneylenders, not zamindars (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Debt records were the target, showing a different repertoire.
**Back:** *"Seventy-five: bonds burned, moneylenders hunted."*

### Flashcard MOD-1174 — Famines: 1770, 1876–78, 1896–1900
**Front:** Which famines does the owner use as key markers?
**Definition (learn):** The Bengal famine of 1770, the famines of 1876–78 (south and west India) and 1896–1900 (✅ owner 07 §1, §2, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- 1770: Bengal under early Company revenue
- 1876–78: south and west India
- 1896–1900: late-nineteenth-century famines
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 07). No mortality figures (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Links:** MOD-1175, MOD-1176, MOD-1088.
**Trap:** Do not cite mortality figures.
**Mains hook:** Famines as failures of entitlement and policy (✅ owner 07 §8.8).
**Back:** *"Seventy, seventy-six, ninety-six: the famine list."*

### Flashcard MOD-1175 — Strachey Famine Commission (1880)
**Front:** What was the Strachey Famine Commission?
**Definition (learn):** A commission that reported in 1880 after the 1876–78 famine, recommending famine administration principles (✅ owner 07 Adv mini-timeline, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1880
- Trigger: 1876–78 famine
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1880 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1174, MOD-1176.
**Trap:** The commission reports and codes are distinct; relief practice changed over time (✅ owner 07 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Doctrine versus codes.
**Back:** *"Eighty: Strachey reports on the famine."*

### Flashcard MOD-1176 — Concept: famine as entitlement failure
**Front:** Why were colonial famines more than rainfall failures?
**Definition (learn):** Rigid cash revenue, export cropping, rail-driven grain movement towards paying demand and a hostile doctrine of non-interference converted scarcity into mortality (✅ owner 07 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Revenue: rigid cash
- Market: export cropping and rail
- Doctrine: non-interference
- Vulnerability: landless labourers, artisans, tribal groups
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1174, MOD-1175, MOD-1181.
**Trap:** Do not claim one cause (✅ owner 07 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Use for "discuss colonial famine policy" (✅ owner 07 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Scarcity plus policy equals mortality."*

### Flashcard MOD-1177 — Forest policy: from oak to the Indian Forest Act (1878)
**Front:** How did colonial forest policy develop?
**Definition (learn):** British attention to Indian forests from 1806 (oak for Royal Navy); railway sleepers later; a Forest Department in 1864; Government Forests Act 1865; Indian Forest Act 1878 giving the state monopoly and reserved, protected and unclassified forests (✅ owner 07 Adv §8.8 evidence unit; owner-only, Wikipedia fetch failed).
**Breakdown:**
- 1806: oak timber for shipbuilding
- 1864: Forest Department
- 1865: Government Forests Act
- 1878: Indian Forest Act; reserved (felling prohibited), protected (use for personal, not sale; charges), unclassified
- c.1900: about 20 per cent of land under government forest administration (✅ owner 07)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1178, MOD-1179, MOD-1181.
**Trap:** Do not date the classification scheme to 1865, and attribute the 20 per cent to c.1900 (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Converted a commons into state property.
**Back:** *"Oak in 1806, Forest Dept in 1864, the grid in 1878."*

### Flashcard MOD-1178 — Forest resistance and jhum: examples
**Front:** Which communities and rebellions resisted forest laws?
**Definition (learn):** Hunter-gatherers and jhum cultivators were most affected; examples include the Chenchus (Hyderabad), Baigas (central India), Saoras (Ganjam), the Rampa rebellion of 1879 (podu prohibition and toddy tax), the Marias and Murias of Bastar (1910), the Bhils of the Dangs and Tehri Garhwal dhandak protests (✅ owner 07 Adv §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Causes: jhum prohibition, subsistence hunting ban, game laws
- Variation: Tehri Garhwal (princely) versus Kumaun (directly administered)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1879 Rampa; 1910 Bastar; Dangs (1860, 1907, 1911, 1914) (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1177, MOD-1193.
**Trap:** Do not present forest policy as uniform (✅ owner 07 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Intensity tracked ecology and land system.
**Back:** *"Podu stopped, Rampa rose in seventy-nine."*

### Flashcard MOD-1179 — Santhal Parganas: uprising (1855–56) and the 1855 Act
**Front:** How did the colonial state respond to the Santhal revolt?
**Definition (learn):** After the Santhal insurrection (1855–56), suppressed by force, the Santhal-inhabited tracts were constituted into a separate district, the Santhal Parganas, created by Act 37 of 1855 (✅ owner 07 Adv §8.8; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Revolt: 30 June 1855 – 3 January 1856; Sidhu and Kanhu Murmu (✅ Wikipedia)
- State response: military suppression, then administrative exceptionalism
- Later: Santhal Parganas Tenancy Act 1876 (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1855; 1856; 1876 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1177, MOD-1178, MOD-1193.
**Trap:** The Santhal Parganas was a response after the revolt, not a pre-existing arrangement (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Military suppression followed by administrative exceptionalism is the template of tribal policy.
**Back:** *"Fifty-five revolt, fifty-five Act: the Santhal district."*

### Flashcard MOD-1180 — Railways, telegraph and canals: purpose and effects
**Front:** Were railways and telegraph development or exploitation?
**Definition (learn):** Built for administrative control, troop movement, export evacuation and British capital; backward linkages to Indian industry were weak (✅ owner 07 Adv §8.9; ⚠️ guaranteed-return terms not owner-verified).
**Breakdown:**
- Intended purpose: imperial trade and control
- Unintended effects: market integration, mobility, national labour market
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1181, MOD-1182.
**Trap:** Do not deny the network's utility; the exam point is the absence of industrial policy (✅ owner 07 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Development or exploitation: judge purpose and consequence separately.
**Back:** *"Built for empire, used for nation."*

### Flashcard MOD-1181 — Modernisation without development (verdict)
**Front:** What is the "instruments without engine" verdict?
**Definition (learn):** India received the instruments of a modern economy without its engine: physical modernisation without industrial policy, technical education or capital formation for Indians (✅ owner 07 §8.12).
**Breakdown:**
- Physical: railways, canals, ports, telegraph
- Missing: industrial policy, technical education
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1180, MOD-1176.
**Trap:** Do not conclude that railways did not exist or were useless.
**Mains hook:** Colonial underdevelopment defined by the combination, not the absence (✅ owner 07 §8.12).
**Back:** *"Modern tracks, no modern engine."*

### Flashcard MOD-1182 — Rupee–sterling ratio and Gandhi's 1s 4d demand
**Front:** What exchange-rate demand did Gandhi put in his eleven-point ultimatum?
**Definition (learn):** Gandhi's eleven-point ultimatum to Irwin (31 January 1930) included lowering the rupee–sterling exchange rate to 1s 4d, alongside protective tariffs on foreign cloth and coastal shipping reservation (✅ owner 07 §8.10, Bandyopadhyay).
**Breakdown:**
- Demand: lower rupee–sterling ratio to 1s 4d
- Mechanism: over-valued rupee cheapened imports and cut exporters
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 31 January 1930; 1s 4d (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1183, MOD-1184.
**Trap:** Do not attach a ratio to the official rate without a source; say "above the nationalist demand" (✅ owner 07 §8.10).
**Mains hook:** Currency policy was a live colonial-economic grievance.
**Back:** *"Thirty: one shilling four, the rupee's ask."*

### Flashcard MOD-1183 — Hilton-Young Commission (1926): bounded factual gap
**Front:** What is the Hilton-Young Commission and what can be said of its exchange rate?
**Definition (learn):** The Royal Commission on Indian Currency and Finance (1926), which the repository mentions in Economy and in a 2026 Prelims note; it supports the qualitative point that the fixed rupee–sterling rate aided British remittances and India's creditworthiness (✅ owner 07 §8.10; provisional 2026 key).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner's qualitative point: fixed rate aided remittances
- Not verified: the rate recommended or adopted (⚠️)
- A widely circulated figure is not attested in the local sources and is not written here (✅ owner 07 §8.10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1926 (✅ owner 07). Wikipedia page not reachable (404, ⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1182, MOD-1194.
**Trap:** Do not assert any exact ratio for Hilton-Young (✅ owner 07 §8.13).
**Mains hook:** Use the mechanism, not the ratio, in answers.
**Back:** *"Twenty-six: the commission names the rate; we say only 'above one-four'."*

### Flashcard MOD-1184 — Trap: drain is not deindustrialisation
**Front:** Are drain and deindustrialisation the same?
**Definition (learn):** No. Drain is a transfer from India to Britain; deindustrialisation is the loss of craft production through trade and policy (✅ owner 07 §Mechanism control).
**Breakdown:**
- Drain: net transfer of value
- Deindustrialisation: production decline
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1156, MOD-1160.
**Trap:** Do not merge these terms in an answer (✅ owner 07).
**Mains hook:** Separate the two concepts in any "how far" or "drain" question.
**Back:** *"Drain moves money; deindustrialisation kills crafts."*

### Flashcard MOD-1185 — PYQ: 2024 GS-I Q13 on the Industrial Revolution and handicrafts
**Front:** What does the 2024 Mains question ask?
**Definition (learn):** "How far was the Industrial Revolution in England responsible for the decline of handicrafts and cottage industries in India?" (250 words; ✅ owner 07 Adv PYQ application).
**Breakdown:**
- English mechanisation: necessary condition
- Colonial tariffs and monopoly: transmission
- Patronage loss: independent cause
- Railways and markets: intensifier
- Verdict: industrial in origin, colonial in transmission
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-I Q13 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1160, MOD-1161, MOD-1163, MOD-1192.
**Trap:** Do not present the English factor as sufficient (✅ owner 07 Adv).
**Mains hook:** Use the 250-word verdict structure.
**Back:** *"Machines necessary, colonial policy decisive."*

### Flashcard MOD-1186 — PYQ: 2024 Prelims Q57 (officially dropped)
**Front:** What is the status of the 2024 Prelims Q57 item on Cornwallis revenue?
**Definition (learn):** Revenue collection under Cornwallis (Ryotwari or Permanent Settlement). Officially dropped; retained only as a demand-control (✅ owner 07 Recent PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Status: officially dropped (disposition X)
- Use: content still examinable on the land-system triad (MOD-1168)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 Q57 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1164, MOD-1168.
**Trap:** Do not assign a correct option; none recorded (✅ owner 07).
**Mains hook:** Study the triad regardless.
**Back:** *"Dropped item, kept lesson: the land triad."*

### Flashcard MOD-1187 — PYQ: 2026 Prelims Q2 on Hilton-Young (provisional key)
**Front:** What does the 2026 Prelims Q2 test?
**Definition (learn):** The Hilton-Young Commission's rupee-sterling exchange rate and British fiscal rationale; a provisional 2026 Set-A key exists locally, with no answer recorded (✅ owner 07 2026 block).
**Breakdown:**
- Focus: fixed exchange rate and remittance rationale
- Status: provisional key; not inferred
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2026 GS-I Q2 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1183, MOD-1182.
**Trap:** Do not assert the ratio (✅ owner 07).
**Mains hook:** Use the mechanism.
**Back:** *"Twenty-six Q2: remittance, not the number."*

### Flashcard MOD-1188 — PYQ: 2022 GS-I Q3 on spurt in famines
**Front:** What does the 2022 Mains question test?
**Definition (learn):** The spurt in famines in colonial India since the mid-eighteenth century (10 marks, 150 words; ✅ owner 07 PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: "Why"
- Framework: entitlement, revenue, market, relief, doctrine
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-I Q3 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1174, MOD-1176, MOD-1175.
**Trap:** No mortality figures (✅ owner 07).
**Mains hook:** Use the entitlement-failure concept.
**Back:** *"Twenty-two: why famines multiplied."*

### Flashcard MOD-1189 — PYQ: 2018 Prelims Q52 and Q68 on economic results and export commodities
**Front:** What do the 2018 routed items test?
**Definition (learn):** Economic results of British rule in nineteenth-century India (Q52), and East India Company export commodities from Bengal mid-century (Q68) (✅ owner 07 PYQ block).
**Breakdown:**
- Q52: results of British rule
- Q68: export list (see MOD-1161)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 Q52, Q68 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1161, MOD-1156.
**Trap:** Official keys unavailable locally; no answer is inferred (✅ owner 07).
**Mains hook:** Know the pre-colonial export list.
**Back:** *"Eighteen: the export list and the economic results."*

### Flashcard MOD-1190 — Historiography: nationalist, Marxist and revisionist readings
**Front:** How do nationalist, Marxist and revisionist readings differ?
**Definition (learn):** Nationalists (Naoroji, Dutt) stress drain and poverty; Marxist historians stress dependent integration into world capitalism; Cambridge or revisionist readings question uniform nationalist narratives (✅ owner 07 Adv historiography, ⚠️ interpretive labels).
**Breakdown:**
- Nationalist: drain, poverty, deindustrialisation
- Marxist: dependent integration
- Revisionist: intermediaries and regional variation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1157, MOD-1163, MOD-1191.
**Trap:** Do not quote historians verbatim (✅ owner 07).
**Mains hook:** The safest answer is balanced: quantum debated, direction central.
**Back:** *"Naoroji shouts, Marx explains, Cambridge qualifies."*

### Flashcard MOD-1191 — Verdict: drain as transfer system, not quantum
**Front:** How should the drain be defended in an answer?
**Definition (learn):** As a description of a transfer system and as the origin of economic nationalism, not as a precise quantum (✅ owner 07 §8.12).
**Breakdown:**
- Defensible: channels and political effect
- Indefensible: precise figure
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1156, MOD-1190.
**Trap:** Never quantify (✅ owner 07).
**Mains hook:** Use the owner's drain verdict.
**Back:** *"Describe the channels, skip the sum."*

### Flashcard MOD-1192 — Concept: "How far" structure for the Industrial Revolution question
**Front:** How should a "how far was X responsible" answer distribute causes?
**Definition (learn):** Concede the external mechanism, then insert colonial policy variables, regional and craft variation, and give a qualified verdict (✅ owner 07 §8.1, §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- External: mechanised cost advantage
- Colonial: tariff asymmetry, patronage loss, railways, absence of protection
- Verdict: shared, with Indian policy the decisive variable (✅ owner 07 §8.12)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner verdict (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MOD-1185, MOD-1160.
**Trap:** Do not assign all blame to England or to the Company alone.
**Mains hook:** Distribute responsibility rather than naming a single cause.
**Back:** *"Share the blame; name the policy."*

### Flashcard MOD-1193 — Current link: colonial reparations and restitution
**Front:** Why does drain and deindustrialisation matter to reparations debates?
**Definition (learn):** Debates on restitution, museum holdings and colonial extraction can be anchored in the drain theory and underdevelopment debate (✅ owner 07 §6, ⚠️ not pegged to a news item).
**Breakdown:**
- Drain: surplus transfer
- Deindustrialisation: lost capacity
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1156, MOD-1179.
**Trap:** Present as analysis, not settled policy.
**Mains hook:** Historical responsibility and its limits.
**Back:** *"Old drain, new claims."*

### Flashcard MOD-1194 — Trap: no quantified drain, mortality or unsourced exchange rate
**Front:** What must never be quantified in answers on the economic impact?
**Definition (learn):** Drain totals, famine mortality totals and unsourced exchange rates are barred (✅ owner 07 §Mechanism control, §8.13).
**Breakdown:**
- Drain: no amounts
- Famine: no mortality figures
- Exchange rate: no unsourced ratio
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1156, MOD-1174, MOD-1182, MOD-1183.
**Trap:** Quantitative claims without a source cost marks.
**Mains hook:** Describe mechanism and political effect.
**Back:** *"No sums, no death tolls, no unsourced ratio."*

### GROUP — Administrative Organisation (Civil Service, Army, Police, Judiciary)

### Flashcard MOD-1195 — Concept: the district as basic unit of colonial power
**Front:** What made the district the basic unit of colonial power?
**Definition (learn):** The civil servant was collector, magistrate, administrator and the local face of the state, combining fiscal and magisterial power (✅ owner 08 Adv §1; ✅ owner 08 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Collector-magistrate: revenue plus magisterial power
- Supporting: police (SP), courts, army as guarantee
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1196, MOD-1210, MOD-1211, MOD-1226.
**Trap:** Not a neutral administrative office (✅ owner 08 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The district model is the most durable colonial bequest.
**Back:** *"One officer, fiscal and magisterial power."*

### Flashcard MOD-1196 — Cornwallis and the covenanted civil service (1793)
**Front:** What did Cornwallis do for the civil service?
**Definition (learn):** He founded the covenanted civil service with high salaries and anti-corruption discipline while reserving higher posts for Europeans (✅ owner 08 §1, §8.4 A).
**Breakdown:**
- Reform: professional discipline, high salaries
- Exclusion: superior posts European
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1793 (✅ owner 08 timeline, Cornwallis Code 1793).
**Links:** MOD-1195, MOD-1197, MOD-1219.
**Trap:** Cornwallis professionalised but did not democratise (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Mains hook:** Professionalisation and exclusion designed together.
**Back:** *"Ninety-three: paid well, mostly European."*

### Flashcard MOD-1197 — Haileybury College: training before the exam era
**Front:** What was Haileybury College's role?
**Definition (learn):** It trained Company civil servants before the competitive-exam era (✅ owner 08 §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: pre-exam training of covenanted servants
- Context: Company patronage
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1196, MOD-1198.
**Trap:** Haileybury is not the 1855 exam venue; the exam was in London (✅ owner 08).
**Mains hook:** Training institutions shaped the closed service.
**Back:** *"Haileybury trains the Company's servants."*

### Flashcard MOD-1198 — Competitive civil service: 1853, 1854 and 1855
**Front:** How did the competitive civil service develop after 1853?
**Definition (learn):** The 1853 Charter Act ended patronage and authorised competition; the Macaulay Committee reported in 1854; the first open exam followed in 1855 in London (✅ owner 08 §1; see MOD-1140, MOD-1141).
**Breakdown:**
- 1853: Act
- 1854: Macaulay Committee
- 1855: first open exam, London
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1140, MOD-1141, MOD-1142.
**Trap:** Do not treat 1853 as Indianisation (✅ owner 08 §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** Formal eligibility versus effective access.
**Back:** *"Fifty-three, fifty-four, fifty-five: the examined service."*

### Flashcard MOD-1199 — Lytton's Statutory Civil Service (1878–79)
**Front:** What was the Statutory Civil Service?
**Definition (learn):** A nominated side-entry scheme under Lytton (1878–79) offering a limited alternative route for Indians (✅ owner 08 §4, §8.4 B).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: nominated side-entry
- Purpose: contain Indianisation demand
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1878–79 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1200, MOD-1201.
**Trap:** It was not equal entry; do not overstate (✅ owner 08 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** A concession designed to contain pressure.
**Back:** *"Seventy-eight: a side door, nominated."*

### Flashcard MOD-1200 — Aitchison Commission (1886)
**Front:** What did the Aitchison Commission examine?
**Definition (learn):** Public services and the Indianisation demand, in 1886 (✅ owner 08 §4, §8.4 B).
**Breakdown:**
- Subject: classification of services
- Issue: Indianisation demand
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1886 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1199, MOD-1201.
**Trap:** Do not claim its results opened the higher service (✅ owner 08 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** The higher service remained European well into the twentieth century.
**Back:** *"Eighty-six: services examined, service stays European."*

### Flashcard MOD-1201 — Concept: Indianisation as a cycle
**Front:** What pattern did the Indianisation demand follow?
**Definition (learn):** Demand, then inadequate concession, then renewed demand, recurring through the constitutional story (✅ owner 08 §8.4 B).
**Breakdown:**
- Demand: Indian pressure
- Concession: nominated or formal route
- Renewed demand: after limits
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1853; 1878–79; 1886 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1198, MOD-1199, MOD-1200.
**Trap:** Do not overstate results (✅ owner 08 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** Trace the process as political, not administrative.
**Back:** *"Ask, get a little, ask again."*

### Flashcard MOD-1202 — Fort William College (Calcutta, pre-1802)
**Front:** What did Fort William College do?
**Definition (learn):** It trained European civil servants in Indian languages and law at Calcutta under Wellesley before its closure in 1802 (⚠️ owner-only detail; not fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: language and law training
- Date: closure 1802 (⚠️)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1802 closure (⚠️ owner 08 §8.4 A).
**Links:** MOD-1196, MOD-1198.
**Trap:** Do not confuse with Haileybury (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Language training as part of the closed-service model.
**Back:** *"Fort William taught the language, then closed."*

### Flashcard MOD-1203 — Army: sepoys under European officers and the post-1857 reorganisation
**Front:** How was the colonial army organised after 1857?
