**Definition (learn):** A sepoy army under European officers, reorganised after 1857 with a higher European ratio, artillery kept in European hands and recruitment by caste, region and religion (✅ owner 08 §2, §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Before 1857: conquest and internal control
- After 1857: higher European ratio; artillery European
- Recruitment: divided by caste, region, religion
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1857 (✅ owner 08). Ratios not stated (owner forbids figures).
**Links:** MOD-1204, MOD-1205, MOD-1206, MOD-1207.
**Trap:** The post-1857 army did not trust Indian soldiers more (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Mains hook:** The lesson of 1857 was to organise soldiers so they could not act together.
**Back:** *"Fifty-seven taught caution: divide the ranks."*

### Flashcard MOD-1204 — Martial races doctrine (1880s)
**Front:** What was the "martial races" doctrine?
**Definition (learn):** A colonial recruitment ideology, streamlined in the 1880s, that identified groups as ideally suited to soldiering and held them "warlike, trustworthy, but ... intellectually deficient, so that they could fight but not lead" (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.4 C, quoting Bandyopadhyay as owner records).
**Breakdown:**
- Groups named: Pathans (NWFP), Jats (Punjab), Rajputs (north India), Gurkhas (Nepal)
- Logic: fit to fight, unfit to command
- Effect: justified permanent European command
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1880s (✅ owner 08 Adv, citing Bandyopadhyay).
**Links:** MOD-1203, MOD-1205, MOD-1206.
**Trap:** "Martial races" is a colonial category, not a factual taxonomy (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Knowledge and power: classification as control.
**Back:** *"Fight yes, command no: the martial races myth."*

### Flashcard MOD-1205 — Omissi: three-quarters of Indian infantry by 1914
**Front:** What share of Indian infantry came from Punjab, Nepal and the NWFP by 1914?
**Definition (learn):** David Omissi calculates that by 1914 about three-quarters of the Indian infantry came from Punjab, Nepal or the North-West Frontier Province (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Attribution: Omissi
- Date: 1914
- Caution: do not generalise to other dates (⚠️ owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** About three-quarters; 1914 (✅ owner 08 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1204, MOD-1207.
**Trap:** Attribute the figure to Omissi and 1914 (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** Regional concentration of recruitment shaped political weight.
**Back:** *"Fourteen: three-quarters from Punjab, Nepal, the frontier."*

### Flashcard MOD-1206 — Army recruitment: keeping communities apart and loyalty engineered
**Front:** How did the army keep Indian soldiers from forming a common identity?
**Definition (learn):** Communities were kept separate and loyalty secured by encouraging each group's religious traditions and sense of honour; peasants joined mainly for a lucrative career (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.4 C.1).
**Breakdown:**
- Separation: caste, region, religion
- Inducement: lucrative career
- Culture: honour and religion reinforced
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1203, MOD-1204.
**Trap:** Loyalty was purchased and culturally reinforced, not assumed (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.4 C.1).
**Mains hook:** Manufactured dependence and divide-and-rule as personnel policy.
**Back:** *"Keep them apart, pay them, honour them."*

### Flashcard MOD-1207 — Political consequences of the army settlement
**Front:** How did army recruitment shape politics?
**Definition (learn):** Concentrated recruitment in Punjab, NWFP and Nepal gave those regions a soldier-and-pension constituency and official anxiety about agitation there; wartime recruitment pressure became a grievance in the First World War (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.4 C.2).
**Breakdown:**
- Regional weight: soldier and pension constituency
- Wartime: recruitment pressure as grievance
- Post-independence: civilian supremacy inherited
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** WWI (✅ owner 08 Adv; WWI link to basic 18).
**Links:** MOD-1205, MOD-1208.
**Trap:** Mark ⚠️ claims about official anxiety as interpretive (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.4 C.2).
**Mains hook:** The army policy linked to the post-war upsurge.
**Back:** *"Recruit the region, watch the region."*

### Flashcard MOD-1208 — Failure of the doctrine: Peshawar (1930) and INA/RIN (1946)
**Front:** Where did the army's cohesion-preventing doctrine break down?
**Definition (learn):** The Garhwali refusal to fire at Peshawar (1930) and the INA and RIN revolt of 1946, whose cross-communal composition was what the doctrine had been designed to prevent (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.4 C.2; cross-links basic 22 and 26).
**Breakdown:**
- 1930: Garhwali refusal to fire, Peshawar
- 1946: INA and RIN revolt
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1930; 1946 (✅ owner 08 Adv).
**Links:** MOD-1206, MOD-1207.
**Trap:** Do not treat these as ordinary mutinies; they were cross-community breaches (✅ owner 08 Adv).
**Mains hook:** The guarantee broke twice.
**Back:** *"Thirty Peshawar, forty-six navy: the ranks unite."*

### Flashcard MOD-1209 — Police: thana and daroga under Cornwallis (owner attribution)
**Front:** What was the thana and daroga system?
**Definition (learn):** A police structure with thanas and darogas, which the owner attributes to Cornwallis (✅ owner 08 §2, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Unit: thana
- Officer: daroga
- Function: enforce collection and suppress protest (✅ owner 08 §8.5)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner attributes creation to Cornwallis (⚠️ check before use, as thana structures are older than the owner's attribution; not verified here).
**Links:** MOD-1210, MOD-1195.
**Trap:** The thana/daroga system predates the Police Act 1861 (✅ owner 08 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Police designed for revenue enforcement and control.
**Back:** *"Thana and daroga: the first beat was for the revenue."*

### Flashcard MOD-1210 — Police Act, 1861: provincial IG and district SP
**Front:** What hierarchy did the Police Act of 1861 create?
**Definition (learn):** Provincial Inspector-General and district Superintendent of Police under executive control (✅ owner 08 §2, §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Provincial: Inspector-General
- District: Superintendent of Police
- Control: executive
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1861 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1209, MOD-1195.
**Trap:** The 1861 Act was not designed around citizen rights; it was order and surveillance (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Mains hook:** Accountability problem persists after independence (✅ owner 08 §8.4 D).
**Back:** *"Sixty-one: Inspector-General above, Superintendent in the district."*

### Flashcard MOD-1211 — Judiciary: civil and criminal courts, Sadar courts and the Cornwallis Code
**Front:** How was the colonial judiciary structured?
**Definition (learn):** Civil and criminal courts, Sadar courts and codified law; the Cornwallis Code (1793) separated revenue and judicial functions in principle (✅ owner 08 §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Courts: civil, criminal, Sadar
- Code: Cornwallis Code 1793
- Principle: separation of revenue and judicial functions
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1793 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1212, MOD-1213, MOD-1195.
**Trap:** Separation in principle was not always achieved in practice (⚠️ interpretive).
**Mains hook:** Rule of law in theory, racial hierarchy in practice.
**Back:** *"Ninety-three: courts apart from the collector, in principle."*

### Flashcard MOD-1212 — Indian Penal Code (1860): codification milestone
**Front:** When was the Indian Penal Code passed and when did it come into force?
**Definition (learn):** Passed on 6 October 1860, prepared by the First Law Commission (set up 1834) under Macaulay's chairmanship; in operation from 1 January 1862 (✅ owner 08 §4; ✅ Wikipedia IPC, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Drafting: 1834 commission; draft 1835; drafting finished 1850; presented 1856 (✅ Wikipedia)
- Statute book: after 1857 (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 6 Oct 1860; 1 Jan 1862; Macaulay died late 1859 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1211, MOD-1213, MOD-1138.
**Trap:** Macaulay died before the code took effect (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Codification as modern law and instrument of control.
**Back:** *"Sixty: enacted; sixty-two: in force."*

### Flashcard MOD-1213 — Codification and repression: sedition in the same system
**Front:** How did codified law also serve control?
**Definition (learn):** Sedition and repressive provisions came from the same codified system that gave uniform law (✅ owner 08 §5, §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Uniform code: predictable procedure
- Repression: sedition and dissent offences
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1212, MOD-1211.
**Trap:** Codification did not automatically mean justice (✅ owner 08 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the Indian Penal Code as an example of modernity with control.
**Back:** *"Clear law, sharp edges."*

### Flashcard MOD-1214 — Ilbert Bill (1883): Indian judges and European defendants
**Front:** What was the Ilbert Bill and what happened to it?
**Definition (learn):** A bill to let Indian judges and magistrates try European defendants, introduced in the Imperial Legislative Council in February 1883 under Lord Ripon, drafted by Courtenay Ilbert (✅ owner 08; ✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Proposal: Indian judges over European cases
- Reaction: European opposition, Indian nationalist support
- Outcome: enacted in a compromised form (1884)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Introduced 9 February 1883 (✅ Wikipedia); 1883 controversy (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1215, MOD-1216.
**Trap:** Ilbert was not the Law Member; the owner's wording is "Ilbert Bill" (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** The Bill is the decisive evidence unit for racial limits on equality before law.
**Back:** *"Eighty-three: Indian judges, European outrage."*

### Flashcard MOD-1215 — Ilbert compromise (1884): jury and the limits of concession
**Front:** What compromise ended the Ilbert controversy?
**Definition (learn):** The compromise passed on 25 January 1884 as the Criminal Procedure Code Amendment Act, 1884; Indian and European district magistrates and sessions judges could try Europeans, but a European defendant could demand a jury with at least half European members (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
**Breakdown:**
- Passed: 25 January 1884 (✅ Wikipedia)
- Effect in force: 1 May 1884 (✅ Wikipedia)
- Limit: European jury right
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1884 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-1214, MOD-1227.
**Trap:** The compromise was not full equality (✅ owner 08 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Organised racial privilege could defeat the government itself.
**Back:** *"Eighty-four: a European jury for a European accused."*

### Flashcard MOD-1216 — Ilbert Bill as decisive evidence of racial privilege
**Front:** Why is the Ilbert Bill the decisive evidence unit?
**Definition (learn):** It shows the limit on equality before law was organised racial privilege that could defeat the government, not administrative inertia (✅ owner 08 §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: equality limited by race
- Evidence: 1883–84 episode
- Significance: privilege beat the government
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1883–84 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1214, MOD-1215, MOD-1217.
**Trap:** Do not present it as a legal technicality (✅ owner 08 Adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use for "rule of law in theory" questions.
**Back:** *"Ilbert shows which law won: race."*

### Flashcard MOD-1217 — Concept: steel frame and bureaucratic despotism
**Front:** What do "steel frame" and "bureaucratic despotism" mean?
**Definition (learn):** The steel frame describes an efficient, closed civil service that held the colonial state together; bureaucratic despotism describes centralised rule without representation or consent (✅ owner 08 Adv §2; ⚠️ both are characterisations, not quotations from a named authority).
**Breakdown:**
- Steel frame: competence, permanence and prestige with a colour bar
- Bureaucratic despotism: absence of popular consent
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1196, MOD-1198, MOD-1218.
**Trap:** Do not present these as quotations (✅ owner 08 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** "Modern in technique but despotic in spirit."
**Back:** *"Steel for competence, despotism for consent."*

### Flashcard MOD-1218 — Rule of law in theory, racial discrimination in practice
**Front:** What is the contradiction of colonial rule of law?
**Definition (learn):** Formal legal equality and codified law coexisted with racial privilege, executive discretion and repressive statutes (✅ owner 08 §1, §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Formal principle: equality before law
- Practice: racial exemption
- Decisive episode: Ilbert Bill
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1216, MOD-1213, MOD-1227.
**Trap:** Rule of law does not mean social equality (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Mains hook:** Colonial law was universal in form and racial in operation.
**Back:** *"Universal in form, racial in use."*

### Flashcard MOD-1219 — Trap: 1853 is not Indianisation
**Front:** Did Indians freely enter the civil service after 1853?
**Definition (learn):** No; the exam was in London and conditions restricted Indian entry, so formal eligibility differed from effective access (✅ owner 08 §5, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Formal: competition open
- Effective: London, cost, age limits
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1853; 1855 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1198, MOD-1142.
**Trap:** Do not write 1853 as Indianisation (✅ owner 08 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Formal versus effective access is the examinable distinction.
**Back:** *"Open on paper, closed in London."*

### Flashcard MOD-1220 — Trap: Cornwallis professionalised but did not democratise
**Front:** Did Cornwallis democratise the administration?
**Definition (learn):** No; he professionalised it and kept higher posts European (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Professional: salaries, discipline
- Exclusion: European superior posts
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1793 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1196, MOD-1217.
**Trap:** Professional does not mean representative.
**Mains hook:** Efficiency and exclusion were designed together.
**Back:** *"Paid well, kept European at the top."*

### Flashcard MOD-1221 — Trap: Police Act 1861 is order, not public service
**Front:** Was the 1861 police designed around citizen rights?
**Definition (learn):** No; it was designed for order, surveillance and coercion (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Function: regime security and revenue enforcement
- Accountability: executive, not community
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1861 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1210, MOD-1209.
**Trap:** Do not read modern police-reform categories back into 1861 (✅ owner 08 §8.4 D).
**Mains hook:** Continuity of design, not of intention.
**Back:** *"Sixty-one: order first, service never."*

### Flashcard MOD-1222 — Trap: the post-1857 army was not less communal
**Front:** Did the post-1857 army become less communalised?
**Definition (learn):** No; recruitment was divided by caste, region and religion under martial-race logic (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Recruitment: separated groups
- Doctrine: martial races
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-1857 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1203, MOD-1204, MOD-1206.
**Trap:** Do not claim the army trusted Indians more (✅ owner 08 §5).
**Mains hook:** Divide-and-rule as institutional engineering.
**Back:** *"Post-1857: divided, not trusted."*

### Flashcard MOD-1223 — Concept: the four pillars and how they interlocked
**Front:** How did civil service, police, courts and army interlock?
**Definition (learn):** Revenue demand, district collector-magistrate, thana/daroga police, codified courts to legitimise coercion, sepoy army as final guarantee, and covenanted European service keeping command of all four (✅ owner 08 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Revenue → collector → police → courts → army → covenanted command
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner mechanism (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1195, MOD-1209, MOD-1211, MOD-1203, MOD-1196.
**Trap:** The four pillars were not independent; each supported the others.
**Mains hook:** Use the mechanism for a 20-mark institutional answer.
**Back:** *"Collect, police, judge, guard: command in London's hands."*

### Flashcard MOD-1224 — Current link: police reform and sedition debates (⚠️)
**Front:** Why do police reform and colonial-law debates link to the 1861 model?
**Definition (learn):** Debates on police accountability, colonial-era laws and criminal-justice reform trace to the 1861 police model and colonial codification (✅ owner 08 §6, ⚠️ not pegged to a news item).
**Breakdown:**
- Police: accountability problem
- Law: colonial-era codes
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-1210, MOD-1213.
**Trap:** Do not present as settled policy.
**Mains hook:** Continuity of colonial design into the present.
**Back:** *"Old police model, new reform debates."*

### Flashcard MOD-1225 — Verdict: the district model as legacy
**Front:** What is the most durable colonial bequest?
**Definition (learn):** The district-based administrative model: collector, superintendent of police, magistracy and codified law, which independent India inherited and only partly reformed (✅ owner 08 §8.2 legacy thesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Collector, SP, magistracy, codes
- Inheritance: partly reformed
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1195, MOD-1223.
**Trap:** Do not assume the colonial district was reformed fully.
**Mains hook:** "Independent India inherited an administration built for control and had to re-purpose it for development."
**Back:** *"Collector, SP, magistrate, code: inherited whole."*

### Flashcard MOD-1226 — Verdict: rule of law and the Ilbert settlement
**Front:** What is the rule-of-law verdict?
**Definition (learn):** Colonial law was universal in form and racial in operation; the Ilbert Bill settled which of the two prevailed when they collided (✅ owner 08 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Universal form
- Racial operation
- Test case: Ilbert
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1883–84 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MOD-1218, MOD-1216.
**Trap:** Do not present the verdict as a quotation (✅ owner 08 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Rule-of-law answer scaffold.
**Back:** *"Universal form, racial reality, Ilbert the test."*

### Flashcard MOD-1227 — Concessions and genuine achievements of the colonial state
**Front:** What genuine achievements should an answer concede?
**Definition (learn):** Uniform codes, predictable procedure, an examined and less venal higher bureaucracy, durable institutional continuity (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Codes and procedure
- Examined service
- Continuity
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1212, MOD-1198, MOD-1225.
**Trap:** Balance requires conceding achievements.
**Mains hook:** Balanced answer avoids both extremes (✅ owner 08 Adv historiography).
**Back:** *"Concede the codes, the exam, the continuity."*

### Flashcard MOD-1228 — Variation: non-regulation provinces, princely and tribal areas
**Front:** Was administrative uniformity complete?
**Definition (learn):** No; non-regulation provinces, princely states and tribal or excluded areas had different and often more personalised arrangements (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Non-regulation provinces
- Princely states
- Tribal and excluded areas
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1179, MOD-1195.
**Trap:** Do not present colonial administration as uniform (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Administrative uniformity was itself uneven.
**Back:** *"Not all India had the same collector."*

### Flashcard MOD-1229 — Indian participation in subordinate services, bar and lower judiciary
**Front:** Where were Indians substantially present in the colonial state?
**Definition (learn):** In subordinate services, the bar and the lower judiciary, which is why law became a route into nationalist leadership (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Subordinate services
- Bar and lower judiciary
- Outcome: law as path to leadership
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1211, MOD-1218.
**Trap:** Do not conflate subordinate with higher service (✅ owner 08 Adv §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Indian participation below the top explains nationalist leadership.
**Back:** *"Bar and lower court: the route to leadership."*

### Flashcard MOD-1230 — PYQ status: no direct routes in owner 08
**Front:** Does owner 08 carry a verified PYQ route?
**Definition (learn):** Zero direct PYQ routes in 2018–2026; adjacent questions belong to other topics (✅ owner 08 Semantic-completeness block).
**Breakdown:**
- Adjacent: Medieval Topic 24 (hierarchy), Modern Topic 05 (Company armies)
- Practice items in owner: original (not official keys)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-1126, MOD-1195.
**Trap:** Do not invent an official key (✅ owner 08).
**Mains hook:** Use the owner's original practice for answer structure.
**Back:** *"No direct route; adjacency is the study."*

## MODERN INDIAN HISTORY — Part 2: Reforms, 1857, Early Nationalism, Militancy and Communalism

> Section note: Prefix `MOD`; ID block `MOD-2001` onward (sequential, no gaps). Owner files 09–17 (basic and advanced read in full).
> Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` = grounded in the repository owner (basic/advanced); `(✅ Wikipedia: <page>)` = confirmed on a page fetched 2026-11-01; `⚠️` = unverified, memory-only, or an owner-vs-source conflict (flagged inside the card).

### GROUP — Social & Cultural Policy: Education & the Press

### Flashcard MOD-2001 — Colonial education and press as state–society negotiation
**Front:** Why is colonial social and cultural policy best read as a negotiation rather than a one-way gift or a pure control instrument?
**Definition (learn):** Colonial education and press policy created a new public sphere (English and vernacular print, associations, universities) that the state needed for administration and then tried to regulate. The outcome was double-edged: the same policy that produced cheap clerical staff produced the critics of colonial rule. (✅ owner 09, §1, §8.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Policy stages: Orientalist support (1781, 1791) → Anglicist turn (1835) → system blueprint (1854) → universities (1857) → press repression (1878) and repeal (1882).
- Boundary: Topic 06 owns the Charter Act constitutional mechanics; Topic 08 owns administration; Topic 10 owns reform movements; Topic 09 owns reform statutes only as state-policy outputs (✅ owner 09, Semantic-completeness).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1780 Hicky's Bengal Gazette; 1781 Calcutta Madrasa; 1791 Sanskrit College Banaras; 1813 Charter Act; 1835 Macaulay/Bentinck; 1854 Wood's Despatch; 1857 three universities; 1878 Vernacular Press Act; 1882 repeal (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2002, MOD-2009, MOD-2016, MOD-2022, MOD-2026 (reform movements).
**Trap:** Do not present colonial education as either pure benevolence or pure exploitation. Both motive and consequence must be stated.
**Mains hook:** "English education was intended to serve empire, but it helped create nationalism." Qualified verdict: it worked as designed at the level of recruitment and failed at the level of loyalty (✅ owner 09, §8.7).
**Back:** *"Built to staff the Raj, it staffed the critics."*

### Flashcard MOD-2002 — Calcutta Madrasa (1781)
**Front:** Which early Orientalist institution was founded in 1781, and under whom?
**Definition (learn):** The Calcutta Madrasa was an early state-supported institution for Islamic learning, founded under Warren Hastings in 1781, representing the Orientalist phase of colonial education policy. (✅ owner 09, §1, §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Orientalist phase = support for classical and traditional learning (Persian/Arabic/Sanskrit) as a way to govern through indigenous learned elites.
- Paired with Sanskrit College, Banaras (1791), MOD-2003.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1781; Warren Hastings (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2003 (Sanskrit College), MOD-2004 (Orientalist–Anglicist debate).
**Trap:** Do not confuse Hastings' Orientalist institutions (1781, 1791) with Macaulay's 1835 Anglicist turn.
**Mains hook:** Use with MOD-2004 to show that Orientalism was an instrument of rule, not a pro-Indian policy.
**Back:** *"1781, Hastings, classical learning first."*

### Flashcard MOD-2003 — Sanskrit College, Banaras (1791)
**Front:** Which Sanskrit institution of 1791 represents early Orientalist policy, and what did it reflect?
**Definition (learn):** A Sanskrit College founded at Banaras in 1791 under Orientalist influence, supporting classical Indian learning inside state policy. (✅ owner 09, §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Same logic as the Calcutta Madrasa: governance through classical learning and traditional elites.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1791, Banaras (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2002, MOD-2004.
**Trap:** Do not place it in the Anglicist phase; it predates Macaulay by over forty years.
**Mains hook:** Shows that the medium debate of 1835 was a reversal, not a first step.
**Back:** *"Banaras 1791: Sanskrit before Macaulay."*

### Flashcard MOD-2004 — Orientalist–Anglicist controversy
**Front:** What was the Orientalist–Anglicist controversy about?
**Definition (learn):** A debate over whether state funds should support traditional/classical learning (Orientalist) or Western knowledge taught through English (Anglicist). It was about the medium and content of state-funded knowledge, not merely language. (✅ owner 09, §8.4 B)
**Breakdown:**
- Orientalist side: classical and Indian languages, traditional elites.
- Anglicist side: English medium, Western sciences, creation of an English-educated class.
- Outcome: Anglicist victory after Macaulay's Minute and Bentinck's 1835 resolution.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Orientalist institutions 1781 and 1791; Charter Act 1813 (₹1 lakh for education, admitted missionaries); Anglicist settlement 1835 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2002, MOD-2003, MOD-2005, MOD-2006, MOD-2008.
**Trap:** The Orientalists were not anti-colonial; they were a governance tool. The Anglicist victory is not a victory for Indians as a whole.
**Mains hook:** Analyse the controversy as a debate over knowledge, power and governance (✅ owner 09, adv §7).
**Back:** *"Classics versus English: 1835 settled the medium of power."*

### Flashcard MOD-2005 — Charter Act 1813: education clause
**Front:** What did the Charter Act of 1813 provide for education and missionaries?
**Definition (learn):** The Charter Act of 1813 sanctioned an annual sum of ₹1 lakh for education and permitted Christian missionaries to work in India. (✅ owner 09, §1, §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Education grant: ₹1 lakh (owner figure, ✅ owner 09; the owner also says "annually" in the advanced file).
- Missionary access: opened the door to missionary education, which later competed with state schooling.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1813; ₹1 lakh; missionaries (✅ owner 09). ⚠️ The phrase "annually" appears in the advanced owner only; check the Charter Act text before using it in an answer.
**Links:** MOD-2004 (Orientalist–Anglicist), MOD-2001.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1813 education clause with the 1835 Macaulay decision or the 1854 Wood's Despatch.
**Mains hook:** The 1813 clause shows that state education began as a small grant, long before the 1854 system.
**Back:** *"1813: one lakh and the missionary door."*

### Flashcard MOD-2006 — Macaulay's Minute (1835)
**Front:** What did Macaulay's Minute of 1835 advocate, and what was its famous aim?
**Definition (learn):** Thomas Babington Macaulay's 1835 Minute supported English education and the aim of forming a class "Indian in blood and colour, but English in taste". (✅ owner 09, §1, §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Medium: English, not classical languages.
- Content: Western knowledge.
- Target class: an intermediary class of loyal, English-speaking Indians.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1835 (✅ owner 09). The owner gives the quoted phrase as Macaulay's aim, not as a verified verbatim quotation for the answer; do not present it as verbatim in exam answers (✅ owner 09 §8.8 caution).
**Links:** MOD-2007 (downward filtration), MOD-2008 (Bentinck), MOD-2004.
**Trap:** Macaulay's Minute (1835) and Wood's Despatch (1854) are different documents with different functions (✅ owner 09, §5).
**Mains hook:** The class Macaulay wanted became the professional-nationalist elite; a double-edged outcome.
**Back:** *"1835: English in taste, loyal in blood."*

### Flashcard MOD-2007 — Downward filtration theory
**Front:** What is the downward filtration theory and why is it important?
**Definition (learn):** The assumption that educating a small English-educated elite would spread knowledge down to the wider population. (✅ owner 09, §1, adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Premise: knowledge trickles down from the top.
- Reality: mass and vernacular education were not resourced, so the policy succeeded at the top and failed at the base.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Associated with the 1835 Anglicist settlement (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2006, MOD-2009 (Wood's Despatch vernacular recommendation).
**Trap:** Filtration is a theory of the policy, not a measured outcome. Do not quantify colonial literacy (✅ owner 09, §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "The policy succeeded at the top and failed at the base": the origin of colonial literacy deficits (✅ owner 09, §8.2).
**Back:** *"Trickle down, not spread out."*

### Flashcard MOD-2008 — Bentinck's Resolution (1835)
**Front:** What did Bentinck's 1835 resolution do for English education?
**Definition (learn):** Lord William Bentinck's 1835 resolution accepted English as the medium for higher education and implemented the Anglicist preference. (✅ owner 09, §1, adv §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Implemented the Macaulay line in policy.
- Applied to higher education.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1835 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2006, MOD-2004.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the 1835 medium decision to Wood's Despatch of 1854.
**Mains hook:** Shows the 1835 decision was an executive resolution as much as an argument.
**Back:** *"Bentinck signed what Macaulay argued."*

### Flashcard MOD-2009 — Wood's Despatch (1854)
**Front:** What did Wood's Despatch of 1854 recommend, and who wrote it?
**Definition (learn):** The 1854 despatch from Sir Charles Wood, President of the Board of Control, to Governor-General Dalhousie, which set the framework of the modern Indian education system. (✅ Wikipedia: Wood's Despatch; ✅ owner 09)
**Breakdown:**
- Provincial Departments of Public Instruction.
- Universities at Calcutta, Bombay and Madras.
- Grants-in-aid for affiliated private schools.
- Government schools in each district (at least one per district, per the page).
- Teacher-training schools.
- Vernacular medium in primary schools, English at higher levels; state support for women's education; secular education.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 19 July 1854 (✅ Wikipedia); Charles Wood; Dalhousie (✅ Wikipedia). Departments of Public Instruction appeared from 1855 (✅ Wikipedia). Universities 1857 (MOD-2010).
**Links:** MOD-2010, MOD-2007, MOD-2012 (Hunter 1882), MOD-2013 (Universities Act 1904).
**Trap:** The "Magna Carta of English education" label is a conventional nickname; the Wikipedia article fetched does not use it (⚠️ owner 09 §8.6 also flags it as a label). Vernacular primary education was recommended but largely unfunded in practice.
**Mains hook:** Cite the recommendation and the implementation gap together. Grants-in-aid brought private bodies into a state-regulated system.
**Back:** *"1854: grants, departments, universities, vernacular base."*

### Flashcard MOD-2010 — Universities of Calcutta, Bombay and Madras (1857)
**Front:** Which three universities were established in 1857?
**Definition (learn):** The universities of Calcutta, Bombay and Madras, founded in 1857 on the model of London's university, following Wood's Despatch. (✅ owner 09; ✅ Wikipedia: Wood's Despatch)
**Breakdown:**
- Calcutta, Bombay, Madras.
- Became the recruiting ground of the professional and nationalist elite (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1857 (✅ owner 09, ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2009, MOD-2013.
**Trap:** Do not date the universities to 1854 (the despatch) or to 1904 (the Universities Act). Only the 1857 date is correct.
**Mains hook:** Links education policy to the professions that later led nationalism.
**Back:** *"Three cities, one year: 1857."*

### Flashcard MOD-2011 — Fort William College (Calcutta, under Wellesley)
**Front:** What was the stated purpose of Fort William College at Calcutta, and whom did it train?
**Definition (learn):** A college founded under Governor-General Wellesley at Calcutta to train European civil servants in Indian languages and law before posting. It trained Europeans, not Indians. (✅ owner 09, §8.4 A)
**Breakdown:**
- Wellesley's minute of 1800: the Indian empire "should not be administered as a temporary and precarious acquisition".
- Civil servants from all presidencies took three years of training there.
- Purpose also included containing the spread of French Revolutionary ideas (owner citing Sekhar Bandyopadhyay).
- Closed in 1802 by the Court of Directors; continued only as a language school.
- Replaced in the training role by East India College at Hertford (1805), moved to Haileybury (1809).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Wellesley arrived 1798; minute 1800; closed 1802; Haileybury 1809 (✅ owner 09). The founding year is not stated in the owner text, so do not give one without a source (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-2001, MOD-2006, MOD-2004.
**Trap:** Do not present Fort William as part of Indian education policy. The owner flags its role in Bengali prose as a real but separate claim (⚠️ owner 09).
**Mains hook:** The college shows that colonial knowledge policy was administrative, ideological and political at once (✅ owner 09, §8.4 A).
**Back:** *"Fort William trained the rulers, not the ruled."*

### Flashcard MOD-2012 — Hunter Commission (1882)
**Front:** Which commission reviewed education in 1882, and what did it examine?
**Definition (learn):** The Hunter Commission (Education Commission, 1882) reviewed the working of the education system after Wood's Despatch, especially primary and secondary education. (✅ owner 09, §2, adv §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Reviews Wood's system, primary and secondary focus.
- Same year as Ripon's repeal of the press restrictions (MOD-2017).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1882 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2009, MOD-2013.
**Trap:** Wood's Despatch (1854) is a policy framework; Hunter (1882) is a review. Do not interchange them (✅ owner 09, adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Shows that by 1882 the state accepted that primary education had been neglected.
**Back:** *"1882 Hunter reviews, 1854 Wood frames."*

### Flashcard MOD-2013 — Universities Act 1904 and Sadler Commission 1917
**Front:** What did the Universities Act of 1904 and the Sadler Commission of 1917 represent?
**Definition (learn):** Curzon's Universities Act (1904) tightened official control over universities; the Sadler Commission (1917) reviewed university education after the 1904 era. (✅ owner 09, §2, adv §4)
**Breakdown:**
- 1904: Curzon's Universities Act, official control.
- 1917: Sadler Commission review of university education.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1904 (Universities Act, Curzon); 1917 (Sadler) (✅ owner 09). Owner 15 also lists the 1904 Universities Act as reactionary Curzonian (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MOD-2010, MOD-2135 (Curzon measures), MOD-2012.
**Trap:** The 1904 Act is not a Wood's Despatch measure. It was an official-control measure that nationalists read as anti-representative.
**Mains hook:** Use with MOD-2131 to show Curzon's reform was control, not expansion.
**Back:** *"1904 Curzon controls, 1917 Sadler reviews."*

### Flashcard MOD-2014 — Hicky's Bengal Gazette (1780)
**Front:** What was India's first newspaper, and when did it start?
**Definition (learn):** Hicky's Bengal Gazette, started in 1780, is recorded as India's first newspaper. It marks the beginning of the Indian press. (✅ owner 09, §1, §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Start of an English-language press in Calcutta.
- The press later became a major tool of nationalism.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1780 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2015 (Metcalfe), MOD-2016 (VPA).
**Trap:** Do not present the pre-1878 press as free; licensing and prosecution existed throughout (✅ owner 09, adv §8.4 D).
**Mains hook:** Use to show the press began as a colonial English institution before becoming an Indian-language public sphere.
**Back:** *"1780: the first paper, the first public sphere."*

### Flashcard MOD-2015 — Metcalfe, "liberator of the press" (1835)
**Front:** Why is Sir Charles Metcalfe called the "liberator of the press"?
**Definition (learn):** Metcalfe's 1835 press reforms liberalised press restrictions, earning him the title "liberator of the press". (✅ owner 09, §1, §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Metcalfe (1835): liberalisation.
- Lytton (1878): restriction (MOD-2016).
- Ripon (1882): repeal (MOD-2017).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1835 (✅ owner 09). The 1857 Licensing Act restrictions (MOD-2018) came later (✅ owner 09 adv §1).
**Links:** MOD-2014, MOD-2016, MOD-2017.
**Trap:** Metcalfe did not restrict the press; the restricting Viceroy was Lytton (✅ owner 09, §5).
**Mains hook:** Metcalfe and Lytton are two phases of one relationship: the state's ambivalence about an autonomous public sphere.
**Back:** *"Metcalfe freed it, Lytton fenced it."*

### Flashcard MOD-2016 — Vernacular Press Act (1878)
**Front:** What did the Vernacular Press Act of 1878 target, who passed it, and what did it do?
**Definition (learn):** Lytton's Vernacular Press Act (1878) targeted Indian-language newspapers, while English papers were exempt. (✅ Wikipedia: Vernacular Press Act; ✅ owner 09)
**Breakdown:**
- Viceroy: Lytton; proposed it. Passed 14 March 1878 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Target: vernacular (Indian-language) press; English papers exempt (✅ Wikipedia).
- Provisions: warnings for seditious content; magistrates could require securities or bonds whose decision was final with no court appeal; forfeiture and seizure of press for repeat offences (✅ Wikipedia).
- Exemption by submitting proofs to a censor was dropped in October 1878; the bail-bond remained (✅ Wikipedia).
- Amrita Bazar Patrika became an all-English weekly within a week, escaping the Act (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 09 adv).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1878; Lytton; vernacular only (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2015, MOD-2017, MOD-2001.
**Trap:** The Act was not a general newspaper law. It targeted Indian-language papers only (✅ owner 09, §5).
**Mains hook:** The state feared criticism that reached beyond the English-reading elite (✅ owner 09, adv §8.4 D).
**Back:** *"1878: the vernacular fence, English escapes."*

### Flashcard MOD-2017 — Repeal of the Vernacular Press Act: 1881 or 1882?
**Front:** Who repealed the Vernacular Press Act, and in which year?
**Definition (learn):** The Vernacular Press Act was repealed under Lord Ripon, the owner dates the repeal to 1882; Wikipedia says the Act was withdrawn in 1881 under Ripon and that its formal repealing statute is the Seditious Publications Act, 1882. (✅ owner 09; ✅ Wikipedia: Vernacular Press Act)
**Breakdown:**
- Ripon, 1880–84 Viceroy.
- Owner: repealed 1882 (✅ owner 09).
- Wikipedia: withdrawn 1881; the infobox lists the Seditious Publications Act 1882 as the repealing statute.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ripon; 1881 (withdrawal) and 1882 (repealing Act) (✅ Wikipedia). ⚠️ Source conflict: owner gives 1882 as the repeal; Wikipedia gives 1881 for withdrawal.
**Links:** MOD-2016, MOD-2015, MOD-2012.
**Trap:** In an answer, say "withdrawn under Ripon, 1881; formally repealed by the Seditious Publications Act, 1882" if the examiner tests the difference. Otherwise use 1882 as the owner does.
**Mains hook:** The repeal shows agitation could reverse repression, a qualified achievement.
**Back:** *"Ripon undid it: 1881 withdrawal, 1882 law."*

### Flashcard MOD-2018 — Licensing Act 1857 (press restriction after the revolt)
**Front:** What press restriction came after the 1857 crisis?
**Definition (learn):** Later crises produced restrictions such as the Licensing Act of 1857, which tightened press control after the revolt. (✅ owner 09 adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Post-1857 security climate.
- Press control before the 1878 Act.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1857 (✅ owner 09 adv). ⚠️ The owner gives only the name and year; the provisions are not stated in the owner and are not verified here.
**Links:** MOD-2015, MOD-2016, MOD-2011 (Chapter: 1857 consequences MOD-2068).
**Trap:** Do not treat the 1878 Act as the first press restriction.
**Mains hook:** Shows that repression of the press followed the 1857 lesson.
**Back:** *"1857 licensing, 1878 vernacular."*

### Flashcard MOD-2019 — Abolition of sati (1829), Regulation XVII
**Front:** How was sati abolished in 1829, and who was behind it?
**Definition (learn):** Lord William Bentinck abolished sati in 1829 through Regulation XVII, with the campaign of Raja Rammohan Roy as a major Indian pressure. (✅ owner 09, §4, adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Instrument: Regulation XVII (1829).
- Indian pressure: Rammohan Roy.
- Also: suppression of female infanticide and thugee (adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1829; Bentinck; Regulation XVII; Rammohan Roy (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2020, MOD-2027 (Brahmo Samaj), MOD-2024.
**Trap:** Do not credit only the British. The owner stresses the joint credit (✅ owner 09, §5).
**Mains hook:** Social legislation as interaction between Indian reform and colonial state (✅ owner 09, §8.4 E).
**Back:** *"1829: Bentinck's regulation, Rammohan's campaign."*

### Flashcard MOD-2020 — Slavery (1843) and Widow Remarriage Act (1856)
**Front:** What were the 1843 slavery measure and the 1856 widow remarriage law, and who is linked with the latter?
**Definition (learn):** The owner records the legal addressing of slavery in 1843 and the Widow Remarriage Act of 1856, which Vidyasagar's campaign made possible. (✅ owner 09, §4, §8.4 E)
**Breakdown:**
- 1843: slavery legally addressed (owner).
- 1856: Widow Remarriage Act; associated with Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1843; 1856 (✅ owner 09). ⚠️ The 1843 measure's precise provisions are not in the owner text.
**Links:** MOD-2019, MOD-2042 (Malabari & Age of Consent), MOD-2040 (Vidyasagar).
**Trap:** Legislation is not social change; enforcement was weak (✅ owner 09, §8.4 E limit).
**Mains hook:** The 1856 Act shows Indian reformers provided the argument and the state the statute.
**Back:** *"1843 slavery, 1856 widows, Vidyasagar's voice."*

### Flashcard MOD-2021 — Madanapalle and *Songs from Prison* (PYQ routes, inferred)
**Front:** What are the two 2021 Prelims routes in this owner, and what is their answer status?
**Definition (learn):** Two 2021 Prelims GS-I routes: (a) Madanapalle's historical significance (Tagore's English rendering of *Jana Gana Mana* in 1919, per supplementary sources); (b) *Songs from Prison*, a translation of ancient religious lyrics associated with M.K. Gandhi (1934). (✅ owner 09, §8.9)
**Breakdown:**
- Madanapalle: Tagore's 1919 stay at Besant Theosophical College; Margaret Cousins linked to musical notation (owner, supplementary).
- *Songs from Prison* (1934): translations of Indian lyrics, prepared with John S. Hoyland (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 Prelims Q36 and Q44; key not held locally; status **INFERRED — NOT LOCALLY OFFICIALLY KEYED** (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2022 (Gandhi–Tagore 2023).
**Trap:** Do not state the answer letter; the official key is not in the repository.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; use only for Prelims recall.
**Back:** *"Madanapalle: Tagore's song. Songs from Prison: Gandhi's lyrics."* ⚠️ answer letter unverified.

### Flashcard MOD-2022 — Gandhi and Tagore on education and nationalism (2023 Mains route)
**Front:** How should an answer to the 2023 GS-I question on Gandhi and Tagore on education and nationalism be structured?
**Definition (learn):** A 10-mark (150-word) GS-I question on the difference between Gandhi and Tagore on education and nationalism; cross-cutting with the owner 09 education policy context. (✅ owner 09 adv, PYQ integration 2023 Q2)
**Breakdown:**
- Context: colonial English education and its nationalist critique.
- Core dimensions: education purpose (national character vs universal humanism), language, nationalism and its limits.
- Evidence: named statements of each thinker (paraphrase only; no verbatim quotes unless verified).
- Counterpoint and a concise conclusion.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2023 GS-I Q2; 10 marks; 150 words (✅ owner 09 adv).
**Links:** MOD-2001, MOD-2006, MOD-2023.
**Trap:** Do not attribute verbatim quotations unless verified. The owner says the route is bounded.
**Mains hook:** Double-edged thesis: English education served empire and produced the critics of empire (✅ owner 09, §8.2).
**Back:** *"Gandhi and Tagore: two schools of education, one nation."*

### Flashcard MOD-2023 — Social & cultural policy: concept card for Topic 09 PYQ coverage
**Front:** Which six PYQ-routed demands does Topic 09 cover, and how are they bounded?
**Definition (learn):** Six direct Prelims routes across 2018, 2020 and 2021 plus one 2023 Mains route on education (✅ owner 09, PYQ integration).
**Breakdown:**
- 2018 Q19 Wood's Despatch provisions; 2018 Q76 English education factors; 2018 Q89 colonial institution founders matching.
- 2020 Q34 Fort William College purpose.
- 2021 Q36 Madanapalle; 2021 Q44 Songs from Prison.
- Key status: official 2018–2023 keys not held locally; no answer inferred except the supplementary "INFERRED" labels on 2021.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Six routes; 2023 Mains Q2 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2009, MOD-2011, MOD-2021, MOD-2022.
**Trap:** Do not present an unkeyed Prelims question as solved.
**Mains hook:** Use the owner's answer architecture (§8.3): 10 marks = 2–3 landmarks; 20 marks = 6–8 units including institutions and statutes.
**Back:** *"Six Prelims routes, zero keys invented."*

### Flashcard MOD-2024 — Who was excluded from colonial education?
**Front:** Who benefited least from colonial education policy, and why?
**Definition (learn):** Access was concentrated among upper-caste, urban, propertied and male students in presidency regions; girls' and lower-caste access advanced mainly through Indian initiative rather than state provision (⚠️ owner 09 §8.4 F).
**Breakdown:**
- Filter: class, caste, gender, region.
- Filtration theory (MOD-2007) explains why the top was served and the base was not.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No colonial literacy or enrolment statistics are cited (✅ owner 09 caution).
**Links:** MOD-2007, MOD-2010, MOD-2036 (Bhide Wada), MOD-2042 (Malabari).
**Trap:** Do not cite colonial literacy numbers. Do not present the state as the sole source of girls' schooling.
**Mains hook:** Provide the mandatory balance paragraph: who was excluded, and the Indian counter-initiative.
**Back:** *"Top served, base starved, girls led by Indians."*

### Flashcard MOD-2025 — Press as public sphere: the "contested-sphere" thesis
**Front:** What does the contested-sphere thesis claim about the colonial press?
**Definition (learn):** The press was the first genuinely modern Indian public institution; because it created an autonomous political sphere, the state alternately liberated and muzzled it (✅ owner 09, §8.2).
**Breakdown:**
- Liberation: Metcalfe 1835.
- Repression: Lytton 1878.
- Repeal: Ripon 1882.
- The state feared criticism reaching the vernacular public.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1780, 1835, 1878, 1882 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** MOD-2014, MOD-2015, MOD-2016, MOD-2017.
**Trap:** Do not describe the pre-1878 press as free (✅ owner 09, adv §8.4 D).
**Mains hook:** "The colonial state created the legal space for a public sphere and then discovered it could not control what that sphere concluded" (✅ owner 09, §8.7).
**Back:** *"Liberated, muzzled, repealed: the press as a battleground."*

### GROUP — Socio-Religious Reform Movements

### Flashcard MOD-2026 — Reform as a dialogue: reformist versus revivalist
**Front:** How should "reformist" and "revivalist" be distinguished in an answer on nineteenth-century reform?
**Definition (learn):** Reformist means reinterpreting tradition with modern reason; revivalist means returning to a purified ancient or religious source. The distinction is about the source of authority, not about who was more progressive. (✅ owner 10, §1, adv §2; ⚠️ the definitions are the owner's analytical framing)
**Breakdown:**
- Modernising aim: attack sati, child marriage, caste rigidity, purdah, denial of women's education.
- Two streams: reformist (Brahmo, Prarthana, Aligarh) and revivalist (Arya, Singh Sabha, Deoband).
- Both streams used modern organisation, print and schools.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Brahmo 1828; Arya 1875; Deoband 1866; Aligarh MAO College 1875 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2027, MOD-2031, MOD-2038, MOD-2039, MOD-2049.
**Trap:** Do not treat "reform" as automatically liberal and "revival" as automatically conservative. Arya Samaj's revivalist idiom carried an anti-caste, pro-education programme (✅ owner 10, §8.6).
**Mains hook:** "Was revivalism a barrier to modernity or a vehicle for cultural self-confidence?" Qualified answer: both, depending on the programme (✅ owner 10, adv §7).
**Back:** *"Reform reinterprets, revival returns: judge the programme, not the label."*

### Flashcard MOD-2027 — Brahmo Samaj (1828), Raja Rammohan Roy
**Front:** When and where was the Brahmo Samaj founded, by whom, and what were its core commitments?
**Definition (learn):** Founded by Raja Rammohan Roy in Calcutta in 1828 (after the Atmiya Sabha). It combined monotheism, rational religion, opposition to sati and idolatry, support for English education and journalism. (✅ owner 10, §1, §4; ✅ owner 10 adv)
**Breakdown:**
- Predecessor: Atmiya Sabha.
- Press: *Sambad Kaumudi* used for public opinion.
- Method: argue from texts; voluntary association; print.
- Social base: urban, educated, largely upper-caste Bengal (✅ owner 10 adv §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1828, Calcutta, Rammohan Roy (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2019 (sati), MOD-2028, MOD-2030, MOD-2026.
**Trap:** The Brahmo Samaj was not founded in Bombay (✅ owner 10, §5). Do not confuse with Prarthana Samaj (1867, Bombay).
**Mains hook:** Roy's importance lies in method: textual reason against custom (✅ owner 10 §8.5).
**Back:** *"1828 Calcutta, reason against sati."*

### Flashcard MOD-2028 — Brahmo succession and the 1878 split (Sadharan Brahmo Samaj)
**Front:** Who carried the Brahmo tradition after Rammohan Roy, and what split followed?
**Definition (learn):** The tradition continued under Debendranath Tagore and Keshab Chandra Sen; internal differences produced a split, leading to the Sadharan Brahmo Samaj in 1878. (✅ owner 10, §1, §8.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Debendranath Tagore: continuity.
- Keshab Chandra Sen: later leader; split origin.
- Sadharan Brahmo Samaj (1878): the split institution.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1878 (✅ owner 10). Owner 10 mini-timeline also places the Keshab split in 1878.
**Links:** MOD-2027, MOD-2029.
**Trap:** Do not place the split in 1828 or confuse Sadharan with Adi Brahmo Samaj (not in owner; ⚠️ not included).
**Mains hook:** The split shows the difficulty of holding rationalism and devotional practice together (✅ owner 10 §8.5).
**Back:** *"Debendranath carried it, Keshab split it, 1878 Sadharan."*

### Flashcard MOD-2029 — Prarthana Samaj (1867, Bombay)
**Front:** Where and when was the Prarthana Samaj founded, and what were its social aims?
**Definition (learn):** A reform society in Bombay founded in 1867, with a devotional (bhakti-based) approach to reform, emphasising widow remarriage, women's education and the end of caste, child marriage and infanticide. (✅ Wikipedia: Prarthana Samaj; ✅ owner 10 §1, §8.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Founders and leaders: Atmaram Pandurang (with Waman Abaji Modak); M.G. Ranade made it popular; R.G. Bhandarkar credited (✅ Wikipedia).
- Method: cautious, "least resistance" reform (✅ owner 10 §8.6).
- Political afterlife: moderate politics in western India.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1867, 31 March, Bombay (✅ Wikipedia). Owner 10 gives the leaders but not the year.
**Links:** MOD-2028, MOD-2038, MOD-2026.
**Trap:** Atmaram Pandurang, Ranade and Bhandarkar are linked to it, but the Wikipedia article lists Pandurang and Waman Abaji Modak as the founders; Ranade joined later (✅ Wikipedia). Do not call Ranade the founder.
**Mains hook:** Ranade's "whole man" approach: reform the whole person, not one side (✅ Wikipedia).
**Back:** *"1867 Bombay, devotion plus widow remarriage."*

### Flashcard MOD-2030 — Young Bengal and Henry Vivian Derozio
**Front:** What was Young Bengal, and who led it?
**Definition (learn):** A radical rationalist group in Bengal led by Henry Vivian Derozio, stressing free thought, attack on orthodoxy and modern education. (✅ owner 10, §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Method: debate, journalism, iconoclasm.
- Political afterlife: intellectual radicalism with a limited base.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Derozio (✅ owner 10). ⚠️ No founding year in owner text.
**Links:** MOD-2027, MOD-2026.
**Trap:** Young Bengal is radical rationalist; Brahmo Samaj is reformist. Do not merge them.
**Mains hook:** The 2021 PYQ on Young Bengal and Brahmo Samaj routes here (✅ owner 10 adv PYQ integration, 2021 GS-I Q2).
**Back:** *"Derozio's Young Bengal: free thought, no temple."*

### Flashcard MOD-2031 — Arya Samaj (1875) and Dayananda Saraswati
**Front:** What did Dayananda Saraswati's Arya Samaj (1875) stand for?
**Definition (learn):** Founded by Swami Dayananda Saraswati in 1875, the Arya Samaj gave the slogan "Back to the Vedas", rejected idolatry and caste rigidity, and used shuddhi (reconversion), DAV schools and the text *Satyarth Prakash*. (✅ owner 10 §1, §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Authority: Vedic.
- Method: preaching, shuddhi, DAV education.
- Social programme: anti-idolatry, education, anti-caste rigidity.
- Political afterlife: assertive Hindu identity; contested communal effects (✅ owner 10 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1875; Dayananda; *Satyarth Prakash*; DAV schools (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2026, MOD-2032, MOD-2049 (trap), MOD-2183 (Hindu Mahasabha).
**Trap:** Arya Samaj opposed idolatry; it did not support idol worship (✅ owner 10 §5). It was not only backward-looking; it used modern schools and print (✅ owner 10 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use Arya Samaj to show revivalism carrying an anti-caste programme.
**Back:** *"1875: Vedas back, idols out, DAV in."*

### Flashcard MOD-2032 — Ramakrishna Mission (1897) and Vivekananda at Chicago (1893)
**Front:** Who founded the Ramakrishna Mission, when, and what was its significance?
**Definition (learn):** Swami Vivekananda founded the Ramakrishna Mission in 1897, popularising practical Vedanta and service. He represented India at the Parliament of Religions in Chicago in 1893. (✅ owner 10 §1, §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Founder: Swami Vivekananda (not Ramakrishna Paramhansa, the teacher).
- Programme: relief, education, service; national self-confidence.
- Political afterlife: cultural nationalism.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1893 Chicago; 1897 Mission (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2031, MOD-2033.
**Trap:** Common error: attributing the Mission to Ramakrishna Paramhansa. Founder is Vivekananda (✅ owner 10 §5, §8.10).
**Mains hook:** Chicago 1893 converted spiritual confidence into national self-respect (✅ owner 10 adv §1).
**Back:** *"Chicago 1893, Mission 1897, Vivekananda both times."*

### Flashcard MOD-2033 — Theosophical Society in India (Adyar, 1882)
**Front:** Where did the Theosophical Society's headquarters shift in India, and when?
**Definition (learn):** The Theosophical Society was founded outside India in 1875 by Blavatsky and Olcott; its headquarters shifted to Adyar near Madras in 1882, and Annie Besant later linked it to Indian public life. (✅ owner 10 adv, §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Religious universalism, Vedanta, cultural self-confidence.
- Advanced-tier enrichment; not part of the Basic core.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1875; 1882 Adyar (✅ owner 10 adv).
**Links:** MOD-2032, MOD-2026.
**Trap:** Do not place the Theosophical Society in the Basic reform core; it was founded outside India.
**Mains hook:** Use only as a comparison of universalist reform (adv).
**Back:** *"1875 abroad, 1882 Adyar."*

### Flashcard MOD-2034 — Satyashodhak Samaj (1873) and Jyotirao Phule
**Front:** Who founded the Satyashodhak Samaj and when, and what was its central claim?
**Definition (learn):** Jyotiba Phule founded the Satyashodhak Samaj in 1873 in Maharashtra, arguing that Brahman domination over power and opportunity lay at the root of the predicament of the Shudra and Ati-Shudra castes. (✅ owner 10, §4, §8.4 Bank A)
**Breakdown:**
- Phule was a leader of the Mali (gardener) caste (✅ owner 10).
- Inverted the Aryanisation theory: Brahmans as progeny of alien Aryans who subjugated original inhabitants (✅ owner 10).
- Aim: unite non-Brahman peasant castes and dalits.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1873; Satyashodhak Samaj (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2035, MOD-2036, MOD-2047, MOD-2046.
**Trap:** Do not present Phule as a Brahmin-led reformer; he attacked upper-caste reform's limits (✅ owner 10 adv §1).
**Mains hook:** 2025 GS-I 15-mark Phule question (✅ owner 10, PYQ integration 2025 Q11).
**Back:** *"1873: caste is power, not custom."*

### Flashcard MOD-2035 — Phule's *Gulamgiri* and the caste-as-power thesis
**Front:** What is the "social-location" thesis for Phule, and what is the balance paragraph?
**Definition (learn):** Phule reframed social reform as a question of power rather than custom: where upper-caste reformers purified a tradition they owned, Phule identified the tradition itself as an instrument of domination. (✅ owner 10 §8.2, §8.4 Bank A)
**Breakdown:**
- Writing: *Gulamgiri* (named in owner).
- Limit: from the 1880s–90s, mobilisation of the Kunbi peasantry against the Poona Sarvajanik Sabha; ✅ Rosalind O'Hanlon's point about a risk of Sanskritising claims (owner cites "at times perilously close to a simple Sanskritising claim").
- Historiography: ⚠️ owner attribution of O'Hanlon's quotation not verified here.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1873; 1880s–90s shift (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2034, MOD-2036, MOD-2046.
**Trap:** Do not write a hagiographic account; present the Kunbi-Maratha shift as the balance paragraph.
**Mains hook:** "Phule relocated the caste question from custom to power; his limit was that the peasant-caste solidarity could drift toward status claims" (✅ owner 10 §8.9).
**Back:** *"Gulamgiri: power, not custom; watch the Kunbi turn."*

### Flashcard MOD-2036 — Bhide Wada girls' school (Poona, 1848) and Savitribai Phule
**Front:** Where and when did Jyotirao and Savitribai Phule open their girls' school, and what is Savitribai's significance?
**Definition (learn):** The Phules opened the Bhide Wada girls' school at Poona in 1848; Savitribai co-founded and ran it, teaching rather than only being taught. (✅ owner 10 §8.4 Bank A, §8.7)
**Breakdown:**
- 1848, Poona, Bhide Wada.
- Savitribai's role: teaching and co-running the school.
- Expanded educational work in the following years (✅ owner 10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1848 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2034, MOD-2037, MOD-2044 (gender agency), MOD-2024 (exclusion).
**Trap:** Do not attribute organisations or writings to Savitribai beyond the owner's record (⚠️ owner 10 §8.7 caution).
**Mains hook:** The gender answer: her classroom did more for girls' education than any resolution (✅ owner 10 §8.7).
**Back:** *"1848 Bhide Wada: Savitribai teaches."*

### Flashcard MOD-2037 — Pandita Ramabai and women's autonomy
**Front:** Why is Pandita Ramabai central to the gender-agency argument?
**Definition (learn):** Pandita Ramabai shifted the argument from improving women's condition to asserting women's autonomy, and directed her critique at upper-caste patriarchy itself rather than at particular customs. (⚠️ owner 10 §8.7 table)
**Breakdown:**
- Shift: welfare → autonomy.
- Target: upper-caste patriarchy.
- Owner gives no dated organisation; do not add one (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None verified in owner beyond the role.
**Links:** MOD-2036, MOD-2044.
**Trap:** Do not treat Ramabai as only a women's-education figure; the owner stresses autonomy.
**Mains hook:** Gender answer: "reform for women" versus "by women" (MOD-2044).
**Back:** *"Ramabai: autonomy over improvement."*

### Flashcard MOD-2038 — Aligarh and MAO College (1875), Syed Ahmad Khan
**Front:** What was Aligarh's approach and which college did Syed Ahmad Khan open in 1875?
**Definition (learn):** Syed Ahmad Khan's MAO College at Aligarh (opened 1875) sought reconciliation of Islam with modern science and Western education, avoiding agitational politics. (✅ owner 10 §4, §8.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Method: modern education; avoidance of agitation.
- Social programme: Muslim access to service and profession.
- Political afterlife: separate political trajectory (see MOD-2183 for Hindu communal bodies and MOD-2181 for the League).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1875 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2039, MOD-2026, MOD-2181.
**Trap:** Aligarh and Deoband are different responses; do not call them identical (✅ owner 10 §5).
**Mains hook:** Muslim reform was not one movement (✅ owner 10 adv §5).
**Back:** *"Aligarh: modern learning. Deoband: orthodox learning."*

### Flashcard MOD-2039 — Deoband (1866): orthodox Islamic learning
**Front:** What was Deoband's programme, and how does it differ from Aligarh?
**Definition (learn):** Founded in 1866, Deoband was an orthodox Islamic educational movement that stressed seminary learning and religious authenticity. (✅ owner 10 §4, §8.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Method: seminary education.
- Social programme: religious authenticity.
- Later political strands: anti-colonial and pro-Congress.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1866 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2038, MOD-2026.
**Trap:** Deoband is not a Congress-linked body in origin; the pro-Congress strands are later (✅ owner 10 §8.6 "later").
**Mains hook:** Contrast with Aligarh in a 10-mark answer.
**Back:** *"1866 Deoband: learn the text, keep the faith."*

### Flashcard MOD-2040 — Bethune School (Calcutta, 1849) and Vidyasagar as Secretary
**Front:** What was the Bethune School, when was it founded, and what was Vidyasagar's role?
**Definition (learn):** The Bethune School (Calcutta, 1849) was the first major fruit of the women's-education movement of the 1840s–50s. Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar was its Secretary, not its founder. (✅ owner 10 §8.7, §8.10)
**Breakdown:**
- Missionaries' girls' education began in 1821 (✅ owner 10 §8.7), with Christian instruction limiting acceptance.
- Bethune School struggled to secure students; pupils and parents faced abuse and boycott (✅ owner 10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1849 Calcutta; Vidyasagar as Secretary (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2041, MOD-2044, MOD-2024.
**Trap:** The Routed 2021 Prelims Q46 asks for the Bethune Female School secretary; answer is Vidyasagar on owner evidence (✅ owner 10 PYQ integration). Do not call him founder. Answer letter not recorded.
**Mains hook:** Institution founding is not social acceptance.
**Back:** *"Bethune 1849: Vidyasagar secretary, not founder."*

### Flashcard MOD-2041 — Vidyasagar: 35 girls' schools and 25 widow remarriages (1855–60)
**Front:** What did Vidyasagar do for girls' schooling and widow remarriage, and by what dates?
**Definition (learn):** As Government Inspector of Schools, Vidyasagar organised thirty-five girls' schools; he secured twenty-five widow remarriages between 1855 and 1860. (✅ owner 10 §8.7)
**Breakdown:**
- Role 1: Secretary of the Bethune School (MOD-2040).
- Role 2: Government Inspector of Schools (girls' schools).
- Role 3: Widow remarriage campaign leading to the 1856 Act (MOD-2020).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 35 schools; 25 remarriages, 1855–60 (✅ owner 10). ⚠️ These figures are the owner's; not verified on a fetched page.
**Links:** MOD-2020, MOD-2040, MOD-2044.
**Trap:** The 1856 Act is a statute; the 25 remarriages are actual marriages. Do not conflate.
**Mains hook:** Gives the gender answer a real institutional spine (✅ owner 10 §8.7).
**Back:** *"35 schools, 25 widows, 1855–60."*

### Flashcard MOD-2042 — Malabari's 1884 Note and the Age of Consent Act (1891)
**Front:** What did Behramji Malabari's 1884 Note achieve, and what did the Age of Consent Act of 1891 change?
**Definition (learn):** Malabari's 1884 Note on child marriage and enforced widowhood provoked a countrywide debate; the Age of Consent Act (1891) raised the age of consent from ten to twelve. (✅ owner 10 §8.7; ✅ Wikipedia: Rukhmabai)
**Breakdown:**
- 1860: earlier law prohibited consummation for a Hindu girl below ten (✅ owner 10).
- 1891: age raised to twelve (✅ Wikipedia).
- Backlash: orthodox mobilisation with a wider base, using the nationalist language of non-interference (MOD-2050).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1884; 1860; 1891; ten→twelve (✅ owner 10; ✅ Wikipedia). The Wikipedia page for the 1891 Act was not reachable (404); the 1891 provisions rest on the Rukhmabai article.
**Links:** MOD-2043, MOD-2050, MOD-2020.
**Trap:** Do not conflate 1860 (first age law, age ten) with 1891 (age twelve). The Rukhmabai case is not the Act.
**Mains hook:** By 1891 the reform question had become a national question (✅ owner 10 §8.9).
**Back:** *"1860 ten, 1891 twelve, Malabari's Note."*

### Flashcard MOD-2043 — Rukhmabai case (1884–88), Bombay High Court
**Front:** What was the Rukhmabai case, what was decided, and what was the outcome?
**Definition (learn):** Rukhmabai, married in infancy, refused to recognise her husband's conjugal rights; her husband Dadaji Bhikaji sued for restitution of conjugal rights in the Bombay High Court (1884–88). (✅ owner 10 §8.7; ✅ Wikipedia: Rukhmabai)
**Breakdown:**
- Timeline per Wikipedia: legal notice March 1884; first hearing 1885 where Justice Pinhey ruled for her; retrial 1886 with appeal upheld 18 March 1886; March 1887 Justice Farran ordered her to join her husband or face six months' imprisonment; she chose jail; July 1888 settlement with the husband receiving two thousand rupees (✅ Wikipedia).
- Defence Committee formed by supporters including Eva and Walter McLaren and Adelaide Manning (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1884–88; Bombay High Court; age at marriage eleven (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2042, MOD-2044, MOD-2050.
**Trap:** ⚠️ Source conflict: owner says she "lost" the case and avoided imprisonment through a compromise; Wikipedia shows an initial 1885 ruling in her favour, a 1887 imprisonment order that she accepted, and a 1888 settlement. Use the owner's "did not win in court" line with the Wikipedia sequence, not the bare word "lost".
**Mains hook:** Rukhmabai's refusal made child marriage a public-law question (✅ owner 10 §8.7).
**Back:** *"1884–88: she refused, the court ordered, the settlement paid."*

### Flashcard MOD-2044 — Gender: reform done to women or by women?
**Front:** How should the gender-agency question be answered: reform imposed on women or achieved by them?
**Definition (learn):** The founding campaigns were male-led (Roy on sati, Vidyasagar on widow remarriage, Malabari's Note). The decisive evidence was often female (Rukhmabai, Savitribai Phule). The honest answer states reform moved from "of" women to a claim "by" women. (✅ owner 10 §8.7)
**Breakdown:**
- For "imposed": male-led campaigns; statutes by men and a colonial state; reform argued in terms of community honour.
- For "achieved": Rukhmabai's refusal; Savitribai's classroom; Ramabai's autonomy argument; women in Swadeshi and Civil Disobedience (⚠️ owner link to basic/15 and 22).
- Institutional resistance: Bethune School boycott.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sati 1829; widow remarriage 1856; age of consent 1860/1891 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2037, MOD-2036, MOD-2040, MOD-2043.
**Trap:** Do not present institutional founding as social acceptance (✅ owner 10 §8.7).
**Mains hook:** "Colonial-era legislation improved women's legal position marginally and their political standing considerably" (✅ owner 10 §8.9).
**Back:** *"Begun for women, won by women."*

### Flashcard MOD-2045 — *Vital-Vidhvansak* (1888) and Gopal Baba Walangkar (INFERRED)
**Front:** Which 1888 journal is linked with the first monthly aimed at untouchable readers, and who is associated with it?
**Definition (learn):** *Vital-Vidhvansak* (1888) is associated with Gopal Baba Walangkar in supplementary sources as the first monthly journal aimed at untouchable readers. (⚠️ supplementary source cited in owner 10 §8.10)
**Breakdown:**
- Journal: *Vital-Vidhvansak* (1888).
- Associated figure: Gopal Baba Walangkar (supplementary).
- Official 2020 key not held locally.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1888 (owner supplementary, checked 4 September 2026 per owner). Answer: **INFERRED — NOT LOCALLY OFFICIALLY KEYED**.
**Links:** MOD-2046, MOD-2034.
**Trap:** Do not state the 2020 Prelims answer as official. The route is 2020 Q28.
**Mains hook:** Shows the anti-caste press as a separate public sphere.
**Back:** *"1888 Vital-Vidhvansak: Walangkar, inferred not keyed."*

### Flashcard MOD-2046 — Sri Narayana Guru and the Justice Party
**Front:** How did Sri Narayana Guru's movement and the Justice Party challenge caste hierarchy?
**Definition (learn):** Sri Narayana Guru in Kerala represented a more spiritually framed route to challenge hierarchy; in Madras the Justice Party represented non-Brahman politics. (✅ owner 10 §2, §8.8; ⚠️ Narayana Guru's date not in owner)
**Breakdown:**
- Anti-caste reform beyond upper-caste social reform (✅ owner 10 §1).
- Justice Party lost the 1926 elections to the Swarajists; non-Brahmans returned to Congress; by 1946 it did not field a candidate (✅ owner 10 §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1926 election; 1946 non-candidacy (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2034, MOD-2047.
**Trap:** Do not present the Justice Party as the origin of the Self-Respect movement (MOD-2047).
**Mains hook:** Anti-caste correction to the renaissance narrative (✅ owner 10 adv §1).
**Back:** *"Narayana Guru's spiritual route; Justice Party's electoral one."*

### Flashcard MOD-2047 — Periyar and the Self-Respect movement
**Front:** Who founded the Self-Respect movement, when did he leave the Congress, and what was its core argument?
**Definition (learn):** E.V. Ramaswamy Naicker ("Periyar") built the Self-Respect movement in south India, arguing that before self-rule what was needed was self-respect, and identifying multiple structures of subjection for Shudras, Adi-Dravidas and women. (✅ owner 10 §8.8)
**Breakdown:**
- Left the Congress in 1925; 1927 Madras tour trigger (owner).
- Critique of Aryanism, Brahmanism and Hinduism.
- Ramayana inversion (Ravana as ideal Dravidian; Rama as evil Aryan).
- Limit: reservations about privileging Tamil (⚠️ owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1925; 1927 (✅ owner 10). 2025 Prelims Q78 asks the founder (✅ owner 10 PYQ integration).
**Links:** MOD-2046, MOD-2048 (Mains tension).
**Trap:** The 1927 Madras episode is a trigger, not the founding date (✅ owner 10 §8.10).
**Mains hook:** Outlived the Justice Party because more inclusive in appeal (✅ owner 10 §8.8).
**Back:** *"1925 out of Congress; self-respect before self-rule."*

### Flashcard MOD-2048 — Parsi and Sikh reform: Rahnumai Mazdayasnan Sabha and Singh Sabha
**Front:** Which bodies represent Parsi and Sikh reform, and what did they emphasise?
**Definition (learn):** Parsi reform through the Rahnumai Mazdayasnan Sabha and Sikh reform through the Singh Sabha; both used association, education and law for religious purification and women's legal status. (✅ owner 10 §2, §8.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Association, education, law.
- Religious purification; women's legal status.
- Political afterlife: community modernisation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond names in owner (⚠️ dates not in owner; not added).
**Links:** MOD-2026, MOD-2038.
**Trap:** Do not treat Parsi and Sikh reform as Hindu or Muslim reform.
**Mains hook:** Shows community-specific reform for the "community-specific" demand family.
**Back:** *"Parsi and Sikh reform: community modernisers, law and school."*

### Flashcard MOD-2049 — Trap: revival is not automatically backward
**Front:** Why is it a trap to call revivalists backward and reformists progressive?
**Definition (learn):** Both streams could contain modern and conservative elements. Arya Samaj's revivalist idiom used modern schools, print, associations and public mobilisation (✅ owner 10 adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Revivalist with modern organisation.
- Reformist with selective reinterpretation.
- Analytical use: source of authority, not progress.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Arya 1875 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2026, MOD-2031.
**Trap:** Do not claim Indian reform was a direct copy of European Enlightenment; it was selective synthesis (✅ owner 10 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The 2021 PYQ on Young Bengal and Brahmo Samaj is answerable with this comparison.
**Back:** *"Revival can modernise; reform can conserve."*

### Flashcard MOD-2050 — Backlash 1891 and the nationalist language of non-interference
**Front:** How did orthodoxy turn reform into a national question by 1891?
**Definition (learn):** Each reform advance produced orthodox counter-mobilisation with a wider mass base than the reformers; by 1891 orthodoxy spoke the nationalist language that foreign rulers should not interfere in custom (✅ owner 10 §8.7, §8.9).
**Breakdown:**
- Age of Consent Act (1891): orthodox backlash.
- Ironic detail: the same orthodoxy used the British legal system in the Rukhmabai case and accepted government legislation on cow slaughter (✅ owner 10).
- Interpretation: Bandyopadhyay's "last sovereign space" framing (⚠️ present as interpretation).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1891 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** MOD-2042, MOD-2043, MOD-2016 (press), MOD-2050 (backlash and religion pledge).
**Trap:** Do not present the backlash as wholly anti-British; it was partly anti-reform and partly anti-interference.
**Mains hook:** "The orthodox discovered that opposing reform could be presented as opposing foreign rule" (✅ owner 10 §8.9).
**Back:** *"1891: opposing reform became opposing the Raj."*

### GROUP — The Revolt of 1857

### Flashcard MOD-2051 — 1857 as a composite revolt (mutiny, popular revolt, not a national war)
**Front:** What is the accurate composite characterisation of the Revolt of 1857?
**Definition (learn):** 1857 began as a military mutiny, acquired the character of a popular revolt in Awadh and the upper Doab where agrarian dispossession had occurred, and remained restricted by region, class and political imagination. (✅ owner 11 §8.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Military origin: Bengal army, Meerut 10 May 1857.
- Popular depth: Awadh and upper Doab.
- Limits: regional; no common programme; not fully nationalist.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Meerut 10 May 1857; Delhi symbolic centre; recapture September 1857 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** MOD-2052, MOD-2063, MOD-2064, MOD-2067.
**Trap:** Do not choose one label; the answer must assess dimension by dimension (✅ owner 11 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** Composite verdict is the strongest evidence-backed answer (✅ owner 11 §8.11).
**Back:** *"Mutiny in origin, revolt in Awadh, war nowhere."*

### Flashcard MOD-2052 — Barrackpore (29 March 1857) versus Meerut (10 May 1857)
**Front:** What happened on 29 March 1857 and on 10 May 1857, and which did the revolt begin with?
**Definition (learn):** Mangal Pandey's incident at Barrackpore on 29 March 1857, and the sepoy outbreak at Meerut on 10 May 1857 that spread to Delhi. The revolt began at Meerut, not Delhi. (✅ owner 11 §1, §8.12; ✅ Wikipedia: Indian Rebellion of 1857)
**Breakdown:**
- Barrackpore: Mangal Pandey, 34th BNI (✅ Wikipedia).
- Meerut: outbreak; then move to Delhi.
- Delhi: Bahadur Shah Zafar proclaimed (May 1857).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 29 March 1857; 10 May 1857 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2053, MOD-2059.
**Trap:** Do not begin the revolt at Delhi (✅ owner 11 §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the Barrackpore–Meerut sequence to show the military origin.
**Back:** *"Barrackpore March, Meerut May, Delhi next."*

### Flashcard MOD-2053 — Enfield cartridge: immediate trigger, not cause
**Front:** What was the Enfield cartridge issue and why is it only the trigger?
**Definition (learn):** The rumour that Enfield rifle cartridges were greased with cow and pig fat was the immediate trigger of 1857, not its full cause. (✅ owner 11 §1; ✅ Wikipedia: Indian Rebellion of 1857, with the note that little direct evidence shows the cartridges were issued before the revolt)
**Breakdown:**
- Weapon: Pattern 1853 Enfield (✅ Wikipedia).
- Rumour: cow and pig fat.
- Evidence caution: Kim Wagner's point (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pattern 1853 Enfield; 1857 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2054, MOD-2051, MOD-2052.
**Trap:** "The cartridge was the cause" is the classic error (✅ owner 11 §5, §8.12).
**Mains hook:** Distinguish long-term grievances from the immediate trigger.
**Back:** *"Cartridge lit the fuse; grievance was the powder."*

### Flashcard MOD-2054 — General Service Enlistment Act (1856)
**Front:** What did the General Service Enlistment Act of 1856 do, and why did it matter for sepoys?
**Definition (learn):** An 1856 Act (dated 25 July 1856, signed by Canning) that applied to new Bengal Army recruits and raised fear among high-caste sepoys that its obligations would be extended to serving men (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Rebellion of 1857; ✅ owner 11 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Service: liability for overseas service (owner).
- Caste fear: sepoys' fear of losing caste.
- Military grievance: pay, status, service conditions (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 July 1856 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2053, MOD-2055.
**Trap:** The Act applied to new recruits; the fear was about extension to serving men. Do not say it applied to all serving sepoys.
**Mains hook:** Military grievances were one strand among several.
**Back:** *"1856 new recruits, serving sepoys feared."*

### Flashcard MOD-2055 — Awadh annexation (1856) and the taluqdars
**Front:** When was Awadh annexed, on what grounds, and why did that matter in 1857?
**Definition (learn):** Awadh was annexed in 1856 on the ground of alleged misgovernance, not by the Doctrine of Lapse. The annexation hurt taluqdars, court dependants, soldiers and peasants, and many Bengal Army sepoys came from the region. (✅ Wikipedia: 1856 annexation on alleged misgovernment; ✅ owner 11 §8.5, adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Political: annexation and loss of court.
- Social: taluqdari authority.
- Military: Bengal Army recruitment from the region.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1856 (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 11).
**Links:** MOD-2056, MOD-2061, MOD-2064.
**Trap:** Awadh 1856 is not a Doctrine of Lapse case (✅ owner 11 §8.12).
**Mains hook:** Why Awadh became the main theatre (✅ owner 11 adv §7).
**Back:** *"1856 Awadh: misgovernment, not lapse."*

### Flashcard MOD-2056 — Doctrine of Lapse and dishonoured dynasties
**Front:** How did the Doctrine of Lapse and the treatment of Mughal and Peshwa lines feed the political causes of 1857?
**Definition (learn):** The Doctrine of Lapse annexed states where an heir was denied adoption; the disregard for Mughal and Peshwa dignity fed the political grievances of the dispossessed (✅ owner 11 §1, §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- Lapse annexations: dispossessed rulers (owner §8.4 table).
- Mughal and Peshwa lines: dignity and symbolic legitimacy.
- Rebel idiom: restoration of old rulers.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Doctrine of Lapse (owner; the specific states are not listed here).
**Links:** MOD-2055, MOD-2059, MOD-2052.
