**Trap:** The Doctrine of Lapse was not the reason for Awadh's annexation in 1856 (MOD-2055).
**Mains hook:** The political causes show why rulers joined the revolt.
**Back:** *"Lapse took thrones, dignity fed the revolt."*

### Flashcard MOD-2057 — Economic causes: artisans, peasants and the 110,000 acres (1853)
**Front:** What economic pressures drove peasants and artisans, and what index is cited for land sales?
**Definition (learn):** Over-assessment, debt and land auction radicalised the countryside; artisans lost craft patronage; moneylenders became targets (✅ owner 11 §8.4, §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Peasants: over-assessment on owner-cultivators; debt; land auctions.
- Artisans: decline linked to deindustrialisation (MOD-2068 links to basic/07).
- Targets: revenue records, courts and moneylenders' bonds.
- Index: 110,000 acres sold at auction in the North-Western Provinces in 1853 (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 110,000 acres; NWP; 1853 (✅ owner 11). ⚠️ The 110,000-acre figure was **not found** on the fetched Revolt of 1857 page (Wikipedia, partial read); it rests on the owner alone and is province- and year-specific.
**Links:** MOD-2058, MOD-2061, MOD-2062.
**Trap:** Do not generalise the figure to all India or the whole decade (✅ owner 11 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Land alienation, not revenue rate alone, radicalised the countryside (✅ owner 11 §8.6).
**Back:** *"1853 NWP: 110,000 acres; debt turned rebels."*

### Flashcard MOD-2058 — Pre-1857 risings: Sanyasi, Chuar, Kol and Santhal Hool (1855–56)
**Front:** Which pre-1857 revolts are listed and why must the Santhal Hool be kept separate from 1857?
**Definition (learn):** Sanyasi and Chuar rebellions, southern poligar revolts, and Kol and Santhal tribal uprisings arose from dispossession, revenue pressure, outsider penetration and attacks on customary authority. The Santhal Hool (1855–56) under Sidhu and Kanhu was a distinct anti-diku tribal uprising, not a stage of the sepoy revolt (✅ owner 11 §1, §8.12, adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Local, recurrent, not one coordinated movement.
- Santhal Hool: 1855–56, Sidhu and Kanhu.
- Shows that the "passive acceptance" view is wrong without creating a single conspiracy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Santhal Hool 1855–56 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** MOD-2067 (nature debate), MOD-2069 (2019 PYQ).
**Trap:** Do not count the Santhal Hool as part of the 1857 sepoy revolt (✅ owner 11 §8.12).
**Mains hook:** The 2019 GS-I question on 1857 as culmination of recurrent rebellions (MOD-2069).
**Back:** *"Santhal Hool 1855, a separate hool; Sanyasi and Chuar before."*

### Flashcard MOD-2059 — Bahadur Shah Zafar: symbolic emperor, Bakht Khan in command
**Front:** Who was proclaimed emperor at Delhi in 1857, and who directed the rebel command there?
**Definition (learn):** Bahadur Shah Zafar was proclaimed symbolic emperor at Delhi in May 1857; Bakht Khan held rebel command. Zafar did not direct operations. (✅ owner 11 §4, §8.7, §8.12; ✅ Wikipedia: Bakht Khan as commander, to the extent the fetched page covered it)
**Breakdown:**
- Symbol: Mughal legitimacy.
- Operations: Bakht Khan.
- Recapture: British retook Delhi in September 1857 (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** May 1857; September 1857 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** MOD-2052, MOD-2056, MOD-2068.
**Trap:** Do not present Zafar as directing operations (✅ owner 11 §8.12).
**Mains hook:** Restorationist idiom limited the political programme (MOD-2066).
**Back:** *"Zafar the symbol, Bakht Khan the command."*

### Flashcard MOD-2060 — Kanpur: Nana Saheb, Tantia Tope and Azimullah Khan
**Front:** Who were the principal Kanpur leaders in 1857?
**Definition (learn):** Nana Saheb, Tantia Tope and Azimullah Khan were the leaders associated with Kanpur (Cawnpore). (✅ owner 11 §4, §8.7; ✅ Wikipedia: Nana Sahib and Tatya Tope named in the commander list; ⚠️ Azimullah not found on the fetched page)
**Breakdown:**
- Nana Saheb: civilian disquiet and claims rejected (✅ Wikipedia).
- Tantia Tope: commander list; executed (✅ Wikipedia). Owner calls him Tantia Tope.
- Azimullah Khan: owner-only; not confirmed on fetched page (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Kanpur 1857 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** MOD-2059, MOD-2062.
**Trap:** Tantia Tope and Tatya Tope are the same person; the spelling varies.
**Mains hook:** Use the centre-wise leader list to show geographic spread.
**Back:** *"Kanpur: Nana, Tantia, Azimullah."*

### Flashcard MOD-2061 — Begum Hazrat Mahal in Awadh
**Front:** Who was the leading figure in Awadh in 1857 among the rebel leaders named?
**Definition (learn):** Begum Hazrat Mahal led the revolt in Awadh (owner); she appears in the Wikipedia commander list (✅ Wikipedia), though the Lucknow section was not read.
**Breakdown:**
- Region: Awadh (taluqdars, soldiers, peasants).
- Link to the taluqdar question (MOD-2064).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1857 Awadh (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** MOD-2055, MOD-2064.
**Trap:** Do not treat Hazrat Mahal as a Delhi figure.
**Mains hook:** Awadh's popular depth.
**Back:** *"Awadh: Begum Hazrat Mahal."*

### Flashcard MOD-2062 — Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi
**Front:** Who led the revolt at Jhansi, and why did she resist?
**Definition (learn):** Rani Lakshmibai led resistance at Jhansi; she resisted British authority after her adopted son was denied recognition as successor (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Rebellion of 1857, listed as commander, killed in action).
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: denial of adoptive succession.
- Death in action (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jhansi 1857 (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2056 (Lapse).
**Trap:** The Lapse-style denial is a political cause; do not treat Jhansi as a Doctrine-of-Lapse annexation in this owner.
**Mains hook:** Use Jhansi to illustrate the political cause of disputed succession.
**Back:** *"Jhansi: succession denied, the Rani fought."*

### Flashcard MOD-2063 — Kunwar Singh (Bihar) and Khan Bahadur Khan (Bareilly)
**Front:** Which two leaders are linked to Bihar and to Bareilly in 1857?
**Definition (learn):** Kunwar Singh led in Bihar (Arrah); Khan Bahadur Khan is associated with Bareilly (owner). Kunwar Singh is confirmed as a commander on Wikipedia; Khan Bahadur Khan is not confirmed on the fetched page (⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Bihar: Kunwar Singh; Arrah.
- Bareilly: Khan Bahadur Khan; Rohilkhand.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1857 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** MOD-2051, MOD-2068.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Bareilly's leadership with Delhi or Awadh.
**Mains hook:** The geography of participation: Delhi, upper Doab, Awadh, Rohilkhand, Bundelkhand, parts of Bihar (✅ owner 11 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Bihar: Kunwar Singh. Bareilly: Khan Bahadur Khan (unverified)."*

### Flashcard MOD-2064 — The Awadh taluqdar question (2026 Prelims Q17)
**Front:** What revenue policy towards Awadh taluqdars after annexation links 1857 to later conciliation?
**Definition (learn):** After the 1856 annexation the summary settlement reduced taluqdari authority so that taluqdars were, in law, no different from their tenants. When the revolt began they returned to their villages without resistance; after the revolt they were restored to positions of authority. (✅ owner 11 §8.5; ⚠️ Metcalf's kinship argument)
**Breakdown:**
- Before: loss of status.
- During: return with little resistance; ⚠️ Metcalf: kinship and feudal loyalty.
- After: restored to authority (owner; do not specify numbers of estates).
- Complementarity of interest: regional, not all-India.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1856 annexation; post-revolt restoration (✅ owner 11). The 2026 Prelims key is provisional; no answer letter recorded.
**Links:** MOD-2055, MOD-2061, MOD-2068 (post-1857 alliance).
**Trap:** Do not claim a general landlord–peasant alliance across India (✅ owner 11 §8.5).
**Mains hook:** This chain answers a cause, a regional social depth and the post-1858 conciliation logic.
**Back:** *"Annexed, returned, restored: taluqdar complementarity."*

### Flashcard MOD-2065 — Why the revolt did not spread: four variables
**Front:** What four variables explain why southern and western India largely stayed out of the revolt?
**Definition (learn):** Army composition, land settlement, annexation history and the age of colonial contact. The revolt was a Bengal-army event where recently destroyed landed authority existed; the Madras and Bombay presidencies had different armies, zamindars and mirasidars whose power was buttressed, older colonial contact, and no recently displaced ruling houses (✅ owner 11 §8.7; ⚠️ the four-variable explanation is the owner's analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- Army: Madras and Bombay armies did not share the Bengal sepoy's grievances.
- Settlement: zamindars in Bengal and mirasidars in Madras were buttressed by the Cornwallis and Munro systems (✅ owner 11, citing Bandyopadhyay).
- Annexation: concentrated in the north and centre.
- Contact: older commercial and educated classes in Bombay and Madras.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Bengal army; Cornwallis and Munro systems (owner).
**Links:** MOD-2064, MOD-2051.
**Trap:** Non-participation was not always loyalty; caution and rivalry also operated (✅ owner 11 §8.7 limit).
**Mains hook:** The map of the revolt tracks the map of recent dispossession, not "India" (✅ owner 11 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Army, land, annexation, contact: the four walls of silence."*

### Flashcard MOD-2066 — Why the revolt failed: rebel deficits and British advantages
**Front:** What were the rebel-side deficits and the British-side advantages that explain failure?
**Definition (learn):** Rebels lacked unified command and a common programme beyond expulsion, were restoration-oriented, regionally bounded, and resource-poor. The British had interior lines, railway and telegraph communication, loyal Indian troops (including Punjab's manpower), and support or neutrality of most princes and commercial classes (✅ owner 11 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Rebel side: no unified command; restoration politics; regional bounds; resources and armament inferiority.
- British side: communications; loyal troops; allies; global resources and naval reinforcement.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Railway and telegraph (owner).
**Links:** MOD-2059, MOD-2065, MOD-2067.
**Trap:** British victory was not only bravery; disunity and resources mattered (✅ owner 11 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Structural asymmetry: the empire moved faster than the rebels could coordinate (✅ owner 11 §8.11).
**Back:** *"Rebels agreed what to destroy, not what to build."*

### Flashcard MOD-2067 — Interpretations: Seeley, Savarkar, R.C. Majumdar, S.B. Chaudhuri, Eric Stokes
**Front:** Which five interpretations of 1857 must an answer name, and what is each's claim?
**Definition (learn):** Official British and Seeley: a sepoy mutiny; Savarkar: First War of Independence; R.C. Majumdar: not a national war (regional and feudal-restorative); S.B. Chaudhuri: civil rebellion; Eric Stokes and the subaltern/local-society lens: peasant and local society shaped it (✅ owner 11 adv §2, §8.10; ⚠️ attributions as owner's).
**Breakdown:**
- Seeley/official: military indiscipline; denies political content.
- Savarkar: patriotic unity (⚠️ overstates modern consciousness).
- Majumdar: sceptical (⚠️ understates anti-colonial content).
- Chaudhuri: civil rebellion (needs region evidence).
- Stokes: local structures.
- Bipin Chandra synthesis: sepoy revolt plus popular discontent (✅ owner 11 adv).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2051, MOD-2068.
**Trap:** Do not attribute "first war of independence" to a specific author unless certain; describe it as a later nationalist label (✅ owner 11 §8.10).
**Mains hook:** "Conclude that 1857 was a major anti-colonial uprising with popular elements, but not yet modern mass nationalism" (✅ owner 11 adv).
**Back:** *"Mutiny, war, not-national, civil, local: name the five."*

### Flashcard MOD-2068 — Consequences of 1857: constitutional, military, social and ideological
**Front:** What changed after 1857 in government, army, princes and racial policy?
**Definition (learn):** Company rule ended and the Crown took over under the Government of India Act 1858; the army was recomposed with a higher European ratio, artillery kept in European hands and recruitment reorganised along caste/region/religion lines; princes and landed magnates became collaborators; and racial distance deepened with a doctrine of non-interference in religion (✅ owner 11 §8.9).
**Breakdown:**
- Constitutional: 1858 Act (MOD-2073).
- Military: army recomposition (MOD-2088).
- Social alliance: princes and taluqdars (MOD-2087).
- Ideological: racial distance; non-interference doctrine (⚠️ owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1858 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** MOD-2073, MOD-2087, MOD-2088.
**Trap:** The Revolt ended Company rule but did not give Indians representation.
**Mains hook:** "The revolt failed and still changed everything" (✅ owner 11 §8.11).
**Back:** *"Company ends, army remade, princes allied, race hardened."*

### Flashcard MOD-2069 — 1857 timeline and the 2019 "culmination" PYQ
**Front:** What is the 1857 chronology and what does the 2019 GS-I question on 1857 ask?
**Definition (learn):** Key dates: 29 March 1857 (Barrackpore); 10 May 1857 (Meerut); May 1857 (Zafar proclaimed); September 1857 (Delhi recaptured); 1857–58 centres (Delhi, Kanpur, Lucknow, Jhansi, Bareilly, Bihar/Arrah); 1858 suppression and Government of India Act (✅ owner 11 mini-timeline).
**Breakdown:**
- 2019 GS-I Q2 (10 marks; 150 words): 1857 as culmination of earlier recurrent rebellions (✅ owner 11 PYQ integration).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** MOD-2052, MOD-2058, MOD-2066.
**Trap:** Earlier rebellions were local and isolated, not one continuous national conspiracy (✅ owner 11 adv §1).
**Mains hook:** Earlier revolts show resistance; 1857 was its largest culmination.
**Back:** *"29 March, 10 May, September 1857, 1858."*

### Flashcard MOD-2070 — Label caution: "First War of Independence" and "sepoy mutiny"
**Front:** How should the labels "First War of Independence" and "sepoy mutiny" be used in an answer?
**Definition (learn):** "Sepoy mutiny" is the official framing whose purpose was to deny political content; "First War of Independence" is a later nationalist characterisation that overstates unity and programme (✅ owner 11 §8.10).
**Breakdown:**
- Use "sepoy revolt with wide civil rebellion in several regions" for balanced answers (✅ owner 11 adv §4).
- Do not attribute the phrase to a specific author unless certain.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2051, MOD-2067.
**Trap:** Do not give casualty figures or the number of rebel-held districts (✅ owner 11 §8.12).
**Mains hook:** A balanced historiographical position is rewarded (✅ owner 11 adv §5).
**Back:** *"Label with care: mutiny, war, or revolt, qualified."*

### Flashcard MOD-2071 — 1857 Mains verdict and the composite thesis
**Front:** What is the graded verdict for a 20-mark question on the causes, character and failure of 1857?
**Definition (learn):** A composite verdict: 1857 was a mutiny in origin, a popular revolt in Awadh and the Doab, and a national war nowhere; the accurate answer is the composite (✅ owner 11 §8.11).
**Breakdown:**
- Structure for 20 marks: thesis → causes → geography of participation and non-participation → leadership → failure → consequences → historiographical caution → verdict (✅ owner 11 §8.3).
- Evidence load: 6–8 units across military, agrarian, princely and regional evidence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As in MOD-2069.
**Links:** MOD-2051, MOD-2065, MOD-2066, MOD-2068.
**Trap:** Do not present causes as a single list; separate the groups (✅ owner 11 §8.4).
**Mains hook:** "The rebels were defeated by an empire that could move faster than they could co-ordinate."
**Back:** *"Mutiny, revolt, not-a-nation: all three, qualified."*

### GROUP — Administrative & Constitutional Changes after 1858

### Flashcard MOD-2072 — 1858: change of sovereign, not of the machinery
**Front:** What changed in 1858, and what stayed the same?
**Definition (learn):** Sovereignty passed from the East India Company to the Crown, while the machinery of government (collector, police, code, revenue demand) largely stayed in place (✅ owner 12 §1, §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Changed: Company and Board of Control replaced; Crown rule; Secretary of State.
- Unchanged: revenue, order, racial hierarchy (owner §8.2).
- Concession: departmental government and provincial finance (owner §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1858 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MOD-2073, MOD-2091.
**Trap:** Do not describe 1858 as introducing elected legislatures (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** Continuity thesis: sovereignty changed hands without changing the purposes of government (✅ owner 12 §8.2).
**Back:** *"1858 changed the sovereign's address, not the collector's."*

### Flashcard MOD-2073 — Government of India Act 1858
**Front:** When did the Government of India Act 1858 receive assent and come into force, and what did it end?
**Definition (learn):** The Act ended the East India Company's rule over India and transferred its functions, territories, property, treaties and contracts to the Crown; India was to be governed in the Queen's name (✅ Wikipedia: Government of India Act 1858).
**Breakdown:**
- Royal assent: 2 August 1858 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Commencement: 1 November 1858, per section 74 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Created a Secretary of State for India as channel for communications; assisted by an advisory council of fifteen (✅ Wikipedia).
- An Indian Civil Service was to be created under the Secretary of State's control (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2 August 1858 (assent); 1 November 1858 (commencement); fifteen-member council (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2074, MOD-2075, MOD-2076.
**Trap:** ⚠️ The Wikipedia page does not say the Board of Control was abolished in terms; the owner states it. Do not cite a section number for the abolition. The Governor-General's change to "Viceroy" is not stated on the page (owner only).
**Mains hook:** The Act moved control from a corporation to a Cabinet minister answerable to Parliament (✅ owner 12 §8.4 A).
**Back:** *"2 August 1858 assent, 1 November in force."*

### Flashcard MOD-2074 — Secretary of State for India and the Council of fifteen
**Front:** Where did the Secretary of State for India sit, and how large was his advisory council?
**Definition (learn):** The Secretary of State for India sat in Britain as a Cabinet-level minister answerable to Parliament, assisted by a Council of India of fifteen members (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 12 §8.4 A).
**Breakdown:**
- Location: London.
- Council: fifteen members (✅ Wikipedia).
- Secret despatches: could be sent without consulting the council (✅ Wikipedia).
- First holder: Wikipedia names Edward Stanley, 15th Earl of Derby, as the first holder, described as vague in the article (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fifteen members (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2073, MOD-2075, MOD-2086.
**Trap:** Secretary of State is not the Viceroy (MOD-2089).
**Mains hook:** Indian political demand thereafter targeted London as well as Calcutta (✅ owner 12 §8.4 A).
**Back:** *"Secretary in London, fifteen advisers."*

### Flashcard MOD-2075 — Viceroy and Lord Canning
**Front:** Who was the first Viceroy, and what was his role?
**Definition (learn):** The Governor-General of India also became Viceroy, representing the Crown in India; Lord Canning was the first Viceroy (✅ owner 12 §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Viceroy: Crown representative in India.
- Canning: Governor-General and first Viceroy; the portfolio system (MOD-2078) and the 1861 Act (MOD-2079).
- ⚠️ The Wikipedia text fetched for the 1858 Act does not state the Viceroy title change; rely on the owner for this.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Canning (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MOD-2074, MOD-2078.
**Trap:** Owner adv §4 calls Canning the "last Governor-General under Company transition and first Viceroy under Crown rule"; use the same wording (✅ owner 12 adv §4).
**Mains hook:** Viceroy governed in India; Secretary of State controlled from Britain.
**Back:** *"Canning: first Viceroy, governing in India."*

### Flashcard MOD-2076 — Queen's Proclamation (1858)
**Front:** What did the Queen's Proclamation of 1858 promise?
**Definition (learn):** The Proclamation promised non-interference in religion, respect for treaties with princes, and equality in public employment (✅ owner 12 §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Religion: non-interference.
- Princes: treaty respect.
- Services: equality (largely unfulfilled).
- Effect: a conciliatory charter after 1857 (adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1858 (✅ owner 12). Owner adv gives "1 November Proclamation" in §8.10 mechanism note; the date of the Proclamation is named as 1 November in the owner (⚠️ verify before exam use).
**Links:** MOD-2077, MOD-2087, MOD-2044 (religion pledge link).
**Trap:** The 1858 Act and the 1 November Proclamation are distinct (✅ owner 12 §Semantic-completeness).
**Mains hook:** Charter of grievance: nationalists measured British practice against the promise (✅ owner 12 §8.2).
**Back:** *"1858 promise: religion, princes, services."*

### Flashcard MOD-2077 — The equality promise that was not kept
**Front:** Which promise in the Queen's Proclamation was not implemented, and what was its later use?
**Definition (learn):** Equality in public employment remained largely unfulfilled because of racial barriers and examination obstacles; nationalists used the gap in the campaign for Indianisation and in the Ilbert Bill controversy (✅ owner 12 §8.4 B, adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Promise: equality in public employment.
- Practice: racial barriers; European dominance of the higher bureaucracy (⚠️).
- Religion: honoured more consistently, slowing later social legislation (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2076, MOD-2090.
**Trap:** Do not present the Proclamation's equality as fully implemented (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Charter of grievance" thesis and the "promise versus practice" directive (✅ owner 12 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Equality promised, racial barriers kept."*

### Flashcard MOD-2078 — Indian Councils Act 1861: portfolio system and nominated non-officials
**Front:** What did the Indian Councils Act of 1861 introduce?
**Definition (learn):** The Act restored legislative councils, allowed nominated non-official Indian members, and formalised the portfolio system under Canning; it began legislative devolution to the provinces (✅ owner 12 §1, §8.4 C; ✅ Wikipedia: Indian Councils Act 1861).
**Breakdown:**
- Royal assent: 1 August 1861 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Portfolio system: each ordinary member headed a department (home, revenue, military, law, finance; public works after 1874) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Commander-in-Chief an extraordinary member (✅ Wikipedia).
- Nominated, not elected; no provision for elections (✅ Wikipedia).
- Advisory only; no budget power (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 August 1861 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2079, MOD-2082, MOD-2083.
**Trap:** 1861 allowed nomination, not direct election (✅ owner 12 §5); the 1892 Act added indirect principles (MOD-2082).
**Mains hook:** "Consultation without power" (MOD-2085).
**Back:** *"1861: portfolios, nominees, no votes."*

### Flashcard MOD-2079 — 1861 Act: 1862 nominees, provincial councils and ordinance powers
**Front:** Who were the first non-official nominees in 1862, which provincial councils did the 1861 Act provide, and what ordinance power did the Viceroy hold?
**Definition (learn):** In 1862 Canning nominated the Raja of Benaras, the Maharaja of Patiala and Sir Dinkar Rao; the Act provided councils for Bengal (1862), North-Western Provinces (1866) and Punjab (1897), and restored legislative powers to Bombay and Madras (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Councils Act 1861).
**Breakdown:**
- Nominees: upper-class zamindars, princes and nobles (✅ Wikipedia).
- Bombay and Madras: legislative powers restored (Charter Act 1833 had removed them, per the page).
- Ordinance: in emergency, Viceroy could issue ordinances lasting up to six months (✅ Wikipedia).
- The Calcutta council could legislate for all British India (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1862 (Bengal and nominations); 1866 (NWP); 1897 (Punjab) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2078, MOD-2082.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1861 council with the 1909 Imperial Council (MOD-2084) or the 1892 Act's expansion.
**Mains hook:** Nomination was a channel that heard loyal Indian opinion while controlling it (✅ owner 12 §8.4 C).
**Back:** *"1862 nominees; 1866 NWP; 1897 Punjab; six-month ordinance."*

### Flashcard MOD-2080 — Lord Mayo's financial decentralisation (1870)
**Front:** What did Lord Mayo's 1870 financial decentralisation do?
**Definition (learn):** From 1870 Mayo gave provinces limited financial responsibility for some heads of revenue and expenditure, a fiscal expedient with a political function (✅ owner 12 §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Provinces: limited financial responsibility.
- Purpose: administrative convenience and cost devolution (owner §8.5).
- Not federalism (owner adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1870 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MOD-2081, MOD-2086.
**Trap:** Decentralisation was not federalism; it was administrative and financial convenience under imperial control (✅ owner 12 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Decentralisation of costs with control retained (✅ owner 12 §8.5).
**Back:** *"1870 Mayo: provinces pay, Delhi decides."*

### Flashcard MOD-2081 — Ripon's Resolution (1882): the "Magna Carta of local self-government"
**Front:** What did Lord Ripon's 1882 Resolution do, and what label is attached to it?
**Definition (learn):** Ripon's Resolution of 1882 encouraged municipal and district boards and is conventionally called the "Magna Carta of local self-government" (✅ owner 12 §1, §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Local bodies: municipal and district boards.
- Political training: candidature, canvassing, budgets, factional organisation (owner).
- Limits: financially starved; district officers often chairmen; official supervision (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1882 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MOD-2080, MOD-2086, MOD-2014 (press, Ripon period).
**Trap:** Ripon's bodies were not fully autonomous (✅ owner 12 §5). ⚠️ The "Magna Carta" label is conventional, not an official description (owner §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Political training without self-rule (✅ owner 12 adv §7).
**Back:** *"1882 Ripon: boards taught the trade, not the power."*

### Flashcard MOD-2082 — Indian Councils Act 1892: enlarged councils and indirect election
**Front:** What did the Indian Councils Act of 1892 change?
**Definition (learn):** The 1892 Act enlarged the legislative councils, let universities, district boards, municipalities, zamindars and chambers of commerce recommend members, and allowed discussion of the annual financial statement and questions (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Councils Act 1892; ✅ owner 12 §1, §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Royal assent: 20 June 1892 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Central council additional members rose from twelve to sixteen (✅ Wikipedia; ⚠️ the page's own range of "10 to 16" is flagged citation-needed).
- Provincial figures per the page: Bombay 8, Madras 20, Bengal 20, NWP and Oudh 15 (⚠️ flagged citation-needed).
- No direct elections; recommendations by bodies (✅ Wikipedia).
- Replaced by 1909 Morley–Minto (MOD-2084).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 20 June 1892 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2083, MOD-2084, MOD-2123, MOD-2125.
**Trap:** Indirect election principle in practice; not direct election; not responsible government (✅ owner 12 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Consultation becomes partial choice" (✅ owner 12 §8.7).
**Back:** *"1892: recommended, not elected; discussed, not voted."*

### Flashcard MOD-2083 — 1892 budget discussion: discussion without a vote
**Front:** What exactly could members do with the budget under the 1892 Act, and what could they not do?
**Definition (learn):** Councils could discuss the annual financial statement but could not vote on it or move amendments (✅ owner 12 §8.4 D; ✅ Wikipedia: budget discussion without vote).
**Breakdown:**
- Allowed: discussion; questions (six days' notice; no supplementary questions per the page, ✅ Wikipedia).
- Not allowed: vote; amendments (owner; the fetched page did not confirm the amendment rule, ⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Six days' notice for questions (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2082, MOD-2125.
**Trap:** Do not claim 1892 gave budget control or responsible government (✅ owner 12 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Discussion of the budget was the procedural concession that made the argument shift toward control (✅ owner 12 §8.4 D).
**Back:** *"1892: talk the budget, never vote it."*

### Flashcard MOD-2084 — Indian Councils Act 1909 (Morley–Minto): separate electorates
**Front:** When did the Indian Councils Act 1909 receive assent, and what did it introduce for Muslims?
**Definition (learn):** The Morley–Minto Act of 1909 gave Muslims separate electorates, with Muslim voters electing Muslim representatives, and enlarged the councils (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Councils Act 1909; ✅ owner 12 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Royal assent: 25 May 1909; commencement 15 November 1909 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Viceroy: Lord Minto. Secretary of State: John Morley (✅ Wikipedia).
- Satyendra P. Sinha: first Indian on the Viceroy's Executive Council (✅ Wikipedia).
- Elected Indians could table resolutions, debate the budget, ask supplementary questions; foreign policy and princely relations excluded; executive veto kept (✅ Wikipedia).
- Simla Deputation (October 1906) was the Muslim demand's origin (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 May 1909 assent; separate electorates (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2082, MOD-2146 (Morley–Minto in the Swadeshi group), MOD-2180, MOD-2181.
**Trap:** Separate electorates came in 1909, not 1919 (✅ owner 12 §8.8, owner 15).
**Mains hook:** Institutionalisation of communal representation (✅ owner 17 adv §1).
**Back:** *"1909: Muslims elect Muslims; Minto and Morley."*

### Flashcard MOD-2085 — Association, not representation: the 1861–1892 trajectory
**Front:** What is the "association-not-representation" thesis for the Councils Acts of 1861 and 1892?
**Definition (learn):** The Acts created consultation without power: Indians could be nominated, later indirectly recommended, could ask questions and discuss the budget, but could not vote a government out, amend a demand, or hold an executive responsible (✅ owner 12 §8.2).
**Breakdown:**
- 1861: nomination, portfolio system.
- 1892: recommended members; discussion of the budget; questions.
- 1909 (MOD-2084): the next step, not in this card.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1861; 1892 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MOD-2078, MOD-2082, MOD-2083.
**Trap:** Do not project later parliamentary democracy backward (✅ owner 12 adv §Trap).
**Mains hook:** "Between 1861 and 1892 Indians moved from being consulted to being partially chosen" (✅ owner 12 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Consulted, recommended, questioned; never governing."*

### Flashcard MOD-2086 — Local self-government as political training
**Front:** Why are local bodies described as a "school" for Indian politicians rather than self-rule?
**Definition (learn):** Municipal and district boards gave early Indian politicians practice in candidature, canvassing, budgets and factional organisation, but they were financially starved and under official supervision (✅ owner 12 §8.4 E, §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Training: candidature and budgets.
- Limits: official supervision; European-led control.
- Presidency towns had older traditions; variation across provinces (owner §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1882 (MOD-2081).
**Links:** MOD-2081, MOD-2080.
**Trap:** Describe as political training, not self-rule (✅ owner 12 §8.4 E limit).
**Mains hook:** "Ripon's boards mattered less for what they governed than for what they taught" (✅ owner 12 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Boards taught the trade."*

### Flashcard MOD-2087 — Princes and landed magnates: conciliation with paramountcy
**Front:** How did the post-1857 state convert princes and landed magnates into allies, and what did princes lose?
**Definition (learn):** Treaty guarantees under the Proclamation, the end of Lapse-style annexation, and restoration of the Awadh taluqdars made princes and magnates stakeholders; princes gained security and lost autonomy under tighter paramountcy (✅ owner 12 §8.4 F).
**Breakdown:**
- Guarantees: treaties and religion.
- Restoration: Awadh taluqdars (MOD-2064).
- Cost: paramountcy.
- Result: princely India and the landlord order largely outside the national movement for decades (✅ owner 12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1858 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MOD-2064, MOD-2076, MOD-2104 (princely states).
**Trap:** Conciliation did not mean equality of states; paramountcy grew.
**Mains hook:** The states' peoples' question arose late and separately (✅ owner 12 §8.4 F).
**Back:** *"Princes got security, lost autonomy."*

### Flashcard MOD-2088 — Post-1857 army: divide and rule
**Front:** How was the army reorganised after 1857?
**Definition (learn):** The army increased its European proportion, kept artillery mainly with Europeans, and divided recruitment by caste, region and religion, reflecting divide-and-rule fears (✅ owner 12 §1, adv §2, adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- European ratio raised; artillery guarded.
- Recruitment divided on caste/region lines.
- Peel Commission is named in the owner's advanced table (⚠️ not verified here).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** MOD-2068, MOD-2051.
**Trap:** Army reform was not neutral professionalisation; it reflected 1857 fears (✅ owner 12 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The security logic of 1857 (✅ owner 12 §8.5).
**Back:** *"European artillery, divided recruits."*

### Flashcard MOD-2089 — Secretary of State is not the Viceroy
**Front:** Why is it a trap to conflate the Secretary of State and the Viceroy?
**Definition (learn):** The Secretary of State controlled India from London; the Viceroy governed India as Crown representative (✅ owner 12 §Trap, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Secretary of State: London; Council of fifteen (MOD-2074).
- Viceroy: India; Crown representative (MOD-2075).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond above.
**Links:** MOD-2074, MOD-2075.
**Trap:** This is the owner's headline trap; also "1861 nomination is not election".
**Mains hook:** Use the two offices to explain the dual structure of Crown rule.
**Back:** *"London directs, Calcutta governs."*

### Flashcard MOD-2090 — Ilbert Bill and the equality promise
**Front:** What links the Ilbert Bill to the Queen's Proclamation?
**Definition (learn):** The owner ties the Ilbert Bill controversy to the unfulfilled equality clause; Ripon's Resolution was diluted by the same European opinion that defeated the Ilbert Bill (✅ owner 12 §8.4 B, §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Equality clause: nationalist argument.
- Ilbert Bill: racial jurisdiction dispute (owner; year and details not in the owner text; ⚠️ not added).
- Ripon: liberal intent, diluted by officials (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric in owner.
**Links:** MOD-2077, MOD-2081.
**Trap:** Ripon's intent was liberal; the cynical reading is only partial (✅ owner 12 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Shows the limits of liberal intent under imperial pressure.
**Back:** *"Ilbert defeated where Ripon planted."*

### Flashcard MOD-2091 — Charter of grievance: the Proclamation as text, and zero PYQ routes
**Front:** Why did the Queen's Proclamation matter more as text than as policy, and how many direct PYQ routes does this owner hold?
**Definition (learn):** The Proclamation gave nationalists a British commitment to measure practice against (✅ owner 12 §8.2). The owner records zero direct 2018–2026 PYQ routes; all questions are original practice (✅ owner 12 PYQ control).
**Breakdown:**
- Text: religion, treaties, equality.
- Use: nationalist critique of practice.
- Boundary: Topic 06 (Company constitutional ladder), Topic 08 (services), Topic 14 (Moderate councils), Topic 17 (separate electorates).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Zero direct PYQ routes (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MOD-2076, MOD-2077, MOD-2082.
**Trap:** Do not present an original practice question as a past PYQ.
**Mains hook:** "The Queen's Proclamation as both a conciliatory charter and an instrument of imperial consolidation" (✅ owner 12 adv §7).
**Back:** *"A promise on paper, a ledger in practice."*

### GROUP — India and Her Neighbours (Frontier Policy)

### Flashcard MOD-2092 — Frontier policy as a defensive ring with expansionist effect
**Front:** How should British frontier policy in Afghanistan, Burma, Nepal, Tibet and the north-west be characterised?
**Definition (learn):** British India treated neighbours as security extensions of the empire, using buffers, treaties, close-border and forward policies. Defensive in conception, expansionist in effect; each measure to secure an existing border created a new frontier (✅ owner 13 §1, §8.2).
**Breakdown:**
- Theatres: Afghanistan (Great Game), north-west frontier (close vs forward), Burma (annexation), Nepal (buffer), Tibet (mission), Sikkim/Bhutan (protectorate-style).
- Differentiated objectives: buffer where a rival power was present; annexation where none was (owner §8.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** See cards MOD-2093 to MOD-2103.
**Links:** MOD-2093, MOD-2094, MOD-2104, MOD-2107.
**Trap:** Do not treat the frontier as only military history; link to geopolitics, buffers and boundary legacies (✅ owner 13 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** "Each immediate problem was solved by creating the next one" (✅ owner 13 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Defend a ring, enlarge the empire."*

### Flashcard MOD-2093 — The Great Game and Russian fear
**Front:** What was the Great Game, and what was its real content?
**Definition (learn):** The Great Game is a conventional label for Anglo-Russian rivalry in Central Asia and Afghanistan; the British feared Russian advance (✅ owner 13 §1, adv §5). The Russian threat was a perception that shaped policy, and its realism was contested (✅ owner 13 §8.4 A).
**Breakdown:**
- Afghanistan was valued as a buffer.
- Great Game is a historical label, not an official policy name (✅ owner 13 §8.6).
- Not chiefly about Burma (adv §5 trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2092, MOD-2100, MOD-2101.
**Trap:** The Great Game was not mainly about Burma; it concerned Afghanistan and Central Asia (✅ owner 13 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Analyse as the geopolitical background to Afghan policy (✅ owner 13 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Russia's shadow over Kabul."*

### Flashcard MOD-2094 — Close-border versus forward policy; NWFP (1901, Curzon)
**Front:** What were the close-border and forward policies, and what administrative answer came in 1901?
**Definition (learn):** Close-border: avoid costly occupation beyond settled borders, leaving influence to rivals. Forward: advance to pre-empt rival influence, buying influence at the cost of tribal warfare. The North-West Frontier Province was created in 1901 under Curzon (✅ owner 13 §1, §8.4 A).
**Breakdown:**
- Close-border: cheap, but ceded influence.
- Forward: costly, permanent tribal conflict.
- NWFP (1901): administrative exception for tribal frontier management.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1901 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MOD-2092, MOD-2103.
**Trap:** Forward policy did not always mean annexation; it could be influence, missions or control of external relations (✅ owner 13 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The oscillation is the analytical content (✅ owner 13 §8.4 A).
**Back:** *"Close is cheap, forward is costly, 1901 NWFP."*

### Flashcard MOD-2095 — Treaty of Sagauli (1816): Anglo-Nepalese War and Nepal's settlement
**Front:** When was the Treaty of Sagauli signed, what war did it end, and what did Nepal cede?
**Definition (learn):** The Treaty of Sugauli (spelled also Sagauli) ended the Anglo-Nepalese War of 1814–16; it was signed and ratified on 4 March 1816 after a draft initialled on 2 December 1815 (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Sugauli).
**Breakdown:**
- Parties: East India Company and Kingdom of Nepal; signatories Parish Bradshaw and Guru Gajraj Mishra (✅ Wikipedia).
- Cessions: Kumaon and Garhwal hill territories west of the Kali; lowlands and Tarai (✅ Wikipedia).
- Sikkim: Nepal withdrew from conquests (✅ Wikipedia).
- Byans: seven villages British, Changru and Tinkar to Nepal; seeded the later Kalapani dispute (✅ Wikipedia).
- British Residency at Kathmandu (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 March 1816 (✅ Wikipedia); 1814–16 war (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MOD-2096 (Gurkhas), MOD-2092.
**Trap:** Sagauli is Nepal, not Afghanistan or Burma; Yandabo is Burma (MOD-2097).
**Mains hook:** Nepal was a buffer, not an annexation, after 1816 (✅ owner 13 adv §1).
**Back:** *"Sagauli 1816: Nepal ceded hills, took a Resident."*

### Flashcard MOD-2096 — Gurkha recruitment and the Residency in Nepal
**Front:** What did Gurkha recruitment give the British after the Treaty of Sagauli?
**Definition (learn):** Employing Nepal's army was a main aim of the Company; Gurkha troops later aided the British in the 1857 Rebellion (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Sugauli). The owner calls recruitment the most economically consequential outcome of the Himalayan policy (✅ owner 13 §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Residency: permanent British resident at Kathmandu.
- Recruitment: manpower for the Indian Army.
- Border: 1860 Boundary Treaty (Wikipedia note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1857 Gurkha aid; 1860 Boundary Treaty (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2095, MOD-2068 (1857 consequences).
**Trap:** Nepal was not annexed after Sagauli (✅ owner 13 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** A defeated adversary converted into a manpower source (✅ owner 13 §8.4 D).
**Back:** *"Nepal: Resident in, Gurkhas out."*

### Flashcard MOD-2097 — Treaty of Yandabo (1826): end of the First Anglo-Burmese War
**Front:** When and where was the Treaty of Yandabo signed, and what did Burma cede and pay?
**Definition (learn):** Signed on 24 February 1826 at Yandabo, ending the First Anglo-Burmese War (1824–26) between the East India Company and the Kingdom of Ava (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Yandabo).
**Breakdown:**
- Cessions: Assam, Manipur, Rakhine (Arakan), the Tanintharyi (Tenasserim) coast; Tripura in signing section (✅ Wikipedia).
- Indemnity: one million pounds sterling in four instalments; first £250,000 in bullion (✅ Wikipedia).
- Burma stopped interfering in Cachar and Jaintia; envoys exchanged (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 24 February 1826 (✅ Wikipedia); 1824–26 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MOD-2098, MOD-2092.
**Trap:** Yandabo is Burma, not Nepal or Afghanistan (✅ owner 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** The treaty followed a British army 50 miles from Ava, so terms were not negotiated (✅ Wikipedia).
**Back:** *"Yandabo 1826: Burma pays a million and cedes its west."*

### Flashcard MOD-2098 — Three Anglo-Burmese wars and staged annexation
**Front:** What were the three Anglo-Burmese wars, their treaties and annexations?
**Definition (learn):** First (1824–26) ended at Yandabo; Second (1852) annexed Lower Burma under Dalhousie; Third (1885) annexed Upper Burma under Dufferin (✅ owner 13 §1, §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- 1824–26: Yandabo; territories ceded (MOD-2097).
- 1852: Lower Burma (Dalhousie).
- 1885: Upper Burma (Dufferin).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1824–26; 1852; 1885 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MOD-2097, MOD-2099.
**Trap:** Match war → treaty → region → date (✅ owner 13 §5 headline trap).
**Mains hook:** Burma is the clearest counter-case to the purely defensive reading (✅ owner 13 §8.4 C).
**Back:** *"Yandabo, Lower, Upper: 1826, 1852, 1885."*

### Flashcard MOD-2099 — Burma administered as part of India until 1937
**Front:** Until when was Burma administered as part of British India, and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** Burma remained administratively attached to India until 1937; its separation in 1937 was not decolonisation, since Burma remained a British possession (✅ owner 13 §8.4 C, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Attachment explains migration and commerce between the two.
- 1937 separation: administrative, not independence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MOD-2098.
**Trap:** Do not treat 1937 as decolonisation (✅ owner 13 §8.4 C limit).
**Mains hook:** The administrative attachment explains later Indian migration and the 1937 separation.
**Back:** *"Part of India till 1937, still a colony."*

### Flashcard MOD-2100 — First Anglo-Afghan War (1839–42) and the Kabul retreat
**Front:** What happened in the First Anglo-Afghan War, and what was its lesson?
**Definition (learn):** The First Anglo-Afghan War (1839–42) ended in a disastrous retreat from Kabul, showing the danger of aggressive intervention (✅ owner 13 §2, adv §1). The owner's adv §4 associates it with Auckland.
**Breakdown:**
- Intervention, reverse, negotiated buffer, boundary line: the pattern (✅ owner 13 §8.4 B).
- Lesson: intervention is costly.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1839–42 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MOD-2101, MOD-2093.
**Trap:** Gandamak is not the First war; it belongs to the Second (1878–80) (✅ owner 13 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** Use as evidence that Afghanistan was a buffer that had to be fought for (✅ owner 13 §8.4 B).
**Back:** *"1839–42: Kabul retreat, not conquest."*

### Flashcard MOD-2101 — Second Anglo-Afghan War and the Treaty of Gandamak (1879)
**Front:** When was the Treaty of Gandamak signed, by whom, and what did Afghanistan concede?
**Definition (learn):** Signed 26 May 1879 near Gandamak by Emir Mohammad Yaqub Khan and Sir Louis Cavagnari; Afghanistan surrendered control of its foreign relations to British "advice and wishes" (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Gandamak).
**Breakdown:**
- Territories: Britain took Kurram and Pishin valleys, Sibi district and the Khyber Pass (✅ Wikipedia).
- British mission with European members in Kabul (✅ Wikipedia).
- Telegraph and trade (✅ Wikipedia).
- Ratified by Lytton 30 May 1879 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Outcome: Cavagnari killed at Kabul on 3 September 1879; a second invasion followed; Abdur Rahman installed (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 26 May 1879; 30 May 1879 ratification; 3 September 1879 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2100, MOD-2102.
**Trap:** Gandamak belongs to Afghanistan, not Burma; it followed the Second war (✅ owner 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Negotiated buffer followed by a boundary line (✅ owner 13 §8.4 B).
**Back:** *"Gandamak 1879: Afghanistan's foreign affairs to Britain."*

### Flashcard MOD-2102 — Durand Line (1893): spheres of influence, not a settled border
**Front:** When was the Durand Line agreed, between whom, and what was its stated purpose?
**Definition (learn):** The Durand Line Agreement of 12 November 1893 between Mortimer Durand (ICS, for British India) and Amir Abdur Rahman Khan of Afghanistan; its stated purpose was "to fix the limit of their respective spheres of influence" (✅ Wikipedia: Durand Line).
**Breakdown:**
- Length: 2,640 km per the page (and 2,611 km in geography section) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Pakistan inherited it in 1947; now the Afghanistan–Pakistan border (✅ Wikipedia).
- Afghanistan argues it marked influence zones and has not recognised it as a border; reaffirmed in 1919 and 1921 treaties (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 12 November 1893 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2101, MOD-2109.
**Trap:** Durand Line (1893) is not the McMahon Line or the 1904 Tibet mission (MOD-2103, MOD-2105).
**Mains hook:** The legacy of colonial boundary-making: a line contested by the state that inherited it (✅ owner 13 §8.7).
**Back:** *"1893 Durand: spheres, then a disputed border."*

### Flashcard MOD-2103 — Younghusband Mission (1904) and the Lhasa Convention
**Front:** What was the Younghusband Mission, when did it reach Tibet, and what followed?
**Definition (learn):** Curzon sent the Younghusband Mission, which reached Tibet in 1904 and produced the Lhasa Convention, an example of forward frontier activism (✅ owner 13 §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Motive: fears of Russian influence in Tibet (owner).
- Outcome: Lhasa Convention (owner).
- Curzon's frontier activism.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1904 (✅ owner 13). ⚠️ The Wikipedia page for the Convention was not fetched.
**Links:** MOD-2094, MOD-2105.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1904 Tibet mission with the 1893 Durand Line; the Durand agreement is not Younghusband (✅ owner 13 adv §Mechanism; owner Semantic-completeness).
**Mains hook:** Precedents for later boundary and status questions (✅ owner 13 §8.4 D).
**Back:** *"1904 Tibet: Younghusband, Lhasa, Curzon."*

### Flashcard MOD-2104 — Sikkim and Bhutan: treaty-buffer protectorates
**Front:** How were Sikkim and Bhutan managed in the Himalayan buffer strategy?
**Definition (learn):** Sikkim and Bhutan were drawn into treaty and protectorate-style relationships as Himalayan buffers (✅ owner 13 §2, §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Himalayan belt: Nepal, Tibet, Sikkim, Bhutan treated by treaty dependence rather than conquest (owner).
- Sikkim: Nepal's conquests withdrawn under Sagauli (MOD-2095).
- Detailed treaty dates for Sikkim and Bhutan are not in the owner; not added (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** MOD-2095, MOD-2107.
**Trap:** Do not present them as annexed territories in this owner's framework.
**Mains hook:** Treaty-managed north versus militarised north-west (✅ owner 13 §8.6).
**Back:** *"Himalayan buffers by treaty, not conquest."*

### Flashcard MOD-2105 — McMahon Line is not the Durand Line
**Front:** Why must the McMahon Line not be confused with the Durand Line?
**Definition (learn):** The owner notes the McMahon Line is a later Tibetan frontier issue, not part of the early Afghan frontier context of the Durand Line (1893) (✅ owner 13 adv §5). ⚠️ The McMahon Line was not fetched or verified here.
**Breakdown:**
- Durand Line: Afghanistan, 1893.
- McMahon Line: Tibet frontier, later (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Durand 1893 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2102, MOD-2103.
**Trap:** Mixing these two lines is a common error (✅ owner 13 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Keep modern boundary status out of historical answers (✅ owner 13 §8.8).
**Back:** *"Durand is Afghan; McMahon is Tibetan."*

### Flashcard MOD-2106 — Cost and the nationalist critique of frontier policy
**Front:** Who paid for frontier wars and garrisons, and how does this link to the nationalist economic critique?
**Definition (learn):** Frontier wars and the standing garrison were charged to Indian revenue while the strategic benefit was imperial (⚠️ owner 13 §8.4 E). The owner asks for argument of incidence, not amounts.
**Breakdown:**
- Incidence: Indian revenue.
- Benefit: imperial.
- Bridge: to the colonial-economy critique (`basic/07`).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None quantified (owner caution).
**Links:** MOD-2092, MOD-2107.
**Trap:** Do not quantify military expenditure (✅ owner 13 §8.4 E limit).
**Mains hook:** India paid for a defence perimeter defined by British interests (✅ owner 13 §8.4 E).
**Back:** *"Indian rupees, imperial frontiers."*

### Flashcard MOD-2107 — Five theatres: comparison engine
**Front:** How do the five frontier theatres compare on threat, instrument, outcome and long shadow?
**Definition (learn):** Afghanistan (Russian advance; war then buffer and boundary, Durand 1893); north-west (tribal autonomy; alternating policies; NWFP 1901); Burma (commercial access and rivalry; three wars and annexation; 1937 separation); Nepal (post-war settlement; Sagauli; Gurkha recruitment); Tibet (rival influence in Lhasa; armed mission; Lhasa Convention) (✅ owner 13 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Table of five rows (owner §8.5).
- Each has a different long shadow: boundary, governance, migration, manpower, status questions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates as cards MOD-2095 to MOD-2103.
**Links:** MOD-2092, MOD-2094, MOD-2098, MOD-2095, MOD-2103.
**Trap:** One label does not fit all three regional models (north-west militarised, north-east and Himalayan treaty-managed, east annexed) (✅ owner 13 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Use the table for a 15–20 mark comparison.
**Back:** *"Five theatres, five methods."*

### Flashcard MOD-2108 — Annexation or buffer: two frontier models
**Front:** When did Britain choose a buffer and when did it choose annexation?
**Definition (learn):** Buffer where a rival great power was involved (Afghanistan, Tibet, Nepal); annexation where none was (Burma) (✅ owner 13 §8.2 differentiated-objective thesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Buffer: Afghanistan, Nepal, Bhutan, Sikkim (adv §2).
- Annexation: Burma.
- Forward policy: not always annexation (MOD-2094).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond cards.
**Links:** MOD-2092, MOD-2098.
**Trap:** Nepal was not annexed like Burma (✅ owner 13 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The differentiated-objective thesis for a comparison question.
**Back:** *"Great powers get buffers; kingdoms get provinces."*

### Flashcard MOD-2109 — Post-1947 legacy: colonial lines and the Afghan–Pakistan border
**Front:** What is the legacy of colonial frontier-making for independent India's neighbourhood?
**Definition (learn):** Colonial lines drawn for imperial convenience outlived the empire; independent India inherited a neighbourhood whose boundaries it had to defend as a nation (✅ owner 13 §8.7 legacy verdict). Durand Line now the Afghanistan–Pakistan border (✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Durand Line: Pakistan inherited; Afghanistan does not recognise as border (✅ Wikipedia).
- Burma and Himalayan lines: later boundary and strategic concerns (⚠️ owner, not verified).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Durand 1893; Pakistan 1947 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2102, MOD-2105.
**Trap:** Do not assert the modern legal status of any colonial-era line; confine claims to the historical agreement (✅ owner 13 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Colonial frontier-making as a legacy for post-1947 security.
**Back:** *"Colonial lines, national burdens."*

### Flashcard MOD-2110 — Frontier doctrine verdict and the zero-PYQ status
**Front:** What is the graded doctrinal verdict for frontier questions, and does this owner have PYQ routes?
**Definition (learn):** "British frontier policy solved each immediate problem by creating the next one, which is why a defensive doctrine produced a century of expansion" (✅ owner 13 §8.7). The owner records zero direct 2018–2026 Modern-History PYQ routes.
**Breakdown:**
- Verdicts: doctrine; comparative; legacy (owner §8.7).
- Boundary: Topic 05 (expansion), Topic 12 (Crown governance).
- Current neighbourhood questions are outside this owner.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Zero direct PYQ routes (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MOD-2092, MOD-2107, MOD-2109.
**Trap:** Keep the historical answer separate from current IR and boundary claims (✅ owner 13 Semantic-completeness).
**Mains hook:** Use the verdict to close a 20-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Defend, expand, defend again."*

### GROUP — Foundation of the INC & the Moderate Phase (1885–1905)

### Flashcard MOD-2111 — Origins of the Congress: national forces, not a British device
**Front:** How should the origin of the Indian National Congress be explained?
**Definition (learn):** The INC emerged from developing national consciousness, English education, the press, railways, economic critique and provincial associations; Hume's organisational role was real but secondary to Indian political development (✅ owner 14 §1, adv §1; ⚠️ the owner's interpretive framing from Bipan Chandra).
**Breakdown:**
- Pre-1885 associations: MOD-2112 to MOD-2115.
- INC founded 1885 at Bombay (MOD-2116).
- Safety-valve theory is disputed (MOD-2119).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1885 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2112, MOD-2116, MOD-2119, MOD-2128.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the origin debate with the historical role of the Congress (✅ owner 14 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The Congress was "Indian in cause and mixed in occasion" (✅ owner 14 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Indian cause, mixed occasion."*

### Flashcard MOD-2112 — British Indian Association (1851) and the Landholders' Society
**Front:** What early political association was formed in Bengal in 1851, and what other Bengal body belongs to the pre-Congress set?
**Definition (learn):** The British Indian Association (1851) was an early political association in Bengal; the Landholders' Society is listed in the same pre-1885 set (✅ owner 14 §1, mini-timeline).
**Breakdown:**
- Early elite political articulation in Bengal.
- Trained leaders in petitions and memorials (owner §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1851 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2111, MOD-2113.
**Trap:** Do not project a national movement backwards into these provincial elite bodies (✅ owner 14 §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** Shows the repertoire the Congress later scaled.
**Back:** *"1851 Bengal elite voice."*

### Flashcard MOD-2113 — East India Association (1866), Dadabhai Naoroji in London
**Front:** Who founded the East India Association, where and when?
**Definition (learn):** Founded by Dadabhai Naoroji in London in 1866 to influence British opinion on Indian affairs (✅ owner 14 §4, adv §1 mini-timeline).
**Breakdown:**
- Founder: Dadabhai Naoroji.
- Place: London.
- Aim: British public opinion.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1866 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2117, MOD-2123.
**Trap:** It is London-based; do not confuse with Poona Sarvajanik Sabha (MOD-2114).
**Mains hook:** Naoroji's drain critique began in this forum (owner §8.4 C).
**Back:** *"1866 London: Naoroji speaks to Britain."*

### Flashcard MOD-2114 — Poona Sarvajanik Sabha (1870) and Indian Association (1876)
**Front:** Which two bodies of 1870 and 1876 are linked to western India and to Bengal, and who founded the 1876 one?
**Definition (learn):** The Poona Sarvajanik Sabha (1870) became a key western-India political body; the Indian Association (1876) was founded by Surendranath Banerjea and Ananda Mohan Bose (✅ owner 14 §4, mini-timeline).
**Breakdown:**
- Poona Sarvajanik Sabha: western India; Brahman-dominated in the 1880s–90s per owner 10 (MOD-2035).
- Indian Association: Bengal; mobilisation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1870; 1876 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2035, MOD-2113.
**Trap:** Indian Association (1876) is not the Indian National Association (not in owner; ⚠️ do not add).
**Mains hook:** Use with the Phule-Sabha dispute to show the western-Indian politics of representation.
**Back:** *"1870 Poona, 1876 Indian Association."*

### Flashcard MOD-2115 — Madras Mahajan Sabha and Bombay Presidency Association
**Front:** Which two presidency bodies were part of the pre-1885 associational set?
**Definition (learn):** The Madras Mahajan Sabha and the Bombay Presidency Association are listed in the owner's pre-Congress set (✅ owner 14 §1, adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Madras and Bombay presidencies.
- Trained cadres and habits of collective political work (adv §1).
- Dates not given in the owner; not added (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** MOD-2111, MOD-2112.
**Trap:** Do not present the Madras or Bombay bodies as Congress branches.
**Mains hook:** Pre-1885 groundwork as the basis of the INC.
**Back:** *"Madras and Bombay: the presidency pre-Congress."*

### Flashcard MOD-2116 — INC first session, Bombay, December 1885
**Front:** Where and when was the first INC session held, and what was its venue?
**Definition (learn):** The INC's first session was held at Bombay, 28–31 December 1885, at Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College; it was originally planned for Poona but moved because of a cholera outbreak (✅ Wikipedia: Indian National Congress).
**Breakdown:**
- Dates: 28–31 December 1885 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Venue: Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College, Bombay (✅ Wikipedia).
- Founded with A.O. Hume's organisational help (✅ owner 14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 28–31 December 1885; Bombay (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2117, MOD-2118, MOD-2119.
**Trap:** Do not place the first session in Calcutta or Poona.
**Mains hook:** The annual session became a national ritual (owner adv §8.5).
**Back:** *"Bombay, December 1885, Gokuldas Tejpal."*

### Flashcard MOD-2117 — W.C. Bonnerjee: first INC president
**Front:** Who presided over the first INC session in 1885?
**Definition (learn):** W.C. Bonnerjee presided over the first session (✅ owner 14; ✅ Wikipedia: Indian National Congress). The Wikipedia text also gives a variant name, "Umesh Chandra Banerjee".
**Breakdown:**
- Name variant: W.C. Bonnerjee / Umesh Chandra Banerjee (same person per Wikipedia text).
- Owner calls him the first president.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1885 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2116, MOD-2118.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Bonnerjee (1885 president) with Dadabhai Naoroji (1886 president; MOD-2120).
**Mains hook:** Use Bonnerjee to show the lawyer-led composition of the early INC.
**Back:** *"1885: Bonnerjee in the chair."*

### Flashcard MOD-2118 — 72 delegates, mostly lawyers, at the first session
**Front:** How many delegates attended the first INC session, and what was their profession mix?
**Definition (learn):** The first session had 72 delegates, mostly lawyers (✅ Wikipedia: Indian National Congress; ✅ owner 14 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Delegates: 72.
- Profession: mostly lawyers (Wikipedia).
- Owner: English-educated middle class, presidency towns (adv §8.4 E).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 72 delegates (✅ owner 14; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2116, MOD-2127.
**Trap:** Do not quantify later Congress membership beyond the verified 72 (✅ owner 14 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** The professional lawyers' method suited constitutional agitation (owner §8.4 E).
**Back:** *"72 delegates, mostly lawyers."*

### Flashcard MOD-2119 — A.O. Hume and the safety-valve controversy
**Front:** What did Hume contribute to the Congress, and what is the safety-valve theory's status?
**Definition (learn):** Hume helped organise the Congress; the safety-valve theory (that the Congress was a British device to channel discontent) is a historiographical claim, not documented British policy (✅ owner 14 §8.4 B; ⚠️ owner's caution).
**Breakdown:**
- Hume: organisational help.
- Safety-valve: disputed; do not cite alleged secret documents (owner §8.4 B).
- Owner's analytic: Hume explains the timing and convenience of 1885, not the causation (owner §8.4 B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1885 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2111, MOD-2116.
**Trap:** The "lightning conductor" role (adv §1) does not make the Congress a colonial creation (✅ owner 14 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** "Hume opened a door that Indian political development had already reached" (✅ owner 14 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Hume held the door; India walked through."*

### Flashcard MOD-2120 — Dadabhai Naoroji as the 1886 Congress president
**Front:** Who was elected Congress president in 1886?
**Definition (learn):** Dadabhai Naoroji was elected president in 1886 (✅ Wikipedia: Indian National Congress). The city is not given in the fetched text.
**Breakdown:**
- Role: drain-theory leader (MOD-2123).
- Founded the East India Association (MOD-2113).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1886 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2113, MOD-2117, MOD-2123.
**Trap:** Naoroji was president in 1886, not the first session president of 1885.
**Mains hook:** Use Naoroji as the representative Moderate economist.
**Back:** *"1886: Naoroji in the chair."*

### Flashcard MOD-2121 — Moderate leaders roster
**Front:** Who are the principal Moderate leaders named in the owner?
**Definition (learn):** Dadabhai Naoroji, Gopal Krishna Gokhale, Pherozeshah Mehta, Surendranath Banerjea, M.G. Ranade, Badruddin Tyabji, W.C. Bonnerjee and R.C. Dutt (✅ owner 14 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Economic critique: Naoroji, Dutt, Ranade, Gokhale (adv §1).
- Western India: Mehta, Ranade, Tyabji.
- Bengal: Banerjea, Bonnerjee, Dutt.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2113, MOD-2120, MOD-2122.
**Trap:** Keep the Moderate roster separate from the Extremist trio Lal-Bal-Pal (MOD-2144).
**Mains hook:** Use the roster to show regional spread.
**Back:** *"Naoroji, Gokhale, Mehta, Banerjea, Ranade, Tyabji, Bonnerjee, Dutt."*

### Flashcard MOD-2122 — "3 P's" and "political mendicancy"
**Front:** What do "3 P's" and "political mendicancy" signify?
**Definition (learn):** "Petition, prayer and protest" is a popular shorthand for Moderate method; "political mendicancy" is a later critic's label for the same method (⚠️ owner 14 §1 says both are later labels, not self-descriptions).
**Breakdown:**
- Petition: memorials and resolutions.
- Prayer: humble appeal.
- Protest: speeches and press criticism.
- Later characterisation, not contemporary self-description (adv §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2121, MOD-2126.
**Trap:** Do not erase the Moderates' intellectual achievement with the shorthand (adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use as labels only in critical analysis.
**Back:** *"Petition, prayer, protest: a later sneer."*

### Flashcard MOD-2123 — Drain of wealth and economic nationalism
**Front:** What was the Moderates' main economic critique of colonial rule?
**Definition (learn):** The drain of wealth (Naoroji), poverty and land-revenue pressure, deindustrialisation, high military expenditure and exclusion from higher services, extended by R.C. Dutt's economic history (✅ owner 14 §1, §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Drain theory: Naoroji.
- Land-revenue pressure and poverty.
- Deindustrialisation: link to `basic/07`.
- Military expenditure: incidence on India.
- Exclusion from higher services.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Do not quantify the drain (owner caution).
**Links:** MOD-2106, MOD-2113, MOD-2124.
**Trap:** Economic nationalism did not begin with Swadeshi; the Moderates had it first (✅ owner 14 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** "Converted colonial rule from a political inconvenience into an economic indictment" (✅ owner 14 §8.4 C).
**Back:** *"Drain, poverty, deindustry: the economic indictment."*

### Flashcard MOD-2124 — Moderate demands: ICS, Indianisation, councils and civil liberties
**Front:** What were the Moderates' core demands?
**Definition (learn):** Expansion of legislative councils and elections; Indianisation of higher services; simultaneous ICS examination in India and Britain; civil liberties (press, speech, association); reduced military expenditure (✅ owner 14 §2, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Constitutional: council expansion, elections.
- Administrative: Indianisation; simultaneous ICS examination.
- Civil liberty: freedom of speech, press and association.
- Economic: lower military expenditure.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2123, MOD-2125.
**Trap:** Their frame was reform within empire, not independence (MOD-2128).
**Mains hook:** Demands show the reform frame of early nationalism.
**Back:** *"Councils, ICS, Indianisation, liberties, lower army cost."*

### Flashcard MOD-2125 — Welby Commission and the Indian Councils Act 1892 achievement
**Front:** What concrete gains did Moderate pressure win by 1892?
**Definition (learn):** The Indian Councils Act 1892 followed Congress pressure; the Welby Commission debate carried Indian financial criticism into an official forum; the simultaneous ICS demand became a standing item (✅ owner 14 §8.4 D; ✅ owner 12 for the 1892 Act).
**Breakdown:**
- Act 1892: MOD-2082 (budget discussion, questions).
- Welby Commission: owner gives no date; ⚠️ not verified.
- Simultaneous ICS demand: standing item.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1892 (✅ owner 14; ✅ Wikipedia: 1892 Act).
**Links:** MOD-2082, MOD-2083, MOD-2124.
**Trap:** The 1892 Act gave discussion, not control, of the budget; no responsible government (✅ owner 14 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Each gain small in power, large in precedent (✅ owner 14 §8.4 D).
**Back:** *"1892: small gains, big precedents."*

### Flashcard MOD-2126 — The Moderate ceiling: procedure conceded, power refused
**Front:** Why did the Moderate method reach a ceiling?
**Definition (learn):** The state conceded procedure (1892) and refused power, showing that appeals to British justice could not by themselves move a colonial regime (✅ owner 14 §8.2 ceiling thesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Achievement: procedural gains.
- Ceiling: no responsibility, no elected majority, no executive accountability (owner §8.4 D).
- Result: Extremist turn from the demonstrated limits (MOD-2131).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1892; 1905 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2131, MOD-2082.
**Trap:** The Moderates did not demand independence before 1905 (MOD-2128).
**Mains hook:** "The Moderates failed politically but succeeded ideologically" (✅ owner 14 §7).
**Back:** *"Procedure granted, power withheld."*

### Flashcard MOD-2127 — Social base of the early Congress
**Front:** Who made up the early Congress, and who was excluded?
**Definition (learn):** An all-India platform of the English-educated professional middle class (lawyers, journalists, teachers, publicists) concentrated in presidency towns; peasants, workers, women and most lower castes were outside (⚠️ owner 14 §8.4 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Composition: professionals.
- Method suited: law and print.
- Later need: a peasant and worker base, which Gandhi built (owner §8.4 E).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 72 delegates 1885 (MOD-2118).
**Links:** MOD-2118, MOD-2130, MOD-2150.
**Trap:** Do not describe the Moderates as anti-national (MOD-2129).
**Mains hook:** Social base explains both method and limits.
**Back:** *"Lawyers in the hall, peasants outside."*

### Flashcard MOD-2128 — Trap: Moderates did not demand independence
**Front:** What did the Moderates demand, and why is "independence from the beginning" a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Their early demands were constitutional reform, representation and Indianisation, not complete independence (✅ owner 14 §5, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Reform within empire (owner §8.4 C).
- Swaraj came later with mass politics (⚠️ dates not in owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Before 1905 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2124, MOD-2126.
**Trap:** Do not claim Moderates demanded swaraj before 1905 (✅ owner 14 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Use to set the Moderate-Extremist contrast accurately.
**Back:** *"Reform first, swaraj later."*

### Flashcard MOD-2129 — Trap: Moderates were limited in method, not irrelevant
**Front:** Why is "Moderates achieved nothing" a trap?
**Definition (learn):** They built the drain critique, public opinion, council politics and an all-India platform (✅ owner 14 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Achievements: analysis, platform, council practice.
- Limits: narrow social base, faith in British justice.
- Balance: loyalist language with sharp critique (adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2123, MOD-2126, MOD-2127.
**Trap:** The INC was not created only as a safety valve (MOD-2119).
**Mains hook:** "Moderates succeeded ideologically" argument (owner §7).
**Back:** *"Limited method, lasting platform."*

### Flashcard MOD-2130 — Continuity between Moderates and Extremists
**Front:** What was the continuity and rupture between Moderates and Extremists?
**Definition (learn):** Shared analysis of colonial economic exploitation; divergent method and idiom; what changed in 1905–07 was method, mobilisation and idiom (✅ owner 14 §8.1, §8.4 F).
**Breakdown:**
- Continuity: drain and deindustrialisation critique.
- Rupture: rejection of petitioning; boycott, Swadeshi, passive resistance.
- Trigger: Curzon's measures and Partition (MOD-2134).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1905 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2131, MOD-2134.
**Trap:** Do not present the split as generational or temperamental (✅ owner 14 §8.4 F limit).
**Mains hook:** Compare as "complementary stages" (✅ owner 14 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Same analysis, different method."*

### Flashcard MOD-2131 — Extremist rise: why the Moderate method was discarded
**Front:** Why did the Extremist turn follow the Moderate phase?
**Definition (learn):** The Extremist turn came from the demonstrated limits of petitioning, while retaining the drain critique; the trigger conditions came from Curzon's measures and the 1905 partition (✅ owner 14 §8.4 F; ⚠️ owner's causal framing).
**Breakdown:**
- Extremists kept the economic critique; rejected petitioning.
- Trigger: Curzon's measures; Partition (MOD-2134).
- Method: boycott, passive resistance, national education (MOD-2141).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1905 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2126, MOD-2130, MOD-2141.
**Trap:** Do not say Extremists rejected nationalism's economic critique (✅ owner 15 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Generation trained in argument turns to boycott" (✅ owner 14 §8.5 mechanism).
**Back:** *"Petition failed, so boycott was born."*

### Flashcard MOD-2132 — Moderates' role in preparing the freedom movement (2021 PYQ)
**Front:** What is the 2021 GS-I route on the Moderates, and how should it be answered?
**Definition (learn):** 2021 GS-I Q11 (15 marks; 250 words): the Moderates' role in preparing a base for the wider freedom movement (✅ owner 14 adv PYQ integration; ✅ owner 14 §PYQ ownership).
**Breakdown:**
- Context: pre-1885 associations.
- Core: constitutional method, economic critique, all-India platform, social base limits.
- Counterpoint: ceiling and procedural concessions.
- Conclusion: foundation of the mass movement.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-I Q11; 15 marks; 250 words (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MOD-2111, MOD-2123, MOD-2126.
**Trap:** Do not answer as if the Moderates led mass politics.
**Mains hook:** "Ideological success, institutional platform, ceiling in power."
**Back:** *"Moderates: the platform and the analysis."*

### Flashcard MOD-2133 — Surat session (December 1907) and the Congress split
**Front:** Where and when was the Surat session that produced the Congress split, and what did it reflect?
**Definition (learn):** The Congress session at Surat in December 1907 produced the split between Moderates and Extremists, reflecting conflict over methods and control of the session (✅ Wikipedia: Indian National Congress, Surat session December 1907; ✅ owner 15 §8.5, adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Venue: Surat; December 1907 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Split: Moderates vs Extremists (owner).
- Lal-Bal-Pal context (MOD-2144).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** December 1907 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2144, MOD-2145.
**Trap:** Swadeshi preceded Surat; Surat did not cause Swadeshi (✅ owner 15 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The institutional expression of two currents (✅ owner 15 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Surat December 1907: the Congress splits."*

### GROUP — Militant Nationalism, Swadeshi & the Partition of Bengal

### Flashcard MOD-2134 — Curzonian provocation and the anti-partition movement
**Front:** How did Curzon's policies and the Partition of Bengal produce militant nationalism?
**Definition (learn):** Curzon's reactionary measures (Calcutta Corporation Act 1899; Universities Act 1904) and the 1905 partition of Bengal convinced nationalists of divide-and-rule and deepened distrust of official reform. The anti-partition agitation became the Swadeshi and Boycott movement (✅ owner 15 §1, §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Causes: Moderate failure, Curzon's reaction, economic distress and famine/plague, the 1905 Japanese victory over Russia and Ethiopia's resistance (✅ owner 15 §1).
- Leaders who revived pride: Tilak, Vivekananda, Bankim Chandra (✅ owner 15).
- Swadeshi: boycott, indigenous enterprise, national education, samitis (MOD-2137 to MOD-2143).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1899; 1904; 1905 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MOD-2135, MOD-2136, MOD-2137, MOD-2145, MOD-2130.
**Trap:** Swadeshi began as an anti-partition movement with a political trigger, not as a general economic campaign (✅ owner 15 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Swadeshi as both an anti-partition movement and a laboratory of future techniques" (✅ owner 15 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Curzon cut Bengal; Bengal cut the cloth."*

### Flashcard MOD-2135 — Calcutta Corporation Act (1899) and Universities Act (1904)
**Front:** What do the 1899 Calcutta Corporation Act and the 1904 Universities Act show about Curzon's approach?
**Definition (learn):** The Calcutta Corporation Act (1899) reduced elected Indian influence in municipal administration; the Universities Act (1904) increased official control over universities (✅ owner 15 mini-timeline, adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- 1899: anti-representative municipal reform.
- 1904: official control of higher education.
- Both read by nationalists as proof that official reform was retrograde.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1899; 1904 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MOD-2013 (Universities Act), MOD-2134.
**Trap:** The 1904 Universities Act is a control measure, not a 1904 Wood's Despatch measure.
**Mains hook:** Curzon's reforms were control, not expansion (owner adv §8.2).
**Back:** *"1899 municipal shrink, 1904 campus control."*

### Flashcard MOD-2136 — Partition of Bengal: announced July 1905, effective 16 October 1905
**Front:** When was the partition of Bengal announced and when did it take effect?
**Definition (learn):** Curzon announced the partition in July 1905; it took effect on 16 October 1905; nationalists read it as divide-and-rule (✅ owner 15 §4, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Announcement: July 1905 (owner).
- Effect: 16 October 1905, observed as a day of protest (owner §8.4 A).
- Wikipedia notes that the partition decision was first taken in December 1903, and gives no October date (⚠️ conflict in emphasis; the owner's dates are the exam dates).
- Religious split: Hindu-majority west and Muslim-majority east (✅ Wikipedia: Swadeshi movement).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** July 1905; 16 October 1905 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MOD-2134, MOD-2137, MOD-2147.
**Trap:** Keep the announcement date (July) and the effective date (16 October) distinct (✅ owner 15 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The partition's religious divide later connects to separate electorates (MOD-2146).
**Back:** *"July 1905 announced; 16 October enforced."*

### Flashcard MOD-2137 — 7 August 1905: Swadeshi formally launched at the Calcutta Town Hall
**Front:** What happened at Calcutta on 7 August 1905, and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** The Swadeshi movement was formally started from the Calcutta Town Hall on 7 August 1905, where the boycott resolution was passed (✅ Wikipedia: Swadeshi movement; ✅ owner 15 §8.4 A).
**Breakdown:**
- Purpose: curb foreign goods and encourage domestic production (✅ Wikipedia).
- Boycott Resolution named in the owner; the Wikipedia text does not name it explicitly (⚠️).
- Owner: distinguish this formal resolution from the 17 July call (MOD-2138).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 7 August 1905 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2138, MOD-2139, MOD-2136.
**Trap:** Do not merge the 17 July call and the 7 August resolution (✅ owner 15 §8.4 A).
**Mains hook:** The formal birth date of Swadeshi as a political technique.
**Back:** *"7 August 1905, Town Hall, Swadeshi proclaimed."*

### Flashcard MOD-2138 — 17 July 1905: Surendranath Banerjea's boycott call (owner claim)
**Front:** What is the owner's claim about a 17 July 1905 boycott call, and what is its verification status?
**Definition (learn):** The owner says Surendranath Banerjea called for a boycott of British goods and institutions at a Calcutta meeting on 17 July 1905, initiated by a Moderate leader (✅ owner 15 §8.4 A, ⚠️ not verified).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner distinguishes this call from the 7 August resolution (MOD-2137).
- Wikipedia's Swadeshi article does not mention Banerjea or 17 July (✅ Wikipedia; ⚠️ unverified).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17 July 1905 (⚠️ owner only).
**Links:** MOD-2137, MOD-2121 (Moderate roster).
**Trap:** Do not cite the 17 July date as verified; keep the 7 August resolution as the formal anchor.
**Mains hook:** The Moderate initiative in boycott shows Moderates were not bystanders (✅ owner 15 §8.6).
**Back:** *"17 July: Banerjea's call, unverified; 7 August: the resolution."*
