### Flashcard MOD-2139 — National Handloom Day (7 August) and the 1905 link
**Front:** Why is National Handloom Day observed on 7 August?
**Definition (learn):** National Handloom Day is observed on 7 August because the Swadeshi movement was proclaimed on that date in 1905 (✅ Wikipedia: Swadeshi movement; first observance 2015 by Prime Minister Modi per the page).
**Breakdown:**
- First observance: 2015 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Link: 1905 Town Hall proclamation (MOD-2137).
- Owner: "exactly this is why the link is understood, not memorised" (✅ owner 15 §8.4 A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 7 August 1905; 7 August 2015 first observance (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2137, MOD-2154.
**Trap:** Do not present Handloom Day as the day of the partition's effect; 16 October is the effect date (MOD-2136).
**Mains hook:** Shows cultural memory of Swadeshi.
**Back:** *"Handloom Day remembers Town Hall, 7 August."*

### Flashcard MOD-2140 — Boycott effects: Manchester cloth, Liverpool salt and Barisal crowds
**Front:** What evidence shows that the boycott had measurable effect, and where?
**Definition (learn):** Surendranath Banerjea toured urging boycott of Manchester cloth and Liverpool salt; Bipan Chandra records a sharp fall in British cloth sales in some mofussil districts between September 1904 and September 1905; Barisal protest drew ten to twelve thousand (✅ owner 15 §8.4 B; ⚠️ the figures are the owner's district-level evidence, not verified).
**Breakdown:**
- Effect: local, short-term, district-level.
- Protest scale: Barisal crowds of 10,000–12,000 (owner).
- Caution: do not extend to all India.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** September 1904–September 1905 (⚠️ owner).
**Links:** MOD-2137, MOD-2141.
**Trap:** The fall was district-level and year-specific, not an all-India statistic (✅ owner 15 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Boycott worked, briefly and regionally" (✅ owner 15 §8.4 B).
**Back:** *"Manchester out, briefly; Barisal in the thousands."*

### Flashcard MOD-2141 — National Council of Education (1906) and Bengal National College
**Front:** What national education institutions did the Swadeshi movement create, and who was associated with Bengal National College?
**Definition (learn):** The National Council of Education was founded in 1906; the Bengal National College is associated with Aurobindo Ghosh (✅ owner 15 §2, §4; ⚠️ the Wikipedia Swadeshi page fetched did not mention either institution).
**Breakdown:**
- National education: an institution that outlived the agitation (owner §8.4 C).
- Limit: reach was limited; graduates faced employment barriers (owner §8.5).
- Model for later constructive programme.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1906 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MOD-2142, MOD-2143, MOD-2131.
**Trap:** Do not confuse National Council of Education (1906) with the 1904 Universities Act (MOD-2135).
**Mains hook:** National education was a constructive side that protest alone lacked.
**Back:** *"1906 National Council; Bengal National College."*

### Flashcard MOD-2142 — Constructive swadeshi: enterprise, arbitration and national schools
**Front:** What constructive programme distinguished Swadeshi from mere boycott?
**Definition (learn):** Encouragement of indigenous enterprise and Indian goods; swadeshi enterprises in textiles, soap, matches, banking, insurance and small manufacturing; swadeshi stores; arbitration courts; national schools and physical culture (✅ owner 15 §8.4 C, adv §1; ⚠️ the sector list is owner-stated without verified firms).
**Breakdown:**
- Boycott is withdrawal; Swadeshi is construction (owner §8.8).
- Enterprises were undercapitalised and short-lived (⚠️ owner §8.4 C).
- Do not name firms, capital or output (owner caution).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None verified.
**Links:** MOD-2140, MOD-2141.
**Trap:** Do not say Swadeshi was only buying Indian cloth (✅ owner 15 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Gandhi's constructive programme owed a debt here (MOD-2150).
**Back:** *"Swadeshi builds what boycott refuses."*

### Flashcard MOD-2143 — Aurobindo Ghosh and Bengal National College
**Front:** What was Aurobindo Ghosh's role in Bengal's militant nationalism?
**Definition (learn):** Aurobindo Ghosh was associated with the Bengal National College and with militant nationalist politics (✅ owner 15 §4; owner 16 notes the Aurobindo–Barindra circle in the Alipore case).
**Breakdown:**
- Institution: Bengal National College.
- Political writings: assertive nationalism (adv §2).
- Link to revolutionary circle (MOD-2159).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1906 (college association per owner).
**Links:** MOD-2141, MOD-2159.
**Trap:** Do not present Aurobindo as the head of the Ghadar Party (✅ owner 16 §5 style trap).
**Mains hook:** Use Aurobindo to bridge open Swadeshi and underground revolution.
**Back:** *"Bengal National: Aurobindo's classroom."*

### Flashcard MOD-2144 — Lal-Bal-Pal: the Extremist trio
**Front:** Who were Lal, Bal and Pal, and why are they the best-known Extremist leaders?
**Definition (learn):** Lala Lajpat Rai, Bal Gangadhar Tilak and Bipin Chandra Pal, the best-known Extremist leadership trio (✅ owner 15 §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Lal: Lala Lajpat Rai (Punjab).
- Bal: Bal Gangadhar Tilak (Maharashtra).
- Pal: Bipin Chandra Pal (Bengal).
- Wikipedia notes Lal-Bal-Pal became inactive after 1908 because of deportations and arrests (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1908 inactivity (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2145, MOD-2165, MOD-2121.
**Trap:** Lajpat Rai was an Extremist leader, not a member of a secret society (✅ owner 16 §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** Use the trio to show regional spread of Extremism.
**Back:** *"Lal, Bal, Pal: Punjab, Maharashtra, Bengal."*

### Flashcard MOD-2145 — Surat split (1907): Swadeshi's institutional sequel
**Front:** How does the 1907 Surat split relate to Swadeshi?
**Definition (learn):** The Surat split (1907) was the institutional expression of the Moderate–Extremist division sharpened by the Swadeshi movement; Swadeshi preceded Surat (✅ owner 15 §8.2, adv §5; ✅ Wikipedia: Surat session December 1907).
**Breakdown:**
- Two currents: Moderate annulment-seeking and Extremist regeneration-seeking (⚠️ interpretive scheme).
- Surat session: December 1907 (MOD-2133).
- Decline thereafter under repression and internal split (owner adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1907 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MOD-2133, MOD-2134, MOD-2146.
**Trap:** Surat did not cause Swadeshi (✅ owner 15 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The split as the institutional expression of two currents (✅ owner 15 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Surat 1907: Swadeshi's divorce."*

### Flashcard MOD-2146 — Morley–Minto (1909): separate electorates for Muslims
**Front:** What did the Morley–Minto reforms of 1909 introduce, and why does it matter for the Swadeshi story?
**Definition (learn):** The Indian Councils Act 1909 introduced separate electorates for Muslims, while enlarging councils; it is the state's concession after the Swadeshi-era division (✅ owner 15 §4, §8.4 E; ✅ Wikipedia: Indian Councils Act 1909).
**Breakdown:**
- Muslims elected by Muslim voters (MOD-2084).
- Enlarged councils.
- Division and concession as the state's twin response.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1909 (✅ owner 15; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2084, MOD-2180, MOD-2148.
**Trap:** Separate electorates were not introduced by the 1919 Act (MOD-2148).
**Mains hook:** Sequence 1907 → 1909 → 1911 shows division and concession (✅ owner 15 §8.4 E).
**Back:** *"1909: separate electorates, the state's concession."*

### Flashcard MOD-2147 — Partition annulled (1911) and the capital moved to Delhi
**Front:** When was the partition of Bengal annulled, and what was the capital shift?
**Definition (learn):** The partition was annulled in 1911, and the capital shifted from Calcutta to Delhi (✅ owner 15; ✅ Wikipedia: reunification 1911).
**Breakdown:**
- Annulment: 1911 (✅ owner 15; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Capital shift: Calcutta to Delhi (owner).
- Presented as a qualified victory, six years later (owner §8.4 E).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1911 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MOD-2136, MOD-2149.
**Trap:** The partition was not permanent (✅ owner 15 §5).
**Mains hook:** A genuine achievement, delayed and compensated (✅ owner 15 §8.5).
**Back:** *"1911 annulled, capital to Delhi."*

### Flashcard MOD-2148 — Trap: separate electorates came in 1909, not 1919
**Front:** Why is "separate electorates began in 1919" a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Separate electorates for Muslims were introduced by Morley–Minto in 1909, not by the Government of India Act 1919 (✅ owner 15 §5, owner 17 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- 1909: Muslims.
- 1919: extension to Sikhs and others (owner 17 card).
- Keep the distinction for evaluation questions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1909; 1919 (✅ owner).
**Links:** MOD-2146, MOD-2186.
**Trap:** The Morley–Minto reforms "satisfied" nationalist demands is also wrong; they enlarged councils and deepened communalism (✅ owner 15 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use as a precise chronology check.
**Back:** *"1909 not 1919: keep the Acts apart."*

### Flashcard MOD-2149 — Trap: annulment was qualified, not a triumph
**Front:** Why is the 1911 annulment a qualified victory?
**Definition (learn):** The annulment came six years after partition and alongside the capital shift; the movement had already declined under repression and internal limits (✅ owner 15 §8.4 E, adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Six-year delay.
- Capital shift as compensation (owner).
- Movement's decline before 1911.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1905–1911 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MOD-2147, MOD-2145.
**Trap:** Swadeshi failed not because partition was not annulled but because it had declined (✅ owner 15 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Qualified achievement in an evaluation answer.
**Back:** *"Six years late, and the movement was already tired."*

### Flashcard MOD-2150 — Swadeshi as the precursor of Gandhian politics
**Front:** What did Swadeshi bequeath to Gandhi, and what deficit did Gandhi correct?
**Definition (learn):** Boycott, passive resistance, self-reliance, national education and constructive work passed to Gandhi; Swadeshi lacked a village-reaching organisation, a discipline of non-violence, and a leadership that included Muslims and peasants (✅ owner 15 §8.4 F; ⚠️ owner interpretive).
**Breakdown:**
- Continuity: techniques.
- Deficit: organisation, non-violence, inclusion.
- Do not describe Swadeshi as non-violent in principle (owner caution).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2142, MOD-2156.
**Trap:** Passive resistance coexisted with revolutionary violence (owner §8.4 F).
**Mains hook:** "Swadeshi supplied the techniques and Gandhi the organisation" (✅ owner 15 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Swadeshi gave the tools; Gandhi gave the hands."*

### Flashcard MOD-2151 — Unresolved Prelims: the Mazzini–Garibaldi biographer (2018 Q11)
**Front:** What is the status of the 2018 Prelims routed question on the nationalist who wrote biographies of Mazzini and Garibaldi?
**Definition (learn):** The owner says no source in the repository identifies the author of biographies of Mazzini and Garibaldi; the official key is not held locally, so the answer is not given here (✅ owner 15 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Supported fact: Mazzini and Garibaldi were political heroes of Western-educated Indian nationalists (✅ owner 15, Bipan Chandra).
- Unresolved: author identity.
- Single-fact Prelims point with no Mains consequence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-I Q11 (owner).
**Links:** MOD-2152.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the biographies to a named person without evidence (owner caution).
**Mains hook:** None; Prelims only.
**Back:** *"Mazzini and Garibaldi: heroes known, author unrecorded here."*

### Flashcard MOD-2152 — Unresolved Prelims: Deuskar's *Desher Katha* (2020 Q26)
**Front:** What is the status of the 2020 Prelims route on *Desher Katha* by Deuskar?
**Definition (learn):** The owner says the authorship and circulation impact of Deuskar's *Desher Katha* have no supporting content in the source books held in the repository (✅ owner 15 §8.8). The owner does not state circulation figures.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner refuses to attribute circulation figures without evidence.
- Official key not held locally.
- ⚠️ Not verified here; do not add details.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 Prelims Q26 (owner).
**Links:** MOD-2151, MOD-2154.
**Trap:** Do not state circulation figures for *Desher Katha* (owner caution).
**Mains hook:** None.
**Back:** *"Desher Katha: named in the route, unresolved here."*

### Flashcard MOD-2153 — Tilak's "Swaraj is my birthright" slogan
**Front:** How should Tilak's slogan "Swaraj is my birthright" be used?
**Definition (learn):** The owner links the slogan to assertive nationalist mobilisation; it is not verified as a verbatim quotation in the owner text (⚠️ owner 15 adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Mobilisation: Extremist swaraj demand.
- Use in exam: paraphrase unless the exact wording is verified.
- Tilak's festivals: part of the cultural mobilisation (owner adv §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2144, MOD-2154.
**Trap:** Do not present the slogan as a 1905 official resolution.
**Mains hook:** Use as an example of swaraj as a self-assertive demand.
**Back:** *"Swaraj as birthright: paraphrase, do not forge."*

### Flashcard MOD-2154 — Vande Mataram and samitis: cultural mobilisation with a narrowing idiom
**Front:** How did Vande Mataram, festivals, songs and samitis extend Swadeshi, and what did the idiom cost?
**Definition (learn):** Vande Mataram, festivals, songs, processions and samitis carried politics beyond petitions and reached students, women and urban crowds; the Hindu-inflected symbolism limited Muslim participation (✅ owner 15 §8.4 D; ⚠️ the Hindu symbolism point is the owner's interpretation).
**Breakdown:**
- Reach: students, women, urban crowds.
- Cost: difficulty of Muslim participation.
- Do not assert that Swadeshi caused the League's formation (owner caution).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2181, MOD-2155, MOD-2193.
**Trap:** Keep Swadeshi and the League as connected developments in a shared context, not cause and effect (✅ owner 15 §8.4 D).
**Mains hook:** "Energised and narrowed" ambivalence verdict (✅ owner 15 §8.7).
**Back:** *"Songs carried the crowds; the idiom narrowed them."*

### Flashcard MOD-2155 — Vande Mataram 150-year commemoration (current affairs, owner-cited)
**Front:** What commemoration is linked to Vande Mataram, and how should it be dated?
**Definition (learn):** The owner cites a 150-year commemoration of Vande Mataram running from 7 November 2025 to 7 November 2026, approved by the Union Cabinet and reported by PIB (owner adv §6). ⚠️ The PIB release is not verified here.
**Breakdown:**
- Current-affairs anchor, as-of date from owner: 7 November 2025.
- Do not treat 2025 as Bankimchandra's composition or first political use.
- Historical anchor: anti-partition and Swadeshi mobilisation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 7 November 2025–7 November 2026 (⚠️ owner-cited PIB).
**Links:** MOD-2154.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the commemoration year with the composition date.
**Mains hook:** Use as a current-affairs bridge only.
**Back:** *"2025–26 commemorates; 1905 mobilised."* ⚠️ commemorative dates unverified.

### Flashcard MOD-2156 — Mains verdict: Swadeshi's achievements, limits and precursor role
**Front:** What is the evaluation verdict on Swadeshi's achievements and limits?
**Definition (learn):** "Swadeshi lost the immediate argument for six years and won the long one permanently: it made boycott, self-reliance and mass symbolism the normal grammar of Indian politics" (✅ owner 15 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Achievement–limitation ledger: political (annulment; boycott technique), economic (local cloth fall; undercapitalised ventures), educational (NCE), cultural (samitis), social (first move beyond petition class) (✅ owner 15 §8.5).
- Structure for 20 marks: thesis, causes, 1905 sequence, four techniques, achievements, social and communal limits, transmission to Gandhi, graded verdict.
- Owner PYQ ownership: five direct routes (2018, 2019, two in 2020, 2023) (✅ owner 15 PYQ control).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As per cards MOD-2136 to MOD-2150.
**Links:** MOD-2150, MOD-2148, MOD-2149.
**Trap:** Do not present Swadeshi as failed; it was transitional (✅ owner 15 adv historiography note).
**Mains hook:** "Swadeshi was not a failed episode but a transitional repertoire" (✅ owner 15 adv historiography).
**Back:** *"Lost the year, won the grammar."*

### GROUP — Revolutionary Nationalism (Phase I, 1907–1917)

### Flashcard MOD-2157 — Revolutionary nationalism as exemplary action
**Front:** What was the theory of change behind early revolutionary nationalism?
**Definition (learn):** After Swadeshi declined under repression, young nationalists turned to secret societies and armed action, believing dramatic attacks on officials would inspire courage, avenge repression and awaken the nation (✅ owner 16 §1, adv §1). Prefer "revolutionary nationalism" or "armed revolutionary activity" to "revolutionary terrorism" (⚠️ owner 16 §8.4 A).
**Breakdown:**
- Causation: state suppression of legal agitation is part of the explanation for illegal agitation (owner §8.4 A).
- Arenas: Bengal, Maharashtra, Punjab, London, Europe, North America.
- Method: physical training, oath-bound discipline, propaganda, arms, selective violence (adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1907–1917 phase (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MOD-2134, MOD-2158, MOD-2175, MOD-2177.
**Trap:** Revolutionary nationalism is not Gandhian satyagraha (⚠️ owner 16 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Could inspire sacrifice but not overthrow colonial rule" (✅ owner 16 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Repression closed the hall; the bomb opened the door."*

### Flashcard MOD-2158 — Chapekar brothers (1897, Poona): Rand and Ayerst
**Front:** Who did the Chapekar brothers kill in 1897, and where?
**Definition (learn):** The Chapekar brothers killed W.C. Rand (plague commissioner) and Lt Ayerst at Poona in 1897, amid anger over plague administration (✅ owner 16 §4, adv mini-timeline).
**Breakdown:**
- Victims: W.C. Rand; Lt Ayerst.
- Place: Poona (Maharashtra).
- Context: plague administration.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1897 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MOD-2157, MOD-2160.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the Chapekar killings (1897) with the Muzaffarpur attack (1908).
**Mains hook:** Early armed current in Maharashtra, before Swadeshi.
**Back:** *"1897 Poona: Rand and Ayerst."*

### Flashcard MOD-2159 — Alipore Bomb Case and the Aurobindo–Barindra circle
**Front:** What was the Alipore Bomb Case, and which circle did it involve?
**Definition (learn):** The Alipore Bomb Case followed the Muzaffarpur attack and involved the Aurobindo–Barindra Ghosh revolutionary circle in Bengal (✅ owner 16 §4, §8.4 B; ✅ owner 16 §9 dates 1908–09).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1908–09 (owner §9).
- Circle: Aurobindo and Barindra Ghosh.
- Courtroom platform: public martyrs created by the trial (owner §8.4 B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1908–09 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MOD-2143, MOD-2160, MOD-2161.
**Trap:** The trial, not the bomb, was the decisive political event (✅ owner 16 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** Use to show how courtroom platforms converted conspiracy into propaganda.
**Back:** *"Alipore: the trial made the martyrs."*

### Flashcard MOD-2160 — Muzaffarpur attack (April 1908): Kingsford, Khudiram Bose and Prafulla Chaki
**Front:** What was the 1908 Muzaffarpur attack, and who was involved?
**Definition (learn):** An attack in April 1908 targeting Kingsford, involving Khudiram Bose and Prafulla Chaki; it marked the Bengal revolutionary escalation (✅ owner 16 §2, §4, §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Target: Kingsford.
- Persons: Khudiram Bose; Prafulla Chaki (owner).
- Next: Alipore case (MOD-2159).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** April 1908 (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Links:** MOD-2159, MOD-2161.
**Trap:** Khudiram Bose and Prafulla Chaki belong to Muzaffarpur, not the Delhi Conspiracy (✅ owner 16 §5).
**Mains hook:** Bengal's organisational core (owner §8.4 B).
**Back:** *"Muzaffarpur 1908: Kingsford, Khudiram, Chaki."*

### Flashcard MOD-2161 — Bengal organisations: Anushilan Samiti, Jugantar, Barindra Ghosh and Bhupendranath Datta
**Front:** Which organisations and leaders formed Bengal's revolutionary core?
**Definition (learn):** Anushilan Samiti and Jugantar were the Bengal revolutionary organisations; Barindra Kumar Ghosh and Bhupendranath Datta were associated with this milieu (✅ owner 16 §1, §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Anushilan Samiti: secret society (owner adv §1).
- Jugantar: revolutionary centre.
- Barindra Ghosh: Aurobindo–Barindra circle.
- Bhupendranath Datta: milieu association.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** MOD-2159, MOD-2160.
**Trap:** Do not attribute later HSRA ideology to this phase (✅ owner 16 §8.4 B limit).
**Mains hook:** Bengal's student-intellectual base (owner §8.6).
**Back:** *"Anushilan and Jugantar: Bengal's two cores."*

### Flashcard MOD-2162 — Madame Bhikaji Cama's Stuttgart flag (1907)
**Front:** What did Madame Bhikaji Cama do at Stuttgart in 1907?
**Definition (learn):** She unfurled an Indian nationalist flag at Stuttgart in 1907, symbolising international propaganda (✅ owner 16 §4, §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Place: Stuttgart.
- Symbol: Indian nationalist flag.
- Role: overseas propaganda, not the founding of the Ghadar Party (MOD-2173).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1907 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MOD-2163, MOD-2173.
**Trap:** Madame Cama did not found the Ghadar Party (✅ owner 16 §5, §8.4 D).
**Mains hook:** Diplomacy of anti-colonialism before 1914 (owner §8.4 D).
**Back:** *"1907 Stuttgart: the flag abroad."*

### Flashcard MOD-2163 — India House, London and Shyamji Krishna Varma
**Front:** What was India House and who led it?
**Definition (learn):** India House in London was associated with Shyamji Krishna Varma; it internationalised the demand alongside Madan Lal Dhingra and Madame Cama (✅ owner 16 §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Leader: Shyamji Krishna Varma.
- Circle: Madan Lal Dhingra; Madame Cama.
- Effect: forced Britain to defend its record before European opinion (owner §8.4 D).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** MOD-2164, MOD-2162.
**Trap:** India House is London, not Bengal (owner).
**Mains hook:** Overseas networks widened the geography of the struggle.
**Back:** *"India House: Varma's London hub."*

### Flashcard MOD-2164 — Madan Lal Dhingra and the Curzon-Wyllie killing (1 July 1909)
**Front:** What did Madan Lal Dhingra do in 1909, and when?
**Definition (learn):** Dhingra killed Curzon-Wyllie in London on 1 July 1909 (✅ owner 16 §4, §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Victim: Curzon-Wyllie (London).
- Effect: India House came under intense scrutiny (owner adv mini-timeline).
- Not Curzon the Viceroy; the victim was Sir William Curzon-Wyllie.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 July 1909 (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Links:** MOD-2163.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Curzon-Wyllie with Lord Curzon the Viceroy (⚠️ the owner's text names only Curzon-Wyllie; the identity note is standard knowledge).
**Mains hook:** Overseas assassination as propaganda.
**Back:** *"1 July 1909 London: Dhingra, Curzon-Wyllie."*

### Flashcard MOD-2165 — Ajit Singh and Lala Lajpat Rai: the Punjab agrarian current (1907)
**Front:** How did Punjab's revolutionary current link to agrarian unrest?
**Definition (learn):** Ajit Singh and Lala Lajpat Rai were associated with the 1907 agrarian unrest in Punjab; this was the one point where the revolutionary current touched a mass constituency (✅ owner 16 §2, §8.4 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Agrarian grievance, not student secret societies.
- Lajpat Rai: Extremist leader, not a secret-society member (owner §8.4 C limit).
- Military link: Punjab's agrarian and military base (owner §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1907 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MOD-2144, MOD-2166.
**Trap:** Keep Lajpat Rai's public role distinct from armed conspiracy (✅ owner 16 §8.4 C).
**Mains hook:** Punjab as the agrarian-military variant.
**Back:** *"Punjab 1907: agrarian anger met the revolution."*

### Flashcard MOD-2166 — Abhinav Bharat and V.D. Savarkar
**Front:** What was Abhinav Bharat, and who is associated with it?
**Definition (learn):** An armed nationalist current in Maharashtra associated with V.D. Savarkar (✅ owner 16 §1, §2, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Region: Maharashtra.
- Figure: V.D. Savarkar.
- Contrast: Savarkar's later *Hindutva* (1923) is a different idea (MOD-2184).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** MOD-2158, MOD-2184.
**Trap:** Do not conflate Savarkar's 1900s armed current with his 1923 *Hindutva* identity framework (⚠️ owner 17 for 1923).
**Mains hook:** Savarkar's arc across two currents.
**Back:** *"Abhinav Bharat: Savarkar's armed Maharashtra."*

### Flashcard MOD-2167 — Delhi Conspiracy (23 December 1912): the Hardinge bomb
**Front:** What was the Delhi Conspiracy, and who was linked to it?
**Definition (learn):** A bomb was thrown at Viceroy Hardinge on 23 December 1912 during the imperial capital ceremony; Rash Behari Bose and Sachin Sanyal are linked (✅ owner 16 §4, §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 23 December 1912 (owner §9).
- Target: Hardinge.
- Figures: Rash Behari Bose; Sachin Sanyal (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 23 December 1912 (✅ owner 16 §9).
**Links:** MOD-2168, MOD-2169.
**Trap:** The Delhi Conspiracy is a 1912 India event, not a Ghadar event in North America (✅ owner 16 §5).
**Mains hook:** Capital shift and the attempt on the Viceroy.
**Back:** *"23 December 1912: the Hardinge bomb in Delhi."*

### Flashcard MOD-2168 — Rash Behari Bose and Sachin Sanyal: the Ghadar question (2022 PYQ)
**Front:** Which of the three named figures in the 2022 Prelims Q53 route is supported as Ghadar-associated?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's supplementary evidence identifies only Rash Behari Bose among the three named figures as Ghadar-associated (✅ owner 16 §9). The answer remains INFERRED — LOCAL OFFICIAL KEY UNAVAILABLE.
**Breakdown:**
- Rash Behari Bose: Delhi Conspiracy and wartime plan (owner §8.4 E).
- Sachin Sanyal: Delhi Conspiracy (owner).
- Third named figure: not identified in the owner's supplementary note (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 Prelims Q53 (owner §9).
**Links:** MOD-2167, MOD-2169.
**Trap:** Do not state the answer letter; the official key is not in the repository.
**Mains hook:** Use the Ghadar network answer in a comparison question.
**Back:** *"Rash Behari: Ghadar-linked, inferred, not keyed."*

### Flashcard MOD-2169 — Ghadar's founding: the 1913 date and the place (source conflict)
**Front:** When and where was the Ghadar movement founded, and what was its first base?
**Definition (learn):** The owner dates the Ghadar organisation to 1913 and places its headquarters at San Francisco; the Hindi Association of the Pacific Coast formed the base (✅ owner 16 §4). Wikipedia dates the party's formation to 15 July 1913, at a founding conference at Astoria, Oregon, and names the Pacific Coast Hindustan Association (formed at Portland in 1912) as its predecessor (✅ Wikipedia: Ghadar Party).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: Hindi Association of the Pacific Coast → Ghadar; San Francisco as headquarters (✅ owner 16).
- Wikipedia: founding conference Astoria, 15 July 1913; Yugantar Ashram in San Francisco (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1913 (✅ both); founding city ⚠️ conflict.
**Links:** MOD-2170, MOD-2168.
**Trap:** ⚠️ Source conflict: where the organisation was founded (owner: San Francisco; Wikipedia: Astoria, Oregon). Use "founded in North America in 1913; headquarters at San Francisco" in exam answers.
**Mains hook:** Ghadar drew on Punjabi migrant labourers on the Pacific coast (owner §8.4 E).
**Back:** *"1913, North America; Astoria founded, San Francisco housed."*

### Flashcard MOD-2170 — Ghadr paper, Yugantar Ashram and the Ghadar leaders
**Front:** Who led the Ghadar Party, and what were the paper and the headquarters?
**Definition (learn):** The Ghadar Party's paper was *Ghadr* (also *Hindustan Ghadar*), with the Urdu edition from 1 November 1913 and the Punjabi edition from 9 December 1913; the headquarters was the Yugantar Ashram at 436 Hill Street, San Francisco (✅ Wikipedia: Ghadar Party).
**Breakdown:**
- Sohan Singh Bhakna: president (✅ Wikipedia).
- Lala Har Dayal: general secretary and Urdu editor; arrested March 1914, fled to Europe (✅ Wikipedia).
- Kartar Singh Sarabha: Punjabi editor and fundraiser (✅ Wikipedia).
- Ram Chandra took leadership after Har Dayal left (✅ Wikipedia).
- Flag: red, yellow, green (November 1913) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 November 1913; 9 December 1913 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2169, MOD-2172.
**Trap:** *Ghadr* was the paper; Ghadar Party was the organisation (✅ owner 16 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the paper's rifles-and-blood line (Wikipedia) only as paraphrase.
**Back:** *"Ghadr the paper, Ghadar the party, Yugantar the house."*

### Flashcard MOD-2171 — Komagata Maru incident (1914)
**Front:** What was the Komagata Maru incident and how did it affect overseas Indians?
**Definition (learn):** The 1914 Komagata Maru incident sharpened overseas Indian anger at racial exclusion (✅ owner 16 §1, §4). Wikipedia links it to the Budge Budge firing and to Mewa Singh's 1914 killing of Hopkinson (✅ Wikipedia: Ghadar Party).
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1914.
- Effect: diasporic resentment; Ghadar mobilisation.
- Budge Budge firing (per Wikipedia, in India).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1914 (✅ owner 16; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2170, MOD-2172.
**Trap:** Do not place the incident in the Pacific coast only; the aftermath was in India (Budge Budge) per Wikipedia.
**Mains hook:** Racial exclusion as a driver of transnational politics.
**Back:** *"1914: exclusion at sea, firing at Budge Budge."*

### Flashcard MOD-2172 — The 1915 mutiny plan (21 February 1915)
**Front:** What was the 1915 Ghadar-linked mutiny plan, and why did it fail?
**Definition (learn):** A plan for a mutiny in India set for 21 February 1915; around 8,000 emigrants were reported to have returned; British intelligence anticipated it and many were arrested on arrival at ports (✅ Wikipedia: Ghadar Party; ✅ owner 16 §8.4 E, §9 "failed rising (February 1915)").
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 21 February 1915 (Wikipedia).
- Scale: around 8,000 returnees (Wikipedia).
- Failure: intelligence penetration and arrests (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 21 February 1915 (✅ Wikipedia); ⚠️ the 8,000 figure is Wikipedia's estimate only, not confirmed elsewhere.
**Links:** MOD-2171, MOD-2175.
**Trap:** Present as an attempted rising, not an insurrection (✅ owner 16 §8.4 E).
**Mains hook:** The plan's failure shows the limits of diaspora networks without mass support.
**Back:** *"21 February 1915: 8,000 returned, many arrested."*

### Flashcard MOD-2173 — Madame Cama is not the Ghadar founder
**Front:** Why must Madame Cama be kept separate from the Ghadar Party?
**Definition (learn):** Madame Cama was linked with overseas propaganda and the Stuttgart flag (1907), not with the founding of the Ghadar Party in North America (✅ owner 16 §5, §8.4 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Cama: European propaganda.
- Ghadar: North American organisation.
- Keep propaganda networks and the Ghadar organisation distinct (owner §8.4 D).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1907 (Stuttgart); 1913 (Ghadar).
**Links:** MOD-2162, MOD-2169.
**Trap:** "Madame Cama founded the Ghadar Party" is a false statement (✅ owner 16 §5).
**Mains hook:** Use to distinguish two transnational channels.
**Back:** *"Cama flew the flag; Ghadar printed the paper."*

### Flashcard MOD-2174 — Ghadar did not begin in Bengal; the terrorism label
**Front:** Why is "Ghadar began in Bengal in 1905" a trap, and why avoid "revolutionary terrorism"?
**Definition (learn):** Ghadar was founded in North America in 1913 (MOD-2169), not in Bengal in 1905. "Revolutionary terrorism" is an older colonial-era label; the owner recommends "revolutionary nationalism" or "armed revolutionary activity" (✅ owner 16 §5, §8.4 A).
**Breakdown:**
- Bengal: Anushilan and Jugantar (MOD-2161).
- Ghadar: North America, 1913.
- Label: colonial vs nationalist usage (owner historiography note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1905 (Bengal Swadeshi, not Ghadar); 1913 (Ghadar).
**Links:** MOD-2161, MOD-2169.
**Trap:** Do not use Gandhian mass satyagraha as the comparison term without qualification (⚠️ owner 16 §5).
**Mains hook:** Terminology is part of the historiographical argument (owner adv historiography).
**Back:** *"Not Bengal 1905, not terror: Ghadar was 1913 North America."*

### Flashcard MOD-2175 — 1917 San Francisco conspiracy trial and the Ghadar aftermath
**Front:** What happened at the 1917 San Francisco trial, and what became of the Ghadar Party?
**Definition (learn):** The 1917 San Francisco Hindu–German Conspiracy Trial indicted 105 people, of whom 29 (15 Indians, 14 Germans) were convicted; the party was dissolved in 1947–48 (✅ Wikipedia: Ghadar Party).
**Breakdown:**
- Indictments: 105; convictions: 29 (Wikipedia).
- Dissolution: 1947–48 (Wikipedia).
- Owner's PYQ route: 2022 Q53 (MOD-2168).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1917; 105 indicted; 29 convicted; 1947–48 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2169, MOD-2168.
**Trap:** The figures are Wikipedia's and not cross-checked with a second source; treat as ⚠️ if used in Mains.
**Mains hook:** The state response to transnational conspiracy.
**Back:** *"1917 SF: 105 indicted, 29 convicted."*

### Flashcard MOD-2176 — Limits and contribution of revolutionary nationalism
**Front:** What did revolutionary nationalism achieve, and what were its strategic limits?
**Definition (learn):** Contribution: psychological defiance, martyrdom, internationalisation of the cause. Limits: secrecy, informers, small cells, no mass base, no peasant or worker programme; "heroic but organisationally narrow" (✅ owner 16 §2, §8.4 F, §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Achievement: morale and memory, not power (owner §8.7).
- Limit: organisational ceiling (owner §8.4 F).
- Cost to state: repression, deportation, special legislation (owner §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric; do not quantify revolutionaries (owner §8.8).
**Links:** MOD-2177, MOD-2157.
**Trap:** Do not conclude the movement was futile (owner §8.4 F limit).
**Mains hook:** "Changed what Indians believed possible, never what the state could do" (owner §8.7).
**Back:** *"Morale yes, mass no."*

### Flashcard MOD-2177 — Phase I versus later revolutionary socialism
**Front:** How does Phase I (1907–17) differ from the later revolutionary socialist turn?
**Definition (learn):** Phase I rests on heroic conspiracy and individual sacrifice with a different theory of change from the socialist revolutionary turn of the late 1920s, which is covered in Topic 21 (✅ owner 16 §9 boundary).
**Breakdown:**
- Phase I: Bengal, Maharashtra, Punjab, overseas.
- Later: HRA, HSRA, Kakori and Bhagat Singh (owner boundary; not in this owner).
- Keep ideology distinct (owner §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2157, MOD-2176.
**Trap:** Do not attribute later HSRA ideology to 1907–17 (✅ owner 16 §8.4 B).
**Mains hook:** Compare Phase I's clandestine strategy with open mass politics under Gandhi (owner adv §7).
**Back:** *"1907–17 conspiracy; 1920s socialism is another book."*

### GROUP — Growth of Communalism & the Muslim League

### Flashcard MOD-2178 — Communalism as a modern political ideology
**Front:** How is communalism defined in the Bipin Chandra framework used by the owner, and what is it not?
**Definition (learn):** Communalism is a modern political ideology that converts religious identity into claims about shared secular interests, separating one religious community's interests from others'. It is not religion, religious reform, personal religiosity or a riot (✅ owner 17 §1, §8.4; ⚠️ the framework is an owner-attributed interpretation).
**Breakdown:**
- Bedrock: co-religionists assumed to share secular interests (MOD-2179 stages).
- Roots: colonial structures, middle-class competition, political underdevelopment, weak secular mobilisation (adv §1).
- Modernist, instrumentalist explanation rather than primordial hatred (adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2179, MOD-2191, MOD-2194.
**Trap:** Communalism is not "ancient Hindu-Muslim hatred" (✅ owner 17 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Communalism was born in the modern colonial public sphere" (✅ owner 17 §8.8).
**Back:** *"Faith used as interest, not faith itself."*

### Flashcard MOD-2179 — Chandra's three stages: from common interests to hostility
**Front:** What are the three stages in Bipin Chandra's model, and which are "liberal" and which "extreme"?
**Definition (learn):** Stage 1: co-religionists share common secular interests; Stage 2: those interests are seen as divergent from other communities; Stage 3: they are seen as mutually incompatible and hostile. Stages 1–2 are "liberal" (mild) communalism; Stage 3 is "extreme or fascistic" communalism (✅ owner 17 adv §1, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Locate a movement on this ladder: higher-order answer than calling it "communal" (owner §8.4).
- Label is the author's judgement and should be attributed (adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2178, MOD-2194.
**Trap:** Do not present the "liberal/extreme" labels as neutral classification of organisations (owner adv §1).
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to test any movement's communal character.
**Back:** *"Shared, divergent, hostile: the three rungs."*

### Flashcard MOD-2180 — Simla Deputation (October 1906)
**Front:** What was the Simla Deputation, when was it, and who is said to have led it?
**Definition (learn):** A deputation of Muslim notables that petitioned Viceroy Minto for separate Muslim representation in October 1906, shortly before the League's founding (✅ Wikipedia: All-India Muslim League, October 1906, "successful"; ⚠️ owner 17 names the Aga Khan as leader, which the fetched Wikipedia text does not state).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: October 1906 (✅ Wikipedia); Minto received it on 1 October 1906 (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Councils Act 1909).
- Demand: separate Muslim representation (✅ owner 17).
- The League reconsidered its party issue after the deputation (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 October 1906 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2181, MOD-2084, MOD-2182.
**Trap:** Do not assert an official conspiracy behind the deputation (owner §8.5 A caution).
**Mains hook:** The documented claim is the concession and its consequences, not a scripted plot (✅ owner 17 §8.5 A).
**Back:** *"October 1906: the notables ask Minto."*

### Flashcard MOD-2181 — All-India Muslim League: Dacca, 30 December 1906
**Front:** When and where was the All-India Muslim League founded, and who proposed the name?
**Definition (learn):** Founded 30 December 1906 at the Dacca session of the All-India Muhammadan Educational Conference; Khwaja Salimullah, Nawab of Dacca, proposed the party and the name (✅ Wikipedia: All-India Muslim League).
**Breakdown:**
- Venue: Dacca (Wikipedia text gives Ahsan Manzil and Israt Manzil inconsistently).
- Aga Khan III named first honorary president; did not attend; resigned in 1912 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: leaders Aga Khan and Nawab Salimullah (✅ owner 17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 30 December 1906 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2180, MOD-2187, MOD-2189.
**Trap:** The League is not a post-1918 body; it dates from 1906 (✅ owner 17 §5).
**Mains hook:** Elite foundation; early loyalist phase (adv §5).
**Back:** *"Dacca, 30 December 1906: Salimullah's name."*

### Flashcard MOD-2182 — Separate electorates as institutionalised communal politics
**Front:** Why did separate electorates institutionalise communalism rather than merely reflect it?
**Definition (learn):** They converted community identity into a constitutional category and rewarded leaders who claimed to speak for religious blocs; after 1909 a Muslim politician's constituency was legally only Muslims (✅ owner 17 §1, §8.2 institutionalisation thesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Electoral payoff: communal appeal rewarded; cross-communal appeal not.
- Institutional stake: a permanent structural interest.
- Counterfactual caution (owner §8.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1909 (MOD-2084).
**Links:** MOD-2084, MOD-2146, MOD-2186.
**Trap:** Separate electorates did not create communal sentiment alone; they converted and rewarded it (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** "Separate electorates converted a competitive elite ideology into a permanent constitutional structure" (✅ owner 17 §8.8).
**Back:** *"Identity made into a ballot rule."*

### Flashcard MOD-2183 — All-India Hindu Mahasabha (1915, Haridwar)
**Front:** When and where was the Hindu Mahasabha founded, and which leader is named as founder?
**Definition (learn):** The Hindu Mahasabha umbrella body (Sarvadeshak Hindu Sabha) was formed at Haridwar in April 1915 during the Kumbh Mela; Madan Mohan Malaviya is named as founder in the Wikipedia infobox and lead (✅ Wikipedia: Akhil Bharat Hindu Mahasabha). Owner 17 gives 1915 as the emergence year (⚠️ classification follows Chandra's framework).
**Breakdown:**
- Precursors: Punjab Hindu Sabha (1909); a 1913 Ambala resolution (✅ Wikipedia).
- Preparatory sessions: Haridwar 13 Feb, Lucknow 17 Feb, Delhi 27 Feb 1915 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Renamed Akhil Bharat Hindu Mahasabha in April 1921 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Early role: pressure group for orthodox Hindu interests working within Congress (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** April 1915 Haridwar; April 1921 renaming (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2184, MOD-2181.
**Trap:** The owner's "1915 emergence" and the Wikipedia "April 1915 Haridwar founding" are the same year; the Mahasabha did not become a political party until 1933 per the Wikipedia infobox (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Organised Hindu communal politics expanded after 1909–1916 (owner §8.5 B).
**Back:** *"1915 Haridwar: Malaviya's pressure group."*

### Flashcard MOD-2184 — Savarkar's *Hindutva* (1923) and the RSS (1925)
**Front:** What were Savarkar's *Hindutva* (1923) and the RSS (1925), and how does the owner classify them?
**Definition (learn):** *Hindutva* (1923) articulated a political-cultural Hindu identity framework; the RSS was founded in 1925. The owner places both under the later Hindu communal trajectory following Chandra's framework (✅ owner 17 adv mini-timeline; ⚠️ the 1923 and 1925 dates are owner-stated and not verified on a fetched page).
**Breakdown:**
- *Hindutva*: 1923 (owner).
- RSS: 1925 (owner).
- Classification: attributed to the owner's framework (owner §8.5 B limit).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1923; 1925 (⚠️ owner).
**Links:** MOD-2183, MOD-2166.
**Trap:** Hindu communal bodies did develop before Partition; do not say they emerged only after 1947 (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use as a bounded later trajectory, not the main Muslim League story.
**Back:** *"1923 Hindutva, 1925 RSS: later, not after 1947."*

### Flashcard MOD-2185 — Elite competition as the socio-economic root
**Front:** What socio-economic root does the owner identify for communalism?
**Definition (learn):** Competition among educated middle classes of different communities for scarce government posts, professional openings and legislative seats created by the colonial economy (✅ owner 17 §8.2, §8.5 C).
**Breakdown:**
- Uneven development between and within communities (owner).
- Timing: grew as education and office-seeking expanded, not as religiosity intensified (owner §8.5 C).
- Strongest among professional classes, not the rural poor (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2178, MOD-2182.
**Trap:** Do not reduce communalism to economics alone (owner §8.5 C limit).
**Mains hook:** Elite-competition thesis (owner §8.2).
**Back:** *"Office and seats fed the ideology."*

### Flashcard MOD-2186 — Montagu–Chelmsford (1919): communal representation extended
**Front:** How did the 1919 scheme extend communal representation, and what three mechanisms must be kept distinct?
**Definition (learn):** Under the Montagu–Chelmsford scheme communal representation and reservation were retained and extended: Sikhs received separate electorates, seats were reserved for non-Brahmins in Madras, and "depressed classes" were offered nominated seats, despite theoretical criticism of separate electorates in the Report (✅ owner 17 §8.5 E).
**Breakdown:**
- Separate electorates: Muslims (1909), Sikhs (1919).
- Reserved seats: non-Brahmins in Madras (owner).
- Nominated seats: depressed classes (owner).
- Keep the three mechanisms distinct (owner §8.5 E limit).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1919 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** MOD-2182, MOD-2190, MOD-2188.
**Trap:** Nominated and reserved seats are not separate electorates (✅ owner 17 §8.5 E).
**Mains hook:** The logic generalised to any identity the state chose to recognise.
**Back:** *"1919: Sikhs elect separately; others reserved or nominated."*

### Flashcard MOD-2187 — Lucknow Pact (1916): unity on communal terms
**Front:** What did the Lucknow Pact of 1916 achieve, and what did it concede?
**Definition (learn):** The Lucknow Pact produced Congress–League cooperation on a joint reform demand, while Congress accepted separate electorates with weightage (✅ owner 17 §8.5 D, §4; owner 18 cross-reference).
**Breakdown:**
- Achievement: temporary unity.
- Concession: separate electorates (accepted, not introduced).
- Historians disagree whether the bargain was necessary or a strategic error (owner §8.5 D caution).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1916 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** MOD-2197, MOD-2189, MOD-2182.
**Trap:** The Pact accepted separate electorates; it did not introduce them (✅ owner 17 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "The paradox: inter-communal agreement possible, with communal representation its currency" (✅ owner 17 §8.5 D).
**Back:** *"1916: united on the communal ballot."*

### Flashcard MOD-2188 — Communal Award and Poona Pact: the forward link
**Front:** How does the owner link the 1919 extension to later constitutional episodes?
**Definition (learn):** The 1919 extension is the background to the later Communal Award and Poona Pact (see basic/22) (✅ owner 17 §8.5 E); the Communal Award (1932) and Poona Pact are not verified here.
**Breakdown:**
- 1919: logic generalised.
- 1932: Communal Award (owner mechanism).
- Poona Pact: owner cross-refers to basic/22.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner mechanism lists 1932 Communal Award (adv §8.6); ⚠️ not fetched.
**Links:** MOD-2186, MOD-2189.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1919 extension with the 1932 award.
**Mains hook:** Use the chain 1909 → 1919 → 1932 → 1940 → 1947.
**Back:** *"1919 to 1932 to Partition: one logic."*

### Flashcard MOD-2189 — Lahore Resolution (23 March 1940) and the "independent States" wording
**Front:** When was the Lahore Resolution adopted, who moved it, and what did it say?
**Definition (learn):** Adopted on 23 March 1940, moved by A.K. Fazlul Huq; it recommitted the League to independent Muslim states in the north-west and east, "wholly autonomous and sovereign" (✅ Wikipedia: All-India Muslim League). ⚠️ Source conflict on wording: the owner says the resolution used "independent states" and did not name Pakistan; the fetched Wikipedia page did not find that wording and cites "independent Muslim state" (owner-cited text).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 23 March 1940.
- Mover: A.K. Fazlul Huq (Wikipedia).
- Territories: Sindh, Punjab, Baluchistan, NWFP and Bengal (Wikipedia).
- Owner: later Pakistan demand trajectory; the word "Pakistan" not used.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 23 March 1940 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-2192, MOD-2198.
**Trap:** Do not state the resolution used the word "Pakistan"; the wording issue is ⚠️ until verified against the text.
**Mains hook:** Later stage of the Pakistan demand trajectory (owner §8.5).
**Back:** *"23 March 1940: independent states, Huq moves."*

### Flashcard MOD-2190 — Trap: separate, reserved and nominated seats
**Front:** How do separate electorates, reserved seats and nomination differ?
**Definition (learn):** Separate electorates: only members of a community vote for that community's seat. Reserved seats: seats set aside, which may be elected by general electorate. Nomination: appointed, not elected (✅ owner 17 §5 and §8.5 E; the definitions are the standard constitutional distinction, ⚠️ the owner does not define them in full).
**Breakdown:**
- Separate electorates: Muslims (1909); Sikhs (1919).
- Reserved: non-Brahmins in Madras (1919 per owner).
- Nominated: depressed classes (1919 per owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1909; 1919 (✅ owner).
**Links:** MOD-2186, MOD-2182.
**Trap:** Do not merge the three mechanisms in an answer (✅ owner 17 mechanism control).
**Mains hook:** Use the three-way distinction for a precise constitutional answer.
**Back:** *"Own voters, reserved seats, appointed seats."*

### Flashcard MOD-2191 — Trap: communalism is not religion
**Front:** Why is "communalism is religion" a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Communalism is the political use of religious identity; religion, religiosity, religious reform and communal riots are different things (✅ owner 17 §5, §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- Religion: belief and practice.
- Religious reform: Chapter 10 movements; not communal by definition.
- Communal riot: an event, not an ideology.
- Owner caution: use communalism for political ideology only (adv §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2178, MOD-2026.
**Trap:** Do not present religious reform as communal (✅ owner 17 §8.4).
**Mains hook:** Keep the three categories apart when comparing reform and communalism.
**Back:** *"Politics wearing a faith."*

### Flashcard MOD-2192 — Trap: the League was not founded in 1919 and did not demand Pakistan from birth
**Front:** What two League chronology traps does the owner warn about?
**Definition (learn):** The League was founded in 1906 at Dacca (not after World War I); its early elite loyalist phase differed from the later Lahore Resolution trajectory, so it did not demand Pakistan from its birth (✅ owner 17 §5, adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Founded 1906 (MOD-2181).
- Pakistan demand: later (1940 Lahore, MOD-2189).
- Separate electorates 1909, not 1919 (MOD-2148).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1906; 1909; 1940 (✅ owner).
**Links:** MOD-2181, MOD-2189, MOD-2148.
**Trap:** The two traps appear together in exam questions on dates.
**Mains hook:** Trace the League's trajectory by stage, not as a single demand.
**Back:** *"1906 founded, 1940 demanded, never 1919."*

### Flashcard MOD-2193 — Nationalist limitation: fighting communalism without dismantling its framework
**Front:** Why could nationalism not defeat communalism, according to the owner?
**Definition (learn):** The movement used Hindu cultural symbolism, negotiated within a communal electoral framework it could not abolish, and treated communalism episodically (at moments of agitation) rather than continuously as an ideology (✅ owner 17 §8.5 F; ⚠️ owner's causal argument).
**Breakdown:**
- Symbolism: Hindu idiom (MOD-2154).
- Framework: separate electorates (MOD-2182).
- Episodic response: unity at agitation peaks.
- Concede genuine achievements: Lucknow, Khilafat–Non-Cooperation, formal secularism (owner §8.5 F limit).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2187, MOD-2154, MOD-2198.
**Trap:** Do not present the failure as wholly the nationalists' fault (✅ owner 17 §8.5 F limit).
**Mains hook:** "Every episode of unity rested on the very framework it needed to dismantle" (✅ owner 17 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Unity on the ballot, never beyond it."*

### Flashcard MOD-2194 — Modernist versus primordialist historiography
**Front:** What is the key historiographical contrast on communalism, and which view should an answer adopt?
**Definition (learn):** Instrumentalist or modernist explanations (Bipin Chandra) link communalism to colonial policy, political economy and elite competition; primordialist views treat Hindu–Muslim conflict as ancient and continuous. The owner recommends the modernist argument (✅ owner 17 adv historiography note).
**Breakdown:**
- Modernist: communalism as organised ideology from modern colonial conditions.
- Primordialist: continuous ancient conflict.
- Balanced: religious difference existed earlier; organised communalism is modern (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2178, MOD-2179.
**Trap:** "Communalism is timeless" is the primordialist trap (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** "Evaluate Bipin Chandra's view that communalism was a modern ideology" (✅ owner 17 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Modern politics, not ancient hatred."*

### Flashcard MOD-2195 — Justice Party and the League's weak pre-1937 showing
**Front:** Why does the owner say communal politics was not inevitable?
**Definition (learn):** Communal organisations remained electorally weak for long periods; the Justice Party's collapse and the League's poor showing before 1937 show that communal politics needed specific conditions to succeed (✅ owner 17 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Justice Party: electoral limits (MOD-2046).
- League: poor pre-1937 showing (owner).
- Conditions: specific electoral and social triggers.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937 (owner reference) (⚠️ 1937 election details not in owner).
**Links:** MOD-2046, MOD-2189.
**Trap:** Do not assume communal electoral success before 1937 (owner §8.7).
**Mains hook:** Counter-evidence to inevitability.
**Back:** *"Weak before 1937: conditions made it strong."*

### Flashcard MOD-2196 — Regional variation: Bengal and Punjab against the United Provinces
**Front:** How did communal politics vary by region?
**Definition (learn):** Bengal and Punjab intersected with agrarian class divisions; the United Provinces were primarily a service-and-office competition (✅ owner 17 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Bengal and Punjab: class and agrarian overlay.
- United Provinces: office and service competition.
- Muslim opinion never unified; Deobandi, nationalist-Muslim, regional and class currents opposed the League (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** MOD-2185, MOD-2199.
**Trap:** Do not use one regional label for all of India (owner §8.7).
**Mains hook:** Use regional variation to avoid a single narrative.
**Back:** *"Punjab's fields, UP's offices."*

### Flashcard MOD-2197 — The Lucknow paradox: unity and concession
**Front:** What is the Lucknow paradox, and what is the trap in calling it a disproof of communalism?
**Definition (learn):** The national movement's greatest early unity against communal division (Lucknow 1916) was also a concession to communal representation. The trap: "Lucknow 1916 disproved communal politics" is false; it was temporary and rested on the separate-electorate principle (✅ owner 17 §5, §8.5 D).
**Breakdown:**
- Achievement: Congress–League agreement.
- Concession: separate electorates.
- Historiographical dispute: necessary tactic or strategic error (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1916 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** MOD-2187, MOD-2193.
**Trap:** "Lucknow ended communal politics" is a trap (✅ owner 17 §5).
**Mains hook:** Present both readings.
**Back:** *"Lucknow 1916: a truce on communal terms."*

### Flashcard MOD-2198 — Mains route: why the national movement could not defeat communalism (2018 GS-I)
**Front:** What is the Mains route on why nationalism could not defeat communalism, and what is the verdict?
**Definition (learn):** The 2018 GS-I question is direct to this owner (✅ owner 17 PYQ control); the owner's verdict is that the movement achieved communal unity but not communal transformation (✅ owner 17 §8.8). The provisional 2026 Prelims Q18 route is routed without promoting a provisional answer (✅ owner 17 PYQ control).
**Breakdown:**
- Structure: successes, structural constraints, strategic errors, verdict.
- Evidence: Lucknow 1916; Khilafat–Non-Cooperation; formal secularism; separate electorates; Hindu symbolism.
- Counterpoint: inevitability denied; regional variation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-I (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** MOD-2193, MOD-2187, MOD-2199.
**Trap:** Do not present a provisional key as official.
**Mains hook:** "The movement repeatedly achieved communal unity and never achieved communal transformation" (✅ owner 17 §8.8).
**Back:** *"Unity achieved, transformation never."*

### Flashcard MOD-2199 — No collective blame; intra-community diversity
**Front:** Why must an answer never assign collective agency or blame to a religious community?
**Definition (learn):** The analytical subjects are ideologies, organisations and elites, not religious communities; Muslim and Hindu opinion were both internally diverse (✅ owner 17 §8.7, §8.5 caution).
**Breakdown:**
- Analysis: organisations and elites.
- Diversity: Deobandi, nationalist-Muslim, regional and class currents (owner).
- Avoid communal riot narratives, casualty figures or population percentages (owner §8.9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MOD-2196, MOD-2191.
**Trap:** Do not write collective religious blame (owner caution).
**Mains hook:** Careful framing protects the analysis.
**Back:** *"Blame ideas and elites, never a faith."*

### Flashcard MOD-2200 — Causal chain: from census to Partition
**Front:** What is the owner's causal mechanism from colonial enumeration to Partition?
**Definition (learn):** Colonial census and representation by community, uneven English education, and scarce posts → community-based claim-making → Simla 1906 → League 1906 → separate electorates 1909 → electoral incentives → 1919 extension → communal units in every negotiation → 1932 Communal Award → 1940 Lahore → 1947 Partition (✅ owner 17 §8.6; ⚠️ the causal chain is an owner analytical model).
**Breakdown:**
- Inputs: census, education, posts.
- Institutions: separate electorates; 1919 extension.
- Endpoints: Communal Award, Lahore, Partition (basic/22, basic/27 links).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1906; 1909; 1919; 1932; 1940; 1947 (owner).
**Links:** MOD-2180, MOD-2181, MOD-2182, MOD-2186, MOD-2189.
**Trap:** Do not present the chain as a proven conspiracy (owner §8.5 A).
**Mains hook:** Use the chain for a 20-mark causal answer.
**Back:** *"Census to Partition, step by step."*

### Flashcard MOD-2201 — Separate electorates: the permanent structural verdict
**Front:** What is the institutional verdict on separate electorates?
**Definition (learn):** "Separate electorates converted a competitive elite ideology into a permanent constitutional structure, and no later negotiation could be conducted outside it" (✅ owner 17 §8.8). The owner's PYQ control records 2018 GS-I as direct and the 2026 Prelims route as provisional (✅ owner 17 PYQ control).
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: incentive, structure, negotiation frame.
- Counterfactual: caution (owner §8.1).
- Owner boundary: Topic 10 religious reform; Topic 18 Lucknow provisions; Topic 20 Khilafat; Topic 27 Partition.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1909; 1916 (✅ owner 17).
**Links:** MOD-2182, MOD-2200, MOD-2197.
**Trap:** Separate electorates did not merely reflect communalism; they helped produce and reward it (✅ owner 17 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** "Identity made into a ballot rule" as a single-line verdict.
**Back:** *"Once in the ballot, never out."*

## MODERN INDIAN HISTORY — Part 3: WWI to the Government of India Act 1935

### GROUP — 18 First World War, Home Rule League and the Lucknow Pact

### Flashcard MOD-3001 — WWI, Home Rule and Lucknow: the core chain
**Front:** How did the First World War move Indian politics from loyal petition to assertive demand, and where do the Home Rule Leagues and the Lucknow Pact sit in that chain?
**Definition (learn):** A causal chain: Indian war contribution and expectation of reward → wartime hardship and ideological contradiction → Home Rule agitation and Congress–League unity → official promise of gradual responsible government (Montagu Declaration, 20 August 1917).
**Breakdown:**
- Expectation: India gave men, money and materials to Britain (✅ owner 18).
- Hardship: inflation, heavier taxation, war loans, shortages, recruitment pressure (✅ owner 18).
- Agitation: Tilak's and Besant's Home Rule Leagues (1916) (✅ owner 18).
- Unity: Lucknow Congress and Congress–League Pact (December 1916) (✅ owner 18).
- Promise: Montagu Declaration, 20 August 1917 (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** WWI 1914–18 (✅ owner 18). Tilak's League April 1916; Besant's League September 1916; Lucknow December 1916; Montagu 20 August 1917 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MOD-3002, MOD-3003, MOD-3007, MOD-3010, MOD-3020 (next phase: Gandhi and Rowlatt).
**Trap:** Do not treat the war as the sole cause; the Leagues and the Lucknow reunion were already in motion. Keep the war as an accelerant and expectation-breaker.
**Mains hook:** Discuss whether World War I "removed the arguments" with which Britain postponed reform (qualified: war accelerated a nationalism that already existed).
**Back:** *"Hope, hardship, Home Rule, Lucknow, promise: five links, one ladder."*

### Flashcard MOD-3002 — Tilak's Home Rule League (April 1916)
**Front:** When, where and under whom was Tilak's Home Rule League founded, and what was its territorial remit?
**Definition (learn):** Tilak's Home Rule League was a constitutional agitation organisation demanding self-government within the Empire, founded in April 1916 at Belgaum (Bombay provincial congress).
**Breakdown:**
- Leader: Bal Gangadhar Tilak.
- Zone: Maharashtra (excluding Bombay city), Karnataka, the Central Provinces and Berar (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Home Rule Movement).
- Methods: propaganda, newspapers, lectures, branches (✅ owner 18).
- Slogan: "Swaraj is my birthright and I shall have it" (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** April 1916, Belgaum (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Home Rule Movement; ✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MOD-3003 (Besant's League), MOD-3004 (trap), MOD-3005 (meaning of Home Rule).
**Trap:** Tilak's League is not the Besant League; Tilak's zone is western and central India, Besant's the rest of India.
**Mains hook:** Assess the Home Rule Leagues as a bridge between Swadeshi and Gandhian mass politics (organisational reach, not demand, is the lasting gain).
**Back:** *"Tilak, Belgaum, April: Maharashtra, Karnataka, CP and Berar."*

### Flashcard MOD-3003 — Annie Besant's Home Rule League (September 1916)
**Front:** Where and when did Besant's Home Rule League begin, who were its key associates, and what happened to Besant in 1917?
**Definition (learn):** Besant's Home Rule League, founded September 1916 from Adyar (Madras), was an all-India (rest-of-India) Home Rule organisation built on her Theosophical and English-press networks.
**Breakdown:**
- Founder: Annie Besant (✅ owner 18).
- Associates: George Arundale and B.P. Wadia (✅ owner 18; ✅ Wikipedia: Indian Home Rule Movement).
- Repression: Besant interned June 1917, which intensified sympathy for Home Rule (✅ owner 18).
- Office: Congress president in 1917 after release (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** September 1916, Adyar, Madras (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Home Rule Movement); internment June 1917 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MOD-3002, MOD-3004, MOD-3010.
**Trap:** Besant was a major nationalist leader, not only a social reformer; her League was a separate body from Tilak's.
**Mains hook:** Show how internment converted a moderate demand into a national cause; repression enlarged the movement.
**Back:** *"Besant, Adyar, September: Arundale, Wadia, June-1917 internment."*

### Flashcard MOD-3004 — Two Home Rule Leagues: the trap pair
**Front:** Did Tilak and Besant found one common Home Rule League?
**Definition (learn):** No. They founded two separate leagues in 1916 with different organisational zones and leadership.
**Breakdown:**
- Tilak: April 1916, Belgaum; western and central zones (MOD-3002).
- Besant: September 1916, Adyar; rest of India (MOD-3003).
- Both used the same methods: propaganda, branches, public meetings (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** April 1916 vs September 1916 (✅ owner 18; ✅ Wikipedia: Indian Home Rule Movement).
**Links:** MOD-3002, MOD-3003, MOD-3015 (renaming of the League in 1920).
**Trap:** The "one league" answer is a common distractor. Keep leader, date and place together to separate the two.
**Mains hook:** Use the two-league structure to argue that Home Rule had organisation before it had a national programme.
**Back:** *"Two leagues, two dates: Tilak-April-Belgaum, Besant-September-Adyar."*

### Flashcard MOD-3005 — What Home Rule meant
**Front:** What did the Home Rule demand mean, and how did it differ from Purna Swaraj?
**Definition (learn):** Home Rule was self-government for India within the British Empire, modelled on the Irish Home Rule demand; it was not complete independence.
**Breakdown:**
- Model: Irish Home Rule (✅ owner 18).
- Scope: self-government within the Empire (✅ owner 18).
- Contrast: Purna Swaraj (complete independence) was adopted only at Lahore, December 1929 (MOD-3092).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Irish model; Purna Swaraj Lahore 1929 (✅ owner 18; ✅ Wikipedia: Purna Swaraj).
**Links:** MOD-3001, MOD-3092 (Lahore), MOD-3005.
**Trap:** Do not equate "Home Rule" with "Purna Swaraj" in a comparison question; the owner lists this as a common error.
**Mains hook:** Assess why Home Rule's modest demand still mattered: it trained cadres and expectations for the Gandhian phase.
**Back:** *"Home Rule = self-rule within the Empire; Purna Swaraj = outside it (1929)."*

### Flashcard MOD-3006 — Lucknow Congress (December 1916): the double unity
**Front:** What did the Lucknow Congress of December 1916 achieve?
**Definition (learn):** The Lucknow session reunited the Moderates and Extremists inside the Congress and produced a Congress–League joint reform scheme.
**Breakdown:**
- Internal unity: Moderate–Extremist reunion; Tilak readmitted (✅ owner 18).
- External unity: Congress–League Pact (✅ owner 18; ✅ Wikipedia: Lucknow Pact, joint Congress–League session).
- Repairs the Surat split (✅ owner 18, adv).
- Owner date control: Congress readmission decision December 1915, Lucknow December 1916 kept separate (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** December 1916 Lucknow (✅ Wikipedia: Lucknow Pact).
**Links:** MOD-3007 (Pact provisions), MOD-3017 (trap).
**Trap:** Lucknow Congress is the session; the Pact is the joint scheme. Do not treat them as the same event.
**Mains hook:** Lucknow was both the highest point of nationalist unity and the start of institutional communal representation (double unity thesis).
**Back:** *"Lucknow 1916: Moderates and Extremists reunited, League joined."*

### Flashcard MOD-3007 — The Lucknow Pact provisions
**Front:** What electoral and constitutional terms did the Lucknow Pact contain, and what did Congress concede?
**Definition (learn):** The Congress–League scheme of December 1916 demanded reforms on a joint basis and accepted separate electorates and weightage for Muslims.
**Breakdown:**
- Congress accepted separate electorates for Muslims in Imperial and Provincial Legislative Councils (✅ Wikipedia: Lucknow Pact).
- Muslims were to receive one-third of the seats in the central councils (✅ Wikipedia: Lucknow Pact).
- Weightage: Muslims gained representation of nearly double their population share in Hindu-majority areas (✅ Wikipedia: Lucknow Pact).
- Safeguard: no bill affecting a community could pass unless three-quarters of that community's members backed it (✅ Wikipedia: Lucknow Pact).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** December 1916; separate electorates first introduced by the Indian Councils Act 1909 (✅ Wikipedia: Lucknow Pact; ✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MOD-3008 (ladder), MOD-3006, MOD-3014.
**Trap:** Separate electorates did not begin at Lucknow; Congress accepted an existing 1909 arrangement.
**Mains hook:** The Pact solved nationalist division by institutionalising communal representation; both halves are needed.
**Back:** *"Lucknow gave one-third, weightage and three-quarters: separate, not new."*

### Flashcard MOD-3008 — Separate electorates ladder, 1909 to 1932
**Front:** Trace the chronology of separate electorates from Morley-Minto to the Poona Pact.
**Definition (learn):** A ladder showing how communal electorates were introduced, accepted, extended and then modified.
**Breakdown:**
- 1909: Morley-Minto / Indian Councils Act 1909 introduces separate electorates for Muslims (✅ owner 18; ✅ Wikipedia: Lucknow Pact).
- December 1916: Congress accepts them in the Lucknow Pact (MOD-3007).
- 1919: Government of India Act extends communal representation to Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians and Europeans (✅ Wikipedia: Government of India Act 1919; MOD-3014).
- August 1932: Communal Award extends separate electorates to the Depressed Classes (✅ owner 22; MOD-3102).
- September 1932: Poona Pact replaces them with reserved seats in joint electorates (MOD-3103).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1909, 1916, 1919, August 1932, September 1932.
**Links:** MOD-3007, MOD-3014, MOD-3102, MOD-3103.
**Trap:** Do not place the Lucknow Pact as the origin of separate electorates; the Pact accepted them.
**Mains hook:** Each step widened the communal segmentation; Poona Pact is the only step that reversed the form, not the principle.
**Back:** *"Nine, Sixteen, Nineteen, Thirty-Two, Thirty-Two: separate, extended, then reserved."*

### Flashcard MOD-3009 — India's war contribution and wartime discontent
**Front:** What did India contribute to Britain in World War I, and what hardships followed?
**Definition (learn):** India supported Britain in WWI with men, money and materials; wartime strain generated discontent that made the empire's reform promises politically costly.
**Breakdown:**
- Contribution: men, money, materials (✅ owner 18).
- Strain: rising prices, heavier taxation, war loans, shortages, recruitment pressure (✅ owner 18).
- Expectation: constitutional reward for loyalty (✅ owner 18).
- Contradiction: wartime talk of democracy and self-determination (⚠️ owner 18 analytical).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** WWI 1914–18 (✅ owner 18). No figures for troops or prices: owner says do not quantify without verified data.
**Links:** MOD-3001, MOD-3010, MOD-3028 (Rowlatt as breach).
**Trap:** Do not quote troop numbers or price indices; the owner forbids unverified figures.
**Mains hook:** The gap between contribution and concession drove 1919–22; the Rowlatt repression felt like betrayal (basic/19).
**Back:** *"Gave men, money, materials; got taxes, shortages, and promises."*

### Flashcard MOD-3010 — Montagu Declaration, 20 August 1917
**Front:** What did the Montagu Declaration of 20 August 1917 promise, and what did it not promise?
**Definition (learn):** The British declaration that the policy was the increasing association of Indians in administration and the gradual development of responsible government in India as an integral part of the Empire.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 20 August 1917 (✅ owner 18; ✅ owner 18 Must-Know).
- Promise: gradual, increasing association, responsible government (✅ owner 18).
- Not promised: immediate responsible government; no separation from the Empire (✅ owner 18).
- Political effect: made "responsible government" an avowed British objective; later debates were about pace (✅ owner 18, analytical).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 20 August 1917 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MOD-3011 (1919 Act), MOD-3018 (verdicts), MOD-3001.
**Trap:** "Gradual" is the keyword; do not read it as a grant of immediate self-government.
**Mains hook:** Once the objective was avowed, every later reform was argued over pace and scope rather than principle (the constitutional ratchet to 1919, 1935, 1947).
**Back:** *"Twenty August, seventeen: gradual, not immediate; responsible, within the Empire."*

### Flashcard MOD-3011 — Government of India Act 1919: the architecture
**Front:** What were the main features of the Government of India Act 1919 (Montagu–Chelmsford reforms), including its dyarchy design?
**Definition (learn):** The 1919 Act introduced dyarchy in the provinces: some subjects were transferred to Indian ministers answerable to legislative councils; others stayed reserved under the executive.
**Breakdown:**
- Royal assent: 23 December 1919 (✅ Wikipedia: Government of India Act 1919).
- Transferred subjects: education, health, agriculture, local self-government (✅ owner 18; ✅ Wikipedia lists agriculture, local government, health, education).
- Reserved subjects: law and order and finance (✅ owner 18); Wikipedia also lists land revenue and irrigation as reserved (⚠️ conflict in sources, flagged).
- Reserved-subject ministers were under the Governor/Viceroy (✅ Wikipedia).
- Commencement date not given on the fetched page (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Royal assent 23 December 1919; dyarchy; transferred vs reserved (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MOD-3012 (design to fail), MOD-3013 (women), MOD-3014 (electorate), MOD-3016 (historiography).
**Trap:** The 1919 Act is not the 1935 Act: 1919 created provincial dyarchy; 1935 abolished it and proposed central dyarchy (MOD-3137).
**Mains hook:** Dyarchy conceded the appearance of responsibility while retaining control of law, order and finance.
**Back:** *"Nineteen: transferred to ministers (education, health, agriculture, local); reserved (law, order, finance)."*

### Flashcard MOD-3012 — Dyarchy designed to fail
**Front:** Why did dyarchy under the 1919 Act fail, according to the owner's analytical argument?
**Definition (learn):** Dyarchy conceded responsibility without capacity: the transferred departments were weak in political weight and funds, and the powerful ones stayed reserved.
**Breakdown:**
- Transferred: low-weight, low-fund departments that "would invariably discredit the Indian ministers" (✅ owner 18).
- Reserved: law and order and finance under official control (✅ owner 18).
- Governors' veto and certificate powers; Viceroy's certificate for rejected bills (✅ owner 18).
- Parity between Europeans and Indians in provincial executive councils (✅ owner 18).
- Revenue division: land revenue to provinces, income tax to the centre (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1919 Act (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MOD-3011, MOD-3018 (dyarchy verdict), MOD-3021 (Gandhi's later satyagraha against this order).
**Trap:** The failure lay in which subjects were transferred, not in dyarchy as a principle (owner's analytical point).
**Mains hook:** "Dyarchy failed not because Indians could not govern but because they were given the departments that could not succeed."
**Back:** *"Blame without power: weak departments handed over, strong ones kept."*

### Flashcard MOD-3013 — Women's franchise under the 1919 Act (source conflict)
**Front:** Did the Government of India Act 1919 enfranchise women, and what does the source conflict say?
**Definition (learn):** The owner says the 1919 settlement did not itself enfranchise women; it left removal of the sex disqualification to provincial legislatures, and substantial extension came in 1935.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: no direct enfranchisement; provincial legislatures acted later (⚠️ owner 18).
- Fetched page: a limited number of Indian women were given the right to vote in certain provinces (✅ Wikipedia: Government of India Act 1919).
- Conflict flagged: the two sources differ on how much the 1919 Act itself conferred.
- 1935 extended the women's franchise, still on property and education qualifications (✅ owner 18, 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1919 (limited); 1935 (extended, qualified).
**Links:** MOD-3011, MOD-3145 (1935 franchise), MOD-3157 (PYQ route).
**Trap:** The 2021 Prelims route on "women political rights" links 1919/1935 Montagu-Chelmsford; avoid asserting universal adult suffrage in either Act.
**Mains hook:** Representation widened in numbers but narrowed in its identity-based routes.
**Back:** *"Nineteen: a few women, some provinces; Thirty-five: wider, still qualified."* [⚠️ source conflict]

### Flashcard MOD-3014 — The 1919 electorate and communal extensions
**Front:** How large was the 1919 electorate, and which groups received communal or special representation?
**Definition (learn):** The 1919 reforms enlarged the franchise but extended communal and special representation to new groups.
**Breakdown:**
- Electorate: about 5.5 million for the provinces and 1.5 million for the imperial legislature (✅ owner 18; figures attested in owner only, ⚠️ not separately verified).
- Separate electorates extended to Sikhs; also to Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians and Europeans (✅ Wikipedia: Government of India Act 1919).
- Non-Brahman seats reserved in Madras (✅ owner 18).
- "Depressed classes" offered nominated seats at all levels (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5.5 million provincial; 1.5 million imperial (✅ owner 18, ⚠️ unverified externally).
**Links:** MOD-3008, MOD-3011, MOD-3012.
**Trap:** Separate electorates (Muslims, Sikhs) ≠ reserved seats (non-Brahmans) ≠ nominated seats (depressed classes).
**Mains hook:** The 1919 franchise grew wider in number and narrower in the identity through which voters acted.
**Back:** *"Sikhs got separate seats; non-Brahmans reserved; depressed classes nominated."*

### Flashcard MOD-3015 — All-India Home Rule League renamed Swarajya Sabha (1920)
**Front:** What is the recorded renaming of the All-India Home Rule League, and what should not be confused with it?
**Definition (learn):** The All-India Home Rule League changed its name to Swarajya Sabha in 1920 (fetched source states this).
**Breakdown:**
- Source: "In 1920, All India Home Rule League changed its name to Swarajya Sabha" (✅ Wikipedia: Indian Home Rule Movement).
- Same source records a later dissolution section saying the League merged with Congress that year (⚠️ conflict unreconciled).
- Not the 1923 Swaraj Party (MOD-3069; MOD-3063 trap).
- Owner 18 treats the demand as routed (PYQ 2018 Q79) and not explained further (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1920 renaming (✅ Wikipedia); Swaraj Party 1 January 1923 (✅ Wikipedia: Swaraj Party).
**Links:** MOD-3004, MOD-3063, MOD-3069.
**Trap:** Swarajya Sabha (1920) ≠ Swaraj Party (1923). This is the owner's own warned distinction.
**Mains hook:** By 1920 the Home Rule organisations were being absorbed into Gandhian Congress and "Home Rule" gave way to "swaraj".
**Back:** *"Home Rule renamed Swarajya in 1920; Swaraj Party came in 1923: different bodies."*

### Flashcard MOD-3016 — Historiography of the 1919 reforms
**Front:** Which three interpretive positions on the 1919 reforms should an answer present?
**Definition (learn):** Three scholarly readings of the 1919 reforms, used to frame a critical evaluation.
**Breakdown:**
- Philip Woods: reforms helped establish parliamentary democracy and began decolonisation (⚠️ owner 18).
- Carl Bridge: reforms safeguarded the essentials of the British position (⚠️ owner 18).
- B.R. Tomlinson: reforms mobilised influential Indian collaboration for imperial purposes (⚠️ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Positions attributed in owner only (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-3011, MOD-3012, MOD-3018.
**Trap:** Do not quote these scholars verbatim or attribute a phrase to the wrong scholar (owner rule).
**Mains hook:** A balanced answer concedes genuine gains (a larger electorate, Indian ministers, financial devolution) and states control retained.
**Back:** *"Woods: a democratic start; Bridge: control kept; Tomlinson: collaboration bought."*

### Flashcard MOD-3017 — Lucknow Pact trap: not only Hindu–Muslim unity
**Front:** Did the Lucknow Pact only mean Hindu–Muslim unity?
**Definition (learn):** No. It meant both Moderate–Extremist reunion inside Congress and a Congress–League joint reform scheme, with separate electorates as the price.
**Breakdown:**
