**Mains hook:** Evidence of army disaffection shows nationalism reaching the regime's chief instrument.
**Back:** *"Peshawar, April thirty: two Garhwali platoons said no."*

### Flashcard MOD-3099 — Gandhi's "Sedition has become my religion" (1930 content)
**Front:** What did Gandhi say in the "Sedition has become my religion" statement and at what moment?
**Definition (learn):** At the launch of the Dandi March he declared British rule a curse and said "Sedition has become my religion. Ours is a non-violent battle."
**Breakdown:**
- Content: British rule brought moral, material, cultural and spiritual ruin; he was out to destroy that system (✅ owner 22).
- Quotation: "Sedition has become my religion. Ours is a non-violent battle." (✅ owner 22).
- Placement: 1930 Dandi launch, not 1920–22 (✅ owner 20; ✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 12 March 1930 (✅ Wikipedia: Salt Satyagraha).
**Links:** MOD-3057 (trap), MOD-3094, MOD-3064 (PYQ 2025 Q71).
**Trap:** Do not place it in NCM.
**Mains hook:** The statement marks CDM's break with withdrawal-based politics.
**Back:** *"Thirty at Dandi: sedition, non-violent."*

### Flashcard MOD-3100 — Gandhi–Irwin Pact (5 March 1931)
**Front:** What did the Gandhi–Irwin Pact of 5 March 1931 provide and what did it not provide?
**Definition (learn):** A truce: Congress suspended CDM and agreed to attend the Second RTC; the government released prisoners and conceded some salt and picketing rights.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 5 March 1931 (✅ owner 22; ⚠️ the fetched Salt Satyagraha page did not give the exact day).
- Congress: suspend CD; attend Second RTC (✅ owner 22; ✅ Wikipedia: "talks... led to the Second RTC").
- Government: withdraw ordinances, release non-violent prisoners; salt for personal use in coastal areas; peaceful picketing (✅ owner 22).
- Not conceded: public inquiry into police excesses (✅ owner 22).
- Did not save Bhagat Singh, Sukhdev and Rajguru (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5 March 1931 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MOD-3101, MOD-3079, MOD-3102.
**Trap:** A truce, not an independence settlement (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Congress suspended a movement for procedural undertakings; this asymmetry defines the pact.
**Back:** *"Five March thirty-one: CD suspended, RTC attended, Bhagat not saved."*

### Flashcard MOD-3101 — Round Table Conferences: the three sessions
**Front:** Give the dates of the three RTCs and say who represented Congress at each.
**Definition (learn):** The London conferences on India's constitution: 1930–31, 1931 and 1932.
**Breakdown:**
- First RTC: opened 12 November 1930; Congress absent; many leaders jailed (✅ Wikipedia: Round Table Conferences).
- Second RTC: opened 7 September 1931; Gandhi the sole Congress representative (✅ Wikipedia).
- Third RTC: November–December 1932; only 46 delegates attended (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 22).
- Owner gives 46 of 112 total (⚠️ 112 not found on fetched page).
- White Paper of 1933 (✅ owner 22, ⚠️ not on fetched page).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 12 Nov 1930; 7 Sept 1931; Nov–Dec 1932 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3100, MOD-3137 (GoI Act 1935), MOD-3085.
**Trap:** Congress was absent from the First RTC, not the Second (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** RTCs prove both Congress's indispensability and the limits of negotiation without leverage.
**Back:** *"Thirty absent, thirty-one Gandhi alone, thirty-two thin attendance."*

### Flashcard MOD-3102 — Communal Award (August 1932)
**Front:** What did the Communal Award of August 1932 do, and who announced it?
**Definition (learn):** Prime Minister Ramsay MacDonald's award apportioned representation among communities and extended separate electorates to the Depressed Classes.
**Breakdown:**
- Announced: August 1932, by Ramsay MacDonald (✅ owner 22; ✅ Wikipedia: Poona Pact mentions August 1932).
- Extended separate electorates to untouchables (✅ owner 22).
- Gandhi, in Yeravda jail, opposed it and began a fast unto death (✅ owner 22; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: exact day not given (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1932 (✅ owner 22; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3008, MOD-3103, MOD-3104.
**Trap:** Communal Award (separate electorates) ≠ Poona Pact (reserved seats in joint electorates).
**Mains hook:** The Award forced a social question to the centre of national politics.
**Back:** *"Thirty-two August: Award gave separate seats; Gandhi fasted."*

### Flashcard MOD-3103 — Gandhi's fast and the Poona Pact (24 September 1932)
**Front:** When and where was the Poona Pact signed, and what did it replace?
**Definition (learn):** The Gandhi–Ambedkar settlement signed on 24 September 1932 at Yerwada Central Jail, Poona, which replaced the Communal Award's separate electorates with reserved seats in joint electorates.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 24 September 1932 (✅ Wikipedia: Poona Pact).
- Place: Yerwada Central Jail, Poona (✅ Wikipedia).
- Replaced separate electorates with reserved seats in joint electorates (✅ owner 22; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Pressure: Gandhi's fast unto death (✅ owner 22; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 24 September 1932 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3104, MOD-3102, MOD-3105.
**Trap:** Not separate electorates for the Depressed Classes (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** A coercive context; the pact was a compromise, not a meeting of minds (owner caution).
**Back:** *"Yerwada, twenty-four September: fast, joint seats, reserved places."*

### Flashcard MOD-3104 — Poona Pact: reserved seats, primary election and the 148 conflict
**Front:** How did the Poona Pact's electoral mechanism work and how many reserved seats did it give?
**Definition (learn):** A two-stage system: Depressed Classes voters on the general roll elected a panel of four candidates per reserved seat, and the top four went to the joint electorate.
**Breakdown:**
- Primary: electoral college of Depressed Classes voters with a single vote (✅ Wikipedia: Poona Pact).
- Panel: four candidates per reserved seat go forward (✅ Wikipedia).
- Duration: primary arrangement to last ten years unless ended sooner (✅ Wikipedia).
- Reserved seats: source text says the pact settled on 148 seats, but its per-province list sums to 158 (⚠️ conflict in Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 148 vs 158 (⚠️ conflict); ten years (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3103, MOD-3105, MOD-3008.
**Trap:** Do not state the total seat number without the conflict flag; the owner also refuses seat numbers unless verified.
**Mains hook:** Reservation within joint electorates defined SC representation since.
**Back:** *"Four candidates, ten years, a seat count that will not add up."*

### Flashcard MOD-3105 — Ambedkar, M.C. Rajah and the Depressed Classes' political voice
**Front:** How did Ambedkar and M.C. Rajah differ on electorates for the untouchables?
**Definition (learn):** Rajah favoured joint electorates; Ambedkar argued separate electorates were the only route to political representation.
**Breakdown:**
- M.C. Rajah favoured joint electorates (✅ owner 22).
- Ambedkar saw separate electorates as the only hope (✅ owner 22).
- Gandhi opposed separate electorates but accepted reserved seats (✅ owner 22).
- Ambedkar agreed when reserved seats increased and a two-tier system was recommended (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1932 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MOD-3103, MOD-3104.
**Trap:** Ambedkar's agreement was under the pressure of a fast; present the coercive context.
**Mains hook:** Contrast nationalist integration with Ambedkarite concerns about independent Dalit political voice (owner advanced line).
**Back:** *"Rajah: join. Ambedkar: separate. Pact: reserve in joint."*

### Flashcard MOD-3106 — The Harijan turn and PYQ 2025 Q74
**Front:** After which event did Gandhi take up the upliftment of Harijans as his central preoccupation?
**Definition (learn):** After the Communal Award crisis and the Poona Pact, removal of untouchability became central to Gandhi's public work, and the weekly Harijan became a principal vehicle.
**Breakdown:**
- Event: Communal Award crisis and Poona Pact, September 1932 (✅ owner 22; ⚠️ owner's causal link).
- Vehicle: weekly Harijan with Young India (✅ owner 22).
- PYQ: 2025 Prelims GS-I Q74 (key present locally, answer not recorded here, ✅ owner 22).
- Founding date of the anti-untouchability organisation: not verified (⚠️ owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1932 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MOD-3103, MOD-3104, MOD-3109.
**Trap:** The owner says do not name the organisation's date without verification.
**Mains hook:** A constitutional device forced a social question to the centre of national politics.
**Back:** *"Poona in thirty-two, then Harijan."*

### Flashcard MOD-3107 — CDM's end: resumption and withdrawal (1932–34)
**Front:** How did CDM end after the Gandhi–Irwin Pact, and how does it compare with NCM's end?
**Definition (learn):** CDM resumed under repression in 1932–34 and was withdrawn in 1934, without a decisive victory.
**Breakdown:**
- CDM resumed under repression (✅ owner 22; ✅ owner 22 Advanced).
- Withdrawal in 1934 (✅ owner 22).
- NCM: unilateral withdrawal after Chauri Chaura; CDM: negotiated suspension, resumption, withdrawal (✅ owner 22).
- Comparison table (MOD-3108 verdicts) (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1934 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MOD-3050, MOD-3096, MOD-3108.
**Trap:** Do not present CDM as a straight-line victory.
**Mains hook:** Qualified success: CDM won no constitution, but Britain could no longer ignore Congress.
**Back:** *"Thirty-four: resumed, repressed, withdrawn; no constitution won."*

### Flashcard MOD-3108 — NCM versus CDM comparison and verdicts
**Front:** How do NCM (1920–22) and CDM (1930–34) differ in goal, method, Muslim and women's participation, state response and ending?
**Definition (learn):** A comparison table and verdict set for two mass movements.
**Breakdown:**
- Goal: NCM swaraj in a year and Khilafat; CDM Purna Swaraj (✅ owner 22).
- Method: NCM withdrawal of cooperation; CDM breaking the law (✅ owner 22).
- Muslim participation: high in NCM via Khilafat; lower in CDM except NWFP (✅ owner 22).
- Women's role: present in NCM; wide and prominent in CDM (✅ owner 22).
- State response: restrained until 1921–22 in NCM; mass arrests and ordinances in CDM (✅ owner 22).
- Deadlock verdict: "Civil Disobedience won no constitution..." (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MOD-3107, MOD-3096, MOD-3040.
**Trap:** Keep goal (NCM swaraj in a year vs CDM Purna Swaraj) distinct.
**Mains hook:** "Compare NCM and CDM" by issue, method, participation, geography, state response, outcome.
**Back:** *"NCM withdrew; CDM broke the law; both were judged on what they forced."*

### Flashcard MOD-3109 — PYQ routes for Simon, RTCs and Poona
**Front:** Which PYQs route to owner 22 and what is their status?
**Definition (learn):** Two verified objective routes, with status as recorded.
**Breakdown:**
- 2020 Prelims GS-I Q27: Gandhi–Irwin Pact terms and CDM withdrawal conditions (key unavailable locally, ✅ owner 22). Route: MOD-3100.
- 2025 Prelims GS-I Q74: event after which Gandhi took up Harijan upliftment (key present locally, answer not recorded, ✅ owner 22). Route: MOD-3106.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MOD-3100, MOD-3106.
**Trap:** Do not invent option letters.
**Mains hook:** Use the CDM and Poona sequence for "successes and limits" demands.
**Back:** *"Twenty's Pact terms; twenty-five's Harijan turn: keys held, letters not guessed."*

### GROUP — 23 Left, Peasant, Workers' and States' Peoples' Movements

### Flashcard MOD-3110 — The 1930s: socialist, peasant, labour and princely-state pressure from below
**Front:** How did Left, peasant, labour and states' peoples' movements change the agenda of the national movement?
**Definition (learn):** Socialist, communist, peasant, labour and princely-state currents pushed Congress from political freedom toward social and economic justice; they remained partly autonomous.
**Breakdown:**
- Socialist: CSP (1934) within Congress (MOD-3115).
- Communist: CPI/Kanpur (1925), Meerut case (MOD-3113, MOD-3114).
- Labour: AITUC (1920) (MOD-3111).
- Peasant: Bardoli, AIKS, Faizpur (MOD-3118, MOD-3119).
- Princely states: AISPC (1927) (MOD-3116).
- Congress programmes: Karachi 1931, Faizpur 1936 (MOD-3117).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1920–1947 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3067, MOD-3082, MOD-3117, MOD-3135.
**Trap:** Do not merge left, labour and peasant bodies into one (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** "The 1930s transformed Indian nationalism from political anti-imperialism into a broader social movement."
**Back:** *"Socialists in, peasants out, labour up, princes next."*

### Flashcard MOD-3111 — AITUC (1920): first all-India trade-union platform
**Front:** When was the AITUC founded, and who was its first president?
**Definition (learn):** The All India Trade Union Congress, founded in 1920, was the first major all-India trade-union platform.
**Breakdown:**
- Founded: 1920 (✅ owner 23).
- First president: Lala Lajpat Rai (✅ owner 23).
- Labour visible through textile and railway strikes (✅ owner 23).
- Karachi 1931 brought labour protection into Congress doctrine (MOD-3117).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1920 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3112, MOD-3117.
**Trap:** Do not date AITUC to 1936 (MOD-3112).
**Mains hook:** Labour as a constituency distinct from Congress and the Left (owner analytical).
**Back:** *"Twenty, Lajpat, first all-India union."*

### Flashcard MOD-3112 — AITUC versus AIKS: the date trap
**Front:** Was AITUC founded in 1920 and All India Kisan Sabha in 1936, or the reverse?
**Definition (learn):** AITUC: 1920. All India Kisan Sabha: 1936.
**Breakdown:**
- AITUC 1920, Lajpat Rai (MOD-3111).
- AIKS 1936, Sahajanand Saraswati and N.G. Ranga (MOD-3118).
- The owner calls this the commonest date confusion in the topic (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1920; 1936 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3111, MOD-3118.
**Trap:** AIKS 1920 and AITUC 1936 are both wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use the date gap to explain why peasant organisation came later.
**Back:** *"Union twenty, Kisan thirty-six."*

### Flashcard MOD-3113 — CPI formation point at Kanpur (1925) and M.N. Roy
**Front:** What links the Communist Party of India to 1925 and Kanpur, and who was M.N. Roy?
**Definition (learn):** The CPI is associated with a Kanpur formation point in 1925; M.N. Roy is associated with early communist thought.
**Breakdown:**
- CPI: Kanpur formation point, 1925 (✅ owner 23 standard chronology).
- M.N. Roy: early communist thought (✅ owner 23).
- Conspiracy cases: Peshawar, Kanpur, Meerut (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1925 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3114, MOD-3134.
**Trap:** CSP (1934) and CPI (1925) are different currents (MOD-3134).
**Mains hook:** The CPI's class politics ran under repression while Congress carried the anti-colonial front.
**Back:** *"Kanpur twenty-five: Roy's early communists."*

### Flashcard MOD-3114 — Meerut Conspiracy Case (1929–33)
**Front:** What was the Meerut Conspiracy Case and whom did it target?
**Definition (learn):** A colonial prosecution from 1929 to 1933 against communist and trade-union activists.
**Breakdown:**
- Period: 1929–33 (✅ owner 23).
- Target: communist and trade-union activists (✅ owner 23).
- Shows repression of left labour politics (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1929–33 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3113, MOD-3110.
**Trap:** Meerut is not the Lahore Conspiracy (MOD-3079).
**Mains hook:** Repression grew labour-anti-imperial networks rather than removing them.
**Back:** *"Meerut, twenty-nine to thirty-three: union men on trial."*

### Flashcard MOD-3115 — Congress Socialist Party (1934)
**Front:** When was the CSP formed, where, and who were its leaders?
**Definition (learn):** The Congress Socialist Party was formed in 1934 within Congress, to radicalise the national platform from inside.
**Breakdown:**
- Formed: 1934 (✅ owner 23).
- Inside Congress (✅ owner 23).
- Leaders: Jayaprakash Narayan, Acharya Narendra Dev, Ram Manohar Lohia, Minoo Masani (✅ owner 23).
- Subordinated class politics to the anti-colonial front (✅ owner 23 analytical).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1934 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3134, MOD-3113.
**Trap:** CSP was not a separate party outside Congress at birth (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** The CSP's choice to work inside Congress was the Indian Left's most consequential organisational decision.
**Back:** *"Thirty-four, inside Congress: JP, Narendra Dev, Lohia, Masani."*

### Flashcard MOD-3116 — All India States' Peoples' Conference (1927)
**Front:** What was the AISPC, when was it founded, and what did it do?
**Definition (learn):** A 1927 all-India body coordinating struggles for rights in princely states.
**Breakdown:**
- Founded: 1927 (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia: owner's date).
- Purpose: coordinate princely-state rights struggles (✅ owner 23).
- Created the cadre and legitimacy later used at integration in 1947 (✅ owner 23 analytical; MOD-3135).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1927 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3135, MOD-3137.
**Trap:** States' Peoples' movements did not fight the British directly in British provinces (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** The movements turned princely India from a diplomatic problem into a political one.
**Back:** *"Twenty-seven: princes' subjects, one conference."*

### Flashcard MOD-3117 — Karachi Resolution (1931) and Faizpur Congress (1936)
**Front:** What did the Karachi Resolution of 1931 and the Faizpur Congress of 1936 add to Congress doctrine?
**Definition (learn):** Karachi (1931) adopted a Fundamental Rights and Economic Programme; Faizpur (1936) foregrounded agrarian questions.
**Breakdown:**
- Karachi 1931: fundamental rights and economic programme; labour protection (✅ owner 23).
- Faizpur 1936: agrarian emphasis (✅ owner 23; ✅ owner 23 Mains).
- Karachi "committed the Congress on paper to a social programme" (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1931; 1936 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3110, MOD-3111, MOD-3118.
**Trap:** Karachi is about rights and economy, not about the Lahore Purna Swaraj resolution.
**Mains hook:** Congress could not remain confined to constitutional freedom.
**Back:** *"Karachi thirty-one: rights and economics; Faizpur thirty-six: land."*

### Flashcard MOD-3118 — All India Kisan Sabha (1936): Sahajanand and Ranga
**Front:** When was the All India Kisan Sabha formed and who led it?
**Definition (learn):** The national peasant platform formed in 1936; agrarian questions were institutionalised.
**Breakdown:**
- Formed 1936 (✅ owner 23).
- Leaders: Sahajanand Saraswati and N.G. Ranga (✅ owner 23).
- Institutionalisation of the peasant question (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1936 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3112, MOD-3117, MOD-3054 (Eka).
**Trap:** Not the UP Kisan Sabha of Baba Ramchandra (MOD-3059).
**Mains hook:** The AIKS brought the agrarian question onto an organised national platform.
**Back:** *"Thirty-six, Sahajanand and Ranga: the peasants' national voice."*

### Flashcard MOD-3119 — Bardoli Satyagraha (1928): organisation and the Sardar title
**Front:** What was Bardoli's organisational structure in 1928, and who gave Patel the title Sardar?
**Definition (learn):** Bardoli was a disciplined no-tax satyagraha in Gujarat under Vallabhbhai Patel, with camps, workers, volunteers, a daily paper and an intelligence wing.
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1928; Bardoli taluqa in Surat district (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia: Bardoli Satyagraha).
- Thirteen workers' camps (chhavanis), each with an experienced leader (✅ owner 23).
- One hundred political workers and about 1,500 volunteers (✅ owner 23).
- Publications bureau: daily Bardoli Satyagraha Patrika (✅ owner 23).
- Intelligence wing tracking jabti (confiscation) and wavering peasants (✅ owner 23).
- Tax issue: demand to cancel a 22 per cent hike; settled at a 6.03 per cent increase by commission (✅ Wikipedia).
- Sardar title: given by the women of Bardoli; the Wikipedia article marks this claim as lacking citation (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1928; 13 camps; 100 workers; about 1,500 volunteers (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3052, MOD-3055, MOD-3120.
**Trap:** Bardoli is a taluqa-level campaign; do not generalise its methods (owner caution). Sardar is linked to Bardoli, not to 1947 (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Bardoli converts "disciplined satyagraha" from a name into an argument about organisation.
**Back:** *"Bardoli twenty-eight: thirteen camps, a paper, a spy wing, a Sardar."*

### Flashcard MOD-3120 — Awadh vs Gujarat: why agrarian politics diverged
**Front:** Why did Awadh and Gujarat peasant movements diverge in 1920–30?
**Definition (learn):** Awadh's taluqdari system placed a landlord between the peasant and the state; Gujarat's ryotwari system placed the state directly opposite the cultivator.
**Breakdown:**
- Awadh: taluqdari landlords restored after 1857; tenants faced rent enhancement and eviction; target = landlord (✅ owner 23).
- Gujarat: ryotwari peasant proprietorship; target = revenue demand (✅ owner 23).
- Leadership: Baba Ramchandra and Madari Pasi (Awadh) vs Patel (Gujarat) (✅ owner 23).
- Relation: Awadh autonomous; Gujarat integrated (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1920–30 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3121, MOD-3054, MOD-3119.
**Trap:** Ryotwari is not taluqdari; the Gujarat antagonist was the state, not a landlord.
**Mains hook:** Why did agrarian politics differ across regions? The land system answers most.
**Back:** *"Awadh: landlord in between. Gujarat: state direct."*

### Flashcard MOD-3121 — The land system as explanatory variable
**Front:** What single argument explains why agrarian conflicts took different shapes?
**Definition (learn):** Where an Indian landlord stood between peasant and state, conflict was directed inward and Congress could not lead it without splitting; where the peasant faced the state, agrarian and national grievance merged.
**Breakdown:**
- Landlord intermediary → inward conflict (⚠️ owner 23 analytical).
- Direct state → national convergence (⚠️ owner 23 analytical).
- Links to basic/07 land-system material (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Analytical (⚠️).
**Links:** MOD-3120, MOD-3054.
**Trap:** Analytical, not a documented doctrine; state it as argument.
**Mains hook:** Use it to answer "why did agrarian politics differ?".
**Back:** *"Landlord between, conflict turns inward; state direct, conflict goes national."*

### Flashcard MOD-3122 — Bakasht and Warli (1945)
**Front:** What were the Bakasht struggles and the Warli movement?
**Definition (learn):** Late-colonial agrarian struggles over sharecropping and forced labour: Bakasht in Bihar (late 1930s–40s) and the Warli Adivasi movement (1945).
**Breakdown:**
- Bakasht: Bihar; sharecropping shares and tenancy (✅ owner 23).
- Warli: 1945; Adivasi labour and land (✅ owner 23).
- Radicalisation as colonial state weakened (✅ owner 23).
- Owner Advanced timing: 1937–46 (✅ owner 23 Advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1945 (Warli); late 1930s–40s (Bakasht) (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3123, MOD-3124.
**Trap:** Bakasht is not the same as the Tebhaga movement (both sharecropping; different regions).
**Mains hook:** Tenancy and forced labour radicalised agrarian politics late in the colonial period.
**Back:** *"Bihar Bakasht, Warli forty-five: sharecrop and forced labour."*

### Flashcard MOD-3123 — Tebhaga (1946–47)
**Front:** What did the Tebhaga movement demand, where was it, and which organisation led it?
**Definition (learn):** A Bengal sharecroppers' movement demanding two-thirds of the produce for the cultivator and one-third for the landlord.
**Breakdown:**
- Years: 1946–47; agitation continued in places to 1950 (✅ Wikipedia: Tebhaga movement).
- Demand: two-thirds to sharecroppers (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 23).
- Region: Bengal, including 24 Parganas, Dinajpur, North Bengal (✅ Wikipedia).
- Leader: All India Kisan Sabha, the peasant front of the CPI (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1946–47 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3118, MOD-3122, MOD-3124.
**Trap:** The Tebhaga share is two-thirds for the sharecropper (name means "sharing by thirds").
**Mains hook:** Agrarian radicalism meets the transfer-of-power moment (owner).
**Back:** *"Tebhaga forty-six: two-thirds to the one who tills."*

### Flashcard MOD-3124 — Telangana armed struggle (1946–51)
**Front:** When did the Telangana Rebellion run, in which state, and who led it?
**Definition (learn):** An armed peasant struggle against landlord power in the princely state of Hyderabad, led by communists through the Andhra Mahasabha and the CPI.
**Breakdown:**
- Dates: 4 July 1946 to 25 October 1951 (✅ Wikipedia: Telangana Rebellion).
- State: Hyderabad princely state, Telangana region (✅ Wikipedia).
- Leaders: Communists via Andhra Mahasabha and CPI (✅ Wikipedia).
- Indian Army entry: 13 September 1948 (police action) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Ended with CPI call to lay down arms, 1951 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 July 1946–25 October 1951; 13 September 1948 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3123, MOD-3137 (integration), MOD-3135.
**Trap:** Telangana did not end in 1948; the rebellion continued to 1951 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Telangana shows agrarian radicalism meeting the princely state's integration.
**Back:** *"Forty-six to fifty-one: Hyderabad's armed peasants; army in forty-eight."*

### Flashcard MOD-3125 — Birsa Munda's Ulgulan (1899–1900)
**Front:** What was the Ulgulan of Birsa Munda, and what did its political horizon include?
**Definition (learn):** The 1899–1900 Munda rebellion in Chota Nagpur, against jagirdars, thikadars and moneylenders (dikus) and the Raj, aiming at a Birsa Raj and an end to British rule.
**Breakdown:**
- Period: 1899–1900 (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia: Birsa Munda).
- Grievance: loss of common landholding to dikus (✅ owner 23).
- Vision: 1895; satjug/kaljug idiom (✅ owner 23).
- Christmas Eve 1899 proclamation (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Capture: 3 February 1900 at Jamkopai forest (✅ Wikipedia).
- Death: 9 June 1900, Ranchi jail (✅ Wikipedia; owner says June 1900).
- Non-tribal poor not to be attacked (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1899–1900; 3 February 1900; 9 June 1900 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3126, MOD-3127, MOD-3128.
**Trap:** Birth year: owner says 1874 but Wikipedia says 15 November 1875 (some sources 1872) (⚠️ conflict). Force size: owner says about 6,000 Mundas; Wikipedia says about 7,000 gathered (⚠️ conflict).
**Mains hook:** By 1899 tribal resistance named the Raj as enemy; the ulgulan marks land alienation turning into anti-colonial politics.
**Back:** *"Ninety-nine: Birsa's Ulgulan; ninety-nine's Christmas Eve, nineteen-hundred's jail."*

### Flashcard MOD-3126 — Kol rising (1820–37) and Rampa rebellion (1879)
**Front:** What were the Kol and Rampa tribal risings, and what drove the Rampa revolt?
**Definition (learn):** Earlier tribal risings: the Kols of Chhotanagpur (owner: 1820–37) and the Rampa rising in coastal Andhra (March 1879–1880).
**Breakdown:**
- Kols: 1820–37 in Chhotanagpur; thousands massacred (✅ owner 23; span not fetched ⚠️).
- Rampa: March 1879; hill tribesmen against a government-supported mansabdar and restrictive forest regulations (✅ owner 23).
- Force: regiments of infantry, cavalry, sappers and miners; defeated by end of 1880 (✅ owner 23).
- Rampa is the best comparison case: state-backed intermediary plus forest regulation (✅ owner 23 analytical).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1820–37; March 1879; end of 1880 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3125, MOD-3129.
**Trap:** Kol 1820–37 span is owner's; do not treat it as the 1831–32 rising without checking (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Scale of force required exposes how seriously the colonial state treated tribal revolt.
**Back:** *"Kols twenty to thirty-seven; Rampa seventy-nine, defeated eighty."*

### Flashcard MOD-3127 — Santhal Hool (1855–56) versus Ulgulan (1899–1900)
**Front:** How do the Santhal Hool and the Birsa Ulgulan differ, and why must they be kept apart?
**Definition (learn):** Two separate tribal risings: the Santhal Hool of 1855–56 under Sido and Kanhu, and the Ulgulan of 1899–1900 under Birsa Munda.
**Breakdown:**
- Santhal Hool: 1855–56, Sido and Kanhu (✅ owner 23; basic/07, basic/11).
- Ulgulan: 1899–1900, Birsa Munda (MOD-3125).
- Difference: Ulgulan had explicit political horizon against the Raj (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1855–56; 1899–1900 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3125, MOD-3126.
**Trap:** Do not conflate Birsa's Ulgulan with the Santhal Hool (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** The 1899 political horizon distinguishes late-nineteenth-century movements from earlier localised risings.
**Back:** *"Fifty-five was Sido and Kanhu; ninety-nine was Birsa."*

### Flashcard MOD-3128 — Colonial tribal policy versus post-1947 schedules
**Front:** How do colonial-era and post-1947 responses to tribal grievance compare?
**Definition (learn):** Both eras answered tribal grievance with special legal regimes rather than removing the economic cause.
**Breakdown:**
- Colonial: military suppression, then administrative exceptionalism such as the Santhal Parganas (✅ owner 23).
- Post-1947: Fifth and Sixth Schedules, autonomous councils, statehood (✅ owner 23).
- Continuity: excluded areas and scheduled areas isolate tribal territory under a distinct regime (⚠️ owner 23 analytical).
- Land alienation and displacement persist (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fifth and Sixth Schedules (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3125, MOD-3129.
**Trap:** Excluded areas (colonial purpose) and scheduled areas (post-1947 purpose) differ in purpose.
**Mains hook:** "That single sentence carries a 15-mark comparison" (owner).
**Back:** *"Excluded then, scheduled now: special law, same land question."*

### Flashcard MOD-3129 — Forest grievance and the Indian Forest Act 1878
**Front:** What forest legislation chain produced the grievances behind tribal revolts?
**Definition (learn):** A colonial forest-policy chain, stored in basic/07 §8.8, that restricted tribal forest use.
**Breakdown:**
- 1806 naval-timber interest → railway sleepers → Forest Department 1864 → Government Forests Act 1865 → Indian Forest Act 1878 (✅ owner 23 citing basic/07).
- 1878 Act: reserved, protected and unclassified forests; complete state monopoly (✅ owner 23).
- c. 1900: about 20 per cent of India's land area under forest administration (✅ owner 23).
- Jhum/podu and subsistence hunting restricted (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1806; 1864; 1865; 1878; c.1900 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3126, MOD-3125, MOD-3128.
**Trap:** The owner cites basic/07 for detail; do not duplicate the statutory detail here.
**Mains hook:** Forest regulation was a driver of tribal revolt across eras.
**Back:** *"Sixty-four, sixty-five, seventy-eight: the forests turned into monopolies."*

### Flashcard MOD-3130 — Tripuri crisis (1939)
**Front:** What was the Tripuri crisis and what did Bose's exit reveal?
**Definition (learn):** Bose was re-elected Congress president at Tripuri in 1939 against the Gandhian leadership's wishes; he resigned and formed the Forward Bloc.
**Breakdown:**
- Re-elected at Tripuri, 1939, against Gandhian preference (✅ owner 23).
- Resigned after the crisis; formed Forward Bloc (✅ owner 23).
- Ideology (planning, confrontational war strategy) and organisational control combined (✅ owner 23 caution).
- Reveals that Congress presidency lacked independent authority against the Working Committee (✅ owner 23 analytical).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1939 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3131, MOD-3132, MOD-3117.
**Trap:** Do not reduce Tripuri to a personality clash (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** "The Tripuri crisis was not a clash of personalities but the point at which the Congress had to decide..." (owner thesis).
**Back:** *"Thirty-nine: Bose re-elected, Gandhi's men resist, Bose resigns."*

### Flashcard MOD-3131 — Haripura (1938): Bose's presidency
**Front:** What did Subhas Chandra Bose's Haripura presidency stress?
**Definition (learn):** Bose was elected Congress president at Haripura in 1938, stressing planning and stronger anti-imperialism.
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1938 (✅ owner 23).
- Emphasis: planning and anti-imperialism (✅ owner 23 Advanced).
- Prelude to the 1939 Tripuri contest (MOD-3130).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1938 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3130, MOD-3110.
**Trap:** Haripura is 1938; Tripuri is 1939.
**Mains hook:** Left-radical planning vs Gandhian-majority preference.
**Back:** *"Thirty-eight, Haripura: planning and fight."*

### Flashcard MOD-3132 — Forward Bloc (1939) trap
**Front:** Was the Forward Bloc formed before or after the Tripuri crisis?
**Definition (learn):** After the Tripuri crisis: Bose resigned from the Congress presidency and formed the Forward Bloc in 1939.
**Breakdown:**
- Formed: 1939 (✅ owner 23).
- Sequence: Tripuri, resignation, then Forward Bloc (✅ owner 23 trap).
- PYQ route: 2026 Prelims GS-I Q16 on Forward Bloc and Congress alignments (provisional key, answer not inferred) (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1939 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3130, MOD-3136.
**Trap:** Do not place the Forward Bloc before Tripuri.
**Mains hook:** Bose's exit was the price of the Gandhian choice.
**Back:** *"Tripuri first, resignation next, Forward Bloc after."*

### Flashcard MOD-3133 — Moplah (Mappila) rebellion (1921)
**Front:** What was the Moplah rebellion, where, and why must it not be romanticised?
**Definition (learn):** A 1921 Malabar rising that combined agrarian grievance and anti-colonial anger with a grave communal dimension.
**Breakdown:**
- Period: from August 1921 (✅ owner 20 Bank C).
- Grievances: tenure insecurity, renewal fees, high rents, landlord exactions (✅ owner 20).
- Impetus: Malabar District Congress Conference at Manjeri, April 1920 (✅ owner 20).
- Communal character damaged the Khilafat–Congress alliance (⚠️ owner 20 and 23).
- Must state both agrarian and communal dimensions (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1921; April 1920 (✅ owner 20, 23).
**Links:** MOD-3060, MOD-3054.
**Trap:** Moplah was not only agrarian nor only communal (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Local mobilisation escaping the national frame and acquiring a communal character.
**Back:** *"Malabar twenty-one: rent and religion, both must be said."*

### Flashcard MOD-3134 — Left trap: CSP is not CPI; Left influence is not takeover
**Front:** Are the CSP and the CPI the same current, and does Left influence mean Congress became communist?
**Definition (learn):** No on both counts: CSP (1934) worked inside Congress; the CPI linked to Kanpur (1925) worked outside it; Left influence pressed Congress toward socio-economic programmes.
**Breakdown:**
- CSP: inside Congress, 1934 (MOD-3115).
- CPI: Kanpur 1925 (MOD-3113).
- Left influence: pressure for rights, redistribution and planning (✅ owner 23 Advanced).
- Congress absorbed and limited radical pressure (✅ owner 23 historiography).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1925; 1934 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3115, MOD-3113, MOD-3110.
**Trap:** "Left influence" ≠ communist Congress (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Congress both absorbed and limited radical pressures, keeping the coalition broad.
**Back:** *"CSP inside, CPI outside: influence, not takeover."*

### Flashcard MOD-3135 — States' Peoples' movements: significance and 1947 link
**Front:** Why were the States' Peoples' movements significant for the national movement and for integration in 1947?
**Definition (learn):** They extended the freedom struggle into the princely third of India, giving integration a political base beyond diplomacy.
**Breakdown:**
- Paramountcy insulated princely states from nationalism until AISPC (✅ owner 23).
- AISPC built the cadre and legitimacy used at integration (✅ owner 23).
- Congress long avoided direct intervention to protect its constitutional position (✅ owner 23).
- Telangana and integration (MOD-3124) (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1927 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MOD-3116, MOD-3124, MOD-3137.
**Trap:** Not British provinces (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** States' Peoples' movements as a bridge between anti-colonial nationalism and democratic rights.
**Back:** *"Princes' subjects, Congress caution, integration base."*

### Flashcard MOD-3136 — PYQ routes and verdicts for owner 23
**Front:** Which PYQs route to owner 23 and what verdict lines does the owner provide?
**Definition (learn):** Verified routes and verdict scaffolds.
**Breakdown:**
- 2019 GS-I Q11 (15 marks): many voices in the Gandhian phase (✅ owner 23). Route: MOD-3110.
- 2020 GS-I Q13 (15 marks): ideological strands since 1920s (✅ owner 23). Route: MOD-3082.
- 2023 GS-I Q13 (15 marks): colonial rule and tribal response (✅ owner 23). Route: MOD-3125.
- 2026 Prelims GS-I Q16: Forward Bloc 1939 (provisional key) (MOD-3132).
- 2020 Prelims GS-I Q35: Ulgulan Birsa Munda (key unavailable locally) (MOD-3125).
- Verdicts: agenda; autonomy; princely states; tribal (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MOD-3110, MOD-3125, MOD-3132.
**Trap:** No provisional or unavailable key is promoted.
**Mains hook:** "Popular movements were neither Congress creations nor Congress rivals" (owner verdict).
**Back:** *"Nineteen's voices, twenty's strands, twenty-three's tribes; keys not guessed."*

### GROUP — 24 Government of India Act 1935 and the Congress Ministries

### Flashcard MOD-3137 — GoI Act 1935: advance in form, safeguards in substance
**Front:** What was the Government of India Act 1935 and why is it called "a constitutional advance without sovereignty"?
**Definition (learn):** The 1935 Act gave provincial autonomy and proposed an All-India Federation, but kept defence, external affairs and emergency powers under imperial control, and the federation never began.
**Breakdown:**
- Provincial responsible government with governor safeguards (MOD-3139, MOD-3140).
- Federal scheme never implemented (MOD-3141).
- Central dyarchy proposed, never operated (MOD-3142).
- Franchise and communal representation expanded (MOD-3145, MOD-3146).
- Ministries 1937–39 and resignation (MOD-3149, MOD-3153).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Royal assent 2 August 1935 (✅ Wikipedia: Government of India Act 1935).
**Links:** MOD-3138, MOD-3155, MOD-3156.
**Trap:** Formal provision vs implementation (MOD-3156).
**Mains hook:** "Critically examine" the 1935 Act as advance and safeguard mechanism (owner Mains).
**Back:** *"Thirty-five: provinces given, centre kept."*

### Flashcard MOD-3138 — Sources and royal assent of the 1935 Act
**Front:** What did the 1935 Act draw on, and when did it receive royal assent?
**Definition (learn):** The Act built on the Simon Report, the Round Table Conferences, the 1933 White Paper and the Joint Select Committee; royal assent came on 2 August 1935.
**Breakdown:**
- Simon Report, RTCs, White Paper 1933, Joint Select Committee report 1934 (✅ owner 24).
- Royal assent: 2 August 1935 (✅ Wikipedia: GoI Act 1935).
- Longest colonial constitutional statute (✅ owner 24 Advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2 August 1935 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3137, MOD-3085, MOD-3101.
**Trap:** The RBI came from the RBI Act 1934, not this Act (owner caution; MOD-3147).
**Mains hook:** The 1935 Act supplied the administrative and textual framework the Constituent Assembly later adapted.
**Back:** *"Simon, RTC, White Paper, JSC: thirty-five assent."*

### Flashcard MOD-3139 — Provincial autonomy: dyarchy ended in the provinces
**Front:** What did provincial autonomy under the 1935 Act change?
**Definition (learn):** The Act replaced provincial dyarchy with responsible government in all departments, including law and order.
**Breakdown:**
- Dyarchy abolished in provinces (✅ owner 24; ✅ Wikipedia: Government of India Act 1935).
- Indian ministers held all departments, including law and order (✅ owner 24 Bank A).
- Real but limited constitutional space (✅ owner 24 Advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1935; 1937 elections (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3140, MOD-3011 (1919 dyarchy).
**Trap:** Provincial autonomy did not mean sovereignty.
**Mains hook:** Concede the genuine advance: law and order passed to Indian ministers.
**Back:** *"Provinces: dyarchy out, ministers in, all departments."*

### Flashcard MOD-3140 — Governors' safeguards and the emergency takeover
**Front:** What reserve powers did the governors and the centre keep under provincial autonomy?
**Definition (learn):** Governors' discretion and special powers, including the power to take over a province's administration indefinitely.
**Breakdown:**
- Discretion over summoning legislatures, assenting to bills, tribal regions (✅ owner 24).
- Special powers for minority rights, civil servants' privileges and British business interests (✅ owner 24).
- Power to take over and run the administration indefinitely (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1935 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3139, MOD-3137.
**Trap:** Governors were not ceremonial heads (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** A constitution where the executive can suspend responsible government delegates administration, not sovereignty.
**Back:** *"Governors kept the brakes: discretion and takeover."*

### Flashcard MOD-3141 — Federal scheme and the accession threshold
**Front:** What condition had to be met before the federal part of the 1935 Act took effect?
**Definition (learn):** The federation required states that represented half the states by weight to accede through Instruments of Accession.
**Breakdown:**
- Threshold: "when half the states by weight agreed to federate" (✅ Wikipedia: GoI Act 1935).
- Owner expresses it as more than 50 per cent of princely states, via Instruments of Accession (✅ owner 24).
- Accession would override earlier treaties with the Crown (✅ owner 24).
- Never reached: federation never came into being (✅ owner 24; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Half by weight (✅ Wikipedia); >50 per cent (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3143, MOD-3156, MOD-3137.
**Trap:** Federation did not operate in 1937–39; only provincial autonomy did.
**Mains hook:** The federation failed because it required princes to surrender treaty privileges voluntarily.
**Back:** *"Half by weight, by instrument: never reached."*

### Flashcard MOD-3142 — Central dyarchy and reserved subjects
**Front:** Which subjects stayed under the Viceroy's control under the proposed central dyarchy?
**Definition (learn):** Defence, foreign affairs and internal security were kept under the Viceroy in the federal scheme; the Secretary of State kept control through the Governor-General.
**Breakdown:**
- Defence and external affairs reserved (✅ Wikipedia: GoI Act 1935; ✅ owner 24).
- Internal security reserved (✅ owner 24; ⚠️ not listed in the fetched Wikipedia passage).
- Financial obligations and the British Indian Army (✅ Wikipedia).
- Dyarchy at centre was provided for, never operated (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MOD-3141, MOD-3144, MOD-3157.
**Trap:** Central dyarchy was proposed, not in operation.
**Mains hook:** Reserved defence and external affairs left the empire's core untouched.
**Back:** *"Defence, foreign affairs, internal security: kept by the Viceroy."*

### Flashcard MOD-3143 — Princely nominee seats (40 and 33 per cent)
**Front:** What share of the federal legislature's seats were to be nominated by the princes?
**Definition (learn):** The princes would nominate a large bloc in each chamber, a built-in conservative majority.
**Breakdown:**
- Council of State: 104 of 260 seats (40 per cent) (✅ Wikipedia: GoI Act 1935).
- Federal Assembly: 125 of 375 seats (33 per cent) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: 30–40 per cent (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 104/260; 125/375 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-3141, MOD-3142.
**Trap:** Owner's 30–40 range matches the fetched figures, but the chamber sizes are the exact data.
**Mains hook:** A princely bloc would have kept Congress in permanent minority.
**Back:** *"Forty of 260, thirty-three of 375: the princes' bloc."*

### Flashcard MOD-3144 — Residuary powers: 1935 Governor-General versus 1950 Parliament
**Front:** Where did residuary powers lie under the 1935 scheme, and what did the Constituent Assembly do?
**Definition (learn):** Residuary powers were vested in the Governor-General under the 1935 scheme; the Constituent Assembly reversed this by vesting residuary powers in the Union Parliament in 1950.
**Breakdown:**
- 1935: Governor-General (✅ owner 24; ⚠️ fetched Wikipedia page silent on this).
- Constitution (1950): Union Parliament (✅ owner 24).
- Owner: detailed doctrine belongs to the Polity owner (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1935; 1950 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3142, MOD-3157.
**Trap:** PYQ 2018 Q38 routes here (key unavailable locally) (MOD-3157).
**Mains hook:** Constitutional-history contrast of residuary powers.
**Back:** *"Thirty-five: Governor-General holds the leftovers; fifty: Parliament."*

### Flashcard MOD-3145 — Franchise size: conflicting figures
**Front:** How large was the 1935 electorate, and what do the sources say?
**Definition (learn):** The 1935 Act enlarged the franchise; the owner and fetched source give different sizes.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: about 30 million, roughly 10 per cent of population (✅ owner 24).
- Owner basic: "about 14 per cent" (⚠️ owner 24 internal range).
- Fetched Wikipedia: electorate grew from about five million to 35 million (✅ Wikipedia; conflict flagged).
- Advice: cite a range and the source, not one number (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 30 million; 10–14 per cent; 35 million (conflict flagged).
**Links:** MOD-3013 (women), MOD-3146, MOD-3137.
**Trap:** Do not assert a single franchise figure as certain.
**Mains hook:** Constitutional reform as a competitive instrument: rich and middle peasants enfranchised, as D.A. Low argues (owner).
**Back:** *"Thirty million or thirty-five: the vote was narrow, the figure contested."*

### Flashcard MOD-3146 — Communal and SC reserved seats under 1935
**Front:** Which communal and special representation did the 1935 Act provide?
**Definition (learn):** Separate electorates for Muslims and reserved seats for Scheduled Castes (the new term for the depressed classes).
**Breakdown:**
- Separate electorates for Muslims (✅ owner 24).
- Reserved seats for Scheduled Castes in provincial and central legislatures (✅ owner 24).
- Communal electorates extended (✅ owner 24 Advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1935 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3102, MOD-3104, MOD-3008.
**Trap:** Reserved seats (1935 and 1932 Poona) are not the same as separate electorates (Communal Award).
**Mains hook:** Communal segmentation deepened through 1935 (long-term communalisation).
**Back:** *"Muslims separate, SCs reserved: thirty-five."*

### Flashcard MOD-3147 — Fiscal control, Federal Court and the RBI trap
**Front:** What fiscal and judicial changes came with the 1935 Act, and what should not be attributed to it?
**Definition (learn):** Financial control moved from London to New Delhi; a Federal Court began in 1937; the Reserve Bank of India was created by the RBI Act 1934.
**Breakdown:**
- Financial control moved from London to New Delhi (✅ owner 24).
- Federal Court began functioning 1937 (✅ owner 24).
- RBI derives from the RBI Act 1934 (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937; 1934 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3138, MOD-3149.
**Trap:** The RBI is not a creation of the 1935 Act (owner caution).
**Mains hook:** Fiscal devolution was a long-standing demand (✅ owner 24).
**Back:** *"Money to Delhi, Court in thirty-seven, RBI in thirty-four."*

### Flashcard MOD-3148 — Provinces and territories: Sind, Orissa, Burma, Aden
**Front:** Which provincial changes accompanied the 1935 Act?
**Definition (learn):** Sindh was created, Bihar and Orissa were split, and Burma and Aden were separated (fetched); owner records Sind and Orissa reorganised and Burma separated in 1937.
**Breakdown:**
- Sindh created (✅ Wikipedia: GoI Act 1935).
- Bihar and Orissa split (✅ Wikipedia).
- Burma and Aden separated (✅ Wikipedia; owner: Burma separated 1937).
- Federal Court began 1937 (MOD-3147).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937 (Burma; ✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3147, MOD-3149.
**Trap:** Sind and Orissa were new provinces; Burma was separated, not created as a province of India.
**Mains hook:** New provinces were a genuine advance (owner Advanced).
**Back:** *"Sindh, Orissa, Burma, Aden: the map moved."*

### Flashcard MOD-3149 — Congress ministries (1937): eight of eleven provinces
**Front:** In which provinces did Congress form ministries after the 1937 elections?
**Definition (learn):** After winning majorities, Congress formed ministries in eight of eleven provinces; NWFP and Assam came later.
**Breakdown:**
- 1937: Bombay, Madras, United Provinces, Bihar, Central Provinces, Orissa (✅ owner 24).
- Later: NWFP and Assam (✅ owner 24).
- Total: eight of eleven (✅ owner 24).
- Congress ministries contested elections despite criticising the Act (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937; eight of eleven (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3150, MOD-3151, MOD-3137.
**Trap:** Congress did not boycott the 1937 elections (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Discuss significance and limits of Congress ministries 1937–39.
**Back:** *"Thirty-seven: eight of eleven, boycott never."*

### Flashcard MOD-3150 — "Machine with strong brakes but no engine" and "charter of slavery"
**Front:** How did Nehru and the nationalists describe the 1935 Act?
**Definition (learn):** Nehru's phrase captured the critique that the constitutional machinery existed without sovereignty; the Act was also labelled a "charter of slavery".
**Breakdown:**
- Nehru: "a machine with strong brakes but no engine" (✅ owner 24; ⚠️ attribution of exact phrasing not fetched).
- "Charter of slavery" as nationalist label (✅ owner 24 Advanced).
- Elections used tactically while rejecting the Act politically (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner quotations (⚠️ not independently fetched).
**Links:** MOD-3137, MOD-3149.
**Trap:** Congress rejected the Act politically but used elections tactically; it did not accept it as a final constitution (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Nationalist critique and tactical participation together.
**Back:** *"Brakes without engine; slavery charter; vote anyway."*

### Flashcard MOD-3151 — Ministries 1937–39: achievements and limits
**Front:** What did the Congress ministries achieve and where were they limited?
**Definition (learn):** Two years of limited office: civil liberties, prisoners, education, tenancy attempts, within the limits of finance and governors' powers.
**Breakdown:**
- Released political prisoners; expanded civil liberties (✅ owner 24).
- Promoted education; attempted agrarian and tenancy reforms (✅ owner 24).
- Limits: finance, police discretion, governors' reserve powers, communal polarisation (✅ owner 24 Advanced).
- Protections for civil servants and business (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937–39 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3149, MOD-3152.
**Trap:** Ministries had no full sovereign power (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Two years in office gave administrative experience and a demonstrable civil-liberties record.
**Back:** *"Prisoners freed, schools built, tenancy tried, powers small."*

### Flashcard MOD-3152 — Congress atrocities and the League's estrangement
**Front:** What did the Muslim League's "Congress atrocities" propaganda achieve?
**Definition (learn):** League propaganda about alleged atrocities in Congress-ruled provinces deepened Congress–League estrangement.
**Breakdown:**
- Propaganda: "Congress atrocities" (✅ owner 24).
- Effect: estrangement (✅ owner 24).
- Office showed the League that provincial power meant Congress power (✅ owner 24 thesis).
- Owner: the 1937 election proved League weak in several Muslim-majority areas (✅ owner 24 Advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937–39 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3151, MOD-3153, MOD-3154.
**Trap:** League dominance among Muslims was not complete in 1937 (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** The same evidence produced opposite conclusions in the two parties (owner verdict).
**Back:** *"Atrocities said loudly; estrangement followed."*

### Flashcard MOD-3153 — Congress ministries resign (October–November 1939)
**Front:** Why and when did Congress ministries resign in 1939?
**Definition (learn):** They resigned in October–November 1939 after Viceroy Linlithgow declared India at war without consulting Indian leaders.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: October–November 1939 (✅ owner 24).
- Cause: unilateral war declaration (✅ owner 24).
- Not an electoral loss (✅ owner 24 trap).
- Effect: removed Congress from provincial power; League filled the space (✅ owner 24 Bank E).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Oct–Nov 1939 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3152, MOD-3154.
**Trap:** Resignation was not a result of losing elections.
**Mains hook:** Do not present the resignation as the cause of Partition; it is one link in a chain.
**Back:** *"Thirty-nine: war declared, ministries resigned, not defeated."*

### Flashcard MOD-3154 — Deliverance Day (December 1939) and the Lahore Resolution (March 1940)
**Front:** What did the League do on Deliverance Day, and what did the Lahore Resolution demand?
**Definition (learn):** Deliverance Day (December 1939) celebrated relief from Congress rule; the Lahore Resolution (March 1940) demanded separate "independent states" for Muslims.
**Breakdown:**
- Deliverance Day: December 1939 (✅ owner 24).
- Lahore Resolution: March 1940 (✅ owner 24).
- Wording: "independent states", not "Pakistan" (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** December 1939; March 1940 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3153, MOD-3152.
**Trap:** Deliverance Day (Dec 1939) came before Lahore (Mar 1940).
**Mains hook:** Within four months the demand shifted from safeguards to separation (owner Bank E).
**Back:** *"Deliverance in December; independent states in March."*

### Flashcard MOD-3155 — Verdicts on the 1935 Act
**Front:** What verdict lines does the owner offer on the 1935 Act, the federation and the ministries?
**Definition (learn):** Three qualified verdict scaffolds.
**Breakdown:**
- Statute: gave Indians the provinces and kept the state (✅ owner 24).
- Federation: failed by design; princes would have to legislate away privileges (✅ owner 24).
- Ministries: proved Congress could govern and taught the League what Congress government meant (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner verdicts (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3137, MOD-3141, MOD-3151.
**Trap:** Verdicts are argument scaffolds.
**Mains hook:** Use a graded verdict with criteria.
**Back:** *"Provinces given, state kept; princes' veto; ministers' lesson."*

### Flashcard MOD-3156 — Formal provision versus implementation trap
**Front:** Did the federation under the 1935 Act function in 1937–39?
**Definition (learn):** No. Only provincial autonomy operated; the federation and central dyarchy never began.
**Breakdown:**
- Federation: never began (✅ owner 24).
- Central dyarchy: never operated (✅ owner 24).
- Provincial autonomy: operated 1937–39 (✅ owner 24).
- Test: distinguish formal provision from implementation (✅ owner 24 trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937–39 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3141, MOD-3142, MOD-3137.
**Trap:** Do not say the federation functioned.
**Mains hook:** The Act is famous for provincial autonomy and an unrealised federation.
**Back:** *"Provinces ran; the federation never did."*

### Flashcard MOD-3157 — PYQ routes for the 1935 Act
**Front:** Which PYQs route to owner 24 and what is their status?
**Definition (learn):** Verified routes with key status as recorded.
**Breakdown:**
- 2018 Prelims GS-I Q38: GoI Act 1935 residuary powers (key unavailable locally, ✅ owner 24). Route: MOD-3144.
- 2024 Prelims GS-I Q62: 1935 Act All-India Federation and reserved control of defence and foreign affairs (official Set-A key present locally, answer not recorded, ✅ owner 24). Route: MOD-3141, MOD-3142.
- 2021 Prelims GS-I Q42: women's rights under Montagu-Chelmsford and 1935 (key unavailable locally, ✅ owner 19/18). Route: MOD-3013.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MOD-3144, MOD-3141, MOD-3013.
**Trap:** Do not state an option letter.
**Mains hook:** Use the federation and reserved control route for "why did the federation fail?".
**Back:** *"Residuary in eighteen, federation in twenty-four, women in twenty-one: keys held, letters not inferred."*

### Flashcard MOD-3158 — Office acceptance: the strategic dilemma
**Front:** What was the office-acceptance debate inside Congress and how did it resolve?
**Definition (learn):** Whether entering ministries would blunt mass struggle or open a new arena; Congress contested the 1937 elections and took office, while rejecting the Act politically.
**Breakdown:**
- Debate: blunt mass struggle vs new arena (✅ owner 24 Advanced).
- Resolution: contest and office, tactically (✅ owner 24).
- Cost: League estrangement (MOD-3152).
- Gain: administrative experience, civil-liberties record (MOD-3151).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MOD-3149, MOD-3150, MOD-3152.
**Trap:** The office debate was not resolved by a single Congress decision that accepted the Act as a constitution.
**Mains hook:** Assess office acceptance as a dilemma between mass struggle and constitutional work.
**Back:** *"Thirty-seven: contest, govern, and pay the League price."*

## MODERN INDIAN HISTORY — Part 4: WWII, Independence, Partition, Integration and Early Republic

> Section note: the Modern-Indian-History flashcard subject (prefix `MOD`, block `MOD-4001` onward).
> Scope: owner files 25–31 (basic and advanced). Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` = repository owner;
> `(✅ Wikipedia: Article)` = confirmed on a fetched Wikipedia page; `⚠️` = not confirmed from a fetched
> source or an owner-vs-source conflict (the conflict is stated inside the card).

### GROUP — Second World War, the Cripps Mission & Quit India

### Flashcard MOD-4001 — Concept: the war crisis and the collapse of constitutional trust (1939–42)
**Front:** Why did the Second World War turn Indian constitutional politics from negotiation into demand for immediate power?
**Definition (learn):** Between 1939 and 1942 the British government committed India to war without consent, offered only future constitutional promises, and then faced a mass movement demanding present power. The war made the 1935 Act's provincial autonomy look subordinate to imperial authority.
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: unilateral entry into war (1939) → Congress ministries resign → August Offer (1940) → Individual Satyagraha (1940–41) → Cripps Mission (1942) → Quit India (1942).
- Shift: from accepting promises of a future dominion to demanding real power in the present (⚠️ interpretive reading, owner-flagged).
- Core limit: Congress objected to fighting for democracy abroad while India was denied it at home.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Belligerency 3 September 1939 (✅ owner 25 §9); Congress ministries resigned Oct–Nov 1939 (✅ owner 25 §9); 22 December 1939 "Deliverance Day" (✅ owner 25 §9, as the League's observance); Quit India 8 August 1942 (✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MOD-4002, MOD-4003, MOD-4004, MOD-4006, MOD-4009, MOD-4022.
**Trap:** Do not treat 1939 as a Quit India event; the war crisis is the background of 1939–42, and Quit India is one terminal step, not the whole sequence.
**Mains hook:** "The war did not create the freedom struggle; it removed the last excuse for delaying Indian power." Qualify with the owner's caution that the causal weight is contested.
**Back:** *"War without consent, offers without power, and then a movement that would not wait."*

### Flashcard MOD-4002 — 3 September 1939: India declared a belligerent
**Front:** What did the British decision of September 1939 do to India's status in the Second World War, and who was not consulted?
**Definition (learn):** On 3 September 1939 Britain declared India a belligerent in the Second World War without consulting elected Indian representatives.
**Breakdown:**
- Actor: British government (Viceroy Linlithgow's administration).
- Mechanism: a declaration made on India's behalf, not a decision taken by an Indian legislature.
- Consequence: Congress ministries in the provinces resigned in protest (see MOD-4003).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 3 September 1939 (✅ owner 25 §9); "without the consent of elected Indian representatives" (✅ owner 25 §1).
**Links:** MOD-4001, MOD-4003, MOD-4004.
**Trap:** The declaration was a British decision; it was not a vote in the Central Legislative Assembly. Do not describe the Congress resignations as a response to an Indian war-aims vote.
**Mains hook:** Use it as the first link in the chain "1939 → 1942" and argue that imperial consent-by-proclamation exposed the limits of provincial autonomy.
**Back:** *"Declared, not debated: India was at war before India was asked."*

### Flashcard MOD-4003 — Congress ministries resign (1939) and Deliverance Day (22 December 1939)
**Front:** What did the provincial Congress ministries do in 1939, and what was the 22 December 1939 observance?
**Definition (learn):** The Congress ministries that had governed several provinces under the 1935 Act resigned in October–November 1939 to protest India's involuntary entry into war. The Muslim League observed 22 December 1939 as "Deliverance Day".
**Breakdown:**
- Congress: resignation of provincial ministries (Oct–Nov 1939), protest against war without consent.
- League: observed 22 December 1939 as Deliverance Day (✅ owner 25 §9).
- Result: the Congress left provincial office; the League gained a political opening that it later used (see MOD-4016).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ministries resigned Oct–Nov 1939 (✅ owner 25 §9); Deliverance Day 22 December 1939 (✅ owner 25 §9).
**Links:** MOD-4002, MOD-4016, MOD-4039 (1945–46 elections).
**Trap:** Congress resignation (1939) is not the same as the Quit India "withdrawal" (1942). Deliverance Day is a League observance, not a Congress one.
**Mains hook:** The 1939 resignation shows the Congress choosing principle over office; the League's Deliverance Day shows the same crisis being read differently. Qualify both readings.
**Back:** *"Ministers walked out in 1939; the League celebrated the walk-out."*

### Flashcard MOD-4004 — August Offer (August 1940)
**Front:** What did the August Offer of 1940 promise, and why did Congress reject it?
**Definition (learn):** The August Offer was the Viceroy Linlithgow's wartime offer: dominion status as the eventual goal of constitutional development, an expanded Viceroy's Executive Council, and protection of minority interests. It gave no immediate responsible government.
**Breakdown:**
- Provisions: dominion status as a future goal; enlargement of the Viceroy's Executive Council; a practical minority safeguard.
- Withheld: immediate responsible government and a transfer of power in the present.
- Outcome: Congress rejected it; the Congress rejection marked the shift from accepting promises to demanding present power (✅ owner 25 §1).
- Minority logic: the safeguard gave minorities an effective veto, which the advanced owner reads as entrenching the colonial pattern of using communal safeguards to block national transfer (✅ owner 25 adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1940, Linlithgow (✅ owner 25); Congress rejection (✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MOD-4001, MOD-4005, MOD-4007 (Cripps comparison), MOD-4024 (August Offer vs Cripps).
**Trap:** The August Offer is 1940 and Linlithgow; the "post-dated cheque" remark belongs to the 1942 Cripps offer, not to the August Offer (✅ owner 25 §5).
**Mains hook:** Argue that the August Offer's minority veto made the constitutional question about who could block power, not just about how much power to give.
**Back:** *"August 1940: a dominion promised, a veto granted, power withheld."*

### Flashcard MOD-4005 — Individual Satyagraha (October 1940)
**Front:** What was Individual Satyagraha, who began it, and what was its limited purpose?
**Definition (learn):** Individual Satyagraha (from October 1940) was a limited, symbolic campaign of individually selected satyagrahis opposing India's involuntary participation in the war.
**Breakdown:**
- Form: individual, selected satyagrahis, not mass civil disobedience.
- Purpose: principled opposition to the imperial war policy, not a demand for immediate independence.
- Sequence: Vinoba Bhave was the first satyagrahi; Jawaharlal Nehru the second (✅ owner 25 §2, §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** October 1940 (✅ owner 25); Vinoba Bhave first, Nehru second (✅ owner 25 §4).
**Links:** MOD-4004, MOD-4009, MOD-4017 (comparison with NCM and CDM).
**Trap:** Individual Satyagraha was limited and symbolic; it is not comparable in scale to the Quit India upsurge. Do not describe Vinoba as the second satyagrahi.
**Mains hook:** Individual Satyagraha shows Congress testing the state's tolerance before moving to Quit India; argue that its limits justified the escalation in 1942.
**Back:** *"Oct 1940: one by one, Vinoba first, Nehru second, anti-war, not anti-Raj in full."*

### Flashcard MOD-4006 — Cripps Mission proposals (March–April 1942): provisions
**Front:** What did the Cripps proposals of 1942 offer India, and which provisions were built into the constitutional plan?
**Definition (learn):** The Cripps Mission (March–April 1942), led by Sir Stafford Cripps, offered dominion status after the war and a constitution-making body elected to frame a new constitution.
**Breakdown:**
- Offer: dominion status after the war; a constituent body to frame the constitution.
- Province option: a British Indian province could refuse the new constitution and retain its existing constitutional position (✅ owner 25 §1).
- Princely states: would join through their rulers' decisions, not through popular election (✅ owner 25 adv §1).
- Withheld: immediate responsible government and a national government during the war.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mission arrived in Delhi on 22 March 1942 per the article text (a note gives 23 March; a secondary note gives April 1942 for the mission) (✅ Wikipedia: Cripps Mission; date spread ⚠️); Stafford Cripps was a Labour member of Churchill's War Cabinet (✅ Wikipedia: Cripps Mission); Congress signalled opposition on 2 April 1942 (✅ Wikipedia: Cripps Mission, note 11).
**Links:** MOD-4007, MOD-4008, MOD-4021 (PYQ route), MOD-4024.
**Trap:** Cripps offered post-war dominion status, not immediate transfer of power; it did not concede full independence.
**Mains hook:** Cripps failed because it offered a future constitution to people demanding a present government, and gave provincial opt-out to a movement premised on national unity (✅ owner 25 §8.10 verdict scaffold).
**Back:** *"Cripps: dominion after the war, a constitution-maker later, an exit for provinces now."*

### Flashcard MOD-4007 — Cripps rejection and Gandhi's "post-dated cheque"
**Front:** Why did Congress reject the Cripps proposals, and what did Gandhi call them?
**Definition (learn):** Congress rejected the Cripps proposals because they offered no immediate responsible government and because provincial non-accession endangered unity. Gandhi called the offer a "post-dated cheque drawn on a failing bank".
**Breakdown:**
- Congress objection 1: no immediate responsible government; the war effort would proceed without a national government.
- Congress objection 2: the provincial opt-out threatened national unity.
- Gandhi's image: a cheque that promised freedom later while the bank (British control) was failing now (✅ Wikipedia: Cripps Mission; ✅ owner 25).
- Muslim League: Jinnah said the proposals did not clearly concede Pakistan; the League also rejected them (✅ Wikipedia: Cripps Mission).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Congress stopped talks, signalled opposition on 2 April 1942 (✅ Wikipedia: Cripps Mission, note 11); "post-dated cheque" (✅ Wikipedia: Cripps Mission).
**Links:** MOD-4006, MOD-4008, MOD-4009.
**Trap:** Gandhi's "post-dated cheque" is about Cripps (1942), not the August Offer (1940). The League rejected Cripps for the opposite reason to Congress: it did not concede Pakistan.
**Mains hook:** Contrast the Congress rejection (unity and present power) with the League's rejection (Pakistan not recognised). Both rejections closed the wartime constitutional route.
**Back:** *"Cheque post-dated; Congress wanted cash now, Jinnah wanted Pakistan named."*

### Flashcard MOD-4008 — Stafford Cripps: the 1942 mission vs the 1946 Cabinet Mission
**Front:** Who was Stafford Cripps, and how do his 1942 and 1946 roles differ?
**Definition (learn):** Stafford Cripps led the 1942 Mission and later sat on the 1946 Cabinet Mission. The two missions are separate events with different members and different purposes.
**Breakdown:**
- 1942: solo mission (Labour member of Churchill's War Cabinet), dominion status after war, constituent body, provincial opt-out (✅ Wikipedia: Cripps Mission).
- 1946: one of three Cabinet Mission members with Pethick-Lawrence and A.V. Alexander; objective a united India with a weak centre and grouping (✅ Wikipedia: Cabinet Mission; ✅ owner 26).
- Relation: the 1946 Mission proposed a Constituent Assembly, which the 1942 offer had also foreshadowed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1942 Cripps Mission (March–April 1942); 1946 Cabinet Mission (arrived Delhi 24 March 1946) (✅ Wikipedia: Cabinet Mission).
**Links:** MOD-4006, MOD-4007, MOD-4042, MOD-4053.
**Trap:** Do not merge the two Cripps missions into one event; the 1946 Cabinet Mission rejected Pakistan, while the 1942 offer did not settle the Pakistan question either.
**Mains hook:** A chronological answer on constitutional negotiations should show how the 1942 offer's constituent-assembly idea returned in 1946 with grouping.
**Back:** *"Two Cripps, two missions: '42 offered after the war, '46 proposed a union with groups."*

### Flashcard MOD-4009 — Quit India resolution: AICC Bombay, 8 August 1942
**Front:** When and where was the Quit India resolution adopted, and what did it demand?
**Definition (learn):** On 8 August 1942, the All-India Congress Committee at Bombay adopted the Quit India resolution demanding an immediate British withdrawal from India. Gandhi gave the "Do or Die" call in his speech.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 8 August 1942.
- Body: AICC session at Bombay.
- Demand: immediate end of British rule (immediate British withdrawal).
- Venue: owner says the AICC adopted it at Gowalia Tank.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 8 August 1942 (✅ owner 25; ✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement). ⚠️ Owner-vs-source conflict on venue: the owner places the resolution's adoption at Gowalia Tank; Wikipedia places Gandhi's speech at the Gowalia Tank Maidan and says the Wardha draft "was passed at Bombay". Treat the Gowalia Tank venue for the resolution as ⚠️ until checked against a primary source.
**Links:** MOD-4010, MOD-4011, MOD-4013, MOD-4020 (PYQ route).
**Trap:** Quit India Resolution is 8 August 1942; arrests are 9 August 1942. Cripps (March–April 1942) comes before both.
**Mains hook:** Quit India as the final mass refusal: use the resolution's Bombay context and the arrests that followed to argue that the movement's form was decided by the government.
**Back:** *"Eight August, Bombay: quit now; nine August: the leaders were gone."*

### Flashcard MOD-4010 — "Do or Die" and the 9 August 1942 arrests
**Front:** What was Gandhi's "Do or Die" call, and what happened within hours of the resolution?
**Definition (learn):** "Do or Die" was Gandhi's call in the Quit India speech; on 9 August 1942 almost the entire top Congress leadership was arrested, leaving the movement leaderless from the outset.
**Breakdown:**
- Call: "Do or Die" (also rendered as "We shall either free India or die in the attempt") (✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement).
- Government response: leaders jailed "within hours" of the speech (✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement).
- Consequence: a leaderless upsurge with local initiative and underground direction (✅ owner 25 §8.5).
- Analytical point: the 24-hour gap between resolution and arrests explains the movement's shape (owner's "decapitation thesis").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 8 August 1942 resolution; 9 August 1942 arrests (✅ owner 25 §8.5).
**Links:** MOD-4009, MOD-4011, MOD-4018.
**Trap:** Do not claim that the Congress leadership planned a centrally directed campaign throughout; the owner flags arrests as the point where central direction broke down.
**Mains hook:** The government, not the Congress, decided the form of 1942 by arresting the leadership; argue this with the decapitation thesis and qualify with the Congress's pre-arrest call.
**Back:** *"Do or Die on the 8th; the jail doors on the 9th."*

### Flashcard MOD-4011 — Quit India's mass upsurge: forms of action
**Front:** What forms did the 1942 upsurge take after the leaders were arrested?
**Definition (learn):** After 9 August 1942 the movement became a leaderless popular upsurge featuring strikes, processions, attacks on symbols of authority, damage to communications, underground activity and local seizure of authority.
**Breakdown:**
- Urban phase: strikes, hartals, student action (⚠️ owner's phase description).
- Rural phase: attacks on railways, telegraph lines, post offices and police stations (✅ owner 25 §8.5).
- Underground phase: clandestine organisation, underground bulletins and clandestine radio (✅ owner 25 adv §1).
- Parallel authority in pockets (see MOD-4012).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Movement "leaderless upsurge" (✅ owner 25); no official count of arrests, casualties or parallel governments is supplied in the owners; do not state them.
**Links:** MOD-4010, MOD-4012, MOD-4014, MOD-4019 (violence trap).
**Trap:** "Leaderless" does not mean "purposeless": the movement retained underground continuity (✅ owner 25 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the three forms to answer "Was Quit India spontaneous or organised?": spontaneous at the local level, organised in its underground and parallel structures.
**Back:** *"Strikes, rails, wires, police posts; underground when the doors shut."*

### Flashcard MOD-4012 — Parallel governments: Ballia, Tamluk and Satara
**Front:** Which parallel governments emerged during Quit India, and what is each known for?
**Definition (learn):** Parallel governments were local attempts to exercise authority where British administration had collapsed in 1942: the best-known are Ballia, Tamluk (Jatiya Sarkar) and Satara (Prati Sarkar).
**Breakdown:**
- Tamluk (Midnapore): the Tamluk National Government, which continued until Gandhi asked its leaders to disband in 1944 (✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement).
- Ballia (eastern UP): local insurgents overthrew district administration and released Congress leaders; the British needed weeks to reassert control (✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement; no date given).
- Satara (Maharashtra): listed as a local-impact area; the Prati Sarkar name is not confirmed in the fetched text (⚠️).
- Functions: owner says some performed administrative, judicial and relief functions before repression (✅ owner 25 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Tamluk = Jatiya Sarkar (owner) / Tamluk National Government (Wikipedia), disbanded 1944 (✅ Wikipedia); Satara = Prati Sarkar (✅ owner 25 §2, ⚠️ name not confirmed by fetch).
**Links:** MOD-4011, MOD-4018, MOD-4019.
**Trap:** Parallel governments were regional and temporary; they were not symbolic only, but they were not a national alternative government either.
**Mains hook:** Argue that parallel governments showed Quit India was "more than protest" in pockets, while qualifying that the British restored order within weeks or months.
**Back:** *"Ballia broke the jail, Tamluk ran a government, Satara had its own Sarkar."*

### Flashcard MOD-4013 — Aruna Asaf Ali: AICC session and the flag
**Front:** What is Aruna Asaf Ali associated with in 1942?
**Definition (learn):** Aruna Asaf Ali is the leading figure associated with the August 1942 AICC session and the hoisting of the flag during the movement.
**Breakdown:**
- Role: presided over the AICC session on 9 August and hoisted the flag (✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement).
- Owner link: flag-hoisting at Gowalia Tank (✅ owner 25 §4). ⚠️ Wikipedia does not tie the flag to Gowalia Tank.
- Underground: named among underground leaders (✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Presided over AICC session 9 August 1942 (✅ Wikipedia); owner's Gowalia Tank flag claim ⚠️.
**Links:** MOD-4009, MOD-4015.
**Trap:** Do not attach Aruna Asaf Ali to the Secret Congress Radio; that is Usha Mehta (MOD-4014).
**Mains hook:** Optional: use her as an example of women's leadership in the underground phase.
**Back:** *"Aruna hoisted the flag; Usha ran the radio."*

### Flashcard MOD-4014 — Usha Mehta and the Congress Radio
**Front:** What was the Congress Radio, and who is associated with it?
**Definition (learn):** The Congress Radio was a clandestine broadcasting station used during Quit India to spread messages when leaders were underground; Usha Mehta is the name associated with it.
**Breakdown:**
- Function: broadcast messages during the underground phase (✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement, "clandestine radio stations").
- Person: Usha Mehta (✅ owner 25 §2; ✅ Wikipedia names a book on "Congress Radio: Usha Mehta and the Underground Radio Station of 1942").
- Limit: the fetched text does not describe her operational role in detail (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Secret Congress Radio, 1942 underground phase (✅ owner 25; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MOD-4011, MOD-4013.
**Trap:** Usha Mehta is linked to the radio, not to the flag hoisting, and not to a parallel government.
**Mains hook:** Use her with Aruna Asaf Ali to show women's role in the underground phase, carefully.
**Back:** *"Usha: secret radio, not the flag."*

### Flashcard MOD-4015 — Underground leaders: JP Narayan, Lohia and others
**Front:** Who were the key underground leaders of Quit India, and what conflict arises in the sources?
**Definition (learn):** The owners name Jayaprakash Narayan and Ram Manohar Lohia as underground socialist leaders; the Wikipedia text names other underground leaders.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner list: Jayaprakash Narayan, Ram Manohar Lohia, Aruna Asaf Ali, Usha Mehta (✅ owner 25 §2).
- Wikipedia list: Achyut Rao Patwardhan, Aruna Asaf Ali, Sucheta Kripalani, Daljit Singh and Nana Patil as underground leaders (✅ Wikipedia: Quit India Movement).
- Wikipedia names Narayan only as a supporter of the disobedience movement, and does not name Lohia at all in the fetched text (⚠️ conflict).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner-vs-source conflict on the underground role of Narayan and Lohia; treat the owner's claim as needing verification.
**Links:** MOD-4013, MOD-4014, MOD-4011.
**Trap:** Do not state a Narayan or Lohia underground role as settled fact until verified; the question-answer may hinge on the named leader in an option.
**Mains hook:** Use the broader underground network (socialists, women, local activists) rather than naming one leader.
**Back:** *"Underground names differ across books: check before you cram."*

### Flashcard MOD-4016 — Political alignments during Quit India: League, Communists, princes
**Front:** How did the League, the Communists, the princes and the Hindu Mahasabha position themselves during Quit India?
**Definition (learn):** Quit India was not a united national front.
